THE ART OF

SCREEN ADAPTATION
Top Writers Reveal Their Craft
ALISTAIR OWEN
creative ESSENTIALS

Introduction
My Þrst screenplay commission was an adaptation. By the time IÊhad sketched out a screen story with the producer and was sitting down tostart the script, the location had shifted from Devon to New England,the leads had aged from early teenagers to young adults, and the lastthird of the novel had been jettisoned. The novel didnÕt inspire me, so that wasnÕt a problem. But cut adrift from it, without the compass of the source material or a compelling alternative course, I wrote rubbish
Ð and that was a problem. We had barely begun the second passbefore I, too, was jettisoned. I learned some good lessons, though. Never adapt a book you donÕt love. Never accept a job just for the work. And never, ever, tell yourself, ÔIÕll Þx it in the next draft,Õ because you might not get a chance to write one.
I subsequently turned out other, better adaptations Ð and my Þrst rewrite; adaptation of a different kind. Even so, when Creative Essentials suggested I write a ÔHow ToÕ book on the subject, I still had one key question: can I really tell other people how to adapt when I canÕt back it up with a produced adaptation of my own? The answer was no, I couldnÕt. What I could do, however, was assemble some of the best screenwriters in the business and ask them how it was done. So I did, and here they are: 12 writers, 22 case studies and one career retrospective, exploring adaptations of modern and classic novels, nonÞction books and stage plays, on Þlm and television, in the UK and Hollywood. None of the interviewees has fewer than Þve produced adaptations on their CVs Ð and some of them have scripted a lot more Ð offering a unique and varied insight into the art and craftof adapting for the screen.
Each interview explores the writerÕs general approach to adaptationbefore focusing on two speciÞc case studies Ð with the exception of the conversation with Andrew Davies, perhaps screen adaptationÕs most proliÞc practitioner, where I took the opportunity to couple the set questions with a broader tour of his extensive career. Otherwise, the set questions asked depend on the case studies discussed: Þction, or nonÞction, or both. The case studies were my choice in every instance, and were chosen to provide as wide and as recent a range of feature Þlms and television series as possible Ð although I have deliberatelynot included plot synopses, in the hope that readers will be inspired to go on the same journey I made through the source material and resulting adaptations. I have, on the other hand, included interviews with two writers I have talked to previously Ð Hossein Amini (in Storyand Character: Interviews with British Screenwriters, Bloomsbury, 2002) and Christopher Hampton (in Hampton on Hampton, Faber, 2005) Ð as both can claim signiÞcant adaptation credits since those books appeared, and both have had a considerable inuence on myown writing over the years.
At this point, my thanks must go to all the interviewees for so generously giving up their time, and to their various agents and publishers (particularly Mary Mount at Penguin and Dinah Wood at Faber) for putting me in touch with them. Extra thanks to my Þrst interviewee (chronologically if not alphabetically), Jeremy Brock, who helped me road-test the Q&A format and has remained an unfailing source of encouragement throughout. A big thank you, too, to the entire team at Creative Essentials: Ion, Lisa, Ellie, Elsa, Jennifer, Clare and Claire Ð and my editor, Hannah Patterson, who enthusiasticallyembraced my adaptation of her original pitch. Thanks also to BethanyDavies for her invaluable transcription skills; to Rob Benton and Daniel Rosenthal for their insightful comments on the manuscript(twice over to Daniel for thoughtfully pointing Hannah in mydirection in the Þrst place); and to my book and screen agents at SheilLand Associates, Gaia Banks and Lucy Fawcett, for all their efforts on my behalf while this project was under way. And a huge thank you,Þnally, to my friends and my parents Ð and especially to Louise Halfpenny, the best cheerleader any writer could hope for.
Alistair Owen January 2020

Hossein Amini
Hossein Amini was born in 1966 in Tehran, Iran.
His screen adaptation credits include: Jude (1996, novel Jude the Obscure by Thomas Hardy); The Wings of the Dove (1997,novel by Henry James); *Drive* (2011, novel by James Sallis); Our Kind of Traitor (2016, novel by John le Carr); and The Alienist (2017, novel by Caleb Carr). He also wrote and directed The Two Faces of January (2014), based on the novel by Patricia Highsmith, and co-created and co-wrote the BBC series McMaa (2018), based on the book by Misha Glenny.
The Wings of the Dove was Oscar- and BAFTA-nominated for Best Adapted Screenplay.
APPROACHES TO ADAPTATION
Do you prefer to adapt material which chimes with your own work, or material which is completely di erent and gives you a chance to try out new things?
I like to do both. I think theyÕre almost different challenges, especiallydoing them consecutively. After working on something personal,something I wanted to explore more deeply, the idea of going on an intellectual holiday by reading up on stuff that I donÕt know anythingabout is actually really attractive. Research has always been the bit Ilove most, learning about a completely new subject, and usually IÕll choose assignments based on how interested I am in those subjects.Often they tend to be history, because that was something I studied and have always been quite passionate about. But if a sci-Þ thing comes along and I like the idea of researching space and stars and thecreation of planets, then IÕll go, ÔYeah, thatÕll be fun.Õ I donÕt necessarily only think about the source material; sometimes the work around it is just as interesting.
Do you think adaptations involve a completely di erent set of creative gears to original screenplays?
Yes and no. For me, the research is equally important in both. With an adaptation, IÕll always read 20 other books to inform what IÕm writing. Likewise with an original, IÕll probably read around it. In that way itÕs the same. There are a couple of adaptations IÕve done where the dialogue was there, scenes were there. ThatÕs almost a different gig, because that becomes about selecting and Þlleting and Þlling in the gaps. I generally tend to do adaptations where thereÕs not a lot of scenes you can transcribe literally. Those interest me less, because IÕm more interested in the combination of what the source material has and what I can bring to it as an individual, with my own experiences.
Do you always agree a mission statement or direction of travel with whoever has commissioned the adaptation?
Probably not enough, and as IÕve got more experienced IÕve tried to do that more, just to be sure of what they want. Certainly in the past I used to love the element of mystery and discovery in the writingprocess, but IÕve come unstuck a few times, so I tend to study whoever is commissioning me a lot more carefully now and to have useful discussions with them before starting.
Do you usually produce an outline or treatment before you start writing the script?
That actually relates to the last question. ItÕs something I used to try toavoid doing, because it felt like writing something you already knew Ð and again I didnÕt want to lose that element of discovery, the excitement of a character surprising you or a scene coming out of nowhere. But more and more IÕve done the treatments, and I also prepare more before writing scenes. And you still get surprised. My scripts very rarely end up being that similar to the treatments, but at least IÕve got a road map Ð and someone else has had a chance to comment on that, so you can begin to learn what they want and what they donÕt want.
So the treatment becomes, if you like, the mission statement.
It becomes the mission statement, and it becomes a way of ironing outany differences between you before you start writing.
If the author of the source material is living, do you nd it useful to have their input on the script?
It depends. The one great experience I had at the script stage was the le Carr, Our Kind of Traitor, where I worked with him well before the eventual director came on board. He was one of my literaryheroes, so it was fantastic sitting with him for three days and goingthrough the script. James Sallis on *Drive* was incredibly gracious. I didnÕt sit with him, but he was incredibly nice about the whole thing. Generally itÕs something IÕd avoid in the writing process. ItÕs not even the pressure from them, itÕs that you can start to feel a responsibilityto them and, not necessarily for the best, be too faithful to their workbecause you like them too much. Caleb Carr, for example, on The Alienist. We had dinner after IÕd written the pilot, and he has such a huge, inspiring presence that then, writing a couple of the other episodes, I felt him over my shoulder the whole time. HeÕs prettyformidable.
Is it easier to navigate notes from directors, producers and scripteditors when you have a piece of source material to measure the screenplay against?
IÕm not sure it is, actually, because after the Þrst or second draft everyone whoÕs involved in the adaptation tends to leave the book behind and it really becomes a critique of the script. Sometimes youÕll go back to the book and say, ÔWasnÕt this a great piece that we missed?Õ or theyÕll go back and read it and say, ÕWe think this bit is useful,Õ but on the whole I Þnd, certainly as an arbiter between director, producer and writer, it doesnÕt usually happen.
Have you ever started work on an adaptation and found it harder to adapt than you anticipated?
Every single time. IÕll have an emotional reaction to a book, a moment where I feel, ÔI can capture the essence of that,Õ but thatÕs a much shorter reading experience. When youÕre actually writing it, day by day, itÕs hard to retain whatever that sensation, that feeling, that emotion was. Some days you feel it strongly and other days you donÕt.So the excitement of Þnishing a book and going, ÔI know exactly howto do that,Õ often very quickly turns into a much harder slog.
Have you ever been o ered material to adapt which you felt couldnÕt, or shouldnÕt, be adapted?
If I turn books down itÕs usually not because I think theyÕre so greatthat they shouldnÕt be adapted. Almost never, because thereÕs a challenge in taking on the great books. I mostly turn down books because they donÕt resonate with me personally, or I donÕt feel I can bring my own experiences to them Ð both emotional and life experiences, but particularly emotional. If I donÕt feel thereÕs enough space for me in them, then it becomes more of a technical exercise, cutting some bits and keeping others, and thatÕs not very interesting.
Are there any screen adaptations which you think are especially good or you particularly admire?
Anthony MinghellaÕs adaptations of *The English Patient* and The Talented Mr. Ripley. He brought his own sensitivity and personality to both books, and transformed them from page to screen. TheyÕreadaptations but theyÕre also originals, and could only have been reimagined in that way by an artist like Minghella. For me, theyÕreclassic examples of how adapted screenplays are no less an art form than original screenplays.

ADAPTING FICTION
Some writers try to include as much of the novel as possible by boiling scenes down to their essence. Others are more ruthless in editing it down to a sort of greatest hits, but being true to the spirit of it. Do you favour either of those approaches?
I got a bit burnt on my Þrst adaptation, which was an adaptation of Jude the Obscure. On the whole the reviews were pretty good, but there were a few negative ones which said that it was a reduction of Thomas HardyÕs great work Ð and in a way it was, because it was one of those books where the scenes were pretty much complete and it was a case of choosing the best ones, what you describe as the ÔgreatesthitsÕ. After that, I tended to avoid books which were obvious adaptations. The Wings of the Dove, for example, had virtually no scenes that were written; it was all trains of thought and explainedretrospectively, so I had to invent those scenes. *Drive*, similarly, had an incredible tone and a fantastic character, but the incidents in the script were often quite different to what was in the book, so again it allowed room for invention. So I try not to fall into the trap of the greatesthits, because I think thatÕs the worst kind of adaptation, because itÕs never going to live up to the book. Whereas if you can turn an adaptation into its own story, which is inspired by the original bookbut not enslaved to it, then I think thatÕs the best and certainly for me the most exciting way of writing.
If a novel has an unusual structure, would you try to reproduce that in adapting it?
Yes, because thatÕs part of what draws you to the book Ð though it can also be one of the traps. IÕm working on an adaptation of a nonÞction book about Hiroshima, Shockwave, which has a very holistic view, with lots of different stories. I thought the only way you could tell that story was to take every single aspect of it, from the pilots on Tinian to the politicians in Potsdam to the people on the ground in Hiroshima, and turn it into one of those multi-narrative stories. Similarly, thereÕs a book IÕm adapting called A Terrible Splendor, about a German tennis player in the 1930s, which is structured around a Þve-set tennis match, and after each set there are ashbacks to the lives of both players. I loved the idea of that structure, but IÕve had to make compromises because it was becoming too rigid and almost too neat, so in the end IÕve just kept the essence of it. But structure is one of the interesting things about telling a story, so IÕd absolutely bear it in mind Ð particularly now, post-Dunkirk, when I think traditional, three-act, linear structure is more and more under siege anyway.
Do you try to avoid voiceover in adapting a rst-person narrative, or do you see it as another tool in the toolbox?
I generally donÕt like voiceover. I feel that unless itÕs absolutely organic to the storytelling, itÕs best avoided. But then The Thin Red Line, which is one of my favourite Þlms, uses it absolutely beautifully. So IÕm not completely against it, but IÕm certainly against it when itÕs used as a band-aid or a lazy way of getting inside peopleÕs heads: ÔI donÕt understand this character. Can we have some voiceover?Õ I think there are more interesting ways of telling that in cinema.
Do you use the language of the novel in writing the screenplay, ordo you put the whole thing in your own words?
I put it in my own words, but thatÕs often affected by the way the book is written. *Drive*, for example, had a very terse, Þlm noir style, and I followed that to a certain extent. And itÕs quite fun to write in different styles. Obviously with period Ð Thomas Hardy, say Ð youwouldnÕt write the stage directions that way, but even then IÕd probably use a word which I wouldnÕt have used in a modern adaptation. But I donÕt think it really affects the writing that much.
And dialogue? If you like a line, do you tend to keep it?
Yes.
And what would you do if dialogue felt very literary?
Going back to Jude and The Wings of the Dove, one of the issues I had with literary, particularly nineteenthÐcentury, dialogue was that itÕs often so wordy and elaborate that itÕs hard for an audience to follow. Sometimes that language becomes a pleasure in its own right,but what IÕve tried to do is to simplify it without being anachronistic. IhavenÕt always succeeded, but that was the aim. IÕve worked on two adaptations set in nineteenth-century America, The Alienist and Gangs of New York, and thereÕs an actual recording of an Irish guytalking in the Bowery; it was fascinating to listen to, but the slang was so extreme that you couldnÕt understand a word. So you have to make allowances for audiences.
Do you keep the novel beside you throughout the adaptationprocess, or do you try to internalise it and set it aside somewherealong the way?
Physically, I keep copies of the novel everywhere: one in my ofÞce, one by the bed, probably one in the bathroom. But what I tend to do is read it pretty thoroughly and with each draft go back to it less and less. Then towards the end IÕll have another read just to see if thereÕs any great nugget IÕve missed or forgotten about, or left out bymistake. Generally itÕs a love affair that cools off, I guess.
How much pressure do you feel when adapting a well-known andmuch-loved novel, knowing that you wonÕt be able to please everyone with the nished product?
I donÕt really think about it while IÕm writing. Maybe when the thing comes out. But IÕve noticed a change recently: critics and audiences will still be really brutal about a Þlm, but it wonÕt necessarily be because it didnÕt live up to the expectations generated by the book. Even on The Alienist, which had a very hard-core fan base, that fan base was relatively small compared to the other stuff which getsthrown at a Þlm or TV series. That one got a few criticisms because 20 years had passed, and what Caleb Carr had done was so originalthat in that time other people had imitated it, so the series itself felt like an imitation of series which came before. So there were some comparisons to the book there, but on the whole I havenÕt had too many outraged people saying IÕve destroyed the book.

ADAPTING NONFICTION
YouÕve said that research is the part of the process you love most.With a non ction adaptation how much use do you make of the authorÕs research?
IÕll look at the authorÕs bibliography and Þnd the books that are most interesting to me Ð but IÕll also go through bookshops and look at lists on Amazon and so on. Because IÕm so passionate about the reading, itÕs really exciting for me to do that. I wouldnÕt get a researcher to do it for me. A couple of times production companies have offered me researchers, and IÕve said no. I donÕt want to read the condensed version, I want to read the whole thing.
Do you approach a non ction adaptation in the same way as
ction, or does the strangeness of real events give you licence toexperiment with, say, structure?
With the nonÞction books IÕve done, there has been so little to draw on in terms of speciÞc events and scenes that theyÕre almost like originals: youÕre inventing the story rather than reinventing it. So whatI do much more with nonÞction is to plot it out and write treatments and do step outlines, because that inventing of the story is so critical.
Have you ever encountered events in a non ction book so strange that you couldnÕt use them in the adaptation because audiences wouldnÕt believe they were real?
On McMaa, Misha Glenny told us a story which wasnÕt in the book, about a mob boss whose hobby was dog shows, and we included that in the series, because even though it felt weirdly unbelievable, it had a surrealism which made it oddly credible. So you have to strike a balance between something being so big that itÕll throw audiences andtruth being more interesting than Þction.
How much responsibility do you feel towards historical accuracy when dramatising the lives of real characters?
A fair amount. With Hiroshima, I certainly feel a responsibility to try not to make the characters Þt an argument. It would be very easy to paint certain people as war criminals because of that massive decision, but it was made in the context of a hatred of what the Japanese had done and a genuine desire to try to stop the war. That doesnÕt mean that I condone what happened, but IÕve tried to create three-dimensional characters with arguments back and forth. The tennis story is different, because itÕs an individualÕs life. IÕll certainly stop myself being historically inaccurate if I feel it crosses the line too much, but at the same time you have to turn that person into a dramatic character. So again, itÕs a balance.
And would you continue to hold that line in the face of notes which told you that it was historically accurate but not dramatic enough?
IÕd have to address that note. IÕll still feel I canÕt go beyond the line, but it will make me question how I can make it more dramatic without being somehow untruthful to the reality. I quite like that challenge, though. What it tells me is that IÕve probably been too accurate, or I havenÕt been clever enough within that accuracy.
How do you set about nding the voices of real characters, especially if there is little or no written or audio-visual research material in their own words?
IÕll read any stuff theyÕve written or quotes theyÕve given, but with all adaptations I need to Þnd my own voice in their voices. IÕll imaginewhat emotions theyÕre going through, or try to draw on similar experiences IÕve had or put myself in their shoes in that situation, but their voices probably end up being closer to mine than they do to theirs.
Do you think that being based on a true story in some way gives aproject more weight?
I personally donÕt, but the Academy tends to: you can judge by the number of actors who win Best Actor awards for portraying real people. I love history so I love writing it, but itÕs hard to argue that a true story has more weight than Crime and Punishment or War and Peace. I think Þction can be just as weighty philosophically, thematically and emotionally as true stories.
Are there any benets which you think true stories denitely do bring to the table?
One of the great pleasures of adapting McMaa was that there was an authenticity to the tone, and that notion of authenticity is important to me. But something Peter Morgan does really well is taking those true stories and heightening them to the point where they become myth or comedy or great tragedy, so there are deÞnitely two ways of looking at it. What draws me to true stories is the idea that this is the reality behind the news and the question of how to capture that, andone of the ways to capture it is to be as realistic as you can tonally.

CASE STUDY: *DRIVE*
US, 2011 ¥ Directed by Nicolas Winding Refn ¥ Produced byMarc Platt, Adam Siegel, Gigi Pritzker, Michel Litvak, John Palermo ¥ Screenplay by Hossein Amini, based on the novel by James Sallis ¥ Cast: Ryan Gosling (*Drive*r), Carey Mulligan(Irene), Bryan Cranston (Shannon), Christina Hendricks (Blanche), Ron Perlman (Nino), Oscar Isaac (Standard Gabriel), Albert Brooks (Bernie Rose)
YouÕve dabbled in lm noir before: Killshot, from Elmore LeonardÕs novel; an unproduced adaptation of Dorothy HughesÕ In a Lonely Place; and the noir elements which you brought to, or brought out of, The Wings of the Dove. But *Drive* feels like your most decisive step into that territory.
ItÕs probably the one thatÕs closest to the traditional Þlm noirs that I liked, and also to the neo-noirs of Melville and the American Þlms of the seventies. Killshot, with Elmore LeonardÕs humour, is a different kind of noir to *Drive*, which is very much that spare, samurai-typecharacter in the middle of L.A. Ð which is such a noir city anyway.
The book is very aware of other books: itÕs dedicated to Ed McBain, Donald Westlake and Lawrence Block, and its narrator mentions, among others, George Pelecanos and WestlakeÕs alter ego Richard Stark. The lm, on the other hand, is very aware of othersÕ lms: Walter HillÕs The *Drive*r and Michael MannÕs Thief in particular, along with John BoormanÕs Richard Stark adaptation, Point Blank. Were you aware of all those reference points, bothliterary and cinematic, and how did you navigate between them?
I was very aware of those Þlms. Le Samourai was another one and some of those Clint Eastwood westerns Ð the idea of The Man With No Name. But probably the Þlm that inuenced me most was Shane: the stranger who comes in and takes care of the family and leaves at the end. ItÕs interesting, because I was probably more inuenced by thereferences you just mentioned, from the late sixties and seventies, andNicolas Refn, the director, was more inuenced by the eighties. He brought a David Lynch strokeÉ who was the director who used to do those very colourful teen romance moviesÉ?
John Hughes?
Yeah. Almost a John Hughes element to it. And also his inuences in terms of exploitation cinema. So the combination of those two sensibilities means that the references are a bit crazy. And obviouslyitÕs set in Hollywood and the character is a stunt *drive*r, so itÕs kind of a Þlm about Þlm.
*Drive*rÕs job as a stunt *drive*r was retained, but his screenwriter friend Manny was dropped. HeÕs quite a prominent character in the book, not least as someone *Drive*r can talk to. Did you feel thecharacter was redundant, or simply too self-referential?
That was the Þrst thing: it was a bit self-referential. And the idea of him having friends: I was more interested in the loner aspect. I also felt I could get some of those Manny elements into Shannon and combine the two characters: Shannon is a much smaller part in the book. But youÕre right, it was mainly my slight discomfort with the notion of a writer in this Þlm noir world.
ItÕs a short novel with a sparse style, very cinematic at rst glance, yet itÕs probably the novel youÕve adapted most freely. What were you keen to keep in terms of tone and story, and whatdid you know you would have to change or lose?
I loved the central character. HeÕs brilliantly described in the book. The slow pace at which he lives and his whole ambling nature, and then this sudden turn into violence. So the tone and the rhythm I totally kept. What it didnÕt have was a story that goes from A to B to C, and builds. It was more internal. So I knew I had to invent a story, which is why I spent quite a long time outlining it. But it was always informed by the brilliance of SallisÕs characterisation of *Drive*r, and also the fantastic tone. McMaa was the same: again, I followed tone most closely, IÕd say.
Tone is so important in adaptations. How do you set about translating tone from the pages of a book to the pages of a screenplay, and guard that tone in the transition from the page tothe screen?
IÕve always believed that stage directions are really important in a screenplay, because youÕre basically transcribing what you see, whether or not the director chooses to follow that. IÕve noticed more and more that screenwriting books say you shouldnÕt describe things because thatÕs the directorÕs job, but I Þnd the only way you can capture tone is by describing atmosphere and describing charactersÕ inner emotions. IÕm interested in dialogue which isnÕt explicit, so IÕll tend to write stage directions between lines of dialogue to capture pauses and unspoken reactions. The same with the description of a room, or the light. Those things are really important and the only way I can capture them is to describe them, which is probably why I tend to overwrite for some peopleÕs taste. I almost describe things the way you would in a novel, and thatÕs going to be informed by the tone of the book.
The novel may have a sparse style, but it also has a discursive structure: ashbacks, not just to recent events, but to *Drive*rÕs arrival in L.A. and his life before that Ð right back to his troubled childhood. Did you feel that a lm called *Drive* required a more propulsive, straight-line narrative?
Yes, I did, and also it evolved over the various drafts. So in the early drafts, based largely on studio notes, there was a lot more expositionabout *Drive*rÕs past, even though there were no ashbacks. There was a constant note to make *Drive*r more accessible, and it was somethingI resisted because I loved the idea of The Man With No Name, the avenging angel who comes out of nowhere and you donÕt know anything about him. Then when Nic and Ryan Gosling came on, that was certainly what they were drawn to, so a lot of that exposition got cut out and the thing became sparse again. The Þnal draft was almost closer to the Þrst draft than it was to the second or third draft.
*Drive*r talks slightly more in the novel, and we also have access tohis interior life via the third-person narration. In the absence of
ashbacks or voiceover, do you think that does a ect his accessibility?
I think you write two drafts of a screenplay: one for the reader and one for when it gets made. When youÕre writing something to be read,dialogue is often helping tell the story. Then, when you get into it, the actors Ð someone like Ryan, whoÕs brilliant about wanting fewer lines
Ð will look at it and go, ÔI donÕt need to say that,Õ or ÔI can say that more simply,Õ and so it re-evolves. They also bring their face: itÕs as literal as that. The likeability and the persona of that actor, his face and his features with the camera up close, start to do a lot of the storytelling for you. ThereÕs a scene that was really telling, actually, the Þrst time *Drive*r faces off with his love interestÕs husband, Standard. In the script, I wrote an unspoken competition and tensionbetween them, but Ryan played it almost as if he was unaware of why this guy should be jealous. There was an innocence about the way he played it, and thatÕs a dimension an actor comes in and brings.However well-rounded the characters are, theyÕre still on the page, so when all of those nuances come into it Ð in a face, in a gesture, in an expression Ð you need far less dialogue. ThatÕs why I think writers working with actors is an incredibly important part of the process.Nic was great at allowing me to do that, whereas other directors have been far more protective of their actors, and as a result IÕve felt I wasnÕt able to do my best work. HeÕd go off and do location scouts and be totally relaxed leaving the actors to give me notes. But some directors want to be there with you every second. And other directorsjust donÕt want you there at all.
ItÕs Chapter 6 of the novel before thereÕs any actual driving, and even then itÕs barely a two-page scene. You put *Drive*rÕs rst getaway right up front, nearly six pages in the script. Presumably you wanted to establish who he is and what he does as quickly and dramatically as possible?
We wanted to establish who he is and what he does, but there was also a rhythm element to it. The story takes a little while to unravel, and thereÕs no real action after that until the violence kicks in halfway, so I felt I needed a very propulsive scene at the beginning: to give the audience a taste of what to expect, to tell them it was a thriller about a getaway *drive*r, and to buy myself time to do 20 or 30 pages of character development.
And to establish *Drive*rÕs world, the L.A. landscape he inhabits.
ThatÕs a classic case of where research was really important. There were three stages. The Þrst one, before any actors or a director came on, was that producer Adam Siegel and I went to meet the security guy at Universal, who were at that point the Þnanciers of it, and the Þrst thing he said was, ÔYou canÕt get away from police helicopters.Õ So the notion of him being underneath a structure, both the tunnel he hides in and his destination at the Staples Center, came from that conversation. Then, when I arrived in L.A. to start work, Adam got me a *drive*r Ð because I donÕt *drive* Ð to *drive* me around the areas near the Staples Center, so again that helped Þre my imagination. And then, when I was actually writing the scene, I had a map of that area of L.A., so I was plotting it out street by street. I had so much material that it was a challenge not to make it six pages.
HeÕs also listening to a basketball game on the radio during the scene, which helps guide you through it and adds another narrative element.
One of the dangers with action sequences, particularly car chases, is: whatÕs the story? If the story is simply, heÕs getting away, thereÕs only so much interest you can sustain in that. So the game helped create a sense of mystery, making the audience think: whyÕs he listening to this, whatÕs he up to?
The lm was originally slated to be directed by Neil Marshall with Hugh Jackman as *Drive*r Ð a very di erent team to Refn and Gosling. Did your approach to the material change accordingly?
ThatÕs when it was with Universal. I actually had some really good sessions with Neil Marshall. There was probably more horror in the script I wrote for him. For example, he wanted *Drive*r to chop off someoneÕs head at one stage. So elements like that, which I wasnÕt super-comfortable with, went fairly quickly. But the idea of *Drive*r getting his revenge on Nino without using a weapon was NeilÕs. In the Þrst draft he did it with a gun, and Neil said, ÔHeÕs a *drive*r, he should do it with a car,Õ and Nic took that on board. Hugh Jackman was also a great collaborator and very gracious, but the idea of someone as good looking as him, in his late thirties, not having had any pastrelationships of signiÞcance was a bit of a stretch, so we needed more backstory in that version of the script. Ryan was young enough to have drifted off the street and be working as a mechanic. There was something more abstract about him, which meant he needed less backstory.
In the novel, *Drive*r lives Ð though itÕs stated at the end that heÕs killed further down the line Ð and Irena, StandardÕs wife, dies. In the lm, where sheÕs called Irene, she lives and heÕs badly, possibly fatally, wounded. What was the thinking behind reversing the end points of those charactersÕ journeys?
The Irene story evolved through a whole series of drafts. We had one with her son being taken away. We had one with the man ending up with the boy because the mother is dead. But in the end we went back to a fairy-tale structure: the man ends up on his own, the cowboyrides into the sunset.
Shane again.
Exactly. That was where the Shane inuence came in. The script ended with him in the car and you think heÕs dead, then he opens his eyesand starts the engine. I think thatÕs how Nic originally had it in the cut, and then they felt they had to add that extension where you see him driving. But for me it was keeping it a bit more mysterious: does he live or does he die? And that was in keeping with my notion of himas the angel who comes in: is he a man, or is he a ghost?
Shannon is drawn from three characters in the novel: Manny, *Drive*rÕs agent and a fellow stuntman, whoÕs killed quite quickly in the book. YouÕve also given him a di erent occupation Ð garage owner Ð and a brand new ambition Ð racing stock-cars. Again, what prompted those changes and additions?
The stock-car idea came from the fact that the book didnÕt give us a plot, so we had to invent one Ð and when I say ÔweÕ, it was mainlywith Adam Siegel, who was the producer on the Þrst iteration. The idea of the friend who sees what a genius he is behind the wheel and wants to make something of that was probably inuenced by the character of the coach in the Þrst Rocky movie. He has these bigdreams and is someone things have never worked out for, and is also like the stock character you get in a lot of Þlm noirs, the slightly run.down buddy who was often played by those great character actors. He was very much a type, but he did help bring Bernie Rose into *Drive*rÕs world. Bernie was one of the characters I loved in the book, and I wanted them to have a moment of peace before they went to war, so I needed to engineer a meeting between them. So from ShannonÕs stock-car dream came the idea of Bernie putting money into it and the fact that they had a past, and that started to draw these characters together. There arenÕt a lot of links between the characters in the book, and thatÕs quite hard for a screenplay. You need to identify thesecondary characters from the beginning in order for them to pay offat the end, whereas Bernie comes in really quite late in the book.
Do you think you managed to be true to the tone of the novel?
I certainly felt IÕd captured it in the script, and I think Nic brought a whole other level to it in the way he directed it.
A lyricism.
Absolutely. The book and the script are probably closer to those seventies thrillers, with the very hard-core tough guy, but Ryan brought an innocence to the character and Nic brought a surreal, sort of magical quality to the Þlm, and those three things meshed reallywell.
And do you know what James Sallis thought of the nished lm?
One of the most gratifying things IÕve had in my career was how full of praise James Sallis was for the Þlm. Adam was very good about keeping him included every step of the way. He didnÕt want to read anything, but he was very supportive. HeÕs a poet as much as a novelist, and I hope the Þlm brought more recognition to his work. IÕm a huge fan, so it was a really happy experience in that sense.
CASE STUDY: McMAFIA
UK, 2018 ¥ Directed by James Watkins ¥ Produced by Paul Ritchie ¥ Created by Hossein Amini & James Watkins, based on the book by Misha Glenny ¥ Written by Hossein Amini, James Watkins, David Farr, Laurence Coriat, Peter Harness ¥ Cast: James Norton (Alex Godman), David Strathairn (SemiyonKleiman), Juliet Rylance (Rebecca Harper), Merab Ninidze (Vadim Kalyagin), David Dencik (Boris Godman), AlekseySerebryakov (Dmitri Godman), Sofya Lebedeva (Lyudmilla Nikolayeva)
Non ction adaptations are very popular at the moment, both in lm and TV, with their factual basis usually one of the selling points. ItÕs more unusual for a non ction book to be turned into a
ctional story, which is what you and James Watkins did with Misha GlennyÕs McMa a. Was that always your pitch, or did you consider sticking more closely to fact?
I actually pitched it as a Þlm to Working Title seven years earlier, where I took four of the stories and wanted to tell a Traf-typeversion of it. But by the time it came around again as a TV series, that approach didnÕt feel right. It felt like it had been done. Traf, the original TV series, did it. Babel did it. So we had to Þnd a new way to do it that would incorporate the scale and tone of it, and we came upwith a completely new story which was like a spine for other stories to be attached to.
Not only did you turn non ction into ction, you also introduced some lm genre elements: the nal confrontation between Alex and Vadim reminded me strongly of the ending of Michael MannÕs Heat, and the series as a whole has clear echoes of The Godfather. Were those reference points in your mind from the start as you created the new storyline?
Heat has always been a massive inspiration for me: the notion of two enemies who meet at one stage as friends Ð the same as Bernie Rose and *Drive*r Ð and who, in a different world, could have been friends. And The Godfather was also a huge inspiration Ð inevitably, when you tell a story of an ordinary person sucked into a world heÕs trying not to get into. So, structurally The Godfather was the biggest inuence: the anti-hero that the audience initially sympathises with, then his descent. But spiritually IÕd say it was Heat: the love/hate relationship between these two antagonists.
You mentioned the TV series, Tra k Ð and the lm version, Tra c. Did you look to those for inspiration?
Certainly the original TV series: the idea that you could jump from country to country and that all these things link. But that was several different stories, whereas ours is more linear and has a central character running through it.
The book traces a network of gun runners, drug smugglers, people tra ckers, money launderers and cyber criminals in Bulgaria, the former Yugoslavia, Russia, Israel, the United Arab Emirates, India, Nigeria, South Africa, Canada, Colombia, Japan and China. How did you decide which perpetrators, victims and locations of organised crime you wanted your ctional story to focus on?
If you want to compare it to Game of Thrones, it felt like the two big kingdoms in the modern crime world were the Russian maÞa and the cartels, so that informed some of it. The Lyudmilla story, about human trafÞcking, was really interesting, so that meant Þnding storiesfor Israel which would tie in. Prague is the gateway from Russia intoEastern Europe, so that became inevitable. And we always loved the India storyline. So it was almost like choosing our favourites and building a story around that, though obviously there had to be credible links between them.
Were there any stories or characters in the book which you wanted to include in the series, but didnÕt have room for?
Not really. ThatÕs whatÕs odd about the adaptation: there are probablyonly four pages from the 400-page book that have been used directly.But what is used enormously is MishaÕs tone, which is there throughout.
At least one story idea used in the series wasnÕt in the book when it was rst published in 2008, but is described in the updated edition: remotely hacking into a port computer system to manipulate the records of shipping containers. Were there other new elements which you needed to research and introduce to reect the growing sophistication of organised crime in the ten years since publication?
Absolutely. ItÕs probably the adaptation IÕve done the most research for because itÕs so sprawling, and Misha was a very important part of that. I read half a dozen books on the internal politics of the various factions in Russia, and talked extensively to Misha about that. And we also talked to a lot of experts whom Misha introduced us to, so that informed the story as much as the book did.
With any story set in the world of crime, you have to address the question of relatability: why should the audience care about these characters when all they do is lie, cheat, steal and kill? What was your answer to that, or did you not consider it a problem?
It was hard for me to consider it a problem, because IÕve always lovedthose kind of characters. But IÕm very aware that a large proportion of the audience donÕt necessarily, and relatability is a massive factor in not just commercial, but also critical success. One of the reasons we cast James Norton was because his persona as an actor is fairlylikeable, and the idea was that you keep the audience on board with him for as long as possible before he starts doing the dark and dirty stuff. But itÕs a story about criminals. And personally I Þnd Vadim really likeable because heÕs human. The challenge was always, how doyou take the hero and make him the villain by the end, and how doyou take the villain and make him the one everyone sympathises with, and that may be an experiment that turns off a large part of youraudience. The other disadvantage we had was that it was about Ôthe one per centÕ: rich people. Some of the reviews suggested that it was self-important and wasnÕt about the real London. But what was reallyinteresting to me was the idea that gangsters are gangsters maybe 5 per cent of the time and the other 95 per cent theyÕre exactly like us: theyÕre having family issues, theyÕre taking their kids to school, theyÕredoing all those everyday things. And that means a slower pace,because youÕre dealing with the mundane as well as with action. There probably wasnÕt enough incident in it for some people, but I think thatÕs closer to the real world of these criminals than a lot of crime series that do focus on the action.
Glenny talks to a lot of people on the other side of the fence, those tracking and trying to stop the gangsters. But the forces of law and order play almost no part in your story, except where they are colluding with the criminals. What was the thinking behind that decision?
The idea was to save the law and order element for a second series: to focus on the rise of someone in the maÞa world in the Þrst series, then change perspective and come at the same characters from a different point of view, building that world gradually over the two series.
How did you arrive at an eight-episode structure for the series, and does each episode have its own three-act structure?
We arrived at eight because if youÕre telling the story of someone who goes from relative innocence to a pretty dark place Ð and youÕre takingin all these other storylines as well Ð six just felt too short. Six is more like a mini-series, it almost enforces a single propulsive story, and we wanted this to feel larger, closer to those ten-part series the Americansdo. What I didnÕt realise Ð because I came to TV from Þlm, where the most important thing is what you leave the audience with Ð is that reviewers only really review the Þrst couple of episodes; they veryrarely review the series as a whole. In our case, maybe one or two reviewed the whole series, and they were more positive than the ones who just reviewed episodes one and two. So I probably didnÕt payenough attention to how each episode has to be totally satisfying in its own right. I almost wrote the Þrst two as you would the start of a feature screenplay, which is the set-up, forgetting that the critics are going, ÔItÕs too slow,Õ and the audience is going, ÔIÕm going to watch something else.Õ On The Alienist, one of the things the studio hammered away at was the importance of the pilot. I rewrote that episode half a dozen times, then they got other people to rewrite it, then they did reshoots of it, because they understood how much weight it had to take. Having done two shows, IÕm now much more aware of that Ð and of what you talked about, a clearer structure within each episode. If in Þlm the last act is the most important, IÕd say in TV, pragmatically, the Þrst act is the most important.
In his foreword to the new edition of the book, Glenny talks aboutparticipating in the writersÕ room with you, James Watkins and the other writers you brought in: Laurence Coriat, David Farr and Peter Harness. What was his, and their, contribution to the overall story and structure?
His contribution was the research, the detail. He was like an authenticity Þlter: when the rest of us were sitting around chatting,heÕd come in and weÕd say, ÔWhat would happen in this situation?Õ, or ÔDo you think this scene is credible?Õ James and I had already outlined the story, not in huge detail, but enough to know where we were headed, so a lot of that work had been done. Then we reÞned that in the writersÕ room in terms of each writerÕs episodes. The interestingthing about the other writers was that each one brought their own particular skill set to it: David is brilliant at structure and storytelling;Laurence is more character-based; PeterÕs skill is in the quirky and surreal. The problem is that you end up with a real Frankenstein of a beast, because everyoneÕs styles are so different Ð which is why I had to go through and rewrite all eight episodes. ThatÕs in no way devaluing the other writersÕ contribution, itÕs just that theyÕre very in demand and you only have access to them for a limited amount of time, so when you end up with something which doesnÕt quite gel because they werenÕt really working together, you need to overwrite with a single voice.
David Farr was the writer of another globe-trotting BBC TV seriesfeaturing a large cast of morally compromised characters: John leCarrÕs The Night Manager. Was that also in your mind in terms of its scope and approach?
Anything which strays into the contemporary spy genre is inevitablygoing to be compared to that, and I think we suffered from the comparison. What David and Susanne Bier did brilliantly with The Night Manager was the glamour and excitement, and people were expecting something like that in McMaa and didnÕt get it. Havingdone a John le Carr adaptation, I consciously wanted to avoid that propulsive, heightened form of storytelling. ItÕs very much the le Carr world, and I love that world, but we were trying to do somethingdifferent: slower, more grounded Ð and more difÞcult.
Did you learn anything from writing series one, apart from the importance of the rst act, which you plan to carry through into series two?
IÕd say our female characters were weak, though in our defence IÕd argue thatÕs always been true of the gangster genre. ItÕs easier, and more organic, to have people on the law and order side be women. I know that violent maÞa women exist, but if you read up on it there are very few of them. ItÕs the same problem with a war story: itÕs hard to Þnd interesting, active female characters without somehow forcingthem into the narrative. If youÕre going to do that, you need to choosestories that are actually about them. But we can do better than women as girlfriends in series two, I think.
Even though the book is ten years old, the series seemed to catchthe zeitgeist in terms of BritainÕs relationship with Russia. The title itself is now used by the British press as a term for wealthy Russians, particularly in London.
ItÕs also used to describe a certain kind of post-globalisationcorruption. The traditional maÞas have reinvented themselves as partof the global economy, and now include facilitators, governments, intelligence agencies. We took that idea and ran with it, which hurt us in one sense, in that it had traditional maÞa story undercurrents, but wasnÕt what people were expecting in terms of gunÞghts and so on. However, it did capture the zeitgeist-y idea that this is how crime now works, that itÕs much closer to banks and corporations. You could argue that the thesis of MishaÕs book was in some ways the most powerful thing about it, and some of that found its way into the series, which is what led to people writing about it.



Jeremy Brock
Jeremy Brock was born in 1959 in Malvern, Worcestershire.
He co-created the BBC series Casualty, and his originalscreenplays include: Mrs Brown (1997); I Am Slave (2010); and Diana & I (2017).
His screen adaptation credits include: Charlotte Gray (2001,novel by Sebastian Faulks); *The Last King of Scotland* (2006,novel by Giles Foden); *Brideshead Revisited* (2008, novel byEvelyn Waugh); The Eagle (2011, novel The Eagle of the Ninth by Rosemary Sutcliff); and Dark Crimes (2016, article True Crime: A Postmodern Murder Mystery by David Grann).
*The Last King of Scotland* won the 2007 BAFTA award for Best Adapted Screenplay.
APPROACHES TO ADAPTATION
Do you prefer to adapt material which chimes with your own work, or material which is completely di erent and gives you a chance to try out new things?
I tend not to examine what motivates me to choose work, and onlypost-rationalise the choices IÕve made. Every choice is governed by whether IÕm moved by the ideas inherent in the work, whether itÕs public domain or an adaptation. If you look back itÕs usuallysomething to do with identity, often to do with faith Ð my father was a vicar and IÕm an atheist, so those conicts are internalised. The intricacies of familial and human relationships, and the detail of how we love and fail to love, would be, I think, what *drive*s me. But like I said, I tend not to know.
Do you think adaptations involve a completely di erent set of creative gears to original screenplays?
Yes and no. With a novel youÕre adapting something thatÕs alreadybeen imagined: another writer has done the work of creating narrativestructure, character dynamics, internal and external tension, and plot.But once you internalise its narrative and its dynamics, yourrelationship with the material is the same as if you were writing an original. You read and re-read the book, then at some point in the process you put it to one side and treat the material as if itÕs your own,until you re-emerge as the person who adapted that book. So the initial approach is different, but for a period of time I think itÕs the same.
Have you ever been o ered material to adapt which you felt couldnÕt, or shouldnÕt, be adapted?
Yes, but recalling speciÞc projects is tricky. IÕve just been offered something, and of course I canÕt really talk about it. ItÕs a short story that was in the New Yorker, by an extremely famous British author, and I just donÕt believe the short story can stretch to a full movie. So thatÕs a straightforward choice to do with dramaturgy and whether the piece has the legs. Sometimes IÕve said no because I feel the subject has been worked to death. World War II: lots of potential adaptations of biographies and historical Þction still come my way, and I turn those down on the grounds that I donÕt have anything fresh to sayabout that period. IÕll always look at the material in case thereÕs an interesting detail, because God and the Devil are in the detail, and if itÕs really extraordinary then IÕll think carefully about it. But sometimes Þlm gets a bit stuck, because Þlm tends to follow the zeitgeist. Commercial cinema particularly tends to be reactive rather than proactive. Indie cinema is where youÕre going to Þnd the new approaches to adaptation. So sometimes itÕs subject, sometimes itÕs, ÔHow the hell are you going to adapt something which is ten pageslong into a full movie?Õ
Do you always agree a mission statement or direction of travel with whoever has commissioned the adaptation?
Well, theyÕve usually hired me because theyÕve got an idea of what IÕm going to deliver, and thatÕs what they bring to the room when they sit down with me for the Þrst time to discuss it. I donÕt think thereÕs a mission statement per se, but often in those early conversations you agree an approach and then if theyÕre good producers they leave you alone with it. IÕm sure youÕll hear this from others, but for the Þrst draft youÕre God, and for every other draft your power diminishes according to how close you get to production.
Do you usually produce an outline or treatment before you start writing the script?
Always. The challenge in adaptation is 80 per cent structural. When you compress a novel into a three-act Þlm, you have to think carefully about what you leave out and what you hold on to, and I donÕt think you can free-form on that, you have to have a structure. So itÕs about acknowledging the difference between the two media, and in the treatment being really disciplined and bringing intellectual rigour to the process of reduction and recalibration.
Do you try to get your outline or treatment to a certain length?
If IÕm hitting more than ten pages, IÕm nervous and I probably edit the treatment. I can guarantee that every page of prose you write is going to expand into ten or more pages in the script, so if youÕre hitting ten pages in a treatment youÕre already at 100 there. So ten would be the maximum. If itÕs an original I might go up to 15, but thatÕs because IÕm explaining the nature of the characters and their relationships to one another, so thereÕs a whole load of guff I write which is an attempt to protect the piece of work from producers going, ÔWhat the hell?Õ, because they donÕt have the comfort of a novel. The reason adaptations are still so popular is because theyÕre a security blanket, because thereÕs the knowledge that somewhere out there are a million people who read the book.
If the author of the source material is living, do you nd it useful to have their input on the script?
No, and IÕll give you an example of where that was honoured. I adapted Sebastian FaulksÕs novel Charlotte Gray, and Sebastian was quite brilliant at offering encouragement, while stepping back and acknowledging that the media are bespoke and singular. He understood that whatever gifts he has, and theyÕre massive, belong in a different medium. We had to pitch for it with six others, and for whatever reason he chose us. He then wrote me an email when heÕd read my screenplay and an extremely touching letter after heÕd seen the Þlm, but other than that he left me alone, which I appreciatedbecause you canÕt micromanage the transition between novel and Þlm.The novelist has to trust the screenwriterÕs instincts.
Charlotte Gray was a World War II project. Why did you make an exception for that one?
Well, it was a World War II project at a time when I hadnÕt made the decision that World War II had been done to death. So thatÕs my short answer. But itÕs a very good question. There was something about Charlotte morphing into another person that I found reallyinteresting. Identity, and shifting identities, fascinate me, as they do many people, but IÕm particularly *drive*n by a desire to explore how you work out who the hell you are in any given situation. Where doyou end and where do other people begin? Where, in CharlotteÕs case, does her mission end and her emotional relationship with this guy inFrance begin, and how does she deal with the necessary compromisethatÕs going to smack up and hit her? I love those sorts of tensions, and I think audiences are always drawn Þrst and foremost to the emotional conict and interaction between characters. That remains true even of massive-budget movies. In the end, when you listen to audiences leaving the cinema, theyÕre talking about people. And itÕs true of adaptations, too, however large the canvas.
Is it easier to navigate notes from directors, producers and scripteditors when you have a piece of source material to measure the screenplay against?
No. I would say having a piece of source material to measure the screenplay against is more complex than working with either public domain or original material. It offers too many opportunities for avoidance disguised as Ôloyalty to the originalÕ, and unless youÕrecareful it can cast a shadow over the production once directors and actors get hold of the novel and Ð with just days before shooting Ð say, ÔI was reading the novel again last night and I loved the way thatÉ.Õ If adaptation is often about choosing what to leave out, conate or render in cinematic equivalence, then having an extant document on which the work is based makes you a hostage to fortune.
Have you ever started work on an adaptation and found it harder to adapt than you anticipated?
*Brideshead Revisited*. But weÕll come back to that.
Are there any screen adaptations which you think are especially good or you particularly admire?
I particularly admire Emma ThompsonÕs adaptation of Sense and Sensibility because it feels so authentically Austen-like and avoids theself-conscious archness of other attempts on her work. The screenplay of The Godfather by Mario Puzo, Francis Ford Coppola and the great Robert Towne Ð Chinatown is possibly the greatest original screenplay Ð is awless in its pacing. ItÕs a really good example of great plotting Ð much misunderstood Ð superb character development and a masterlycontrol of tone. In the wrong hands, it would be pastiche.

ADAPTING FICTION
Some writers try to include as much of the novel as possible by boiling scenes down to their essence. Others are more ruthless in editing it down to a sort of greatest hits, but being true to the spirit of it. Do you favour either of those approaches, or does it depend on the material?
It will depend on the material, but I would say the latter is the most effective way of approaching the difference between the two media. The big difference between novels and Þlms is that the novelist is able to step inside any characterÕs head or become GodÕs eye and look down on the whole. A Þlm canÕt do that. Film has a hunger for forward momentum and cannot go inside. It doesnÕt matter how tight the close-up, youÕre still outside. A novelist can internalise, externalise and create a relationship between the language and the reader. In a Þlm, the relationship is between the audience and the characters, andhow the characters manifest their emotions. ItÕs far less intellectual than the process of reading a novel, which starts here [points to his head] and then goes there [points to his heart]. You sit in a cinema and it can go straight there [points to heart]. ThatÕs the beauty of it. But novels can give you the illusion that theyÕre cinematic, particularly iftheyÕre powerfully written. I adapted Ian McEwanÕs Enduring Love for a Hollywood studio, long before it became the movie directed byRoger Michell, and thatÕs a good example of where the brilliance of the writing can be deceptive. The opening chapter, where a man clings to a balloon as it rises into the air, is one of the greatest openings in modern Þction, and youÕre beguiled and left breathless by it, but actually what follows that Þrework of an opening is a much more novelistic novel than you might have thought. So I fell foul of that, because I was quite a young writer at the time Ð and I was also settingit in America, which was a mistake. The east coast, MIT, it made sense but it still didnÕt work, and it didnÕt work because I was trying to adapt the bookÕs style when I should have been adapting its essence.
If a novel has an unusual structure, would you try to reproduce that in adapting it?
No. I think Þlm is ruthless and it doesnÕt forgive you if you donÕt adhere to certain rules. There are exceptions, but theyÕre exceptions to the rule Ð and the exceptions are Charlie Kaufman and Charlie Kaufman.
YouÕre thinking of Adaptation.
I am thinking of Adaptation, which is an extraordinary piece of work. Charlie Kaufman is, for me, one of the greatest screenwriters there ever was and ever will be, and while weÕre on it, Adaptation is one of the greatest Ôfuck youÕ adaptations, and for that reason I love it, but you canÕt do that unless youÕre Charlie Kaufman. There are reasons why Þlms have rules. They have rules not to be a bastard, but because great writers and directors have tried and tested things out. The reason Billy *Wild*er and his co-writer, I.A.L. Diamond, were so successful is because they worked fucking hard on structure, and on structure that worked for Þlm. The moment in Some Like It Hot when Marilyn Monroe walks down the railway station and is goosed by the steam from the train, the Þrst moment the boys have seen her when theyÕre dressed up as women, that moment required *Wild*er and Diamond to dial back through the entire Þrst act to create the time jump that allowed for that cut, which is one of the loveliest cuts in cinema. ThatÕs structure. ThatÕs adhering to the rules, which means theaudience has to be comfortable with where theyÕre being taken, and have enough information without it feeling expositional, to make thattime jump and enjoy it and feel itÕs as delicious as the authors did.
Do you use the language of the novel in writing the screenplay, ordo you put the whole thing in your own words?
Always in my own words. Having said that, IÕm not sure that there wonÕt be passages which Þnd their way in, but the longer IÕve been in the business the less I do that. ItÕs extremely rare to Þnd a line in a novel thatÕs compressed enough to warrant inserting straight into a screenplay. Screenplays are all about compression, about Þnding the most boiled-down version, the version that tells its story with the leastpossible exposition. Novels donÕt need the least possible exposition. Ifthe novelist chooses to expostulate on water in a glass or the meaningof the universe, weÕll sit patiently with them because weÕre dazzled bytheir erudition. Ian McEwan comes to mind again.
The intricacies of brain surgery, say, in his novel Saturday.
If youÕre Ian McEwan, you can do that. If youÕre making the Þlm version, youÕre not going to have a fuck-off chunk of voiceover talkingabout brain surgery, because it wonÕt work.
Unless, possibly, youÕre Aaron Sorkin. I donÕt know if youÕve seen MollyÕs GameÉ
IÕm really glad you mentioned MollyÕs Game, because I think itÕs been under-noticed. Now thereÕs an example of great adaptation, because itÕs unapologetically intelligent, unapologetically verbal and unapologetically fast. A bold, brassy adaptation, beautifully directed.
Do you try to avoid voiceover in adapting a rst-person narrative, or do you see it as another tool in the toolbox?
It is another tool in the toolbox, but I avoid it because of the impact ithas on an audience. I think it does the opposite of what you might expect: I think it takes them out of the Þlm instead of drawing them in. You might think itÕs going to help because itÕll bring the novel into the Þlm, but actually itÕs a device that says to the audience theyÕrewatching a movie. YouÕre not suspending your disbelief when youÕre aware of the voiceover talking, unless itÕs handled with great care. If youÕre asking the audience to forget themselves for two hours in the dark of the cinema, you want to Þnd a way of having the interactionbetween the characters reveal whatever information the voiceover was intending to. So whatÕs the exception? ItÕs probably the bravura Aaron Sorkin voiceover, orÉ
The Shawshank Redemption? You canÕt imagine that lm without voiceover.
No, you canÕt. ThatÕs another good example. And that, too, is an extraordinary piece of work. But I guess the very fact that weÕre talking about Þlms which are so renowned is an indication that youhave to be at the very top of your game. I think MollyÕs Game will go down as a really great movie.
How much pressure do you feel when adapting a well-known andmuch-loved novel, knowing that you wonÕt be able to please everyone with the nished product?
Well, hereÕs something I learned after I adapted *Brideshead Revisited*. You canÕt please everyone, and if you try to youÕll fuck up, so what you do is hold on to the rule of thirds: a third of the people may like it, a third of the people will be indifferent, and a third of the people will hate it. If you hold on to that rule you can grow old in this job, which is my aim.

CASE STUDY: *THE LAST KING OF SCOTLAND*
UK/Germany, 2006 ¥ Directed by Kevin Macdonald ¥ Produced by Andrea Calderwood, Lisa Bryer, Charles Steel ¥ Screenplayby Peter Morgan and Jeremy Brock, based on the novel by Giles Foden ¥ Cast: Forest Whitaker (Idi Amin), James McAvoy (Nicholas Garrigan), Kerry Washington (Kay Amin), Simon McBurney (Stone), Gillian Anderson (Sarah Merrit)
What attracted you to adapting Giles FodenÕs novel?
I actually took over the screenplay from Peter Morgan. Peter had written an extraordinarily powerful adaptation, but wasnÕt able to see it through because he was overwhelmed by work on other projects.My long-time producer Andrea Calderwood, whoÕd worked with me on Mrs Brown, said, ÔI want you to bring yourself to this screenplay and the challenges that Kevin is having as director.Õ So I came in about a year before we went into production and worked very closely with Kevin Macdonald on my version. But, interestingly, one of the things they asked me to do was steer clear of the book.
You must at least have read it?
Yes, I did Ð though IÕm not sure I told them I did. But the many drafts I went through were freer of the book than any other adaptation IÕvedone, and ironically itÕs been one of the happiest and most successful experiences as an adapter IÕve had. I donÕt know what that tells you.
Did your approach to the material di er from Peter MorganÕs?
Not consciously. It was never stated what they thought I would add tothe material, but I understood that I had been hired to bring to bear my interest in the psychology of relationships and the dynamicbetween characters, primarily the complex duality between Nicholas and Idi Amin. When we won the BAFTA for Best Adapted Screenplay, Peter was incredibly positive and generous about my contribution. It was actually a classic Hollywood job, in the sense that heÕd done his work, then I came in and did mine. ThatÕs how almost all ÔcollaborationsÕ take place. If you see two names on the screen, you might assume that the writers worked together, but thatÕs very rarely the case.
Did you see the story in terms of genre, and if so which genre?
If you asked Kevin heÕd probably say thriller. IÕm not sure I quite thinkthat way. I was just busy being mesmerised by NicholasÕs relationship to Idi. I suppose it would have to be political thriller, but it wasnÕt a post-it on my wall.
ItÕs certainly paced like a thriller, particularly in the second half.
I remember Kevin saying to me, ÔI need an opening which getsNicholas to Uganda fast,Õ and I came up with the idea that heÕs spinning a globe and goes Ð thunk! I donÕt think any of that is in the novel, and it took an insane amount of time to write it. But once youÕd got it, you knew that youÕd done the cinematic trick and pulledoff a moment of intense compression that tells an enormous amount of story. Because itÕs not just that he does that and then you cut, itÕs the manner in which he decides on that country. ItÕs so random. That tells you so much about the unpreparedness of this sex tourist. You write for Þnding moments like that. Or for Þnding lines like ÔItÕs not
ying, itÕs falling with styleÕ in Toy Story Ð a line that IÕll forever wish IÕd written.
Picking up your point about compression, itÕs almost halfwaythrough the novel before Nicholas takes up his job in Kampala as personal physician to Amin. In the lm, his bus journey to the hospital in Mbarara is dealt with in the opening title sequence, and heÕs on his way again by the end of act one.
Yes, and thereÕs a very simple reason. Film will not tolerate exposition. Film requires that you learn along the way as youÕre investing in a character and whatever dilemma theyÕve been put in. Nicholas isnÕt in a real dilemma until he meets Idi. Nicholas is having a whole series of illustrative experiences in the novel that show how Uganda is, but illustrative experiences in Þlm just donÕt hack it. You can have so many and then everyone is like, ÔJust tell me some story.Õ And what is story, if not plot? And what is plot, if not something happens and then as a result of that something else happens? ItÕs King Lear. ItÕs Hamlet. Hamlet Þnds out that his uncle killed his dad, bang, youÕve got your story. Until he Þnds that out, itÕs just a bunch of weird shit going on. Plot is just story, and story requires narrative, and narrative is consequential, not sequential. Where Þlm fails more often than at anything else is that it creates sequences of scenes that have no narrative connection, no consequence. You watch a scene and go,ÔYou could have cut that and nothing that comes after it would have changed.Õ For me, adapting novels is often, crudely, about acknowledging that a brilliant novelist can afford to piss about and spend three chapters telling me shit about the world that doesnÕt advance the plot one jot, but a Þlm just canÕt do that.
The rst title card in the lm is ÔInspired by real people and eventsÕ. Did you therefore move closer to the facts in writing yourversion?
Yes. The facts interested me, so I wasnÕt shy of researching whatever public domain evidence there was for what existed in PeterÕs screenplay and for what I wanted to invest in this project. Ever since Mrs Brown, IÕve loved research. ItÕs an element of the work that I consider an honour. How many jobs in the world are there where youÕre given a tertiary education in Ugandan politics circa 1973? So research was a big and important area in my work.
To what extent did you feel a responsibility to accuracy, in terms of the time, the place, the political context and, particularly, the real people portrayed?
I canÕt remember worrying unduly about that. I remember beingfascinated by someone who has just left home and gone out to reinvent themselves, and their relationship with this patriarch who could appear to be one thing and actually be another; that graduation from a naf to someone whoÕs fully cognisant of how much horror theyÕve stepped into, but as with Macbeth theyÕre so far in that itÕs as hard to go back as it is to go forward. ThereÕs a scene where Nicholas is trying to get hold of his passport and his house has been turned over, so he rushes back to Idi and Idi says, ÔUganda embraces you,Õ and you can feel the air freeze around him. That was a central scene for me in the writing: the feeling of IdiÕs power at that point and Nicholas being trapped, like a monkey in a cage Ð because he was accused of being his Ôwhite monkeyÕ. I canÕt remember whether thatÕs in the novel or whether itÕs in the public domain, but I suppose what IÕm saying is, true to the spirit not the facts, *drive*n by that central relationship.
Amin has several long speeches in the novel, but in the lm, especially early on, those speeches seem much more eloquent.
ThereÕs a very good reason why: for the Þrst ush of NicholasÕs seduction, Idi should be as eloquent, cogent and charismatic as possible. I could see no good dramatic reason to reveal him as clumsy, inarticulate or unattractive. ThereÕs no journey then. This is a Þlm about the seductive quality of evil.
Staying with seduction, two characters in the novel Ð Joyce Merrit, wife of the doctor who runs the hospital in Mbarara, and Sara, another doctor at the hospital Ð have been combined in the
lm into a single character Ð Sarah Merrit Ð who feels a strong attraction to Nicholas, but wonÕt allow herself to be seduced byhim. In other words, she acts as a thematic mirror to him.
Absolutely. That was completely intentional. ItÕs important to say that some of these fundamental decisions were made by Peter, but I remember writing and rewriting and re-rewriting the attemptedseduction of Sarah by Nicholas, and what made it difÞcult was that you needed to feel like he wasnÕt a complete idiot. This guy has been shagging away on a bus, but now heÕs turned up at the hospital and heÕs messing about playing football and heÕs not really taking it very seriously Ð in his head, heÕs on holiday. But this total waste of space needs to have sufÞcient charisma to make that feel like a struggle for her, because why would it be a struggle otherwise? Why would she even give him the time of day?
ThereÕs an earlier scene which helps set that up, the scene whereNicholas meets Amin for the rst time, after Amin has hit a cow and crashed his car. For a start, Nicholas shoots the cow to put it out of its misery Ð which also helps make him less passive and more empathetic Ð then he takes o his Scotland football shirt because Amin wants to give it to his son. The combined e ect of those actions is an assertive masculinity which Sarah is obviously struck by and contrasts with her rather earnest husband.
Completely. And I canÕt take credit for that scene, because thatÕs Peter. But this is an interesting point. I worked on every single line of every single page, and some of the changes were radical and some were surgical, and that difference is true of adaptation too. YouÕre constantly in a dance between a radical and a surgical relationship with the material as you adapt it into this new format. Thinking about it now, thatÕs one of the most challenging balances you have to strike. Most of the time youÕre doing it through instinct. How muscular should I be, how delicate should I be? Should I leave it alone and trust that it works, should I Þnd a way of gently teasing it out? So in some ways, with *The Last King of Scotland*, I felt like I was adapting an adaptation.
The scene where Nicholas meets Amin does several things at once, and so does the scene where he tries to seduce Sarah. Her rejection of his overtures is part of what motivates him to accept AminÕs.
Completely. His sense of rejection and his confusion.
But she also represents his last link to goodness and morality. Once he cuts loose of her, heÕs adrift, he just doesnÕt know it yet.
And heÕll come to realise it too late when heÕs mired in evil. But thereÕs a moment later where he sees her through a bus window, and I remember that was an important moment to capture, because you feel how trapped he is Ð and there it is, thatÕs goodness right there, and heÕs lost it.
In the novel, Nicholas receives a message appointing him as AminÕs doctor, then simply leaves for Kampala. In the lm, Amin invites Nicholas to Kampala and makes the o er in person, then Nicholas returns to Mbarara before deciding to accept it, making it a more gradual pulling away.
That just felt so much more cinematic. You need Idi to be the seducer, and you need to make the expression of that seduction as experientialas possible and not have it be second-hand.
You didnÕt use voiceover in the lm and you also didnÕt use captions, apart from the one about real people and events. Were you deliberately dropping the audience into a situation they didnÕt entirely understand, reecting NicholasÕs experience?
Yes. There are occasions when itÕs useful to have the audience be one step behind, and this is a really good example of that. Let them catch up, trust them to use their eyes and their ears, have them hold NicholasÕs hand and walk alongside him and experience things as he does. ThatÕs why itÕs important that Idi is articulate and rumbustious and funny. ItÕs all a bit Trump-ian, but then it stops being self-parodic and just gets darker and darker and darker Ð until, at the end, Nicholas is being strung up and tortured.
The novel increases in tension as it progresses, but it remains a character study throughout. The lm becomes more of a thriller as it goes on, seducing the audience and then turning on them inthe same way Amin does to Nicholas. Did you have that transitionin mind from the start?
IÕd say that was a conscious exercise. I probably wouldnÕt have framed it as elegantly as you have; IÕd probably just have thought that we needed to make this jangling, kaleidoscopic world of Idi Amin as loud and vivid as possible, and only at precisely the right moment should you feel that turn. I talked about the scene between Idi and Nicholas. ItÕs happened long before then. Nicholas knows before he knows. Heknows that bad shit is happening. The terrible moment when he Þnds out that a guy heÕs shopped has been murdered is his realisation that whatÕs going on is very, very dark. With a script like this Ð and I suppose it is a thriller Ð youÕre in the business of peeling away the onion layers and revealing information only where itÕs most powerful. A very different kind of writing to Brideshead.
Well, Brideshead maintains a steady pace, whereasÉ
This tightens up and tightens up and tightens up, until by the end youÕre going at a furious pace and youÕre complete gripped and all youÕre thinking about is whether Nicholas will get out alive, never mind with his conscience intact.
You could argue that his realisation of whatÕs going on happens earlier, in the scene at the airport following the assassination attempt on Amin. Yet he still informs on AminÕs minister after that scene, despite knowing what Amin does to those who cross him.It almost feels like the two scenes are the wrong way round.
ThatÕs a good point. I remember the moment youÕre talking about and I think there may have been some argument about it, but I canÕt remember why the information was revealed like that. It might have worked better the way you described it. IÕm not saying that to sound modest, IÕm saying it because it speaks to a really important point about this whole process: you donÕt always know. You give it yourbest shot, but the translation between the two-dimensional screenplayand the three-dimensional Þlm is to some extent an improvisation.You have to have faith in your director and Ð something else which is really important Ð faith in your actors, who imbue your lines with meanings and emotions you didnÕt think were there. And thatÕs different, too, from the novel. In a novel, the relationship between thereader and the characters is entirely direct. In a Þlm, itÕs mediated by the actor and their lines. That adds a whole extra element to the potential impact it has. WeÕre watching an actor interpret the lines, creating a dynamic on screen that we then reinterpret, inferring certainthings about the story, about the character, about the relationships.What actors do in the process of adapting a novel is key. TheyÕreadapting as well.
