The scenes preceding the assassination attempt do several key things simultaneously: the gift of the car puts Nicholas deeperinto debt with Amin; the *drive* to the airport gives them a personal moment talking about AminÕs epileptic son; and the assassination attempt itself turns Nicholas into a killer when he has to mow down a man with the Mercedes to allow them to escape. Were you consciously trying to pack all that in, or is therean element of good luck in so many elements cohering?
Both. I worked on that and worked on that and worked on that, and I honestly donÕt know whatÕs me and whatÕs Peter in any of those elements, and thatÕs Þne, thatÕs as it should be, because your job is to simply dive in, lose yourself and try to render each moment as effectively as you can. ItÕs a sweet spot when you hit it, when youknow that a scene is carrying so much weight and so many themes but doing it lightly, when nothing feels ponderous or expositional and it just races towards its apogee. But on the whole you donÕt really know,you just look back and go, ÔThat was good. I got away with that.Õ
In the novel Ð drawing on real events Ð one of AminÕs wives, Kay, has an a air with a Ugandan doctor and ends up dead from a botched abortion. In the lm, the a air is with Nicholas and the unborn child is his, which gives him something he doesnÕt have in the novel: a desire to kill Amin. In fact, in the novel, killing Amin isthe one thing Nicholas resists, either because heÕs too much in thrall to him, or because heÕs too much of a coward, or because itÕs his nal remaining principle. Either way, itÕs a signicant change in terms of his character.
Yes, and I remember having conversations about that with Kevin andAndrea. And it seems to me even now a really important change to make, because not only does it give the last third traction, but it showsthat Nicholas is maturing and changing. ItÕs a huge shift for him to make, and itÕs important that he makes it. ItÕs important that itÕs his affair with Kay, not a third partyÕs affair. ItÕs important that his reaction to that horror is to say, ÔI have to take action.Õ And of course he, in turn, is found out and the whole thing accelerates from that moment. But itÕs really important that Nicholas invests himself and takes a risk. A novel can afford to be at one remove, to make a thematic extrapolation about loyalty or principle. Whereas in a Þlm you want something more visceral and direct, because thatÕs what Þlm feeds off and at its best does so powerfully: just puts the man in the room and says, ÔRight: now do something about it, you idiot.Õ
ThatÕs Hamlet again, isnÕt it?
ItÕs Hamlet. It takes him four fucking hours, but he gets there in the end.

CASE STUDY: *BRIDESHEAD REVISITED*
UK/Italy/Morocco, 2008 ¥ Directed by Julian Jarrold ¥ Produced by Robert Bernstein, Kevin Loader, Douglas Rae ¥ Screenplayby Andrew Davies and Jeremy Brock, based on the novel byEvelyn Waugh ¥ Cast: Matthew Goode (Charles Ryder), Ben Whishaw (Sebastian Flyte), Hayley Atwell (Julia Flyte), Emma Thompson (Lady Marchmain), Michael Gambon (LordMarchmain), Patrick Malahide (Mr Ryder), Joseph Beattie (Anthony Blanche)
YouÕve described *Brideshead Revisited* as Ôone of the nest books in EnglishÕÉ
Okay, letÕs talk about that. LetÕs talk about the fact that you approach a masterpiece, whatever the hell that means, with fear and trepidation. It was offered to me by Ecosse Films, who IÕd worked with on Mrs Brown and Charlotte Gray, but when they Þrst approached me with *Brideshead Revisited*, I turned it down. There was already an extremely accomplished screenplay by Andrew Davies, and I also feltthe shadow of the television version, which stands in memory as one of the great pieces of event TV. So I was really nervous about that hurdle, and even after I took the job IÕm honestly not sure we overcame it. I think we were hobbled by the Ôcompare and contrastÕ that the audience did, and I wish in retrospect that IÕd been more cavalier with the material and dealt with the book as if I was a virgin to it. Of course, thatÕs impossible, because of its iconic status, which meant that there was a kind of reputational undertow holding me back from doing the thing I should do best, which is, as I said, to disappear into the novel and re-emerge owning it. You have to own it. And you have to be quite blunt and egocentric about that. But with Brideshead, I didnÕt feel the freedom to move around the material as loosely as I would if I were adapting a contemporary novel. I didnÕt have the same fear when I approached *The Last King of Scotland*, not because Giles FodenÕs novel isnÕt a great work, but because it didnÕt have that huge status.
How did your take on the book di er from Andrew DaviesÕs adaptation?
I remember thinking AndrewÕs adaptation was brilliant, and if I remember correctly heÕd used as a device the idea that Julia is talking to Charles on board the ship and that conversation is a constant running through the Þlm within its own timeframe, while the rest of the Þlm is a series of ashbacks seen through the prism of their remembering Ð their revisiting, if you will. In conjunction with Ecosseand the BBC, I decided that the novel was too important to be altered to that degree. The solution as I saw it was to adhere more closely to the structure of the book. The problem with adhering more closely to the structure of the book is that the book is gargantuan. So my choice was taken with the best will in the world, because I thought the noveldemanded and deserved to Þnd a more direct equivalent in Þlm, but the direct equivalent in a two-hour Þlm is too reduced. So it may well be that Andrew was right and I was wrong, and weÕll never know precisely because of course his version never made it to the screen in that form and was therefore never tested. But that was the big change I made.
You did retain some of the structure you described, though, because the lm uses a double ashback device: it starts in 1943 with Charles returning to Brideshead, then goes back to the shipin 1935, then goes back further to 1925, before looping around to1935 and nally ending in 1943 again.
IÕd forgotten that. That will be me holding on to an element of AndrewÕs idea. But itÕs hard for an audience new to the story to make that double leap back. If you think about the title *Brideshead Revisited*, the book is about the in memoriam nature of lost youth, of love and youth remembered, and how much of that is accurate and how much is really grief. These things are really hard to translate.
The novel also starts with a double ashback: from Charles at Brideshead in the 1940s, to Charles on his way to Brideshead for the rst time with Sebastian in the 1920s, to Charles in London before he goes up to Oxford. The lm reverses the order of those scenes, establishing CharlesÕs claustrophobic life in London before the glories of Oxford open up to him.
You need the claustrophobia. You need a sense of what heÕs runningaway from, so you understand what heÕs running towards. ThereÕs a beautiful shot in the movie where he Þrst sees Brideshead, and the car goes over the hill and the music swells, because thatÕs the relationship heÕs having with this moment. I donÕt think that would be the same if youÕd seen nothing of where he belonged prior to that, because heÕd just be one of that crowd and how would you know he comes from a different background? The rules of Þlm demand you set context for your central characters, otherwise the audience is going to spend the Þrst 20 minutes trying to catch up: why is he so in awe of this, why is he so surprised? As I said earlier, sometimes itÕs useful to take the audience a step back and not have them catch up, but youÕve got to know why youÕre doing that, and here there seemed to be no goodreason for it.
In terms of what Charles is running towards, thereÕs a suggestion in the lm, which might simply be drawing out of the novel something which isÉ
Implicit. ThatÕs a very interesting issue. Go on.
Sebastian says to Charles: ÔYouÕre not in anybodyÕs gang. ThatÕs always been your problem.Õ Lady Marchmain says to him: ÔYou just want to be liked.Õ And Anthony Blanche says to him: ÔThere really is no end to your hunger.Õ None of these lines are in the novel, but together they seem to suggest that, consciously or not, a lot of the things Charles does, including becoming involved with Julia, are *drive*n by a desire to be master of Brideshead.
What youÕre talking about now is a classic difference between novels and cinema, in that the novel can imply and the reader can infer in a way that characters in Þlms have to reveal through dialogue or through their actions. Those lines that you quoted, Anthony BlancheÕsline in particular Ð ÔIs there no end to your hunger?Õ Ð lines like that, delivered as questions, are the cinematic equivalent of what Waugh can do through long passages of discursive and sinuous prose. A ÞlmdoesnÕt have the luxury of time or going inside charactersÕ heads, so you have to Þnd other ways of capturing that. I like the way Þlm can boil it down to a single line, to someone asking a rhetorical question.You know Anthony Blanche doesnÕt expect an answer.
In his preface to the 1959 reissue of the novel, Waugh denes itstheme as Ôthe operation of divine graceÕ, and that doesnÕt seem to be what the lm is about. In the novel, Charles is an agnostic; in the lm, like you, heÕs an atheist. In the novel, heÕs newly converted to Catholicism at the end; in the lm, heÕs still in ight from faith at the end. That shift away from WaughÕs theme has a huge e ect both on the shape of the story and the fate of the characters, so itÕs a considerable alteration to the novel.
It is, and youÕre reminding me now of the unconscious *drive*s that had a massive impact. I probably intellectualised it to myself as: a modern audience will not accept the central premise of the novel; what theywill relate to most closely is the coming to terms with grief, the reconciliation between the experienced and the remembered Ð which is the human experience. If I was being difÞcult about this, IÕd say that faith can have many manifestations; it doesnÕt necessarily have to be religiose. It can be manifested as faith in someoneÕs love for you, faith in their essential integrity and authenticity. The moment when Charles goes to Morocco to see Sebastian springs to mind. I donÕt think I consciously set out to be that cavalier, though youÕre right to remind me that I was.
Another major change in the lm is the love triangle between Charles, Julia and Sebastian, which isnÕt present in the novel, or isnÕt present to the same degree. The scene in Venice where Sebastian sees Charles kissing Julia is entirely new, and though itprovides a clearer reason for something which is slightly opaque in the novel Ð SebastianÕs decline into alcoholism Ð it shifts that reason away from Catholic guilt and towards romantic disappointment. In other words, the script draws various threads of the novel into a more understandable, more commercial whole, but the changes it makes mean that, after a fairly faithful
rst half, the lm basically goes its own way in the second.
Yes, it does. The axis on which the second half shifts is a moment thatÕs not in the book, which has to do with human emotion in its raw state, not with extrapolated guilt. Film struggles with the internal and always will. Plus, I think what *drive*s Sebastian has to be yearning,sexual and emotional, as well as guilt, whether thatÕs acknowledgedby Waugh or not. What holds an audienceÕs attention isnÕt an esoteric thesis about faith, itÕs the agony of a young person seeing their dreamshattered. Even if they half-knew it wasnÕt real, until then they could hold on to the delusion. From that moment on they canÕt, and theyÕre set on a catastrophic path of self-destruction. So for me that moment was incredibly important and, weirdly, I didnÕt have any trouble changing the novel. And the more I talk to you about what I did, the more I realise I was going my own way more than I remember. Another thought which comes to mind, a theme which interested me, is the relationship between Sebastian, Julia and their mother: her power to both lift up and destroy, to shape and damage her childrenÕsprospects for a healthy and balanced future, which is so tightly allied to her faith and Catholicism.
Lady Marchmain struck me as being more austere and autocraticin the lm than in the novel.
More matriarchal Ð sheÕs a matriarch par excellence. I think the mother, the id in all of us, is a really powerful place to play, and the way I could most easily represent the power of a realised faith was through the way she expresses that faith in relationship to her children. ItÕs suffocating. Catholicism is not a milky, Sundays-onlyfaith. It is forever and it is in your DNA. So yes, sheÕs more matriarchal, and that was because I felt she could embody the authority of an all-seeing, all-knowing God.
Charles, as a character, presents two big problems for any adapter: passivity and selshness. Your adaptation makes him both more proactiveÉ
An active participant in the narrative, yes.
Éand more ÔrelatableÕ. For example, the scenes where he tells Julia heÕs going to settle things with Rex, which he doesnÕt in the novel, and where he cleans up SebastianÕs vomit rather than letting his college scout do it. He also has children in the novel, but when he leaves his wife for Julia we never hear about them again. In the lm, he and his wife have no children. So thereÕs a sense of making Charles more sympathetic to try to keep the audience with him.
Yes, and I think this is an example of cultural shifts around the novel. LetÕs be clear: if youÕre making a commercial Þlm, youÕre making a Þlm for a wide audience. Culturally, now, someone with CharlesÕs solipsism isnÕt your natural protagonist. Some of his behaviour in the novel is so self-centred that youÕre only staying with him because of the novelÕs brilliance. Also his passivity isnÕt such a problem in the novel because heÕs part of the novelistÕs controlling imagination. In the Þlm, you donÕt have that relationship, where a novelist can step back and narrate around a character. That characterÕs relationship with the audience is direct and immediate, and unless theyÕre holding the audienceÕs attention either through their empathy or their charisma, then youÕre in trouble and youÕll lose the audience Ð and itÕs the hardest work in the world to get an audience back. So part of what was driving the changes wouldnÕt even have been conscious, it would just have been a need to Þnd a way of making his behaviour seem, as you say, relatable. I noticed you put invisible inverted commas around that word, but actually I think itÕs perfectly justiÞed, because if youÕre asking people to pay ten quid to watch someone who belongs to a culture thatÕs already historical and therefore at one remove from their lives and then is behaving like an assÉ you know?
Talking of narration, voiceover was used extensively in the TV adaptation, but only sparingly in this one: two passages in the script, reduced to one in the lm, spoken by Charles in the opening sceneÉ
How interesting. IÕd forgotten there were two. Did they bookend?
Yes.
That would be my style, yes.
How early did you decide to avoid voiceover to that extent Ð or had Andrew Davies already made that decision?
I donÕt remember AndrewÕs version well enough. It may be that heÕd already made that decision, or it may be that when I addressed the material I felt that, apart from taking you in and taking you out, voiceover wouldnÕt work in a Þlm which was going to be such an intense, boiled-down version of the book. I asked if they bookended because theyÕre the two moments in a Þlm where youÕre conscious ofthe process. At the beginning the voiceover is saying, ÔI know youÕresettling into this, but IÕm just going to introduce you to me or some ideas.Õ And at the end, equally but in mirror, itÕs saying, ÔWeÕre leaving now and IÕm going to draw out this theme.Õ IÕm interested that the second one didnÕt make it, though I canÕt remember why. It was probably deemed irrelevant because it had already been explored, butI wouldnÕt have been part of that decision because screenwriters are in a strange relationship with the Þnished Þlm. Incidentally, whether itÕs an adaptation or an original, I would like to say that screenwriters are the only people in the entire process who deal with inÞnite possibility.Everyone else, including the director, is in the business of interpreting a prima facie document. Not always noted.
Interestingly, the nal line of the single passage of voiceoverÉ
Was it from the book? IÕm wondering if I lifted the book at that point.
Well, the line I noted was, ÔOnly one emotion remains my own: guilt.Õ
I think thatÕs me.
ItÕs a very bold statement, though, and in tune with Waugh.
Yes, because one of the things which has always interested me Ð as the fallen son of a vicar Ð is the imagined original sin, the thing that makes us awed, the thing we are always in ight from. That guilt is what moved me in the book and why I wanted to have a go at it, and that guilt is manifested by Charles primarily in relation to how he handled himself with Julia and Sebastian and less to do with his own relationship to faith, which for Waugh was obviously central. I was approaching it from a more viscerally human, esh and blood point of view, because thatÕs what *drive*s me.
So if you were summarising CharlesÕs journey, summing up what heÕs learned, it would be the extent to which his needs clashed with the needs of othersÉ
And potentially damaged others.
Éand the way in which, while trying to satisfy those needs and without necessarily meaning to, he treated others very badly.
Yes. ThatÕs absolutely right. That is the extrapolation I would make about Charles in the Þlm Ð but not Charles in the book.
How do you feel about the lm now Ð revisiting it?
I was proud of the work before the work became a Þlm, but the Þlm didnÕt catch the zeitgeist in the way I hoped. I remember Emma Thompson saying, ÔYouÕve managed what I thought wasnÕt possible,which is to draw the novel down to its essence.Õ Flattered, I marched forward, and I think she wasnÕt just being kind, I think she believed that, as a lot of people did when they read the screenplay. But hereÕs a thing about screenwriting: thereÕs a Þbre-thin relationship, so easilybroken, between your ambition for the screenplay and its eventual realisation. You take a piece of paper and put it through an industrial process in order, Þnally, to put it on a screen so that it can have an intimate relationship with its audience. ItÕs a very strange arc that a Þlm goes on. ThatÕs not an excuse, by the way. IÕm not excusing the failings in the screenplay. IÕm just trying to tell you why I think some elements of my ambition for the screenplay didnÕt quite make it.


Moira Bufni
Moira Bufni was born in 1965 in Middlewich, Cheshire.
As a playwright, her work includes: Gabriel (1997); Silence (1999); Loveplay (2001); Dinner (2002); Welcome to Thebes (2010); Handbagged (2013); and wonder.land (2015).
Her screen adaptation credits include: *Tamara Drewe* (2010,graphic novel by Posy Simmonds); *Jane Eyre* (2011, novel byCharlotte Bront); Byzantium (2012, from her play A Vampire Story); ViceroyÕs House (2017, books Freedom at Midnight byLarry Collins and Dominique Lapierre, and The Shadow of the Great Game: The Untold Story of Partition by Narendra SinghSarila); and The Dig (2020, novel by John Preston).
She also co-created and co-wrote the ITV series Harlots (2017Ð2019), inspired by the book The Covent Garden Ladies: PimpGeneral Jack & The Extraordinary Story of HarrisÕ List by Hallie Rubenhold.
APPROACHES TO ADAPTATION
Do you prefer to adapt material which chimes with your own work, or material which is completely di erent and gives you a chance to try out new things?
The latter, I think. I have to empathise with the work in some way, but it can be so different in form and tone from my own work and I Þnd that a great pleasure. You learn such a lot about writing by getting inside another writerÕs work, by seeing the world through their eyes Ð and you bring yourself into that as well, so if you think itÕs going to be a bad marriage you just shouldnÕt do it. I would never adapt a Henry James because IÕve never been able to Þnish one of his books, but I would love to adapt other writers who are really different from me, even people with quite different world views.
Do you think adaptations involve a completely di erent set of creative gears to original screenplays?
TheyÕre certainly easier. ThereÕs nothing harder in my job, in myopinion, than making up a story. It sounds so simple, but itÕs actuallyreally hard. With an adaptation, someone else has done the hard work and you have the joy of taking that story and making it accessible to people in a different form. So it can be more pleasurable than, say,writing a play Ð though, at the end of the day, less satisfying.
Do you always agree a mission statement or direction of travel with whoever has commissioned the adaptation?
Absolutely. Films are collaborations. IÕd never go off on my own and present a bunch of producers with something. You know youÕre going to be rewriting it a thousand times, so letÕs at least be on the same page at the start.
Do you usually produce an outline or treatment before you start writing the script?
I try really hard not to. I hate them. I just hate them. I want to win a prize for hating outlines most. I do them for television, because your episode is part of an ongoing narrative that other people have to writearound. I get the point of that. For adaptations, I donÕt get the point. We all know the story. I can sit and tell you what IÕm going to do, you can agree it and someone can write notes, but why am I wasting timewhen I should be doing what IÕve trained my whole life to do?
If the author of the source material is living, do you nd it useful to have their input on the script?
DeÞnitely. IÕd hate to write an adaptation that the author didnÕt like ordidnÕt approve of, and in both cases where IÕve adapted books byliving authors Ð Posy Simmonds and John Preston Ð IÕve met and get on well with them. *Jane Eyre* is a different matter, of course, but I did try to channel Charlotte Bront.
Is it easier to navigate notes from directors, producers and scripteditors when you have a piece of source material to measure the screenplay against?
Yes, though you quickly Þnd that the notes stop being about the source material and start being about the Þlm, so thereÕs not really a lot of difference between notes on an original screenplay and notes on an adaptation. Certainly by the second draft itÕs becoming its own thing.
Have you ever started work on an adaptation and found it harder to adapt than you anticipated?
My own play, A Vampire Story. I thought that would be quitestraightforward, but itÕs the hardest adaptation IÕve ever done. I had no objectivity on it whatsoever and my relationship with the author was really difÞcult. I didnÕt have the same feeling of conÞdence.
And the lm, Byzantium, was made by a writer-director, Neil Jordan, with a strong voice of his own.
A lot of his ideas I absolutely loved, like making Ireland the setting.My backgroundÕs Irish; why didnÕt I have that idea? So there are certain things that are great about working with another big creative force, but I think it was one of those occasions when youÕre both interested in different things in the same story Ð and at the end of the day heÕs a hard guy to Þght your corner against. So then you have to make a choice: do you want to protect your sole vision, or do youwant the project to get made? I wanted it to get made.
Have you ever been o ered material to adapt which you felt couldnÕt, or shouldnÕt, be adapted?
Yes, and IÕve turned them down. IÕve twice been offered adaptations ofbrilliant documentaries, and I just thought, ÔI canÕt take that on. MyÞlm wonÕt be as good.Õ The documentary has profoundly moved me and those are the real people. How can a piece of Þction do anybetter? IÕve also turned down wonderful stories because I havenÕt, at the time, had the emotional resources to deal with them. The place you have to go to write a screenplay is sometimes so lonely and so dark and you know youÕve got to spend such a long time there. There have been several occasions where IÕve loved a story and been veryaffected by it and wanted to do it, but thought, ÔThatÕs too painfulright now.Õ
Are there any screen adaptations which you think are especially good or you particularly admire?
ThereÕs a very long list Ð though often IÕve seen the Þlm and havenÕt read the book because IÕm not a great reader, especially of modern Þction.

ADAPTING FICTION
Some writers try to include as much of the novel as possible by boiling scenes down to their essence. Others are more ruthless in editing it down to a sort of greatest hits, but being true to the spirit of it. Do you favour either of those approaches?
It changes according to the material. In my Þrst draft I try to be as faithful as I can and just say, ÔThis isnÕt really a screenplay: IÕm going to adapt all the central scenes of the book, IÕm going to keep the structure of the book and IÕm going to write it as dramaÕ. That bringsup all the problems straightaway, and I really learnt that on *Jane Eyre*, because you get two-thirds of the way through the book and think, ÔIcanÕt introduce all these new characters 20 minutes before the end of the Þlm Ð no oneÕs going to care.Õ There are some novels where elements of the story that work in a poetic way as prose just donÕt have that Þlm feeling, where youÕve got to tighten and tighten and tighten until you get to the climax. Films are very simple and itÕs often a matter of cutting down in order to let other things carry the weight.ItÕs like John PrestonÕs book, The Dig, which I fell in love with, which is this elegy on time and love and loss. How do you make it dramatic when the two central people in the book are desperately lonely and donÕt really have a relationship with each other? How do you make that work in order to bring that novel to a wider audience? It all comes from your own emotional and political and visceral response to the work. How has it made you feel? What has it made you think?
WhatÕs the biggest, widest resonance of the book? ThatÕs how youhave to approach it, and what youÕre trying to capture.
If a novel has an unusual structure, would you try to reproduce that in adapting it?
I donÕt really know how to answer that. I donÕt think the structure is the reason you adapt anything, itÕs the story within that structure and the characters who are tied up in that story. If the story is good, thatÕs what youÕre adapting; the structure is just the climbing frame. I also think that some things simply arenÕt Þlms. Middlemarch isnÕt a Þlm, itÕs television. Some novels are just too big to lend themselves easily to Þlm. Films are incredibly short and distilled. Writing Harlots, the television series, is like writing a three-dimensional dramatic novel Ð but thatÕs what television is. I think itÕs about choosing your form: does this book lend itself to being a two-hour Þlm?
Do you try to avoid voiceover in adapting a rst-person narrative, or do you see it as another tool in the toolbox?
It depends on the source material. Maybe IÕll read the thing and think, ÔThis has to be voiceover.Õ Mostly I try to avoid it. There are certain Þlms, like Badlands, where the voiceover is the Þlm: Sissy SpacekÕs voiceover in that is absolutely right. But usually, a Þlm that starts with a wide shot of a house and a voiceover, IÕm already asleep.
Do you use the language of the novel in writing the dialogue and stage directions, or do you put the whole thing in your own words?
IÕll take anything that the writer of the novel gives me in terms of dialogue. The trick is to meld it seamlessly with your own, so it all has the same voice. The stage directions are always my own. I never lift prose description from a novel. That doesnÕt feel right for a Þlm.
Do you keep the novel beside you throughout the adaptationprocess, or do you try to internalise it and set it aside somewherealong the way?
For the Þrst draft itÕs right there, IÕm all over it Ð until itÕs a dog-earedwreck, covered in stickers and notes. Then I set it aside. If IÕm stuck, IÕll go back to the book later on in the process. Sometimes itÕs good to go back to a scene several drafts down the line and go, ÔWhere was that scene in the book?Õ ItÕs become so much its own thing that maybe youÕll unblock something.
How much pressure do you feel when adapting a well-known andmuch-loved novel, knowing that you wonÕt be able to please everyone with the nished product?
Particularly with *Jane Eyre*, you feel the weight of world literature on your shoulders. But in that situation I always try being myself when IÞrst read the book. Teenage me: thatÕs who IÕm writing for. IÕm not writing for critics or academics, IÕm writing for that girl whoÕs never read the book before. ThatÕs quite freeing. ThatÕs what keeps those sneering voices off your back.

CASE STUDY: *TAMARA DREWE*
UK, 2010 ¥ Directed by Stephen Frears ¥ Produced by Alison Owen, Paul Trijbits, Tracey Seaward ¥ Screenplay by Moira Bufni, based on the graphic novel by Posy Simmonds ¥ Cast: Gemma Arterton (*Tamara Drewe*), Roger Allam (NicholasHardiment), Bill Camp (Glen McCreavy), Dominic Cooper (Ben Sergeant), Luke Evans (Andy Cobb), Tamsin Greig (BethHardiment)
The rst frame of Posy SimmondsÕ graphic novel is the advert for the writersÕ retreat which forms one of its primary locations, and the rst phrase of the advert is the title of Thomas HardyÕs Far from the Madding Crowd. Did you go back to HardyÕs works in adapting it?
I did read Far from the Madding Crowd, but Hardy is another novelist I couldnÕt adapt and thatÕs the only book of his I can bear. I read about Hardy as well, and the more I read about him the less I ever wanted to adapt one of his novels. I actually canÕt remember whether Glen is a Hardy scholar in the book, but I thought, ÔIf heÕs a Hardy scholar IÕve got to know something about Hardy.Õ Basically, itÕs a irt between Glen and Beth in the scene where he describes Hardy as having snow on the roof and Þre in the cellar, and she Þnds it all so distasteful that Hardy married a younger woman and gave his Þrst wife syphilis Ð and meanwhile her own marriage to the controlling guywhoÕs having an affair with a much younger woman is falling apart around her Ð and you just think, ÔThis is great. This is all feeding the same beast.Õ
And what do you think the relationship is between the Simmondsand the Hardy?
Again, writing a good story is the hardest thing, and this is a great story Ð Bathsheba Everdene and her three suitors Ð and Posy has taken that and concentrated on the things she Þnds interesting. Two bigBritish exports are heritage and literature, and sheÕs taking quite a wrylook at all that. SheÕs essentially having fun with Thomas Hardy, and thatÕs what I love about her, how deliciously tongue-in-cheek she is. You can almost see her smile as she writes. I think sheÕs the Jane Austen of graphic novels. She has that same fantastic ability to send everything up whilst being true to people as well-rounded emotional beings.
The look and feel of the graphic novel seemed to me quite di erent to the look and feel of the lm. The graphic novel struck me as subdued, measured, as dramatic as it is comic. Whereas the lm is light and bright, pacy and jaunty, a romantic comedywith dramatic asides. Was there a particular tone you were tryingto achieve?
I think youÕre right that the Þlm is much more of a romantic comedy than the book, and I think in the Þrst couple of drafts it was even more of a romantic comedy, simply because thatÕs the market. So many of the Þlms that IÕd written in my early years as a screenwriter hadnÕt got made because they didnÕt Þt into a market, they crossed genres. I wrote a horror Þlm that never got made because it was northern and social realist and funny and no one knew what to do with it. I wrote another Þlm that crossed genres Ð it wasnÕt really a romance, it wasnÕt really a drama, it was both Ð and that didnÕt get made. Something with a child protagonist didnÕt get made. So I justthought, ÔIÕm going to write a romantic comedy and see if I can getthat made.Õ And IÕm so glad Stephen Frears found all that reallyboring and made a less genre Þlm. It probably would have done betterbox ofÞce if it had stayed more in that romantic comedy genre, but at the end of the day it probably wouldnÕt have been as good a Þlm. Frears found Glen much more interesting, he found the two girls, Jody and Casey, much more interesting Ð and actually, they are. He took it back closer to PosyÕs book, which is why I think he was the rightdirector for it.
In a regular novel, each reader forms their own impressions of the characters and locations from the authorÕs descriptions. In a graphic novel, that work is done for you. What impact did the visual nature of the source material have on the way you adapted it?
Huge. The graphic novel was always around on set, everyone who was involved had a copy, and I think we recreated it wonderfully. The Þlm is so faithful to PosyÕs world. Even down to the names of the hens, the Buff Orpingtons. I just got Þxated on Buff Orpingtons; I thought they had to be in the screenplay. Posy gave me all that: sheÕd researched everything. I knew Derbyshire really well, where *Jane Eyre*is set, but IÕd never been to Wessex before, and when I got down there I thought, ÔBloody hell, thatÕs exactly what PosyÕs drawn.Õ I inhabited that world in my head for months.
The graphic novel isnÕt just visual, itÕs verbal too: thereÕs obviously dialogue in the cartoon strip, but there are also accompanying passages of prose Ð mainly rst person narration by Beth, Glen and Casey, interspersed with, as it were, un.narrated sections showing Tamara, Nicholas and Andy. How important was point of view in your adaptation?
In the novel, Tamara is always talked about, isnÕt she? SheÕs the other. And thatÕs great. But in a Þlm, if youÕre not inside your lead character, if youÕre not seeing the world through your protagonistÕs eyesÉ I mean, IÕm sure there is a version of the Þlm where Tamara is the other, but I think itÕs colder, and I think you ultimately have to care about her. She canÕt be that unlikeable. SheÕs more of an enigma in the novel, and women like that can be very interesting but often fail to be so in Þlms. They did them really well in the 1940s, but if you try to write that kind of enigmatic woman now you just think, ÔWho are youwriting that for?Õ ItÕs difÞcult.
The Tamara portrayed in the script and played by Gemma Arterton does feel more developed than the Tamara in the graphic novel Ð smarter, sassier, more proactive, more professional Ð and therefore more sympathetic.
I think Tamara is probably more sympathetic in my adaptation.ThereÕs obviously more of me in her than in PosyÕs Tamara, and of course Gemma is such a sympathetic presence, and I think both of those things contributed to her being more likeable. And maybe I just wanted to like her more. Maybe I was more inclined to like her than her three suitors. I probably did take her side more, and try to understand her as a messed-up young woman in that situation Ð like, why does she go for Nicholas, why doesnÕt she go for Andy? I thoughtshe wouldnÕt go for Nicholas unless he was her only port in a storm Ð and, at that point, he is. ItÕs one of those really bad things you do thatfeels good at the time and you hate yourself for afterwards.
A device Simmonds uses a lot is a character reecting on recent events: a page or more in the form of a thought bubble while doing the cooking or taking a walk in the elds. The lm also uses
ashbacks, but for memories further in the past. Did you feel that it was better to keep the action as much in the present as possible?
Yes. If you had too many timeframes, it just wasnÕt clear. I think we tried them, and thereÕs also one Þrst-person direct-to-camera scene.
I was going to ask about that: itÕs the scene where Beth talks to the audience about Nicholas while baking a cake. Why did you decide to use that device at that moment?
Frears suggested it one lunchtime and I said, ÔThatÕs breaking the fourth wall, thereÕs nothing else in the Þlm like it, are you sure it will work?Õ and he said, ÔWhy donÕt you just try it?Õ So I went off and wrote the scene, came back and gave it to him, and he looked pleasedand took it away and shot it. That was a really good day, writing that scene and getting it shot and going, ÔThat actually really works.Õ Sometimes you can overthink things, but I didnÕt have time to overthink that. One thing to say about *Tamara Drewe* is the speed of the production. Alison Owen [producer] gave me the book to adapt in autumn 2008, and by September 2009 I was on set and it was beingshot. Everything fell into place very quickly, which is unusual. Most Þlms take years, as IÕve subsequently learnt. So a lot of it was written on set, on the hoof, really instinctively.
Although the lm is seen through TamaraÕs eyes in a way the graphic novel isnÕt, in terms of point of view your adaptation is asmuch an ensemble piece as anything else.
Yes, I think so. And I loved the ensemble nature of it. Because itÕs so Jane Austen-y, I sort of thought, ÔIÕm the omnipotent author like JaneAusten, IÕve got the joy of eight characters here, and IÕm going to try to get inside everybody and write them much more equally.Õ So the girls are as important as Glen, and Glen is as important as Tamara, and the audience is the conÞdante of all eight characters. I think thatÕshow I view everything, actually. IÕm the audience. IÕm the audience and in that cake-baking scene Beth is conÞding in me.
The lm follows the overall structure of the graphic novel: four parts for summer, autumn, winter and spring. What narrative signicance do the passing seasons have?
It does give you a sense of time passing. Most Þlm stories unfold really quickly, and to have a much longer narrative played out over a year is quite unusual. It had its own challenges as well, not least in trying to keep upping the narrative ante in this longer timeframe. Films usuallyoperate at a very fast pace unless they have virtually no story.
The ending of the lm is somewhat softer than that of the graphic novel: Jody doesnÕt die and Glen gets together with Beth Ð though Beth is confronted more directly with NicholasÕs death. What was the thinking behind those changes?
Really early on, in that meeting I described where you say what youÕregoing to do, one of the Þrst things I said was, ÔI think this Þlm only has room for one death, and I desperately donÕt want that to be JodyÕ
Ð and Posy was in agreement with that. Jody represents one of the girls who dies in Far from the Madding Crowd, I canÕt even remember the characterÕs name, and Posy was happy for me to reprieve her. Those two kids have got these shit boring lives, theyÕre looking at all these educated middle-class people whoÕve got so much more than they have, and I really wanted them to end on a high Ð and they do, they get exactly what they want, they get quality time with their superhero.
It does mean, though, that the life lessons which Casey learns from JodyÕs death in the graphic novel donÕt apply in the lm.
No, they donÕt. Jody is the other in that relationship, sheÕs seen through CaseyÕs eyes, but to make those two characters Þt into the ensemble, it canÕt be CaseyÕs story. In the novel, Casey is reective, so she doesnÕt do as much. Film is all about character through action, and Jody is the active one, the one who does, so she takes on more signiÞcance. So Casey becomes JodyÕs sidekick. But I think that it is one of the losses.
You also lose the scene in the graphic novel where, despite their di erences, Tamara, Beth and Casey console each other over a cigarette around the kitchen table after Nicholas and JodyÕs deaths.
ItÕs deÞnitely lighter because Jody doesnÕt die, but then Far from the Madding Crowd is the only novel of HardyÕs that doesnÕt have a miserable fucking ending. It just about allows Bathsheba and GabrielOak the peace of the Þreside, and I think you can just about allow Tamara and Andy a happy ending in *Tamara Drewe*. Tamara hasnÕt burnt her boats, thereÕs still enough in her to like. SheÕs really lucky, but I think she knows it.
In other words, Tamara becomes the one who learns the life lesson?
Yes. In the novel, sheÕs been through all this shit, but sheÕs still using Andy to cover NicholasÕs baby. She remains the same, whereas in a Þlm if your protagonist doesnÕt change youÕre in a lot of trouble.

CASE STUDY: *JANE EYRE*
UK/US, 2011 ¥ Directed by Cary Joji Fukunaga ¥ Produced byAlison Owen, Paul Trijbits ¥ Screenplay by Moira Bufni, based on the novel by Charlotte Bront ¥ Cast: Mia Wasikowska (*Jane Eyre*), Michael Fassbender (Rochester), Jamie Bell (St John Rivers), Judi Dench (Mrs Fairfax), Sally Hawkins (Mrs Reed), Freya Parks (Helen Burns)
There have been numerous previous adaptations of *Jane Eyre*. What did you think you could bring to it that would be new?
IÕll tell you how I came to do it, because itÕs a job I really chased. I had a play at the Almeida around 2007, and Abi Morgan [playwright and screenwriter] was at the bar one night and we got talking. I said I was looking for another Þlm and she said, ÔI think the BBC want to do an adaptation of *Jane Eyre*,Õ and I said, ÔI have to do it!Õ It was like this Þre came through me. So I went in to meet Alison Owen and Christine Langan [former Head of BBC Films] going, ÔI have to adapt *Jane Eyre*!Õ I didnÕt come to it with any fear that I couldnÕt do it. It was such a visceral feeling that I had to be the person to do it Ð because I loved the book. That book has been a friend all my life. ItÕs one of those books you can turn to when youÕre having a really shit time. Just like itÕs always sunny in Pemberley, *Jane Eyre* is always having a more intensely worse time than you are, and somehow her amazingfortitude gets you through. So I started talking about the book and why I love it so much, and it became a meeting about how we all loved this book, and we carried that enthusiasm into all the meetings that followed. But we had to convince quite a lot of people who were saying, ÔWhy do *Jane Eyre*?Õ
And what was your answer to that question?
ItÕs a wonderful story. ItÕs also an important story, a radical story, written by someone who society regarded as having no value. *Jane Eyre* is RochesterÕs social inferior Ð sheÕs a woman with no money, heÕs the lord of the manor Ð but she regards herself in her living soul as his equal, and by the end of the book he acknowledges the absolute truthof that and they achieve a proper harmony. Some people say that Charlotte Bront had to emasculate Rochester. I think Rochester deserves that punishment for lying his head off for the whole book and for trying to use a young girl as his harlot. But Jane is aware of her own self-worth and she refuses. She refuses to be treated like most women were treated and like many still are, because she knows she has a soul as valuable as RochesterÕs. So itÕs still an important story. And itÕs a radical book in terms of its form as well. ItÕs one of the Þrst books to have a child narration. Charles Dickens subsequently nickedthat for most of his books. I just think itÕs brilliant.
In some ways itÕs a very cinematic novel, full of detailed descriptions of people, places and seasons. How much did thoseguide you when you were conceiving the lmÕs characters and locations?
The scene where Jane is taking the post to the village and Rochester comes by on his horse: I was so in that landscape when I was writingthe scene, and in the book itÕs a wintry landscape so thatÕs what I wrote, and then of course in production you canÕt do it. YouÕve only got so many weeks and you have to compromise. But whatÕs important about that walk is how intensely alone she is and how creeped out she is and how restless she is, because what Charlotte Bront is actually giving you with that landscape is JaneÕs emotional state and those are the things that you try to keep. In the Þrst draft I wrote the most ridiculous detailed descriptions of rooms and crystalglassware and all this kind of stuff. IÕve got so economical in describing things now that people keep asking me, ÔPlease can youwrite more?Õ, because itÕs only the absolute necessities of a scene that end up on the page.
Another extraordinarily detailed aspect of the novel is the lengthyconversations between Jane and Rochester, which are sometimes so high-own as to be hard to follow. How did you set about turning those into believable, understandable dialogue scenes?
Well, you see, I didnÕt Þnd them hard to follow. I really loved them and I really loved writing them. TheyÕre like swordplay, theyÕre like skirmishes, and theyÕre some of my favourite scenes in the whole thing. Also, as a playwright, IÕm not afraid of the long scene, IÕm not afraid of the scene of scale. Sometimes I think, ÔIÕm going to write the play of this scene,Õ and thatÕs what I did in those Jane and Rochester Þreside scenes. TheyÕre such play-like situations. TheyÕre still, which is so rare in a Þlm, and theyÕre surrounded by darkness, which is whatyou get in the theatre.
The lm unfolds in ashback from the point where Jane ees Thorn eld Hall, and there are two good reasons for that choice: the fact that, as you said, a number of new characters Ð specically St John Rivers and his sisters Ð would otherwise enter the story late on; and also the fact that, without ashbacks, you would have to open with young Jane before moving on to an older actor, which would make the story very stopÐstart. And you canÕt cut the school scenes, at Lowood, because theyÕre so important.
The school stuff is really important: it shapes who Jane becomes. Theashbacks acted like JaneÕs memories and they became veryeconomical and distilled. You donÕt have to tell the whole narrative. You can say: what are the really important things about Lowood? Well, itÕs that relationship with Helen Burns. And how do you tell that and make it as moving as it has to be? How do you tell the relationship with Mrs Reed? Who is Mrs Reed? So thereÕs the script, and then thereÕs the next layer, the further distillation which comes in production. Even when you Þlm what you think is a distilled script, you Þnd itÕs massively overwritten and you need far less. IÕve found that in every Þlm IÕve done, and IÕve found the same with television: how little you actually need to tell the story. Actors bring depth and
emotion to lines in a way that never ceases to amaze me, and you Þnd you need fewer words.
JaneÕs friend Helen, who dies of TB at the school, is more of a presence in the script than the novel, almost like a guardian angel, guiding Jane away from Thorn eld and later leading her back. Did you conceive of her as being an actual ghost?
The book has such a spiritual dimension to it: the voices on the moor; the great dog with the red eyes; Jane hearing her motherÕs voice coming to her; her dreams that have such meaning. PeoplesÕ real lives are so constrained and yet their spirits are huge and their imaginations are as epic as the landscape. How do you bring that into the Þlm? How does Jane carry her past with her? Helen Burns as her guardian angel seemed to do all that in a really visual way Ð but none of it ended up in the Þnished Þlm. We shot it, but at the end of the day we cut it, because having written and shot it we found that it was there anyway without having to show it. We didnÕt need it. But IÕm reallyglad it was there in the script and IÕm really glad that we shot it, because everyone knew that was the feeling we were trying to capturein the Þlm and I think, cinematographically, it was there.
Most of the supernatural elements in the script are absent from the nished lm, in fact Ð though strangely I remembered them as being there, which must mean that the essence of them survived.
Oh, thatÕs interesting. Again, there was a lot more written and shot that was cut out, because it just slowed the story down. We loved all the gothic stuff Ð a huge country house with an old lady, a schoolgirl,her maid, some servants, a governess and a madwoman in the attic: whatÕs not to like? Ð but the pace was too slow for modern horror. The Þrst cut was three hours and twenty minutes long. So those elements are there but theyÕre there in their most distilled form, whichis what you get in a good edit Ð and this was a brilliant edit. I was in the edit quite a lot, and I absolutely loved working with the editor, Melanie Ann Oliver. Editors are like writers, in a way: you begin a whole different process in the editing room. The structure changes.
Things that you thought were incredibly important go out the window. You realise that youÕve written too much over here, but you havenÕt written some essential thing over there. I think we did two days of reshoots, or new shoots, on both *Jane Eyre* and *Tamara Drewe*, and they were transformative: you realised how badly you needed that extra scene or two.
Something I noticed in both lms is the way you combine two di erent elements from the books to create a third thing. For example, thereÕs a girl at Lowood who has red hair, but sheÕs a very minor character, and youÕve given that red hair to Helen, which makes her immediately visually distinctive. Do you deliberately look for those moments?
Yes, whenever you can. Again, itÕs distillation. You try to make every character and every scene do as much work as possible. In a novel, you can go off on all these amazing tangents. In a Þlm, everything has to be adding to the emotional journey. ThereÕs not really room in a Þlm to digress.
Similarly, and again during the Lowood sequence, you con ate two scenes from the novel: Jane dropping her writing slate and Helen being punished. In the lm, Jane drops the slate deliberately rather than accidentally, to try to stop the punishment and draw attention away from Helen.
Because the scene canÕt just be Jane watching Helen get punished. ItÕsabout making the protagonist active in every scene.
The secret at the heart of *Jane Eyre* is so well known that thereÕs an academic book named after it: the madwoman in the attic. In adapting the novel, do you approach it as if the audience doesnÕt know that, or are you obliged to navigate that knowledge in someway?
*Jane Eyre* doesnÕt know that, and the people whoÕve never read the book donÕt know that, so again it comes down to: who are youwriting this Þlm for? And when you look at it from JaneÕs perspective,it becomes a perfect horror: heÕs locked up a woman in his house for over a decade and gone off around Europe shagging. Who cares about the rest?
The ending of the lm, JaneÕs reunion with Rochester, is pretty speedy, but I noticed three deleted scenes in a row in the shooting script. Was the ending originally longer and more like the novel?
ThereÕd been an ongoing conversation about the ending, and I remember writing the last couple of lines in one of the caravans on set.The ending I originally wrote was longer and more like the novel, by the Þreside again, but CaryÕs instinct was that it should be much simpler and outside Ð and he was so right. Looking back on it, what I originally wrote was emotionally overwrought, and the simplicity of what we ended up with is really beautiful.
So do you feel that Rochester is redeemed in the lm?
Yes, I do. In the age of TimeÕs Up and #MeToo, heÕs a harder hero to like Ð heÕs certainly not the strong man Orson Welles portrayed in the1943 version, where you get the feeling that his wife was really crazy and actually deserved to be locked up Ð but I do think heÕs redeemed. The other vital thing about JaneÕs position at the end, the revolutionary thing that makes her RochesterÕs equal, is her economicindependence. She could not, and would not, go back to him without that. It was so hard to Þt in all that stuff about the letter from JaneÕs uncle and the news of her inheritance, but it was so important that when she comes back to Rochester sheÕs independent. St John Rivers isreally important to the story, too: a good man who just doesnÕt understand love in the way that Jane does, who doesnÕt understand this awful, terrifying passion that she has for Rochester, and itÕs so brave of Jane to know that sheÕd never be happy with St John and to do the morally wrong thing and go back to Rochester in pursuit of her own happiness. Again, radical: women were supposed to do the morally right thing.
IÕve always understood the denition of drama to be character driving plot, whereas melodrama is plot determining character. Would you agree with that, and is *Jane Eyre* both at once?
I think it is. When I read it as a kid some of the scenes with Bertha Mason had me absolutely choking with fear. Then you read it again as an adult and you think, ÔThatÕs a little bit hokey.Õ And then when we came to Þlm it, even though the actor who played Bertha was absolutely wonderful, it was just too strong a avour Ð so again it didnÕt make it in. But it should always be character driving the story,and if the plot is feeling too much like plot, I just have to go back andembed it better.
Are you more aware now of the type of scenes which wonÕt get shot, or will be shot and then get cut?
Yes. IÕm also much more aware that when a director comes on board itÕs very important for them to feel ownership and conÞdence for when they go into production, so IÕm always really willing to write a good directorÕs draft. *Tamara Drewe* and *Jane Eyre* were shot back to back, my Þrst two Þlms, and the learning curve I went on was absolutely massive. When I stepped on to the set of *Tamara Drewe*, IÕd never been on a Þlm set before. For weeks I had this feeling of, ÔI honestly donÕt know what the fuck IÕm doing.Õ Fortunately, Alison and Christine and Stephen Frears were amazingly good teachers. On *Tamara Drewe*, Frears would say to me [holds her and thumb a few inches apart], ÔThat long,Õ and I knew he wouldnÕt shoot the scene IÕd written, which was about a page-and-a-half, until it was that long, because he knew that was all the screen time it needed. What the director is really shooting is fragments, and itÕs so much better just to go, ÔHow can I distil this down to a tiny shot?Õ ItÕs a wonderful puzzle and I Þnd it so pleasurable.


Lucinda Coxon
Lucinda Coxon was born in 1962 in Derby.
As a playwright, her work includes: Waiting at the WaterÕs Edge (1993); Wishbones (1997); Nostalgia (2001); Vesuvius (2005); Happy Now? (2008); Herding Cats (2010); and Alys,Always (2019).
Her screen adaptation credits include: The Heart of Me (2002,novel The Echoing Grove by Rosamond Lehmann); *Wild* Target (2010, lm Cible mouvante by Pierre Salvadori); *The Crimson Petal and the White* (2011, novel by Michel Faber); The Danish Girl (2015, novel by David Ebershoff); and The Little Stranger(2018, novel by Sarah Waters).
APPROACHES TO ADAPTATION
Do you prefer to adapt material which chimes with your own work, or material which is completely di erent and gives you a chance to try out new things?
Writing original material and adapting are discrete processes for me. What it largely comes down to is that I write original material for thestage and I adapt for Þlm and TV. I havenÕt written original material for the screen in a long time, not least because IÕve not had greatexperiences of doing that. But in terms of the material IÕm attracted toadapt, itÕs very difÞcult to predict. Producers ask, ÔWhat are youlooking for? What do you want to do next?Õ and I never really know until I see it. IÕm fortunate enough at the moment to be offered a greatrange of material, and a lot of it is really intriguing. It will sometimes be classic books, sometimes contemporary books, and I may really love them, but I mostly decline, either because I donÕt have the time, or because I think, ÔYes, I could adapt that, but so could a lot of other people.Õ If itÕs a thing that anyone could do, then why would I do it? YouÕre attached to these projects for a long time, so itÕs got to be a situation where you feel the material needs you and you need it. In fact, the thing IÕm going to do next is exactly what I wasnÕt lookingfor Ð a TV series, eight episodes Ð but when I read the novel I thought, ÔYes, I can live with this for two years. Or ten years, if thatÕs what it ends up taking.Õ The bookÕs humane and bittersweet, itÕs not too issue-y but it speaks to the time Ð and itÕs set in Rome, so IÕm excited about a research trip! But then IÕm often excited about the research trip, anddonÕt actually make it until the writing process is Þnished. I was working on The Danish Girl for 11 years, but only went to Copenhagen when we were shooting.
Do you think adaptations involve a completely di erent set of creative gears to original screenplays?
No, not really. I mean, obviously youÕve got the gift of the material. I adapted The Echoing Grove by Rosamond Lehmann Ð the Þlm was called The Heart of Me, because Americans never knew what a grove was Ð and thatÕs an outstanding book, very rich pickings in terms of the underpinning it gives you. But with your own work, once youÕvegot the material of the Þrst draft, itÕs the same process.
Do you always agree a mission statement or direction of travel with whoever has commissioned the adaptation?
ThereÕs always a general conversation with producers about the book, but no matter how much you talk about the ÔtakeÕ, itÕs hard to know whether youÕre all imagining the same project. ThatÕs often not clear until itÕs too late. I actually think itÕs almost harder to be certain about the shared vision with adaptations than with original material.
Do you usually produce an outline or treatment before you start writing the script?
Not if I can possibly help it. Treatments are comforting for producers, but theyÕre not always very useful for writers. I Þnd them incrediblytime-consuming to write, and in fact a lot of writers now have other people produce them. IÕll often produce a summary of the book for myself, IÕll break the book down, but I wonÕt write an actual treatment. The occasions in the past when I have written verydeveloped treatments have mostly resulted in a script that was justdead on the page. You start writing using the treatment skeleton and youÕre essentially just putting in the dialogue. You end up with something that ticks all the boxes and has all its turning points in theright place, but has no organic ow to it and is at as a pancake. IÕm mixing metaphors now, but youÕre ogging this at pancake through draft after draft after draft and it just becomes a at pancake with all the trimmings. But essentially itÕs still a at pancake, and youÕd be better off with something more alive and unpredictable. I understand why producers want treatments, and of course itÕs another way of trying to work out if youÕre talking about the same book, but in the end itÕs a terrible waste of time for many writers.
If the author of the source material is living, do you nd it useful to have their input on the script?
I Þnd it useful to have their blessing. If the author is living I alwaysask, ÔDo you have any ambition to write this yourself?Õ and if they say,ÔI wouldnÕt mind,Õ then IÕm out. I donÕt want anyone looking over myshoulder in that way. If the material is factually based and youÕredealing with a historian or a biographer that can be helpful, but Þction is a different animal, and IÕve been very lucky that the livingnovelists IÕve worked with have been terriÞcally supportive and handsoff. So, for example, I couldnÕt have asked for better colleagues than Sarah Waters on The Little Stranger and David Ebershoff on The Danish Girl. I knew that The Danish Girl had been a great passion project for David, but when I went to meet him he said, ÔYou mustnÕt worry about me. If you sell your apartment you canÕt get upset when they put in a new kitchen.Õ The one I was most frightened about was Rosamond Lehmann, who wasnÕt living but was famously passionate about spiritualism. I used to think, as I was chopping out hugeswathes of this very autobiographical book, ÔIs Rosamond lookingdown at me, hissing?Õ
Is it easier to navigate notes from directors, producers and scripteditors when you have a piece of source material to measure the screenplay against?
I guess it is. I once worked on a World War II project, which involved a huge amount of historical research, and the producerÕs notes would include things like, ÔIs there any way the Americans could come into the war a bit earlier, because it seems to take ages to get there.Õ OneÕs eyes widen, you know? YouÕre sitting in a meeting saying, ÔHmmÉ youÕre not the Þrst person to wonder that. Churchill was quiteexercised about it himself. But IÕm sorry, the Americans really canÕt come into the war a bit earlier, theyÕve just got to wait for Pearl Harbor.Õ I think, generally, when youÕre dealing with notes from producers and directors, youÕre always dependent on them being good on script Ð and thatÕs not something you can actually take for granted. There are wonderful directors who are not very good on script and there are average directors who are brilliantly good on script: the two donÕt particularly go hand in hand. And there are wonderful directors who are not very interested in script development: they just want you to deliver it. Similarly, there are producers who hate developmentbecause what they love doing is making Þlms, and there are producerswho love development and then get cold feet when it comes to shooting it. You get a curious mix of different people, so itÕs not one size Þts all in that respect.
Have you ever started work on an adaptation and found it harder to adapt than you anticipated?
Always. ItÕs always harder than I anticipate. If I thought about all theproblems, IÕd never start. But IÕm never interested in projects where I can see exactly what the process or trajectory will be. With *The Crimson Petal and the White*, I knew what the emotional arrow ightwas, but then there was that huge trunk of material to deal with. And The Little Stranger has been interesting, because itÕs a sort of hybridnovel Ð it isnÕt horror, itÕs a psychological thriller with horror elements
Ð so itÕs not a straight genre Þlm. I can imagine a version where you said, ÔLetÕs take this and make it into a pure genre piece,Õ but we havenÕt done that. The Þlm has retained a lot of the ambiguity of the novel, including the unreliable narrator. I love an unreliable narrator, but an unreliable narrator in a Þlm is very different to an unreliable narrator in a novel. The novel turns on a series of apparentlysupernatural events that are only reported and never seen, and of course in a Þlm you have to make a decision about whether to show them. So another writer might well have pursued the genre version, but that wasnÕt something that interested me.
Have you ever been o ered material to adapt which you felt couldnÕt, or shouldnÕt, be adapted?
Yes, actually, a book I absolutely loved Ð and I turned it down because I loved it and couldnÕt think of a way to do it without destroying it. ItÕs a novel called The VintnerÕs Luck by Elizabeth Knox, and I still wake up sometimes thinking, ÔMaybe I could do it in the theatre or as a ballet,Õ because I would love to work with it. ItÕs set in France in the nineteenth century, and itÕs about a man falling in love with a male angel who he meets on the same day every year, so itÕs completely sui generis and structurally an absolute nightmare. I genuinely racked my brains thinking, ÔIs there a way in?Õ and in the end I decided I wouldnÕt be able to forgive myself if I was the person who served up the dogÕs dinner of this book. In fact, Niki Caro [writer-director of Whale Rider] took it on, and I gather that didnÕt work either, and I just thought, ÔIf she canÕt do it, then maybe it canÕt be done.Õ
Are there any screen adaptations which you think are especially good or you particularly admire?
ThatÕs almost too big a question to address. But I can certainlyremember the Þrst time I even thought of screen adaptation as an art form, and that was after seeing Laura JonesÕs version of Janet FrameÕs An Angel at My Table, directed by Jane Campion. That would have been in 1990, I suppose, when I was writing for the theatre and just starting to develop some original material for the screen. It was such a brilliant and arresting piece of work. I can absolutely recall that moment of thinking, ÔOkay, this might be an interesting way of collaboratingÉÕ

ADAPTING FICTION
Some writers try to include as much of the novel as possible by boiling scenes down to their essence. Others are more ruthless in editing it down to a sort of greatest hits, but being true to the spirit of it. Do you favour either of those approaches?
I think adapting Þction is pretty much a three-draft process. The Þrst draft is working out how much of the book is going to make it into the screenplay. The second draft is managing the structure of what youÕve got and solving the problems of what youÕve cut. And the thirddraft is taking ownership of it as a writer Ð which can mean lots of different things. The Echoing Grove adaptation took great liberties with the novel, but itÕs such a wonderful book, and Rosamond Lehmann has such a strong voice, that when I was minting fresh dialogue for it I was Þghting to make sure I was absolutely in tune with the existing dialogue. ItÕs very particular Ð particular to a periodand a place, but also highly idiosyncratic Ð and one of my happiest moments was sitting with the producer, Martin Pope, and the director, Thaddeus OÕSullivan, and them being unable to distinguish what IÕd written from what had come from Lehmann. When The Danish Girl came out, I was doing a press tour with David Ebershoff and I had to ask him what was his and what was mine; IÕd been working on the material for so long that I couldnÕt entirely remember. ThatÕs how it should be, really, that youÕre under the skin of it to that degree, but itÕshard to achieve that until youÕve got the nuts and bolts out of the wayat the beginning. IÕm afraid that practical initial response on reading is hardwired into me now. I can hardly even look at a novel on holiday anymore without thinking, ÔYou could cut the brother. She doesnÕt need to have that friend. They wouldnÕt have a dog, itÕs too expensive.ÕItÕs terrible!
If a novel has an unusual structure, would you try to reproduce that in adapting it?
I think youÕve got to decide what the relationship is between the structure and the story. Crimson Petal is full of postmodern tricks and I retained as much of that as I could, but what made those ourishes easy to Þght for was the fact that itÕs also got an enormously strong story motor. The Echoing Grove is probably the novel IÕve had to break down the most, because it goes backwards and forwards in time so often, and when I took it apart to try to Þgure it out I couldnÕt really get it back together again. The chronology didnÕt work and characters would turn out not to be old enough at certain points or too old at others, and in the end I had to say, ÔOkay, if weÕre reallygoing to bring this story to the screen, whatÕs actually useful?Õ So we concentrated on the central love triangle and narrowed it down to, I think, two or three timeframes, as opposed to the twenty-seven or something in the novel Ð and people liked it and were mostly very complimentary about it. But thereÕs easily enough material for another Þlm in that book Ð or a Þve-part series, if you were doing it now. So thatÕs the novel that IÕve had to attack most aggressively, but IÕd probably never take on anything that was super-quirky structurally. I donÕt much like Þlms that are fundamentally a trick.
Do you try to avoid voiceover in adapting a rst-person narrative, or do you see it as another tool in the toolbox?
ThereÕs a great snobbery about voiceover because itÕs viewed as cheating Ð and sometimes it does get put on a Þlm very late in the day to plug the gaps Ð but IÕm interested in telling stories and audiences like being told a story. In Crimson Petal and The Little Stranger, youÕre being told a story by someone you probably canÕt believe, and that makes it more fun, so why wouldnÕt you have voiceover? It can be a hostage to fortune: you can Þnd yourself in the edit, for example, and people are saying, ÔMaybe we can put a bit more voiceover on it,Õ because itÕs the thing thatÕs easiest to rewrite. ItÕs also useful on occasion, being able to shufe the pack of the voiceover. I have absolutely no compunction about it. I like it. I like direct address in the theatre, too. And when you think about a Þlm like American Beauty, what would it be without that opening monologue?
Do you use the language of the novel in writing the screenplay, ordo you put the whole thing in your own words?
It varies. The Þrst draft probably has a bit of the original prose in it, then by the end much less. With something historical like Crimson Petal, Michel Faber had done an extraordinary amount of research Ð I mean, he was writing that novel for years and years and years Ð so there would be some quirky bits of Victorian detail that just had to be in. The Danish Girl has very little of the prose from the novel in it, but that was a slightly different process.
Do you keep the novel beside you throughout the adaptationprocess, or do you try to internalise it and set it aside somewherealong the way?
The latter. I read an interview with a writer Ð a wonderful writer who adapts really well Ð and she was saying that she loads the whole novelinto Final Draft and then chisels away at it like a sculptor, so it begins as a 400-page screenplay and she just keeps cutting bits out. And I justthought, ÔGod almighty, thatÕs terrifying.Õ I certainly couldnÕt do that, I have to make all the marks on the page on my own, but I was impressed by the chutzpah of it. For a moment I thought, ÔAm I missing a trick here? Is that what I should have been doing all these years?Õ But that really wouldnÕt work for me. At the beginning I have to live alongside the book very closely. I had to rip Crimson Petal into four just to carry it around, because I didnÕt have a pocket or bag big enough to hold it. It felt very transgressive, breaking this book into pieces Ð and then I had to buy a new edition, because the pages started
uttering away. But I had to keep it to hand; I couldnÕt just do the surgery on a computer screen.
How much pressure do you feel when adapting a well-known andmuch-loved novel, knowing that you wonÕt be able to please everyone with the nished product?
You hope people will like things, but if IÕm honest, I donÕt really worry about it. A book and a Þlm are different. Terrible books can make marvellous Þlms, and sometimes wonderful books become terrible Þlms, so you just hope for the best. What I was worried about, when I wrote Crimson Petal, was whether Michel Faber would like it. Michel and I have since become very good friends, but I had absolutely no contact with him until weÕd shot it, so I adapted the book in the dark. ItÕs a mighty piece of writing, and I was so under the skin of it that I felt very connected to him. It really mattered to me that he liked it. So I feel lucky: I wanted Michel to love it and he did. He was incredibly generous about it. But in terms of the public, the readers, what can you say? All you can do is cross your Þngers.

CASE STUDY: *THE CRIMSON PETAL AND THE WHITE*
UK/Canada, 2011 ¥ Directed by Marc Munden ¥ Produced byDavid M. Thompson, Steve Lightfoot, Greg Dummett ¥ Screenplay by Lucinda Coxon, based on the novel by Michel Faber ¥ Cast: Romola Garai (Sugar), Chris OÕDowd (William Rackham), Amanda Hale (Mrs Agnes Rackham), ShirleyHenderson (Mrs Emmeline Fox), Mark Gatiss (Henry Rackham Junior), Richard E. Grant (Doctor Curlew), Gillian Anderson (MrsCastaway), Isla Watt (Sophie Rackham)
What was your initial reaction on reading this doorstop of a novel?
IÕd been approached many years before about doing the novel as a Þlm, and that didnÕt go anywhere. At the time, I was very unsure about it. My daughter was still very young, and I couldnÕt imaginegoing to L.A. with a two-year-old. I also couldnÕt imagine a studio making the Þlm, because itÕs Þlthy in a proper, dirt-poor kind of way. And I was worried about the sexual politics of it, wondering whetherthe novel wasnÕt sometimes having its cake and eating it. So I thoughtit was brilliant, but I was nervous about it, and in the end Curtis Hanson was brought in as director and I was immediately out of the conversation. He brought his own writer in; they then had about 20 writers on it sequentially and never made the Þlm. Fast forward almost a decade, David Thompson Ð who had been the executive producer on The Heart of Me Ð got in touch with my agent and said that he now had the rights, and would I think about looking at it again? I still had the book, so I got it down and started re-reading it and about a quarter of the way in I thought, ÔOkay, I know exactly what this is and I have to adapt it.Õ It was the same book, but a completely different experience, and the difference was not in the material, it was in the fact that by then my daughter was much older and I had learned that when you parent a child you have the luxury of, in a sense, re-parenting yourself Ð and I could see that in the book. I could see that the real centre of the novel was the love story between Sugar and her loverÕs daughter, Sophie. Their relationship was as complicated and terrifying and as full of passion and betrayal as anyadult love story, and as long as I could deliver that, the project would sing.
Could you even conceive of it as a feature lm now?
I donÕt know how one could do it as a Þlm. We had to really cut it down, even with four hours of screen time to play with. A lot of secondary characters got a bit shaved and I would like to have had more time with them. I could have done a whole episode that was justAgnes RackhamÕs diary. But in terms of the main story I think it was a pretty rare thing, where we managed to give it a real engine, but also allowed breathing space around the story. In many ways, it was by far my most straightforward experience of adapting Ð or of writing for the screen at all. Mostly because the show was greenlit very quickly and there wasnÕt a lot of time for rewrites. Some things need a lot of reworking to get right, but I came into this at quite a pace, I knew what I was doing with it and I think there is a freshness about it whichwould have been lost in revisions.
When I took it on, having told David I absolutely had to do it, I sortof had panic attacks because I wasnÕt a TV writer at all, and I feared it was going to be one of those bloody awful, badly-lit period dramas weÕve all seen too many of. I called Martin Pope to share my fears, and he was just great. He said, ÔYou canÕt think about it like that. YouÕve got to assume itÕs going to look like Barry Lyndon and have the performances from The Lives of OthersÕ Ð which had just come out. It was brilliant advice. I thought, ÔHeÕs right. IÕve got to set the bar in such a way that itÕs impossible to make it look like one of those things. IÕve got to come in with the high style.Õ So I went into the writing process swinging an axe above my head, in that respect. Then Marc Munden came on board, whoÕs a really fantastic director and no stranger to the high style, and he really got it and wanted to make something extraordinary. I remember him looking at the Þrst two pages and saying, ÔI donÕt want to do all that Dickensian shit. ItÕs got to look like Mumbai,Õ and I thought, ÔYes, exactly.Õ I was very lucky with it and it was a very happy process.
The novel has the denseness and richness of Charles Dickens and Wilkie Collins, but also a frankness and explicitness they could only dream of, the ability to tackle head on subjects which they could only allude to. Did you capture as much of that as you wanted to, given the sensitivities of TV audiences?
When I wrote the Þrst episode there was a rumble among the producers that the scene in the brothel was quite harsh. In fact, someone said, ÔIÊwas thinking the brothel would be more welcomingand sumptuous,Õ and I thought, ÔYes, thatÕs the puntersÕ point of view, and thatÕs not what weÕre making. WeÕre making it from the girlsÕpoint of view.Õ ItÕs a place of constant danger and degradation, but also the only place they can call home. ItÕs a place where people shit in a bucket because they have to, or where people pay to watch someone else shit in a bucket because they can. ItÕs a place where anything can and will happen, and where thereÕs no room for niceties. That seemed really important. I was very mindful that those scenes should not be titillating. I went out of my way to give the viewer no pleasure in that regard. Because what happens around those back streets is not beautiful. It should be clear that these women lived with a threat of violence over their heads constantly. The other thing I was pressured to tone down was SugarÕs friend Caroline, the prostitute, having been beaten. The producers said it was an incredibly depressing way to start, and I thought, ÔYes, but thatÕs the motor, and thatÕs potentiallySugarÕs future: being beaten to death for fun by these vile middle-classmen.Õ She has to operate out of that place of pain. Obviously I thoughthard about the sexual content. But I think the really terrible franknessin the adaptation are the scenes with the children in, because itÕs a world of such terror that any time you see a child on that screen youÕre afraid for them, even though there are people trying to look after them Ð or imagining theyÕre looking after them. In a sense, Sugar is a child: sheÕs her motherÕs child, and her mother is her madam. ItÕs a world where there are no grown-ups, there are just people who are bigger or richer or crueller than you. The little boy she leaves behind when she leaves the brothel; the betrayal of that boy is almost the hardest thing in it for me.
remember David Thompson was somewhat squeamish about SugarÕs psoriasis and used to note me on it, to the point where it became a kind of joke. HeÕd say, ÔYou think I havenÕt noticed that youhavenÕt cut the skin condition, but I have noticed and in the end youÕllhave to cut it.Õ My response was: ÔDavid, you might be right, I mighthave to cut it, but I havenÕt cut it yet,Õ and then we ran out of time and I didnÕt cut it. The psoriasis was a brilliant device for a character who has very few people she can talk to and who is unable to articulate her emotions verbally; having her skin are up when sheÕs under pressure is a fantastic barometer for her. I think itÕs also part of her appeal: itÕs disgusting, itÕs beautiful, itÕs unravelling, itÕs like lace, it makes her vulnerable and porous in the world. ItÕs so many extraordinary things. I mean, why would you lose that? Anyway, that ended up being a battle I won, and DavidÕs been gracious about it since. ItÕs fascinatingthe things that people are squeamish about. With The Danish Girl, the one note that IÕd get endlessly was, ÔIs there any way we can talk about Gerda not being pregnant without talking about her period?Õand I said, ÔThere are only so many ways to Þnd out that youÕre not pregnant!Õ I was sure it wouldnÕt be a problem, but it came up again and again Ð especially in America.
