The opening of the novel has one of the boldest narrative voices IÕve ever read, a direct address to the reader which is both completely of its time and entirely modern. In the series, itÕs Sugar who takes us by the hand and looks us in the eye and leadsus into this world Ð which is equally bold in its way.
I was always very determined and speciÞc about that opening visual. That was always how those pages ran Ð with that set of images and Sugar writing her story Ð and I donÕt think we changed it at all. In the novel, thereÕs an omniscient narrator who introduces the story, then disappears halfway through, and it just becomes a free for all. Obviously that wasnÕt going to work for us, but I also wanted passionately for it to be SugarÕs story. The beginning, with her plunging us into this confusing landscape as sheÕs desperately trying to write her way out of it, seemed very important. And then when we arrive at the end, so much of her anger and confusion have left her, have been poured into this book that is Þnally jettisoned and sheÕs on her way Ð without us. The ending of the novel frustrated people and there was a suggestion that, of course, I would Þnd a less ambiguous way to end the series, and I thought, ÔFuck, no. WeÕve seen this woman from every angle, sheÕs had so little privacy. SugarÕs entitled to keep the next chapter to herself. ItÕs hers, not ours.Õ We did briey talk about whether there would be some kind of sequel Ð what Sugar didnext Ð and there was quite a lot of excitement around that, but for me,she has to disappear, into whatever the future holds. The idea at the end is that she says, ÔYour moneyÕs run out. TimeÕs up.Õ
The novel is in ve parts: ÕThe StreetsÕ; ÔThe House of Ill ReputeÕ; ÔThe Private Rooms and the Public HauntsÕ; ÔThe Bosom of the FamilyÕ; and ÔThe World at LargeÕ. The series is in four parts, compressing the rst two sections of the novel into the rst episode, then more or less following the shape of the book for the next three. Did that compression of the opening sections come from a desire to get things moving as quickly as possible?
ThatÕs probably about right. One of the things that made it a pleasure to write was that the four parts are very different. TheyÕre completely different landscapes, but all of them are dangerous. The streets and the brothel are dangerous, but living as a kept woman and beingisolated is also dangerous. Then the third episode, where she moves into the house, is like a thriller Ð and the fourth episode, when she tries to cross back into those other worlds, is something else again. Tohave those four discrete worlds to play with was very good fun.
A screen adaptation gives you the chance to cross-cut and juxtapose scenes in ways which a novel may not. William showing Sugar the burning lavender elds, for example, as Henry immolates himself while lost in lust for Mrs Fox. Do you feel at those moments like you almost get to improve on the novel?
Compression is often a bonus. YouÕre experiencing something in a completely different timeframe when youÕre watching things on screen, and thereÕs something hugely potent about that. There are lots of pleasures in that novel, and I hope that people saw the series and then went on to read the novel, but the truth is that you can sometimes deliver a different set of pleasures. The ability to work that way, not just with visuals, but with sound design as well, is a gift.Crimson Petal was a truly immersive watch because those elements were so strong. Marc and the designers really delivered an unusuallyrich sensory experience.
The novel doesnÕt teem with characters in the same way that DickensÕ do, and in fact for such a big book a lot of it is quite contained, but you obviously still had to make some pretty hefty cuts. How did you determine what to keep and what to cut in terms of character and plot?
It was actually relatively simple, in that Sugar came Þrst. SugarÕs story was the most important thing, and her trajectory with Sophie, that terrible chain of mothers and daughters. ThatÕs why AgnesÕs diary, fabulous as it is, simply couldnÕt be a big feature. But SugarÕs relationship with Agnes is so brilliant: by helping Agnes escape fromher husband, Sugar simultaneously saves a woman in peril and gets ridof her loverÕs wife Ð just as she rescues Sophie by abducting her. YouÕre so spoiled as a writer to be allowed that sort of ambiguity, to be working in a universe with characters who are so shifting and true. I really loved Sugar as though she were my own. I love that sheÕs not cold and calculating, sheÕs just a survivor, a human being trying to Þnd a safe corner somewhere. I really think sheÕs one of the great female characters. ItÕs a great role and Romola Garai did a brilliant job of inhabiting her.
The novel positively drips with disgust at the way most of its malecharacters treat their wives and lovers Ð and women generally. It feels like you picked up that ball and ran with it Ð and it seems even more relevant several years on.
Absolutely. WeÕre living at a time now where lots of ÔniceÕ men feel tarred by the #MeToo brush and are angry about that. I think WilliamRackham knows just what theyÕre going through: ÔIÕm a nice guy, IÕm not like them.Õ And thereÕs a moment in the story where you think heÕs really going to evolve and become the good guy, but of course thatÕs impossible. His limitations are what they are, heÕs operating in the context that heÕs operating in, and itÕs a crazy fantasy that he mightmarry this prostitute, his mistress. Sugar responds to this betrayal withincredible speed and nerve, stealing the daughter heÕs not Þt to look after and leaving him in a place of abject pain. But where and to whatdoes she take that child? Is history going to repeat itself? Is she savingSophie, or is Sophie going to be a prostitute in ten yearsÕ time? All thatambiguity, all those possibilities, are there in that ending, which justfrays away to eternity.

CASE STUDY: THE DANISH GIRL
UK/US, 2015 ¥ Directed by Tom Hooper ¥ Produced by Tim Bevan, Eric Fellner, Anne Harrison, Tom Hooper, Gail Mutrux ¥ Screenplay by Lucinda Coxon, based on the novel by David Ebershoff ¥ Cast: Eddie Redmayne (Einar Wegener/Lili Elbe), Alicia Vikander (Gerda Wegener), Ben Whishaw (HenrikSandahl), Sebastian Koch (Dr Kurt Warnekros), Amber Heard (Ulla Paulson), Matthias Schoenaerts (Hans Axgil)
I found this novel very moving but quite distressing: LiliÕs physicaland emotional journey is so hard. How did you respond to it?
IÕm embarrassed to say that the book sat on my desk unread for quite a long time, because on the Þrst edition it said, ÔThe story of the worldÕs Þrst sex changeÕ. ThatÕs not the language weÕd use now Ð and itÕs actually not true Ð but it wasnÕt something I felt I was especiallyinterested in. So the book hung around for a while and then after a nudge from my agent I started reading it, and it wasnÕt what IÕd expected at all. I suppose the thing that really gripped me about it was this deeply loving marriage between two artists. I was just fascinated by Einar and GerdaÕs shared trajectory, and by the sense of Lili as her own art project, achieved through their collaboration. GerdaÕs love for Lili and her willingness to embrace incredible change, and LiliÕs bravery in becoming herself Ð the courage and vision involved in the decision still astonishes me. But also the incredible modernity of their imaginations. Lili is so much more than the product of a surgicalprocedure, the Ôsex changeÕ. I have a painting from the set of the Þlm of Eddie as Lili, and it always has a vase of lilies in front of it. I Þnd myself sitting and looking at it sometimes, just wondering about her. I spent so many years trying to really understand and imagine her.
LiliÕs story has always had an ambiguous relationship with historical fact. David Ebersho Õs novel is a ctional story inspired by her life, and he describes her own edited book of diaries and correspondence, Man into Woman, as a Ôsemi-ctional, hybrid biographyÕ. HeÕs also described how she worked on the manuscript in the last years of her life and was Ôkeen to tell her story, but also conscious of creating a myth about herselfÕ. Adapting the novel, which itself drew on her book, what course did you set out to chart between fact and ction?
Well, the novel tells the story from before they met and takes place over a much longer period of time. Also, Gerda is called Greta and sheÕs American, and when I asked why, David was verystraightforward and said, ÔIt was my Þrst novel, and the idea of writing a woman at all was terrifying and the idea of writing a Danish woman was even more terrifying, so I thought if she were American it would give me a much more secure base to imagine her from.Õ ThereÕs also a character central to the novel who doesnÕt appear in the Þlm atall, and thatÕs GerdaÕs brother. I couldnÕt see why we needed him; he seemed to me to be extraneous to the story and to get in the way ofthat central relationship. I wasnÕt interested in the before and I wasnÕtinterested in extra people, but David had done a lot of research, so I thought, ÔWhy would we tell the story if weÕre not going to tell the story? LetÕs take whatÕs factual in the novel, and what David has so brilliantly imagined of the factual, and strip away everything else.Õ
There are so many stories about Lili, but the facts are very slipperyand she remains mysterious in lots of ways. I suppose I dealt with thatby having my point of view in the Þlm be GerdaÕs. I always felt it was GerdaÕs Þlm in many respects: sheÕs the audienceÕs proxy, and I was behind her eyes a lot of the time. That role is a stealth lead, which is perhaps one of the reasons Alicia won the Oscar for it.
How much additional research did you do before writing the script?
I read a lot Ð Man into Woman, of course, and everything else I could Þnd Ð and when I Þrst delivered the script I thought, ÔThis is going to be a cinch to get together. The story is extraordinary, the roles are phenomenal, who wouldnÕt want to make this Þlm?Õ But it turned outthat lots of people didnÕt want to make that Þlm. There were directors who were intrigued, and there were actors queuing up to be in it, but there were also Þnanciers who said, ÔFantastic script, but no one wants to see a Þlm about a man having his dick cut off.Õ And you think, ÔWow. You could have dressed that up a bit, but what a reality check.Õ So the original producers Ð primarily Gail Mutrux and Anne Harrison
Ð tried valiantly for a decade to get the Þlm made. The script didnÕt really change very much over that time, but I constantly thought it was going to have to, because in those ten years trans awareness reallygathered pace. There was so much more interest in the territory, more research material available Ð and, crucially, the internet happened,which meant that previously uncollected documents in French and German started appearing online. Europe was in chaos in the period after LiliÕs death. The hospital in Dresden where she had her operations was Þrebombed by the Allies in 1945, so her medical records vanished. But gradually archives began to be unearthed and I was constantly thinking, ÔOh, Christ, some document is going to appear which will mean IÕll have to completely rewrite the second act.Õ But mostly, when new facts emerged, we were on the right track, so there were very few revelations that surprised me. The only thing I found out quite late in the day that I wished IÕd known earlier was that Gerda was considered very *wild* as a young woman, and her bourgeois parents were thrilled when she met Einar because he seemed so conventional and they thought heÕd settle her down.
In fact, GerdaÕs own sexuality may have been more uid than presented in either the lm or the novel, as suggested by the erotic lesbian element present in some of her drawings and paintings.
Yes, and there were certainly conversations about including that. ThereÕs no question in my mind that Gerda would have loved to continue in a marriage with Lili, and to some extent expected that. But Lili wanted to be a ÔrealÕ girl: a conventional, heterosexual girl. Soin the end societal pressure has its way with our heroines. I was much keener than others to make something of this. Gerda produced a lot of really beautiful, really witty lesbian erotica, and I was interested in including it as an expression of her sexuality and of her love for Lili, but the feeling was that we were already going as far as we could with the Þlm. There were other things I wanted to address that fell by the wayside. One of the reasons Lili pursued further surgery was that she was interested in Þnding out whether or not she could have a baby, and I said that was something extraordinary that we should explore, but again the feeling was that it would be very alienating. It was a sort of madness at the time for her to think that way, but look where we are now with these issues: she was just way ahead of the curve. So I fought that corner, and I fought the lesbian corner, and in the end, even though I would like to see a version with those elements in, it became clear that the producers were absolutely right, and that if you were looking for people to actually Þnance a Þlm in this territory, it was already over the line.
The fateful favour which Gerda asks of Einar Ð to put on womenÕs clothing and take the place of her absent subject in a portrait sheÕs painting Ð comes right at the start of the novel. You spend a little more time establishing Gerda and Einar, including their respective achievements and ambitions as artists, before you getto that scene. What were the key things you were trying to set upin the rst ten pages of the script?
I was interested in it as a love story, but also a love story between two artists. Two artists in a house, thereÕs inevitably an element of competition Ð and for this couple, itÕs also heavily gendered. Gerda was incredibly underrated as a painter, and it seemed to me that we needed to bring that out, to make it clear they knew that being a woman came at a price. We also needed to understand that this relationship was full of give and take, that they were extremelycomfortable as a couple, and that Einar was happy to do this for her. In the Þlm, it comes out of Gerda having had a difÞcult experience,and in that moment Einar will act the woman and Gerda can act the man and she can feel better about herself as an artist and ÔheÕ will enable her. ItÕs about love and art, and gender and selfhood, and release and surrender. ItÕs about personal realisation, I suppose, and about love and art as the tools for that realisation. Stories of courage come in all sorts of guises Ð with guns and on horses and in refugee camps Ð and this happens to be a story of courage that comes in silk stockings. A story of courage and of incredible imagination.
I wrote down a line from the novel about that: ÔHeÕd begun to think of his make-up box as his palette.Õ In other words, Lili becomes EinarÕs lifeÕs work, the ultimate form of self-expression. And, in turn, GerdaÕs best work comes from capturing that transformation and interpreting it in her own style.
When you Þrst see that painting, EinarÕs painting, and Gerda is looking at it, thereÕs a sense that sheÕs looking to understand him as well as looking at the brush strokes. SheÕs fascinated by him as an artist, but sheÕs also just fascinated by him. SheÕs in a relationship with him and she has questions about him, and at that point in the Þlm those questions are not articulated, but by the end of the Þlm sheÕs in the landscape of that painting and, Þnally, she knows. She understandsthe person sheÕs been in love with all these years, no matter what name or gender they have gone by. You know, before a Þlm is released, thereÕs always a terrible marketing meeting where they showyou these charts and tell you the demographic most likely to enjoy it. And obviously, that happened with The Danish Girl. The Þrst time I saw the Þlm with an audience was in Venice at the festival, and thatÕs a very particular industry crowd so you canÕt glean much from the experience. Then, when I saw it in Toronto, it was with a much older, white North American audience, and I was frankly a bit nervous about their reaction. But people found it incredibly moving. There were a lot of middle-aged men who would not have been in our subset on the marketing diagram, and one of them came over to me at the end of the screening and said he had gone out and phoned his wife because the Þlm had made him think about their long journey togetherand he wanted to hear her voice. People who had been in seasoned marriages didnÕt think it was about trans issues; they thought it was about a long road, about the stuff you Þght through together, and thecompromises you make and whether you choose to self-actualise at the expense of someone else. What are the limits of love?
One of the themes of the novel is the extent to which we are all a product of our pasts, and whether we can e ace the parts of our past which we no longer identify with, and there are sections devoted to Einar and GretaÕs lives before they met each other. In the lm, EinarÕs past is dealt with through dialogue, while GerdaÕs past is barely mentioned. Were those easy cuts to make, or did you consider including some of that material via ashback?
We had an opening sequence for a while that was in ashback, of Einar as a child in his motherÕs wardrobe wearing his motherÕs clothes after her death. It was a seductive idea, but actually not a sufÞcientlyspeciÞc one: the little boy in the lovely clothes, and the dad beatinghim because he wants to be pretty, felt a bit generic. It also felt like very hokey psychology: the child who has lost a beloved mother and is now over-identifying with her. The imaginative weight of a detail like that in a Þlm is enormous, and it felt as if we were trying to explainLili Ð and I didnÕt want to explain her, I wanted her to explain herself.So it went. And again, I wasnÕt interested in the before. ItÕs a story thattakes place in the present. As painters, and in their marriage, theyÕre inthe now, all the time.
The novel ends with Lili playing hooky from the hospital in Dresden with Anna and GretaÕs brother Carlisle, after Greta and her lover, Hans, have got together and left for America. The lm ends with Lili dying at the hospital during a visit from Gerda, thenGerda and Hans visiting the place in Jutland which forms the rst shot of the lm and the subject of EinarÕs landscape painting. Both end with an image of unfettered ight Ð a kite in the novel, a scarf in the lm Ð but your ending, in some ways, is sadder than David Ebersho Õs, which is unusual in a commercial lm. Again,can you talk about the interplay of fact and ction there?
I wanted Gerda to be there at the end. I wanted them to go all the waydown the road together. In reality, Gerda wasnÕt there at the end, sheÕdactually remarried Ð a marriage that didnÕt last very long, a rebound. But she and Lili were still very connected. Lili was going to come and stay with Gerda to recuperate when she came out of hospital, and thereÕs a story Ð I canÕt remember if itÕs in Man into Woman or in another account Ð that Gerda was preparing the room for Lili and suddenly felt the air go coldÉ and she knew what had happened. So Ifelt that they still loved one another, that they were still taking care of one another and were still one anotherÕs primary emotional connection, and I wanted to honour that. The question of whether Liliwould die in the Þlm or not was really fraught, because on the one hand I was worried about the hetero-normative ending where the LGBT character dies and the ÔstraightÕ character gets a boyfriend, but on the other hand I wanted to be honest about LiliÕs courage and the fact that, even though she lived, really lived to the fullest degree, the surgery did kill her. They had friends who thought that the surgeonshould have been prosecuted; who thought that she was murdered, essentially, by surgeons who had gone too far. The type of surgery Lili was going through was performed by people who learned their trade on the battleÞelds of the First World War, and I think she knew that she was risking her life. And in the end, I thought it would be wrongto shy away from that.
Tomas Alfredson and Lasse Hallstrm were attached to direct at di erent points before Tom Hooper came on board. You said the script didnÕt change much over a decade, but presumably all three of them had their own ideas about it?
I did maybe ten drafts with small changes before the different directors came in, then I did a pretty big rewrite for Tomas, until we had quite a different Þlm Ð though I think we would have gone back to something more like what weÕd had to begin with Ð then I did a less radical rewrite for Lasse, then I did a very big rewrite for Tom, and then it kind of slipped back to where we started. When directors board a project, theyÕre often playing catch up. If youÕve been working on something for ten years and someone comes in and says, ÔIÕve had this brilliant idea about how to do it differently,Õ you probably have tried to do it differently and thereÕs a good reason why it hasnÕt worked. ThereÕs a process of directors needing to get across the territory, to get up to their elbows in the project, and a lot of the time youÕre conscious that the rewrite youÕre working on, the pages youÕreshowing them, are things that will never make it into the Þlm. You have to decide how much you trust a director, or get more skilled in understanding why theyÕre asking for a rewrite, because the risk is that you deliver something youÕre unhappy with Ð and youÕre then stuck with it. So we did have a very different version of The Danish Girl, and then Tom and Alicia and Eddie went into a week of rehearsals, just the three of them very early on, and Tom rang me and said, ÔThis new version is great, and Eddie and Alicia have had some ideas, and weÕre really looking forward to showing you what weÕve got.Õ I went in and there was this cut-and-paste version theyÕd come upwith, and we all read it and I thought, ÔOkay, itÕs already in a version IÕm not happy about, and now the actors and director have had a goitÕs in a version IÕm even less happy about. So what am I going to do?Õ But I didnÕt have to do anything. There was a pause at the end of the reading where we all looked at each other, then Alicia said, ÔShall we just do the script we had at the beginning?Õ and everyone very quickly said, ÔYes, letÕs do that.Õ So thatÕs what happened. And it was a greatand happy moment for me, as you can imagine. But those detours are important. Everyone had gone through the process they needed to gothrough and we reverted to something very close to the script we had begun with. There was really valuable input from various colleaguesand a fair bit of Þne-tuning, but what Tom shot was, in the main, the last draft I wrote independently Ð the last best draft.


Andrew Davies
Andrew Davies was born in 1936 in Rhiwbina, Cardiff. He has written original series and single dramas for television, plays forstage and radio, and novels for adults and children.
Since 1975 he has amassed more than 50 screen adaptationcredits, including dramatisations of novels by Kingsley Amis,Jane Austen, John Banville, Maeve Binchy, Joanna Briscoe, JohnCleland, Dennis Danvers, Daniel Defoe, R.F. DelderCharles Dickens, Michael Dobbs, Alexandre Dumas, George Eliot, HelenFielding, E.M. Forster, Elizabeth Gaskell, George and Weedon Grossmith, Alan Hollinghurst, Winifred Holtby, Elizabeth Jane Howard, Victor Hugo, Angela Lambert, John le Carr, Boris Pasternak, William Makepeace Thackeray, Leo Tolstoy, AnthonyTrollope, Sarah Waters, Evelyn Waugh, Mary Wesley and AngusWilson.
Four of his TV adaptations have won BAFTA Drama Serial or Single Drama Television Awards, and he was jointly nominatedfor the BAFTA Best Adapted Screenplay Film Award for BridgetJonesÕs Diary.
In December 2018, he was the subject of a BBC documentary, Andrew Davies: Rewriting the Classics.
APPROACHES TO ADAPTATION
Do you think adaptations involve a completely di erent set of creative gears to original screenplays?
Not entirely, but yes, substantially. I think one of the reasons I do a lot of adaptations is that actually thinking up stories is something I Þnd quite difÞcult.
Do you always agree a mission statement or direction of travel with whoever has commissioned the adaptation?
No. TheyÕre getting me because either they love working with me, or they love my work, or they think it will give them a better chance of getting the thing on. I mean, of course we discuss it beforehand and conÞrm that we have a similar approach to the subject.
Do you usually produce an outline or treatment before you start writing the script?
Yes, there has to be one. I need one. Recently IÕve been working with the same people, so we have a lunch and we talk about it and someone starts batting an outline about Ð it might be me, it might be them Ð and it goes back and forth a few times Ð sometimes only once or twice Ð until I feel ready to start writing.
If youÕre o ered material to adapt which has been adapted previously, do you take account of any previous versions when writing yours?
Yes, I do. It can be bad, but I generally Þnd that IÕm not too swayedby theirs. The thing you have to watch out for is unconscious plagiarism: you think youÕve come up with an original idea Ð and bloody hell, there it was in some adaptation by someone else that yousaw years ago. So I do usually look at existing ones if I can bear to.
If the author of the source material is living, do you nd it useful to have their input on the script?
Oh, yes. ItÕs only courteous to meet them, and often IÕve found it extremely helpful. ItÕs good to Þnd out what they feel, and what they think is particularly important. Sarah Waters is a prime example, because she was writing about a world I knew little of. SheÕs also someone whoÕs extremely movie-literate, and understands that adaptations are not going to be literal copyings-out of the novel.
In fact, in the BBC documentary, she says that seeing your screenplays of her novels, and how you get to the heart of scenes, has been *an education* in terms of her own writing.
Yes. Gosh. I thought, ÔThatÕs good!Õ Novelists donÕt often view storytelling in such a ruthless way Ð which you have to when youÕrewriting scripts. Elizabeth Jane Howard [author of Falling] was rather disappointed, I think, that I didnÕt use all of her dialogue and do a kind of setting-out of her book in script form.
Is it easier to navigate notes from directors, producers and scripteditors when you have a piece of source material to measure the screenplay against?
It can be, yes. YouÕre more likely to understand what theyÕre on about.Sometimes, especially if itÕs your own original piece, notes come in and you think, ÔWhat on earth is going through their heads?Õ So yes, it is easier.
Have you ever started work on an adaptation and found it harder to adapt than you anticipated?
Almost every time [laughs]. Some have been more difÞcult than others. Doctor Zhivago was an exceptionally difÞcult one. Of course I was very familiar with the David Lean movie, which tells a wonderful story and has some wonderful scenes in it Ð then you read the book and you realise there are hardly any scenes in it, he just lightly refers to things. So it was a question of making up a lot of the scenes in accordance with what Pasternak must have imagined happened, and also steering clear of what David Lean did. So that was a very hard one.
Have you ever been o ered material to adapt which you felt couldnÕt, or shouldnÕt, be adapted?
Well, often in the sense that I didnÕt think it was worth doing. But things that exist very much on the page? No. No oneÕs ever asked me to adapt some of the more obscure Virginia Woolf things, and IÕd turn them down. One of the ones IÕm working on now, A Suitable Boy, I read ten or Þfteen years ago because someone wanted me to adapt it, and I found it an absolutely lovely read but I thought, ÔItÕs probablymuch better if it stays the way it is.Õ And then, more recently, the people at Lookout Point, who I did *War & Peace* and Les Misrables with, were so enthusiastic about it and said weÕd have such fun doing it Ð and indeed that turned out to be the case. But itÕs such an enormous book that you canÕt really represent the whole complexity, the whole panorama, the vast number of characters and plots in it. WhatÕs come out is six hours, but it should have been eight, and it would have been really good at ten or more. ItÕs going to feel verytight at six. But itÕs such a beautifully written book and so funny, that it was a joy to work on.
Are there any screen adaptations which you think are especially good or you particularly admire?
I can think of a couple off the top of my head. One is ChristopherHamptonÕs script for Dangerous Liaisons and another is Hossein AminiÕs version of The Wings of the Dove Ð a terriÞc adaptation and extraordinarily moving. IÕm not a great reader of Henry James, but it felt of its age and somehow very modern as well. I should also mention two by Tom Stoppard: Anna Karenina and ParadeÕs End. The Þlm of Anna Karenina was disappointing, but the screenplay was exemplary. And ParadeÕs End was so much better than the book: incisive, sharp, funny. And some of the big old traditional things, like David LeanÕs *Great Expectations*. I canÕt remember who actuallywrote the screenplay for that, but with a David Lean movie you think of him having a big input into the script. So those are a few I particularly admire.

ADAPTING FICTION
Some writers try to include as much of the novel as possible by boiling scenes down to their essence. Others are more ruthless in editing it down to a sort of greatest hits, but being true to the spirit of it. Do you favour either of those approaches?
No, not really. What I tend to do is ask fundamental questions like,ÔWhat is this book about?Õ and ÔWhose story is it?Õ and ÔWho do we care about?Õ and ÔHow can they carry us through the story?Õ. Then IÕmhoping to Þnd stuff in the book that I can lift out and make it do the job, and if not IÕm just going to have to make the scenes up. But when I do make scenes up, people donÕt say, ÔYou made those scenes up,Õ they say, ÔThat was so true to the book!Õ
Well, if youÕve got it right, theyÕll say that. If youÕve got it wrong, theyÕre more likely to notice.
I suppose thatÕs true, yes.
If a novel has an unusual structure, would you try to reproduce that in adapting it?
Not necessarily. But sometimes it can be something good and something thatÕs a bit of a revelation because itÕs not something I would have thought of. The Line of Beauty has got big gaps between the three sections of the narrative, and Nick [lead character in the novel] almost seems to be a different person because heÕs in a slightlydifferent world in each one, but of course heÕs not different. So the construction of the novel was very clever, and I did follow that and I think it worked.
Do you try to avoid voiceover in adapting a rst-person narrative, or do you see it as another tool in the toolbox?
I do see it as another tool in the toolbox, but I know it can be used in a very lazy way by people who canÕt make the effort to do it in actions. One of the biggest mistakes IÕve seen in an adaptation Ð though it was a Þlm which was very well liked Ð was the Edith Wharton Þlm, The Age of Innocence, which has a past-tensevoiceover. That immediately tells you, ÔThis is old shit. ItÕs not happening now. It all happened long ago,Õ and holds you back as an audience from entering the story. There were some lovelyperformances in that Þlm and some very beautiful cinematography, but if a movie is anything itÕs in the present tense. So I suppose youcould call it a boldly original use of voiceover, but I just thought it wasa mistake.
Do you use the language of the novel in writing the dialogue, or do you put the whole thing in your own words?
It depends. Some novelists write dialogue that plays well and some donÕt. Jane Austen, you can just copy out. She writes too much for a scene Ð she was very skilful at dialogue and enjoyed exercising that skill at length Ð but I think youÕre a fool if you donÕt use her language. It makes the job so much easier, really. Vikram Seth is another one who writes lovely dialogue: very funny, very apt, very neat. Other people, it seems like good dialogue when youÕre reading the novel, but when you put it down and imagine someone actually speaking it, it doesnÕt work.
Do you keep the novel beside you throughout the adaptationprocess, or do you try to internalise it and set it aside somewherealong the way?
I keep it pretty close. [Sees me eyeing a battered book on the table]ThatÕs not a novel, actually, but IÕm keeping it pretty close. ItÕs a book about the last Empress of China, by Marina Warner. Terribly hard, that, because you have the facts, in a sense, but every time I start to write a scene I have this voice inside me saying, ÔYouÕve got no idea what these people are like!Õ But yes, I do keep the book by me to refer to. There was a producer who was very much a mentor to me years ago, Louis Marks Ð I did Middlemarch with him, amongst other things Ð and he said that when you reach a problem with an adaptation, if itÕs a great writer then youÕll Þnd the answer in the book. I think thatÕs true.
I was quite surprised to see the Marina Warner book there, because among your many adaptations almost none are of non ction.
Mr Selfridge was based on a biography, and what we could Þnd out about his life and his business. But yes, generally I would rather adapta novel.
Perhaps because non ction is closer to original material: youhave to invent more of the story, which you said you nd dicult.
Yes, I expect so.
How much pressure do you feel when adapting a well-known andmuch-loved novel, knowing that you wonÕt be able to please everyone with the nished product?
When I Þrst started Pride and Prejudice I was feeling that pressure,but not so much these days. I certainly donÕt want to disappointmyself, because presumably itÕs a book I love too, but thatÕs really all Ithink about when IÕm adapting it. Of course, I hope everyoneÕs goingto like it Ð but if they donÕt, I donÕt care.

THREE DECADES OF ADAPTATIONS: HOUSE OF CARDS TO LES MISRABLES
Between the late sixties and the early nineties you mainly wrote original material. Since then, youÕve mainly written adaptations. Do you feel that the material you choose to adapt, and the way you choose to adapt it, reects your own voice as much as original work would?
Probably not quite as much, but it canÕt help doing so. ItÕs one of those things thatÕs easier for other people to tell me: ÔThis is just like you.Õ And you think, ÔNo, itÕs just a job I was doing.Õ But I was quitestruck in that documentary by what Jane Tranter [former Controller of Drama Commissioning at the BBC] was saying about what I did. I thought, ÔOh!Õ
House of Cards and its sequels are comparatively unusual among your work in having contemporary settings Ð even the modern
ction youÕve adapted is often set in the past. Do you nd period pieces more interesting?
No, I donÕt, though for the purposes of long-form television nineteenth-century Þction is often much better because thereÕs more story to it, combined with really deep and interesting characters, and some subtlety and reection of society. So thatÕs the reason. IÕve written quite a lot of scripts that are contemporary which never gotmade.
So itÕs dictated by the market? Period pieces are more popular?
And famous books Ð well-known titles. TheyÕre just easier to raise money on.
Do you approach classic and modern ction in the same way, by asking the same questions: what is this book about, whose story is it?
I think I do, yes.
YouÕve written for the stage as well as the screen, and Francis UrquhartÕs monologues in House of Cards are strongly reminiscent of Richard IIIÕs soliloquies. Do you think thereÕs a theatrical element in other adaptations youÕve done?
Sometimes. In House of Cards it was something I felt needed doing,and I was heavily encouraged to do that by Michael Wearing [former Head of Serials at the BBC] and really enjoyed going for it. Usingtalking to camera in television is a dangerous thing, but I come back to it from time to time. In fact, IÕm doing it with this [points to The Dragon Empress]: she talks to us from the nineteenth century, understanding that weÕre in the twenty-Þrst century. Might be great,might be a disaster. ItÕs going to be six parts and IÕve written two so far and those seem to have gone over well, so Þngers crossed.
YouÕve adapted several authors more than once, but the one youÕve returned to most often is Jane Austen. Do you feel a particular a nity with her work?
IÕve always loved her work, ever since I was a boy. I just think sheÕs brilliant. Actually, another of the things IÕm working on is Sanditon [AustenÕs unnovel]. The ambition is to do it rather like Mr Selfridge and turn it into a returning series. In fact, I only found enough material in the novel for half of the Þrst episode. ItÕs about 100 pages, and all she does is introduce the characters and give us the setting Ð but what characters and what a setting, because itÕs such a departure for her. ThereÕs an entrepreneurial businessman trying to turn a seaside town into a fashionable resort, making plans and borrowing money and courting celebrities. ThereÕs the old town and the new town. ThereÕs a West Indian heiress with a huge fortune.
Fancy that: Jane Austen, on her deathbed, writing her Þrst black leading character. So thatÕs the world of the novel, and IÕm reallyexcited about it.
Your approach to Pride and Prejudice was to tell the story as much through Mr DarcyÕs eyes as through Elizabeth BennetÕs. How important is point of view in your adaptations?
ItÕs very important. In *War & Peace* it was a big decision. You couldnÕt say there was a single person at the heart of it. I guess Pierre slightly edges it over Natasha and Andrei, but Natasha and Andrei are also important, and the people close to them are almost as important, and so on. I was consciously thinking that the ideal was to have a scene with the three of them in, but I kept each of them very much in the foreground, and if theyÕre not in a scene it helps to have someone talking about them or thinking about them.
If I had to pinpoint the leading character, I think I would have saidPierre, and not just because Paul Dano is top billed. The way his story threads through all the rest makes him a kind of Candide
gure.
ThatÕs a good analogy. No one else has said that. But itÕs true. HeÕs a bit of a holy idiot, isnÕt he? He makes all these mistakesÉ
But he never stops being lovable.
ThatÕs right. Andrei can be a bit of a cold, sarcastic shit, but he loves Pierre, so you think, ÔWe wonÕt dismiss Andrei, because heÕs got a heart.Õ But heÕs so misogynistic, Andrei.
In the book, or in the adaptation?
Well, both, really. You canÕt escape it. HeÕs so contemptuous of women. He gets it from his father. I suppose, like Darcy, deep inside heÕs just longing for love.
I saw you interviewed at the Hay Festival not long after your TV adaptation of Sense and Sensibility, and in the Q&A an audience member pointed out that it opens with a sex scene, which isnÕt in the novel Ð and you said, ÔNo, but itÕs where I felt Jane Austen was heading.Õ ItÕs a characteristically irreverent answer, but it reects one of the jobs of an adapter, doesnÕt it, which is to dramatise subtext Ð even to write the scenes which the authors perhaps would have written if they could have?
ThereÕs more of a justiÞcation for starting Sense and Sensibility with a sex scene, because you learn late on in the novel that this happened, that Willoughby seduced a 15-year-old girl, had a great time with herin this cottage, then rode off and forgot all about her, which is a veryRegency thing to do. ThereÕs lots of dark and bad sexual behaviour in Jane Austen that is always kind of there but never makes it into the scene, and I thought, ÔIf it happened, letÕs get it on the screen.Õ I also wanted to meet that girl again when sheÕs had her baby Ð sheÕs still hoping that Willoughby is going to come back, and Colonel Brandon has to disillusion her Ð because I wanted the audience to think as badly of Willoughby as I do. I think he normally gets away with it.
So you donÕt necessarily love all the characters you write?
HeÕs a bit of an exception, Willoughby. Usually I do try to love all thecharacters, but I thought Jane Austen herself was far too gentle with Willoughby. I saw him as someone who carried a kind of seduction kit with him, with the appropriate poems and things like that: ÔThis one will do. This one will get this girl on her back.Õ
Bridget JonesÕs Diary is probably your most prominent feature
lm credit, and an appropriate one given the novelÕs debt to Pride and Prejudice. What insights were you able to bring to the formeradaptation from the latter?
When I was asked if IÕd like to write a draft of this Ð and there were many drafts Ð I think it was me who tilted it in the direction of romantic comedy. Helen Fielding had written a Þrst draft and it was very funny, but she was of the view that it was a movie about the urban family and how important these friendships were. I thoughtthat in terms of what the story was about Ð or should be about Ð her friends were an irrelevance and that it was really a comic modern version of Pride and Prejudice. And thatÕs the way it went. But I donÕtthink a single line of dialogue I wrote survived.
Another author youÕve returned to several times is Dickens, and your take on Bleak House was like a nineteenth-century soap opera: speedy, gritty, half-hour episodes, full of twists, turns, con icts and clihangers. Did you have in mind the original serial form of the novel, and did you in any way try to mirror its episode structure?
Yes, we did. When I say ÔweÕ, it was principally me and the producer, Nigel Stafford-Clark, but we were encouraged by Jane Tranter, who more or less challenged us to come up with a new way of telling theseclassic stories. We looked Þrst at doing it in 20 half-hour episodes, andthat shrank to 18 and then to 16 Ð not wholly for budget reasons, it just made it tighter and stronger. So yes, we were aware of the serial structure, but we didnÕt try to follow DickensÕ episodes, because Dickens doesnÕt get the story started until youÕre about 100 pages in,and I wanted to get the story started on page one. The director, Justin Chadwick, was also very inuential in getting across the feeling we wanted.
And Susanna White, presumably, who directed the last seven of the eventual 15 episodes.
Yes. The thing is, when youÕre doing the later episodes youÕre getting into the deeper bits of the story and things coming to an absolute crunch, and she did that beautifully. But really she had to match JustinÕs style, which was revolutionary for a period piece, with a lot of handheld cameras.
To what extent was that style already present on the page? Do you try to guide the camerawork via the stage directions?
Absolutely I do. Directors will say, ÔIÕm not going to work with this bugger,Õ if you try to write shots, but I do try to write what the audience will see, and I do try to write what the characters are feeling
Ð to indicate that I donÕt want some great wide shot. I try to inuence the director as much as I can through the script, because IÕm not going to be able to do it afterwards. So my scripts do clearly indicate howyou could shoot it, but I know that there are other ways Ð and I like tobe surprised in a good way by a director.
The 30-minute, twice-weekly format remains unusual. Do you feel that British TV drama is still too much in thrall to the one-hour Sunday night slot?
No, I donÕt. Well, I donÕt know Ð it suits me! It suits me as an audience member as well. I preferred watching Bleak House in one-hour chunks.
Because the two episodes were repeated back to back on the Sunday of each week?
ThatÕs right. Now, of course, and itÕs happened so quickly, youÕve got the bingeing thing Ð and I binge along with the rest. But I do like going out in weekly intervals because it gives it a chance to sink in, and people Ð you hope Ð are going to talk about it Ð and the papers Ð you hope Ð are going to pick up on it, not just review the Þrst episode and then forget about it. Such a lot of effort is put into makingsomething like The Crown, and itÕs gone in a weekend if youÕre not able to control yourself.
Your more recent adaptations have all been in a six-part, six-hour format Ð though the last episode of Les Misrables was feature length. Is there an overarching three-act structure to that? And do you use three-act structure within episodes?
Six hours just tends to be what people can afford and what peoplelike. Less than that is hard to sell, I think, and more starts being too expensive. But no, IÕve never found three-act structure very helpful. I sometimes wonder where it came from. And actually, most things that people say have a three-act structure, you could say, ÔNo, theyÕre not,theyÕre one act,Õ or, ÔTheyÕre Þve acts.Õ
You just let the stories nd their natural shape?
Yes. I mean, obviously IÕm very traditional about liking a goodcliffhanger ending to an episode, so you leave people thinking, ÔWow! Now what?Õ
*War & Peace* is possibly your most ambitious adaptation to date. A thousand-page novel, even over six hours, doesnÕt give you the luxury of being leisurely. Would you have liked it to be longer?
Yes. It could have been a good eight episodes, really. ItÕs amazing how things spread in the Þlming. Some lovely stuff got written and not Þlmed, and some lovely stuff got Þlmed and didnÕt make it in the edit.
What struck me most about it was not the big sweeping scenes but the small intimate moments, which have more impactbecause of the amount of time you get to spend with the characters. You get to know and care about them, and feel as if youÕve been on a real journey with them, which is harder to achieve in a two-hour feature lm.
ThatÕs absolutely true. I guess you do get that feeling with greatmovies, and great movie directors take the time to just be with characters Ð and often I think the most important thing is to be with them when theyÕre not saying or doing anything.
Do you think it will always be a struggle to condense a novel of more than 300 pages into a 120-page script? That the novel will inevitably require more radical reinvention to t into feature length?
It depends on the novel. Some modern novels, however long, have barely enough in them for half an hourÕs drama. But yes, itÕs tricky. *Brideshead Revisited* is about 300 pages, but thereÕs a lot in those 300 pages, and itÕs difÞcult to do as a movie Ð though I quite enjoyed having a crack at it. Evelyn Waugh, by the way, is one of those people who writes great economical dialogue.
And Brideshead is a project where you denitely have to take account of a previous adaptation.
My notion was to write a sort of counter-text: interpreting it in a waythat Evelyn Waugh would have disliked strongly. My take on it was that it was about some people who had been completely fucked up by their version of the Roman Catholic religion, so the mother was the villain of the piece and I wanted Charles to be more straightforwardlyatheist. I was very pleased with my screenplay, but it was a toughbusiness and there were a lot of drafts Ð more than I usually care to do!
Which is how many?
About three, really. And I aim to Þnish up with something as close to the Þrst draft as possible.
With more epic novels, do you employ any strategies for introducing large casts of characters? In *War & Peace*, for example, we meet a lot of characters in a single scene at a party right at the start.
That was perhaps a bit ambitious in terms of remembering it afterwards. Pierre, I guess, is the crucial character there, and people are going to realise that, but you get to know Andrei a little. But I also needed to get across St Petersburg society, which was very un-Russian and French-iÞed, so there was a lot to do at the same time Ð and you just hope that the audience is going to keep up with it.
And continue to keep up with it Ð which means that you as the dramatist canÕt a ord to lose track of any one story strand for too long.
Yes. Even though Tolstoy goes in for a lot of not very useful subplots and supporting characters, heÕs actually very helpful in that way. You can see he has it in the back of his mind: weÕre going to need to come back to Natasha, or we havenÕt heard from Maria for a bit, so letÕs think of a plot twist, like an army manoeuvre, to bring Nikolai into that part of the country, and so on.
Les Misrables is equally epic, but, initially at least, as fast and
eet as a thriller. Do you often have a particular screen genre in mind when adapting a piece of ction?
No. ItÕs just that when youÕre doing it, it sometimes turns out that way.
ThereÕs more than just character and plot in Dickens, Tolstoy and Hugo, thereÕs history, politics and ideas as well. How do you incorporate those things, which may be intrinsic to the authorÕs intentions, without detracting from the story youÕre telling?
Well, if they really are important, they have to be important to yourfavourite characters, so you have to Þnd a way of introducing them. TolstoyÕs ideas, for example, are expressed to a large extent by Pierre as heÕs fumbling his way towards them.
How do you avoid melodrama Ð in other words, plot drivingcharacter, rather than character driving plot Ð when compressing these huge incident- and coincidence-packed narratives into just a handful of episodes?
I think by reminding myself that character is the most importantthing, and by Þnding ways to make sure that what the characters want Ð or what theyÕre trying to avoid Ð has led them to a certain point.There are still loads of coincidences left in Les Misrables. We drastically reduced them, but werenÕt able to do without them completely.
You have more than 50 adaptations to your credit, not counting unproduced scripts, the vast majority of those in the last three decades. Are you still learning things about the art of screen adaptation?
I hope so. It would be nice to think I was. I feel like I might still begetting better. But I may just be going into a gradual decline [laughs].
*War & Peace* would suggest otherwise.
Thank you. IÕll keep that in mind!


Christopher Hampton
Christopher Hampton was born in 1946 in Faial, Azores. He has written nine original plays, including Total Eclipse (1968, lmed1995) and Tales from Hollywood (1983), and numerous stageadaptations and translations, most notably Les Liaisons Dangereuses (1985), based on the novel by Choderlos de Laclos and lmed as Dangerous Liaisons (1988) from his Oscar- and BAFTA-winning screenplay.
His other screen adaptation credits include: The History Man (1981, novel by Malcolm Bradbury); The Honorary Consul (1983, novel by Graham Greene); The Quiet American (2002,novel by Graham Greene); *Atonement* (2007, novel by Ian McEwan); A Dangerous Method (2011, from his play The Talking Cure and the book A Most Dangerous Method by John Kerr); The Singapore Grip (2020, novel by J.G. Farrell); and The Father (2020, play by Florian Zeller). He also directed his own screenplays of Carrington (1995, book Lytton Strachey byMichael Holroyd); The Secret Agent (1996, novel by Joseph Conrad); and Imagining Argentina (2003, novel by Lawrence Thornton).
The case studies in this chapter, *Atonement* and A Dangerous Method, open with edited extracts from the Faber book Hampton on Hampton. When the book was published, neither
lm had been made (*Atonement* was subsequently Oscar- and BAFTA-nominated for Best Adapted Screenplay); the extracts therefore provide the background to those projects, while new interviews explore their subsequent development.
APPROACHES TO ADAPTATION
Do you prefer to adapt material which chimes with your own work, or material which is completely di erent and gives you a chance to try out new things?
I always like to feel a connection with it. So IÕll accept the commissions that spark something, and sometimes Ð as with *Atonement* Ð IÕll actually go out and campaign to get the job. It would be hard for me to say exactly why *Atonement* struck me so forcibly; it doesnÕt happen very often. I just had a very strong instinct that I wanted to do it.
Do you think adaptations involve a completely di erent set of creative gears to original screenplays?
Clearly it calls on different skills. ItÕs more technical and the decision-making is in a slightly sideways compartment to what you do when you write original stuff. When youÕre doing original stuff, I Þnd youÕve got some big ideas but you donÕt quite know how theyÕre goingto work out, and itÕs only two-thirds of the way through writing them that you start to work out why youÕre doing it and what theyÕreabout. Whereas, of course, with an adaptation, itÕs all laid out for you
Ð though I do like adaptations that give you some leeway and freedom, Dangerous Liaisons being the prime example.
Do you always agree a mission statement or direction of travel with whoever has commissioned the adaptation?
They like to. I donÕt. IÕm about to do a screenplay based on Julian BarnesÕs novel The Noise of Time, about Shostakovich, and I went to St Petersburg last year and discussed with the producers how I was going to do it. Then I found that in the contract they require a treatment Ð which is very puzzling, because youÕre adapting a novel, so what do they want a treatment for? So I said, ÔDo I have to do this?Õ and they said, ÔOh, yes, we always have one to show the investors.Õ But my experience Ð and again, this goes back to *Atonement* Ð is that these things evolve as you do them and get better ideas. What I pitched to Ian McEwan to get that job bore absolutely no resemblance to the Þnished Þlm.
If youÕre o ered material to adapt which has been adapted previously, do you take account of any previous versions when writing yours?
No. I mostly take particular care not to look at anyone elseÕs. I did see the previous version of The Quiet American ages ago, a Þlm which riled Graham Greene tremendously because the American of the title was transformed into a hero Ð and played by a war hero Ð in order to say, ÔThis man is crusading against Communism,Õ not to say what the book said, which is, ÔThis man has misunderstood the situation and will shortly contribute to causing a massive catastrophe.Õ
If the author of the source material is living, do you nd it useful to have their input on the script?
I would have liked to have met Graham Greene on The Honorary Consul, but he refused to meet me Ð and I subsequently discovered that was because he had written his own script and been Þred. So fair enough. If IÕd known that, I wouldnÕt have done it. Apart from that, IÕve had very good relationships with adaptees, Ian McEwan being one of them and Malcolm Bradbury another. On The History Man, I got together with Malcolm to work out new scenes that werenÕt in the book but I felt were necessary, and he joined in with gusto. In fact, he joined in with such gusto that he spent the rest of his life adaptingnovels for television. And Ian was very interesting on *Atonement*, because he was quite involved in the early stages and then graduallywithdrew as it took shape. I used to think he was like the Cheshire Cat, Þnally just a benevolent smile Ð which is the ideal way for an author to be. So if theyÕre around, I really like to have their input, butI suppose it depends on the temperament of the adaptee.
Is it easier to navigate notes from directors, producers and scripteditors when you have a piece of source material to measure the screenplay against?
Yes. I think you can always hold up the original and say, ÔLook: this works.Õ Unless you can make a really good case for why we shouldnÕt stick with the original, letÕs stick with the original. I learned from reading the screenplays of Harold Pinter right at the beginning of my career that the better the source material, the closer youÕd better stay to it, because why change something which is really good or that presumably inspired people to buy the rights in the Þrst place?
Have you ever started work on an adaptation and found it harder to adapt than you anticipated?
I think thatÕs true of a lot of them. Mind you, in a way, the degree of difÞculty that something presents when you want to adapt it is an attraction for me. I like it if itÕs really difÞcult. I like the challenges.
Have you ever been o ered material to adapt which you felt couldnÕt, or shouldnÕt, be adapted?
Michael Mann asked me to write Manhunter, which was the original Hannibal Lecter Þlm. The book is called Red Dragon and I was quiteshocked by it, because it felt so much more extreme than anythingthat had previously appeared. You might have felt the same if youÕd been asked to adapt Psycho, and then you would have kicked yourself for the rest of your life if you hadnÕt. I feel slightly like that about Manhunter. But I did turn it down, speciÞcally because the serial killer in it is a man who works for a photo lab developing home movies, and whenever he sees a particularly happy family he scopes out the area and then murders them all, and I thought that anyone who felt inclined to be a serial killer might think, ÔThatÕs quite a good idea.Õ ItÕs absurd, but thatÕs why I said no Ð and itÕs actually one of the few projects IÕve turned down that I regret.
Are there any screen adaptations which you think are especially good or you particularly admire?
There are lots. I still think Harold Pinter was the beeÕs knees at doingit. The Go-Between is one of my favourite adaptations. He reallysurpassed himself with that Þlm. L.A. Con, by Curtis Hanson and Brian Helgeland, is a brilliant script, from a complicated book.
The Godfather is pretty amazing, isnÕt it? And sometimes thereÕs an adaptation that rewards studying. WhoÕs Afraid of Virginia Woolf?, which is essentially the play, is so well organised, but thatÕs almost more to do with Mike NicholsÕ direction. Likewise, Sidney LumetÕs version of Long DayÕs Journey into Night is a textbook adaptation of a play as far as IÕm concerned, because of how brilliantly he thoughtout every shot and turned it into cinema language without really doinganything to the text.

ADAPTING FICTION
Some writers try to include as much of the novel as possible by boiling scenes down to their essence. Others are more ruthless in editing it down to a sort of greatest hits, but being true to the spirit of it. Do you favour either of those approaches?
No, because I think every project requires a different approach. One of the jobs IÕve done since we did our book was an adaptation of East of Eden. Tom Hooper [director of The KingÕs Speech] asked me if IÕd do it, and I said, ÔAre you insane? ArenÕt you aware that thereÕs already an absolute masterpiece?Õ He said, ÔHave you read the book?Õ I said no. So he gave me the book, which is about 600 pages long, and the James Dean character doesnÕt really appear until page 400: the Þrst 400 pages are about the characters played in the Elia Kazan Þlmby Raymond Massey and Jo Van Fleet, and how she came to leave himand set up a brothel in San Francisco catering mostly to sadomasochists. I realised that the 1950s East of Eden is an absolutelysensational adaptation. ItÕs by a playwright called Paul Osborn, and he and Kazan obviously realised that they were already skating as close to the wind as they possibly could with it, and said, ÔLetÕs just dothis last bit of the book, which is containable.Õ And that was a brilliant decision, but it also meant that I could say yes and do somethingcompletely different. Needless to say, they didnÕt make the Þlm, but the script is one of the ones in recent years that IÕm most pleased with.
If a novel has an unusual structure, would you try to reproduce that in adapting it?
WeÕll talk more about this when we get on to *Atonement*, because that has an unusual structure. But I was anxious to preserve that rightfrom the word go. What I didnÕt anticipate was just how closely I was going to wind up following it.
Do you try to avoid voiceover in adapting a rst-person narrative, or do you see it as another tool in the toolbox?
Unless voiceover is actually built into the plot, as it is in Sunset Boulevard and Double Indemnity Ð Billy *Wild*er was very fond of it Ð and as it was in my original draft of *Atonement*, it always seems to melike an admission of failure. So I donÕt care for it. When The Quiet American was being edited, Phillip Noyce Ð who I got on with verywell and I think made a very good Þlm Ð asked me to do a voiceover, and I said, ÔI donÕt think we need one.Õ In the end they got Anthony Minghella to do it, and on the rare occasions when I see the Þlm it still seems completely unnecessary. Similarly, on A Bright Shining Lie [unproduced adaptation of Neil SheehanÕs nonbook about Lieutenant Colonel John Paul Vann and the war in Vietnam, later directed by Terry George from his own script], Oliver Stone demanded a voiceover when he took over, and I thought that was completely wrong because the main character wasnÕt a ruminative character, he was a man of action. So I refused Ð and later heard that Oliver said I was very uncooperative.
Do you use the language of the novel in writing the dialogue, or do you put the whole thing in your own words?
Some novelists know how to write good dialogue Ð and in that case, why change it? Quite a lot of the dialogue in *Atonement* comes from the novel. If you adapt Joseph Conrad, his plotting is great but his dialogue is hopeless. ThatÕs partly because he wasnÕt English, so all the characters talk alike and none of them sound convincing, and youÕve got to go in and Þnd an appropriate language for them. Graham GreeneÕs dialogue is good, though a touch over-explanatory. Everyauthor presents different problems and opportunities.
Do you keep the novel beside you throughout the adaptationprocess, or do you try to internalise it and set it aside somewhere along the way?
I keep it beside me. I donÕt look at it obsessively, but itÕs pointless not to refer to it when you need to.
How much pressure do you feel when adapting a well-known andmuch-loved novel, knowing that you wonÕt be able to please everyone with the nished product?
I donÕt mind that.

ADAPTING NONFICTION
When adapting a book, ction or non ction, do you rely on the authorÕs research, or do you conduct your own?
I like to do my own. For The Noise of Time, IÕve done quite a lot of research on Shostakovich. His life is very well documented, so itÕs interesting to know the context from which Julian Barnes has selectedparticular incidents, and when youÕre writing a dramatic scene there may be something useful in some of the letters. I enjoy all that anyway. I just love research. With A Bright Shining Lie, I spent about a year on it.
Do you approach a non ction adaptation in the same way as
ction, or does the strangeness of real events give you licence toexperiment with, say, structure?
ThatÕs an interesting question. I did a script two or three years ago, based on a book called Heart of a Soldier, about Rick Rescorla, the Englishman on 9/11 who decided to ignore the instructions comingfrom the Port Authority and got nearly 2,700 people out of the WorldTrade Center. He was head of security for Morgan Stanley, based in the South Tower, and only a handful of people from the Þrm were killed, and one of them was him: he kept going back in until the wholething came down. There was actually a TV documentary about him called The Man Who Predicted 9/11, because he was aware of the threat of Osama Bin Laden, and he kept trying to get people to payattention and they wouldnÕt. But the book is a biography of his life and he was a bit of an adventurer. He joined the Ugandan or Tanganyika police force but balked at colonialism. He made friends with an American soldier in Africa and went back with him to America just as the Vietnam War was beginning, so he volunteered and was in Vietnam for a couple of years. Then he went back to America and was involved in all sorts of security jobs. ThereÕs a lot of incident in it. I discovered that he was at the Battle of Khe Sanh in Vietnam, and again, disregarding orders, he organised a eet of helicopters to get people out who were surrounded and being picked off by the Viet Cong Ð and that seemed to set his character, really, as someone who was interested in saving people. So the Þlm is in three sections. The Þrst section is Vietnam, then you jump 30 years to whenhe Þrst goes to work at the Twin Towers, and the last section is the day of 9/11. Of course, itÕs a massively expensive project, so I donÕt know whether anyone will ever do it, but it certainly isnÕt the sort of script youÕd expect if you read the book.
Have you ever encountered events in a non ction book so strange that you couldnÕt use them in the adaptation because audiences wouldnÕt believe they were real?
No, I think you embrace all that. ThatÕs what makes it enjoyable: the things you couldnÕt make up.
How much responsibility do you feel towards historical accuracy when dramatising the lives of real characters?
Complete. IÕm very conscientious about it. IÕve often had trouble in the past about this, because people say, ÔWhoÕs going to know?Õ Well, IÕm going to know. ItÕs sort of a religious feeling I have, respect for the facts.
How do you set about nding the voices of real characters, especially if there is little or no written or audio-visual research material in their own words?
With A Bright Shining Lie, there were boxes of tapes of John Paul Vann talking to people. The thing that concerned me Ð which is a slight side issue, but germane to what youÕre talking about Ð was that it was the Þrst Þlm IÕd written with entirely American characters, and I wanted to get the language right. So I listened to him not only to hearwhat he was saying, but to focus on his turns of phrase and his linguistic foibles. When I handed it in, I was waiting for someone to say something about that, and eventually I said, ÔIs the language all right?Õ and they said, ÔWhat do you mean?Õ and I said, ÔWell, IÕm writing in American,Õ and they said, ÔOh, we hadnÕt noticedÕ Ð so clearly it worked. But IÕve always been a slightly ventriloquialdialogue writer. IÕm not interested in imposing my style on the dialogue. I like each character to have their own vocabulary if possible, because even if audiences donÕt notice it, I feel it makes the characters believable.
Do you think that being based on a true story in some way gives aproject more weight?
I donÕt, actually. But I do think the prestige of literature is such that you have a slightly easier ride with an adaptation of a well-loved novelthan with a nonÞction book.

CASE STUDY: *ATONEMENT*
UK/France/US, 2007 ¥ Directed by Joe Wright ¥ Produced byTim Bevan, Eric Fellner, Paul Webster ¥ Screenplay byChristopher Hampton, based on the novel by Ian McEwan ¥ Cast: James McAvoy (Robbie Turner), Keira Knightley (Cecilia Tallis), Romola Garai (Briony Tallis, aged 18), Saoirse Ronan (Briony Tallis, aged 13), Harriet Walter (Emily Tallis), Brenda Blethyn (Grace Turner), Vanessa Redgrave (older Briony)
My conversation with Christopher about *Atonement* formed part of a chapter in Hampton on Hampton which focused on his produced and unproduced screen adaptations written between the mid-1980s and the early 2000s, including a commission to adapt Deborah MoggachÕs novel Tulip Fever in 2001 (eventually lmed some 16 years later with a script credited to Moggach herself and Tom Stoppard). I started by asking whether he had
found Ian McEwanÕs novel more dicult to adapt than the recent Tulip Fever.
It was technically more difÞcult, partly because it doesnÕt fall into the usual three-act structure. ItÕs really a two-act structure, with the two acts pretty much equal in length Ð or, if anything, the Þrst act a little longer than the second Ð which means that you have to manage a tremendous gear shift, from country house life in 1935 to the retreat from Dunkirk. I thought the best way to do that was the way The Deer Hunter did it, where you just go Ð bam! Ð from one world to another. I think The Deer Hunter is a more than dubious Þlm, but it does have this brilliant effect in it: an extreme change of direction which galvanises the audience so that they trust you to bring the whole thing together again at the end. You also have to thread into those two acts the very short last section of the book, which reveals that itÕs been written by this character called Briony, whoÕs a 13-year.old when the story starts, and that she wrote it to atone for this crimewhich she inadvertently committed as a girl. ItÕs further revealed that sheÕs been as truthful as she can in 75 per cent of the story, but, unable to come to terms with all the facts, sheÕs invented a kind of happyending for the lovers, which she has to confess is not what actuallyhappened. Although this last section of the book is a real revelation, there are discussions and observations throughout about the nature of Þction which make it retrospectively logical, and you need to reproduce that so the ending doesnÕt just come out of nowhere. So thatÕs the other technical difÞculty.
How did you set about overcoming that?
You have to Þnd a cinematic way of dealing with it, and my way of dealing with it was to start with this old lady and the manuscript on her desk called Ô*Atonement*Õ, without explaining who the old lady is. You donÕt know whether itÕs Briony or Cecilia Ð in fact, youÕre slightly misled in that respect Ð until halfway through the Þlm, when youÞnally want to say, ÔThis is all seen from BrionyÕs point of view.Õ In addition, I thought that this *atonement* of writing the book was not quite dramatically big enough, so I added the notion that sheÕs led an entirely self-denying and celibate life to make up for what she did to her sister and her sisterÕs lover. Everyone seems pretty pleased with the Þrst draft, but itÕs early days yet. I think the thing to do is to write a second draft which is radically different in the way it deals with thatparticular problem, because the more different you can make the two, the more clearly people who are reading them can see which of the approaches works better.
* * *
You did ultimately write a radically di erent draft, after Richard Eyre left to make Notes on a Scandal and Joe Wright came on board in his place. But you wrote further drafts in between, working with Eyre as director and McEwan as executive producer. What form was the script in at the end of that period?
As I said, I originally had a voiceover, from older Briony, and every now and then, in the Þrst half of the story, youÕd see this old lady. For example, when Robbie is arrested and taken away, youÕd cut to the upstairs window and it would be old Briony standing there. Also, thebook is in four sections, getting shorter and shorter, and IÕd intercut section two, the sequence in France leading up to Dunkirk, with section three, when Briony becomes a nurse, so that they happened simultaneously. That seemed to make sense, and Richard and Ian were happy with that approach. When Joe came on to the Þlm, the Þrst thing he said was, ÔI like the script very much, but weÕre going to have to start from scratch.Õ He loved the structure of the novel, so he wanted to get back to that. And it became clear to me that, if youfollowed the structure of the novel, a huge problem was solved. WeÕd always imagined that the same actor would play Briony as a 13-year.old and Briony as an 18-year-old and that we would cast someone in her late teens. But by separating section one and section three bytwenty-Þve minutes, or however long section two lasts, you could cast Saoirse Ronan and then come back to her as Romola Garai. I was worried about whether the audience would buy that, but of course itÕs the simplest thing in the world: you just give them a similar haircut and a mole. You could do it for Vanessa Redgrave as well, playing older Briony. The virtue of it was that you could do what the novel does and surprise everyone at the end, which I hadnÕt thought was as important as it turned out to be.
Earlier you mentioned Harold PinterÕs adaptation of L.P. HartleyÕs novel The Go-Between, which I read was one of your inspirations in writing this script. Do you often have other adaptations in mindwhen approaching one of your own?
The three times IÕve directed, IÕve had a Þlm that I looked at in detail. When I was doing Carrington, I used to watch Jules et Jim everyweekend, because itÕs light and tragic at the same time and thatÕs a very unusual combination Ð which I wanted to try to reproduce. WhenI was doing The Secret Agent, I was looking at Touch of Evil. And when I was doing Imagining Argentina Ð though by that time I didnÕt think Imagining Argentina was really like anything Ð I was watchingCosta-GavrasÕs Þlm about Uruguay, State of Siege, and his Þlm about Chile, Missing. With *Atonement*, it was very helpful for me to think about HaroldÕs version of The Go-Between. First because IanÕs book, broadly speaking, is in the same family. ItÕs about someone whose life is blighted by something that happens beyond their control as a child. Also because, if you have in your mind as a template an unconventional Þlm that worked very well, youÕre not too frightenedof being unconventional yourself. I just thought, ÔIf I can do it half as well as Harold did, weÕll be quids in.Õ
The rst half of the lm Ð Part One of the novel Ð dramatises 187 pages in 47 minutes, and has to convey both the languor of a summer day and the tension of approaching calamity. How did you set about achieving that distillation and balance?
Presupposing that I did, again looking at The Go-Between was veryhelpful: the sense of heat and the claustrophobia of that house was very much in my mind. I also worked very closely with Joe. I wrote I donÕt know how many drafts, and then we went away for two weeks and just worked through it line by line. We had a very clear idea of thelayout of the interior of the house, and who saw what through which door and when. Ian uses an interesting device in the book, where he tells you something at the beginning of a chapter, then goes through all the stages leading up to the thing youÕve been told. We wanted to Þnd a cinematic equivalent of that, so we invented this device we called a ash sideways, where something would happen and then you would go back and explain it. For example, Briony goes to the window to free a wasp and sees whatÕs going on down by the fountain, then you ash sideways and show what led up to that moment. We do that perhaps two or three times during the Þlm, so itÕs not conventional in terms of its sequence of events.
Part One of the novel is told primarily from four points of view: Briony, Cecilia, their mother Emily, and Robbie. YouÕve retained all those except EmilyÕs, removing almost all of the chapter where she lies in bed with a migraine listening to the sounds and voicesof the house and its inhabitants. Was that sort of intensely interior experience beyond the scriptÕs scope?
There was more of Emily in the early drafts, but you reach a moment where you think, ÔPractically, the script ought not to be any longer than this,Õ and when you get to that point, you have to start throwing stuff out. You might do it in advance, or you might do it retrospectively, but you always have to subtract.
The opening of the novel is quite measured. The opening of the
lm is more propulsive, a quick tour of the house and introduction to some of its inhabitants, *drive*n by the clack of the typewriter on the soundtrack. What tone and atmosphere were you aiming to establish and evoke with that opening?
We did that over I donÕt know how many times. The typewriter was an idea that Joe cooked up with the composer, Dario Marianelli, and once he explained it to me, and I realised where it was going, in a waythat dictated the rhythm. He does direct rather musically. The sequence when Robbie is listening to Puccini in the bath and Cecilia isputting on this wonderful green dress was very much worked over because he had a musical notion about the whole thing. Also, aside from all that, IÕm a great believer in getting as fast as possible to the meat of the subject.
In that image you described of Briony opening the window to let out a wasp, she looks directly into the camera as if acknowledging the audience Ð in other words, ÔThis is ctionÕ Ð and foreshadowing her on-camera appearance as an old woman in the TV interview. Was that always the intention?
That was JoeÕs idea, and a very good one. He worked a lot on Þndingreconciling images for the three actors, but I donÕt think that was in the script. I canÕt remember which of us hit on the idea of a TV interview, but itÕs a very good way to transition into that last section. In the book, old Briony has a conversation with her editor where she explains that sheÕs dying and all the rest of it, so I originally had a version of that, but we werenÕt happy with it. IÕm also a great believerin sitting down with the director; I like that process of analysing thescript. At some point we realised that there was water symbolismthroughout, starting with the fountain, then that scene of Brionyjumping in the river and having to be rescued by Robbie, which was a tremendous issue in terms of where it was going to go: it was out and in and out and in. And in researching around the book, we discovered that when Balham tube station was bombed everyone drowned because the water main burst, which gave us the scene of the water coming down the stairs and Cecilia oating away. So the water imagery goes through all the way to the end, and was something else that we became conscious of as we were working.
ThereÕs a deleted scene on the DVD in which Briony, crying, rips the poster for the play sheÕs written, partly because the rehearsal with her cousins didnÕt go well, partly because sheÕs upset and confused by the sexually charged encounter between Robbie andCecilia which she just witnessed from the window. ThereÕs also a detail in the novel about the romantic lead in her play being a poor doctor, obviously a character sheÕs modelled lovingly on Robbie, who plans to go to medical school. ItÕs almost as if, deprived of one drama, her play, she creates another, around Robbie, to make sense of events and feelings she canÕt understand or control. Do you think that the removal of that scene and that detail, and the consequent reduction in prominence of BrionyÕs play in the rst half of the lm, make her motives for accusing Robbie more mysterious on screen than on the page?
Possibly, but you have to work back from a decision that Joe made. A different ending was shot, in which old Briony goes back to the house, now a hotel, and watches her play performed and speaks to the audience Ð and as sheÕs speaking, she sees in the back row, sittingtogether, Robbie and Cecilia. One of the interesting things about theÞlm for me was the willingness to re-examine what went on during theediting. In the last stages of the editing, Joe really transformed the Þlm
Ð and one of the things he did was to decide that the end wasnÕt working. So he threw it away, shot the scene on the beach that ends the Þlm now Ð a more romantic ending that I think was suggested to him by Anthony Minghella Ð and then removed the bits and piecesthat had to do with the play.
