IÕm interested that the scene where Briony jumps into the river sothat Robbie will rescue her was in and out and in and out, because in the absence of the scenes relating to her play, it becomes the clearest explanation of why she reads his letter to Cecilia and then falsely alleges that he attacked her cousin.
Exactly. But it was very awkward to situate. It obviously couldnÕt goin the Þrst section, because it happens something like two years beforethat.
In fact, itÕs placed in the lm more or less where itÕs placed in thebook, which is as a memory during the Dunkirk sequence.
Yes, I think thatÕs right.
The France sequence in the lm Ð Part Two of the novel Ð with the single, sweeping tracking shot of Dunkirk at its heart, is extraordinary, almost hallucinatory. What journey did that go on,from novel to script to screen, to arrive at its nal form?
ThatÕs a perfect illustration of necessity being the mother of invention.In the script, right up until pre-production, it was a longer sequence, with the soldiers making their way through France, encountering columns of refugees, being strafed by Messerschmitts and so on. Then a few weeks before we were due to start, Working Title decided that the budget should be cut. There were whispers of, ÔAre we making a 30 million dollar art movie?Õ and we were tasked with taking out Þve million. Clearly the most expensive bit was the French bit, so we focused on that and came up with this idea of incorporating as manyof the things they would have encountered on their journey into one scene. It was JoeÕs idea to do it in a single shot. If weÕd had that extra Þve million, I guess it would have been shot the way it was written. But we didnÕt have it and I think the Þlm is all the better for it.
The scene in the lm where Briony visits Robbie and Cecilia in Balham Ð a key moment in Part Three of the novel Ð contains a detail which you can convey much more clearly on screen than on the page: the fact that thereÕs no scar on RobbieÕs chest, no sign of the wound he sustained in France, a visual clue that he never recovered from it, or even returned from Dunkirk.
That was probably the most rewritten scene in the entire Þlm. Joe had an idea of what he wanted, but I couldnÕt grasp exactly what he meant. In fact, the scene is quite stylised, and is shot in a quite stylised way, and that was what he meant. He wanted to make it like it was a Þctional scene in the middle of real scenes. ThatÕs what he was tryingto do, and thatÕs what he succeeded in doing. ItÕs quite a strange scene, I think. But we certainly wanted to lay a couple of clues in that sequence.
How did the nished lm compare to the version you had in your head when you rst read the novel?
ItÕs probably one of the projects IÕve done that I was most pleasedwith when I saw it. As weÕve said, you have a vague idea in your mind when you start which hopefully sharpens as you proceed, but everyscreenwriter is familiar with the dreaded moment where you can see it all going off the rails and heading for a different station altogether. That didnÕt ever happen. From the Þrst meeting with Ian to the Þnished Þlm, it was a process of travelling closer and closer to the novel, and I think thatÕs because the novel is a masterpiece. So I was very happy about that.

CASE STUDY: A DANGEROUS METHOD
Germany/Canada/UK/US, 2011 ¥ Directed by David Cronenberg
¥ Produced by Jeremy Thomas ¥ Screenplay by ChristopherHampton, based on his stage play The Talking Cure and the book A Most Dangerous Method by John Kerr ¥ Cast: Keira Knightley (Sabina Spielrein), Viggo Mortensen (Sigmund Freud),Michael Fassbender (Carl Jung), Sarah Gadon (Emma Jung), Vincent Cassel (Otto Gross)
A Dangerous Method had a convoluted journey to the screen. Christopher initially adapted John KerrÕs non ction book, A Most Dangerous Method, into an unproduced screenplay, Sabina, then turned that into a play, The Talking Cure. The play premiered at the National Theatre, London, in 2002, and was remounted at the Mark Taper Forum, Los Angeles, in 2004, before being turned back into the lm. This section of Hampton on Hampton was recorded between the rst and second productions of the play.
The story of Sabina Spielrein and Jung had been in the back of mymind since the late 1970s, when a book called A Secret Symmetry byAldo Carotenuto gave the Þrst inkling of this special relationshipbetween them; but although I thought how interesting their story was and how interesting it would be to write something about psychoanalysis, perhaps the deÞning intellectual notion of the Þrst halfof the twentieth century, I did nothing about it. Then, in the mid-nineties, it surfaced as something that Julia RobertsÕ company was interested in and I went for it.
Was Julia Roberts interested in playing the part of Sabina?
Yes, she was. I was dealing mostly with her development person, a man called Pliny Porter, so things were largely Þltered through him, but I had a lot of conversations with them about it, and I think Julia liked the screenplay. It was one of those projects where people said, ÔWeÕre looking forward to the next draft,Õ without saying what theythought the next draft should contain, so I just let it ride and didnÕt do another draft. Eventually, they produced a load of notes Ð and Fox also produced a load of notes Ð and, boiled down, these notes seemed to be saying that there was too much Jung in it and not enoughSabina. However, what was gradually dawning on me was the exact opposite: that the central character was Jung, because it was Jung who was torn between his wife and Sabina, and between Freud and Sabina, and that he had to be even more at the centre of it. I let various friends of mine read the screenplay and they all encouraged me to follow my instinct, which was to reorganise the material as a play, so thatÕs what I did. [Julia] wrote me a very nice letter saying, ÔGood luck with it.Õ In fact, both she and Fox were exceptionally generous about it.
Do you think the screenplay did things which the play couldnÕt, and vice versa?
The only thing I really miss from the screenplay is the sense of landscape, because thereÕs something about the contrasting landscapesof Zrich and Vienna that tells you a lot about the characters of Jung and Freud, but I donÕt think there was anything essential in the screenplay which didnÕt make it into the play. I think the play is more effective than the screenplay Ð crucially the shift in focus from Sabina to Jung Ð but IÕm actually reworking the play for America, because neither it nor the screenplay gave the FreudÐJung relationship its full weight. I want to get more of a sense of how excited they both were by their initial contact, in order to make the Þnal split between them seem like a more momentous intellectual fracture. At the moment, their Þnal argument is about the expunging of the names of Egyptian kings from their monuments by their sons, so the whole thing happens at one remove, and I think it would be better to have a scene where the gloves are off and they go for each other. IÕm always being asked,in relation to screenplays, ÔWhy donÕt the characters just say whatÕs on their minds?Õ and I usually feel that itÕs more interesting if youÕre able to see whatÕs on their minds without them saying it, but in this case I probably erred in the direction of indirection. I think thatÕs accurate, because I donÕt think Jung and Freud ever did say what was on their minds Ð and I was trying to respect that, because IÕve always been wellserved by being faithful to the facts Ð but perhaps, in this instance, I ought to respect the principles of drama more than the principles of history.
The reason I asked whether the screenplay did things which the play couldnÕt is that the former starts with Sabina being brought to the Burghlzli Clinic in a carriage and kicking and screaming until she gets inside the building, where she sees Jungdescending the main staircase like some kind of god and immediately falls silent Ð a scene which, in the play, Jung simply describes, to less e ect, in my opinion.
Funnily enough, the Þrst change IÕve made to the play is to set the Þrst scene, which is Jung casually discussing the case with his wife at breakfast, against Sabina arriving and being gagged and tied into a chair in the treatment room. In the production at the National, that energy went into creating a web of sound which evoked Victorian lunatic asylums, with clanking doors and people screaming, but I donÕt think the Burghlzli was like that, so I want to start the play in a completely different way. Another problem with the play, which I donÕt know how to solve, is that the Þrst 30 pages, with their intense concentration on this one case, are so powerful that the rest of it struggles to keep up. You have the scene where the dam is breached and Sabina Þnally admits everything about her father, and then youhave the Þrst scene with Freud, and I donÕt know how to get around the sense of anti-climax and regroup.
One benet of turning scenes which were dramatised in the screenplay into scenes which Jung describes in the play is that the character he describes them to, his wife Emma, becomes better developed and therefore a more e ective rival to Sabina.
I think thatÕs right, and I think sheÕs the character who could stand more development on this pass and in the Þlm.
YouÕve solved structural problems in other plays by usingmathematical devices Ð Liaisons, for example Ð and in this case you set up several parallels: the parallel loveÐhate relationships between Jung and Freud, and Jung and Sabina, and the paralleltriangular relationships between Jung and Freud and Sabina, andJung and Emma and Sabina. Was that deliberate?
Yes, whilst leaving the door open for a bit of inspiration Ð like showing the death of Sabina. That was an impulse which came on me in the middle of writing the play, at what was going to be the end of the Þrst act, and you have to decide whether to give way to those impulses or corral them. The challenge is to come up with a structure that feels aesthetically right to the spectator while respecting the anarchy of real life and the spontaneity of the imagination.
Was the ash-forward from one of Jung and SabinaÕs trysts to her murder by German soldiers in Russia in 1942 meant to echo JungÕs premonition, at the end of the play, of rivers of blood sweeping over Europe?
Yes Ð although, in the rewrite, the whole scene unfolds in the bedroom and a double plays her in the future, so the two scenes happensimultaneously in the same way as the two scenes at the beginning.Some people really hated that scene, but itÕs always the original things which people tend to hate, and it is original to say to the audience at the halfway point, ÔThis is what happens to her.Õ It means that youhave that knowledge in your head throughout the second half, especially since the second half deals with issues of anti-Semitism. David Cronenberg was very interested in making a movie of it, and when I told him that the original screenplay didnÕt have that scene, hesaid, ÔItÕs absolutely vital, and in a movie IÕd put it exactly where youÕve put it,Õ which was interesting. It just seemed like the right thing to do.
How did you feel about the rst production as a whole?
ThereÕs no doubt that it didnÕt land with the impact it should have. But then neither did Total Eclipse or Tales from Hollywood in their Þrst productions. If you deal with a complex subject, the task is to do it in such a way that an audience, which is only going to watch it once for two hours, will grasp some of that complexity. ThatÕs a big thing to hope for, because people donÕt apprehend all kinds of things, so to expect a piece to land perfectly the Þrst time out is asking too much, really. It often takes a play a while to Þnd its way. Of course, if it doesnÕt make much impact in the Þrst place itÕs never going to get a chance to Þnd its way, which is something else you have to grapplewith, but it can take a period of consideration and a production or two to teach you what it was you were trying to say. So I suppose IÕm Þnally doing what I was originally asked to do, which is write another draft [É] and having remounted it, I want to go on and make the movie of it, incorporating all the lessons IÕve learned. ThatÕs the plan, anyway.
* * * So, did things go to plan?
Well, hereÕs how it went. In 2004, when I was in Los Angeles Þghting 11 producers over Imagining Argentina, I had a call from David Cronenberg, who I knew very slightly. He hadnÕt seen the play but heÕd read it and said he wanted to do a Þlm of it Ð what did I think? I said I was very pleased, because IÕd always liked his work very much, but I was hoping to direct it myself. A couple of years passed, and Imagining Argentina was such a bruising experience that I thought it would be better to let Cronenberg do it. So I contacted him and he was still interested, so armed with the original script and the play he met me and said, ÔI think the play is much better than the script and I would want to do something closer to the play.Õ So I started on that basis. I said, ÔDo you have any advice before I embark on it?Õ and he said, ÔI like a script to be 87 pages.Õ That was the extent of his advice. Anyway, I did the script Ð which was 103 pages Ð then I had another meeting with him, and of all the meetings IÕve ever had with directors it was the most admirably clear and direct. For example, I said beforeI started, ÔDo we want to keep the scene from the play where Sabina is killed?Õ and he said, ÔYes, itÕs crucial, and itÕs very well placed in the middle.Õ When he read the screenplay, he said there was somethingabout the speciÞcness of it Ð perhaps that it was now so centrallyabout Jung Ð which meant that the scene didnÕt work, and he asked me to take it out. So I went back and did the work on it, sent it to him again, and he asked me to do one or two more things. He was interested in meeting SabinaÕs parents Ð the father who had beaten her
Ð so I put a couple of scenes in and he said, ÔIÕm sorry I asked you to do that. Can you take them out again?Õ Then I did a bit more work on it, and then it was done. Then a few weeks before shooting, he sent me an email saying, ÔI donÕt want you to panicÕ Ð so I immediatelypanicked Ð Ôbut I prefer to do the editing before I make the Þlm ratherthan after, and IÕve taken a few things out.Õ I read the script and it was 87 pages. It involved a bit of rewriting where things had gone and edges had to be smoothed, but I couldnÕt fault it Ð and it was an ideal relationship with a director.
Anyone buying John KerrÕs book on the strength of seeing the
lm is likely to be slightly surprised: the three-handed psychological drama which you carved out of it is just one strand in a dense examination of the birth of psychoanalysis. How did you arrive at the story which formed the basis of the originalversion of the screenplay?
Well, I did a lot of side research. I mean, the book isnÕt designed in that way; it just contains this very interesting story which covers a relatively small section of the narrative. Also, John KerrÕs conclusion was that they hadnÕt had an affair. But one of the interesting things about working on this subject is that lots of stuff about Sabina has emerged in the 20 years since I was originally writing it: her letters and diaries have been published, sheÕs become a recognised and important Þgure, and itÕs now quite clear that they did have an affair. ItÕs also quite clear that her development as a psychoanalyst diverged from Jung in the direction of Freud. IÕd sort of intuited that, but one of the things at the centre of the Þlm version is that Jung is upset not so much by the fact that she leaves him as that she allies herself with Freud, and itÕs that which contributes to the strange nervous breakdown he went into at the end of this period. A lot of JungÕs thinking was set off by his conversations with her, and another thing that the piece is about is how these men, these big beasts, colonised her ideas as well as her body, and drew from her in ways that Freudactually acknowledged more than Jung did.
When we talked before, you said that the facts behind The Talking Cure were extremely inconveniently organised. In what way were they inconvenient?
The sequence of events gets really messy after Sabina and Jung split up. I cheated a bit with their Þnal meeting; we donÕt really know whether she ever saw him again. She did go to Zrich, and she probably went to his house, so she would have seen his wife. But I needed a Þnal scene where they wrapped everything up. So in that sense it was stretching the known facts.
The opening scene we discussed, where Jung appears at the top of the stairs when Sabina is admitted to the clinic, isnÕt in the
lm; it simply cuts from her being carried inside, to him entering the treatment room and introducing himself to her.
The Þrst third of the play was where the cuts mostly fell in the Þlm, and if I was dubious about one thing in the Þlm it would be that her treatment is so abbreviated that itÕs almost like a miracle cure. She comes in, sheÕs in a terrible state, and then sheÕs better. In the play shegets better more slowly, and I think that development is useful.
I suppose the advantage of abbreviating her treatment is that youno longer have the problem of how to regroup when it ends.
Yes. Also, one of the things Cronenberg said to me was, ÔWeÕre going to need to see more of their sexual relationship,Õ and I could see that was something which was missing in the play. So we worked on that, and I was able to put more of it into the next version of the play, which I directed in Vienna in 2014.
In the book, you described Sabina as Ôthe crack down the middle of the JungÐFreud relationship. She brought them together and she drove them apartÕ and I suggested that she was therefore the pivotal character in the play without being the central character. Watching the lm again, I wondered whether Sabina and Jung were in fact joint central characters, with Freud as a supporting character.
I think youÕre probably right. And maybe itÕs not as big an adjustment as I think it is in my head. But moving the needle from Sabina to Jungunlocked the whole subject for me. It had the same positive effect as when I decided to make Carrington about Carrington. ItÕs amazinghow things fall into place when you choose the right central character.
The nal conversation between Jung and Freud remains almost the same in the nished lm as it was in the rst production of the play, with their mutual animosity channelled through their conversation about the names of the Egyptian kings. Did you decide that it was better to be indirect after all?
We obviously discussed the scene, and Cronenberg liked the fact that all the personal hostility is under the surface. But in the scene that now exists in the stage version, I more or less dramatise bits of their Þnal furious correspondence where they insulted each other quiteopenly, and I actually think it works very well. It certainly did in theVienna production.
And how do you feel about the piece now, in its nal form as both a lm and a play?
In terms of what Cronenberg wanted to do, I think that the Þlm is perfectly achieved. He was totally respectful of the script, I was extremely happy with the Þlm and I would love to work with him again. I learned from him that the physical detail of the relationshipbetween Jung and Sabina was very useful dramatically, so that went into the play. ThereÕs also a scene in the Þlm where Freud comes to Zrich, instead of Jung worshipping at the shrine in Vienna, and theyÕre in JungÕs boat and Freud is terribly uncomfortable, this rather inconvenient old man. I liked that very much, so a version of it went into the play and again is very useful, because it was an interestingstage in their development. IÕve been promising Faber for 15 years to deliver Plays 2, of which this would be the last, so IÕve got to sit down and put it in shape. But I hope someone else does the play at some point, because I think IÕve Þnally cracked it Ð and without Cronenberg, it wouldnÕt have gone through those Þnal stages.


David Hare
David Hare was born in 1947 in St Leonards-on-Sea, East Sussex.
Among his many plays are: Plenty (1978, lmed 1985); The Secret Rapture (1988, lmed 1993); Skylight (1995); My Zinc Bed (2000, lmed 2008); Stuff Happens (2004); The Power of Yes (2009); IÕm Not Running (2018); and his trilogy RacingDemon (1990), Murmuring Judges (1991) and The Absence of War (1993, lmed 1995).
His original screenplays include: Licking Hitler (1978); Saigon:Year of the Cat (1983); Wetherby (1985); Paris by Night (1988);Strapless (1989); Collateral (2018); The White Crow (2018);and the Worricker trilogy, Page Eight (2011), Turks & Caicos (2014) and Salting the Battleeld (2014), some of which he also directed.
His screen adaptation credits include: Damage (1992, novel byJosephine Hart); *Denial* (2016, book History on Trial by Deborah
E. Lipstadt); and two Oscar-winning collaborations with director Stephen Daldry, The Hours (2002, novel by Michael Cunningham) and The Reader (2008, novel by Bernhard Schlink).
APPROACHES TO ADAPTATION
Do you prefer to adapt material which chimes with your own work, or material which is completely di erent and gives you a chance to try out new things?
I think probably what most appeals to me is the opportunity to do something different. Like a lot of screenwriters, I get sent a largenumber of heavyweight literary novels and I usually know within 20 or 30 pages whether theyÕre of any interest to me. First of all, the given is respect for the book itself. I have to think that the book is a great book. And the second thing is that thereÕs room for me to do something, that it isnÕt just a matter of transcribing whatever structure or ideas or, indeed, dialogue that the author has offered. ThereÕs got to be room for both of us in the bed.
Do you think adaptations involve a completely di erent set of creative gears to original screenplays?
Yes. I feel less implicated in the result, I donÕt feel itÕs me, and because of that, because IÕm, as it were, messing around with other peopleÕs material, IÕm much freer. I know at once when I read a book whether IÕll be able to do it or whether I wonÕt. ItÕs absolutely pointless saying to me, ÔCan you Þnd a way of doing this?Õ When I read it the way of doing it screams at me at once, and if it doesnÕt scream at me I wonÕt do it. So I turn down a lot of work, just saying, ÔI donÕt know how todo it, and I donÕt want to sit for six months in panic that I donÕt knowhow to do it.Õ With all the ones IÕve done that have pleased me most, itÕs been screamingly obvious to me how to do them on the Þrst day.
Do you always agree a mission statement or direction of travel with whoever has commissioned the adaptation?
It depends whether you have a director on board or not. If you take Damage, that was Louis MalleÕs Þlm, and with a great director like Louis Malle youÕre simply trying to guide him towards the Þlm he wants to make, to enable him to do what he has in mind. *Denial*, on the other hand, was developed with no particular director in mind, so I was completely free. There was no interference from the producers atall. The producers had had *Denial* for ten years and had been unable to Þnd a way to do it. They were in Hollywood and they went to the BBC and said, ÔBritish writers are much more skilled at this kind of political materialÕ Ð and Christine Langan, who was head of BBC Films, sort of bullied me into doing it, on the grounds that if I didnÕt do it, no one would do it. It wouldnÕt get done. So because IÕm fond ofChristine, and owed her a favour for all the favours sheÕd done me, I did *Denial*, but I was given complete freedom to do exactly what I wanted with it Ð until Mick Jackson arrived, and once a director joins then obviously youÕre going to work with the director. YouÕre crazy not to.
Louis Malle sort of bullied you into doing Damage, too, didnÕt he?
Yes. I didnÕt particularly want to do Damage: a) because I was verytired; and b) because it did seem a more than normally difÞcult project. So I said no, I wouldnÕt do Damage, and Louis said, ÔWhat are you doing next?Õ I said, ÔIÕm going on holiday in the south of France,Õ and Louis said, ÕWho are you going with?Õ I said, ÔIÕm going by myself,Õ and Louis said, ÔWhere are you staying?Õ And I named a hotel in Saint-Tropez Ð whereupon, of course, Louis moved into the hotel and ruined my holiday. He didnÕt, actually, but we worked for two weeks Ð and what pleasanter place to work than Saint-Tropez? And at the end of two weeks we had bashed out a structure for the whole Þlm.
Why were you initially so resistant to it?
I was in a very low mood about screenwriting. I made three Þlms in the 1980s: Wetherby, which was my Þrst feature Þlm and won the Golden Bear at Berlin; Paris by Night, which became ensnared in all sorts of legal difÞculties not of my making; and Strapless, which I liked but the world liked less. I felt that artistically I was heading in the wrong direction, that each of my Þlms had been inferior to the previous one. And I decided that it was impossible to excel in the theatre and in cinema, that all the British Þlm directors I most admired
Ð people like Stephen Frears and Mike Leigh and Ken Loach Ð were totally dedicated to Þlm. It wasnÕt until Stephen Daldry and Sam Mendes appeared that you got theatre directors who could move easily between the two forms, and they remain almost the only ones who can. So I took some hard knocks with Paris by Night and Strapless, I was in despair about my screen work, and I thought, ÔI donÕt need all the hurt that cinema is causing me.Õ And it was Louis who, as it were, nursed me back to self-conÞdence.
Do you usually produce an outline or treatment before you start writing the script?
I donÕt want to have to. Going back to Louis Malle, every day he would say, ÔTell me the story of the Þlm,Õ and over that two week period we reÞned the story so that I could give an account of the Þlm in 45 minutes. And at the end of that he said, ÔNow go away and write it,Õ meaning that the structure was completely worked out. I generally donÕt do that. I have to know the ending is going to be great, so I have to know where IÕm going. But everything about screenwriting, to me, is about avoiding what I call binary choices. In other words, I never want to write the kind of Þlm in which, two-thirds of the way through, the hero or the heroine can go one way or another Ð where you can see a split in the road which will annoy or please half the audience whichever route you take. Any Þlm that relies on, ÔAre they going to get married?Õ, ÔIs he going to be a success?Õ, ÔIs she going to die?Õ, ÔWill this football team win the cup?Õ IÕm bored stiff by. What IÕm trying to do always is write stories where you canÕt see where theyÕre going. To me, the second half of a Þlm is inÞnitely more important than the Þrst half. The Þrst half is there to prepare forthe second half, so the structuring of a Þlm is about believing that the story is going to be fulÞlled in a deeply satisfying way. And, by and large, the Þlms you remember are the ones that have a great last 30 minutes. All Þlms have a great Þrst 30 minutes. But to go into something without knowing how youÕre going to end it is a disaster: you have to know that the end is going to deliver.
But you donÕt necessarily feel you have to write that in prose formbefore starting the script?
It depends who IÕm working with. I think it was Evelyn Waugh who said, make sure you go to war with people you like, because youmight as well enjoy it. My version of that is: donÕt make Þlms with people you donÕt trust, because if you donÕt trust them at the beginning, by God you certainly wonÕt trust them at the end. So a condition of my work is to work with people I like, because it justisnÕt worth your time to work with people you donÕt trust. And if you trust them, the chances are they trust you, and if they trust you and you say, ÔJust give me time to work this out,Õ theyÕll structure a contract in a way that allows you to do that. If theyÕre saying to you,ÔCan you tell me in advance what youÕre going to write?Õ theyÕreprobably the wrong people to be working with.
If the author of the source material is living, do you nd it useful to have their input on the script?
Oh, yes. YouÕve got to have that.
Is it easier to navigate notes from directors, producers and scripteditors when you have a piece of source material to measure the screenplay against?
No, itÕs much harder, because of those dreaded words Ôin the bookÕ. The minute you see the book on set you bridle, because you go, ÔWeÕre not making the book. WeÕre making the script.Õ You ought to be able to get everything from the script. When I showed Julianne Moore thescreenplay of The Hours, I said, ÔDo you need to know anythingabout this part?Õ and she said, ÔNo, everything is on the page.Õ And when she said that I knew sheÕd be fantastic in the role. So itÕs incredibly irritating when the book is used as a stick with which to beat the script. On the other hand, returning to the book in order to compare how the author imagined something with how you imaginedit constantly deepens and enriches your screenplay. You go, ÔIÕd forgotten that they wrote that bit,Õ or, ÔThereÕs a wonderful line I hadnÕt noticed.Õ And the book, if itÕs a good book, will change every time you go back to it anyway.
Have you ever started work on an adaptation and found it harder to adapt than you anticipated?
I think I tend to have spotted the problems in advance [laughs]. For example, with *Denial*, we had to make drama out of self-*denial*. The essence of Deborah LipstadtÕs experience of defending herself was that in court she was not allowed to defend herself. Most legal dramas, like Erin Brockovich, depend on a previously inarticulate person becoming articulate. Our drama depended on a highly articulate woman beingsilenced Ð and, indeed, relying on a team of men to do the job for her. So I knew that my job was to prove that her input in her own defence was decisive, even though it was not in court. That was the challenge from the very beginning.
Have you ever been o ered material to adapt which you felt couldnÕt, or shouldnÕt, be adapted?
Oh, yes. The paradox is that those writers who are said to be most cinematic are actually the hardest to adapt for the cinema. Patricia Highsmith is an obvious example. Because her prose is so brilliant, she lures you into believing the plot is the engine, and itÕs not, mood is the engine. Graham Greene is another. ThereÕs a reason why he wrote original screenplays, because he knew his books looked cinematic on the page, but adapting them was a different matter. And JosephConrad is another.
Le Carr? HeÕs in that same lineage of Conrad and Greene.
I think le Carr requires time. IÕve always thought that whatÕs brilliant in le Carr is those sustained virtuoso passages, the interrogations,which is why he works so well on television. Give him six hours and leCarr is great. IÕve been asked to do le Carr, and if I was given six hours then yes, but people were coming to me wanting feature Þlms, so I turned them down.
Are there any screen adaptations which you think are especially good or you particularly admire?
William Wyler had the unfailing gift of going to the centre of a subject. *Wuthering Heights*, The Heiress Ð to me, these are the absolute models. Ben Hecht and Charles MacArthur adapted one, Ruth and Augustus Goetz the other, but as a director Wyler just seems to know what the heart of a story is, what the essential *drive* of a story is. I look at his Þlms and go, ÔYou clever bugger! How do you know?Õ Of contemporary screenwriters, I think Steven Zaillian is completely brilliant Ð heÕs absolutely the king of it all, isnÕt he?

ADAPTING FICTION
Some writers try to include as much of the novel as possible by boiling scenes down to their essence. Others are more ruthless in editing it down to a sort of greatest hits, but being true to the spirit of it. Do you favour either of those approaches?
IÕm very marked by the experience of The Corrections, the Jonathan Franzen novel. I spent three years on that and we did 21 drafts, and at a certain point the director, Robert Zemeckis, said, ÔNo Þlm can contain this whole book, so I want to make a story about just one of the children rather than all three children, because a movie has to have a point of view.Õ Obviously, having made The Hours, I didnÕt agreewith him. On the other hand, I could see that the bagginess of a portmanteau Þlm, which had Þve principal characters and Þve pointsof view, meant it was almost impossible to make. Scott Rudin, the producer, and Jonathan Franzen concluded that the answer to this was to do a television series, which I had nothing to do with and never got past the pilot. But it was true that there wasnÕt any way the feature Þlm could be reduced. In the end, Scott just said to me, ÔWrite exactlywhat you want and give me your preferred version.Õ I wrote a version which probably lasted three hours, and he said, ÔThe days of The Godfather are gone. Cinema managers will no longer tolerate that, least of all for a literary Þlm. Maybe a blockbuster can last three hours, but a literary adaptation canÕt.Õ So that book defeated me, and that was obviously a very painful experience.
If a novel has an unusual structure, would you try to reproduce that in adapting it?
No. But I have a reputation, rightly or wrongly, as the person who does impossible projects, and I get slightly freaked out because I canÕt always understand whatÕs impossible about them. Whereas there are other things where IÕm told itÕs incredibly obvious how you do them, and I just go, ÔI donÕt see this.Õ So you have to know what it is that excites you about writing this particular thing.
Do you try to avoid voiceover in adapting a rst-person narrative, or do you see it as another tool in the toolbox?
I hate voiceover. I think voiceover is a cheat. Plainly there are Þlms Ð like Sunset Boulevard Ð with a great Þrst-person narration, but it shouldnÕt be there to compensate for the fact that the writer canÕt do the job. Because the job of screenwriting is a painless, unnoticed conveying of information. When I was very young and nave I wrote a Þlm about the fall of Saigon, and I had a man standing in front of a blackboard, pointing at a map of Vietnam to show where the Viet Cong were. I said something to the actor about a line not being right, and he said, ÔIt doesnÕt matter what I say in this scene. If IÕm pointing at a map no one is going to be listening to a bloody word.Õ And he was absolutely right. That is not how to convey information in a movie. You must convey information in such a way that the audience doesnÕt know theyÕre receiving it. As soon as they notice informationbeing thrown at them, theyÕll revolt. So IÕm very against voiceover, on those grounds.
Do you use the language of the novel in writing the screenplay, ordo you put the whole thing in your own words?
My own words, but there will be favourite bits of the novel. ThereÕs a wonderful line in The Hours: ÔIt had seemed like the beginning of happiness. Now she knew: it was happiness. That was the moment, right then.Õ I remember saying to Scott Rudin, ÔI cannot imagine a Þlm which does not Þnally include those words,Õ and Scott said, ÔWeÕll seeÕ
Ð because heÕs wiser than me.
It is in the lm, actually, a line of dialogue.
Is it in? I never know whatÕs in and what isnÕt in the end. Okay.
Do you keep the novel beside you throughout the adaptationprocess, or do you try to internalise it and set it aside somewherealong the way?
Jean-Claude Carrire said you adapt a novel by reading it once and throwing it in the waste paper basket. My formula is that the only way to be faithful to a novel is by being lavishly promiscuous. By being promiscuous you will perversely be faithful, whereas if yousimply write down scenes from the novel it never works. TheyÕredifferent media, and they have to be written differently. So I look at the novel less and less while IÕm actually writing, but then I go back toit when IÕve Þnished to check what IÕve missed.
Between drafts, rather than during a draft?
Totally. I go back between drafts and say, ÔIn what way is this novel superior to what IÕve written?Õ and then I try to inject into the screenplay something of what makes the novel great. But not until IÕvegot my own river running, so to speak.
How much pressure do you feel when adapting a well-known andmuch-loved novel, knowing that you wonÕt be able to please everyone with the nished product?
Well, The Hours was a much-loved novel Ð so letÕs talk about that.

CASE STUDY: THE HOURS
US/UK, 2002 ¥ Directed by Stephen Daldry ¥ Produced by Scott Rudin, Robert Fox ¥ Screenplay by David Hare, based on the novel by Michael Cunningham ¥ Cast: Meryl Streep (Clarissa Vaughan), Julianne Moore (Laura Brown), Nicole Kidman (Virginia Woolf), Ed Harris (Richard Brown), Toni Collette (Kitty),Claire Danes (Julia Vaughan), Jeff Daniels (Louis Waters), Stephen Dillane (Leonard Woolf), Allison Janney (Sally Lester),John C. Reilly (Dan Brown), Miranda Richardson (Vanessa Bell)
In the introduction to the published screenplay, you quote producer Scott Rudin as saying that The Hours was perfect for you, and you also say that his reasons for wanting to make it may have been di erent from yours. In what way do you think they were di erent?
I think he very shrewdly saw that these were belting leading roles for the greatest actresses of the day, so he always knew that he would be able to cast the Þlm up to the nines Ð which turned out to be true, because they were three fully realised portraits of three extraordinary women in three different periods, and how often do we get that in the cinema? Almost never. So I think that was what appealed to him. What appealed to me was that it was all interior monologue. The whole art of adaptation is to invent scenes which are the equivalent of what is in the book, but are not the book. All sorts of things happen in a Þlm which donÕt happen in the book, and they happen in order to do what the book does by different means, and that particularlyapplies to interior monologue. It was up to me to invent scenes, because Michael wasnÕt trying to write scenes, he was trying to write states of mind. So, a desperate housewife and her extremely troubledrelationship with her child: he gave indications of that, but I actuallyhad to dramatise it. ThatÕs what I meant about there being room in the bed for me. And I think Michael was the happiest author IÕve ever worked with. He was 100 per cent happy with the Þlm, because his attitude was that the material was created by Virginia Woolf. His book is a take on her book, Mrs Dalloway. My Þlm is a take on his book, The Hours. He regards the whole thing as a baton being passed on, and the fact that the book of Mrs Dalloway went to number one on the bestseller list as a result of the Þlm of The Hours was, for me, the greatest vindication of the process: we had all held the baton and passed it on.
In CunninghamÕs book, Virginia Woolf is in the process of writing Mrs Dalloway, which is then read 26 years later by Laura Brown, the character played by Julianne Moore, and then 50 years later
nds an echo in the life of Clarissa Vaughan, the character playedby Meryl Streep. In other words, the lm isnÕt just an adaptation of a book, itÕs an adaptation of a book which is partly about books. Were you concerned that the nished lm might feel too literary?
The Hours and The Reader are both in a genre which has vanished, which is what I call the Ô25 million dollar arthouse movieÕ. And when you make an arthouse movie for 25 million dollars, what youÕre trying to do is move it over into the mainstream. These Þlms are called crossover Þlms and there are rarely more than one or two of them a year. When I went past a multiplex in California and saw that three of the six screens were showing The Hours, thatÕs the fulÞlment of everything IÕve ever tried to do, which is to make a Þlm on its own terms but to a high level. Twenty-Þve million dollars is unusual for an art Þlm and the subject matter Ð the lesbian element, the suicide, everything Ð is art subject matter. So it is an art Þlm, but I tried not to give it the feel of an art Þlm, by which I mean taking itself elaborately seriously. Thanks to Stephen, itÕs full of life and guts and humour and warmth and fun, as well as everything else. And also it moves. A lot of art cinema is so slow. Self-consciously slow.
Did you do your own research on Virginia WoolfÕs life?
Yes. I had to understand the chronology of her depression. I had to know at what periods of her life sheÕd felt conÞdent and at what periods sheÕd felt particularly low, because obviously we nip around a lot in the story. Michael always said that he was an average American teenager until, at the age of 15, he sought to impress a girl who was reading Virginia Woolf, so he read Virginia Woolf in order to be able to talk to her Ð and suddenly realised that there was somethingbeyond bowling alleys. He represents Virginia Woolf as the personwho opened his eyes to the world, so sheÕd always had this hugeimportance to him and heÕd always known a lot about her. I didnÕt, I had to start from scratch.
And did you bring anything of Mrs Dalloway into your adaptation of The Hours?
I tried to make the Þlm musical. There was a remark of WoolfÕs which I already knew, that all writers spend ten times as long thinking about rhythm as they do about meaning, and it was obvious to me from the start that a Þlm with three strands would have to be rhythmicallybeautiful. Writing the credit sequence took me three weeks. Once the credit sequence was written, it was comparatively plain sailing. But the credit sequence announces the method by which the Þlm is going to proceed, and itÕs a very unusual method. Amores Perros appearedthe same year, and when the Þlms were Þnished Guillermo Arriagaand I contacted each other and he said, ÔI just want to say how much I admire The Hours,Õ and I said, ÔI just want to say how much I admire Amores Perros,Õ and we both said, ÔArenÕt triangular structures wonderful?Õ Ð because you donÕt know which way theyÕre going to go, as opposed to binary. The Nureyev Þlm IÕve written actually has a triangular structure: itÕs about NureyevÕs childhood, itÕs about his education in St Petersburg and itÕs about his time in Paris, and byputting the three together you immediately get something rich that you donÕt get just by ashing back to a single period in time. So I knew it had to be musical, because WoolfÕs writing is musical. ÔMrs Dalloway said she would buy the owers herself.Õ IÕm sure a poetcould tell us what that is; iambic pentameter, I think. The arrival of Philip GlassÕs score was the oddest moment in the whole process,because I basically said, ÔIÕve done this work. It doesnÕt need Philip to do it. IÕve already put the music in.Õ IÕm now reconciled to it, but when I Þrst heard it I was horriÞed.
How did you decide which of the three stories to cut to at any given moment?
Feeling. Pure instinct. IÕve always believed that each cut must progress a story. You donÕt cut back to someone bearing exactly the same expression on their face that they had when you left them. Every good cut in Þlm involves forward movement, so all I was trying to do was Þnd forward movement in each story. There are no thoughtful,contemplative, Ôand now this character is staring out of the windowÕ scenes. ThereÕs always progression in story, and thatÕs how I put the whole thing together. In terms of which story I went to, it was onlywhat felt right. Also, I knew I had this fabulous ending: a character from one story, which has hitherto been separate, turns out to meet a character from another story, and you donÕt know in advance that these characters are going to be connected.
Did you write many drafts of the script?
Countless.
So the scenes didnÕt necessarily land in the right order straight
o the bat.
Well, when I did the publicity tour, IÕd always say, ÔThe screenplay is totally faithful to the book,Õ and Scott would say, ÔHave you read thebook lately? The screenplay doesnÕt resemble it in any way!Õ Similarly,IÕd always say, ÔThere was a lot of messing around but in the end all the scenes were put in exactly the same order that IÕd originallyimagined them.Õ But I havenÕt done the breakdown to know whether Stephen and Scott moved them around signiÞcantly differently.
Finding the right balance between the three stories must also have been vital.
I didnÕt want any of the three stories to be bits and pieces, I wanted allof them to have big set-piece scenes. Everyone was terriÞed of the scene at the railway station with Nicole Kidman and Stephen Dillane, because everyone knew it delivered the Þlm. The Þlm is basicallyabout a womanÕs right to self-assertion, and thatÕs when she asserts herself and says, ÔEven if itÕs at the cost of everything, including us, IÕm going back to London, because it represents life to me.Õ With Julianne MooreÕs character, itÕs the right to leave her child in order to survive, breaking the ultimate taboo. And with Meryl StreepÕs character, itÕs ceasing to feel responsible for Ed Harris: the acceptance of the fact that we have to let go of people and let them make their own decisions about their lives. So I was looking for big scenes with big emotions, and if all three stories had those then it would balance out.
TheyÕre not just big scenes, theyÕre long scenes, with long passages of dialogue Ð which makes some screenwriters and directors nervous.
If I write eight or ten pages of dialogue, I donÕt even notice that IÕm doing it. IÕm just trying to make the dialogue as good as I possibly can, and then nobody will notice. Some of the greatest scenes in the history of the movies are very long dialogue scenes. And some of the greatest sequences have no dialogue at all. But dialogue is part of your weaponry. It is absolute nonsense to say Þlm is a visual medium. It is not. Film is a medium in which words and images smash together in very interesting ways. One of the most memorable moments in Þlm is Bernstein in Citizen Kane talking about the girl with the white parasol, and Welles doesnÕt cut to an image of a girl with a white parasol. He doesnÕt need to. He knows that this character will give you that image. Citizen Kane is a Þlm richer in visual imagery than anything that came before it, but he also trusts that speech can do a job. So word and image are what youÕre looking for.
The book juxtaposes the three stories in chapter-size chunks. The lm juxtaposes them in sequences, scenes, even singleimages. In other words, lm gave you a chance to enhance the e ect of the book. Did that strike you as you were writing it?
Very much so. When I was in Los Angeles after the Þlm had opened, I met Almodvar Ð who I didnÕt know, but who, like me, was in Tower Records buying DVDs Ð and he said, ÔI tried to get the rights to The Hours, and I would have done it completely differently from how youdid it.Õ And I said, ÔI know you would, and it would have been great.Õ I would love to see AlmodvarÕs Þlm of The Hours; I think it would be incredible. In fact, there are so many Þlms in that book that I can imagine Þve different Þlmmakers making Þve different Þlms from it, and all of them would be good. ThereÕs something in MichaelÕs structure that offers inÞnite possibilities.
In the prologue of the book, when Virginia is walking to the river to kill herself, Cunningham mentions the voices in her head Ð presumably ancient Greek voices, which the Richard character also hears in the Mrs Dalloway strand of the novel. Did you consider including those in the lm, and if not, why?
I donÕt know exactly what was going on in Virginia WoolfÕs head when she went to kill herself, and I felt squeamish about attributing to a real-life character an interpretation which was so singular and intrusive. So I was very against it. Stephen and Scott became convinced during the editing that this should be in, so they went to a Greek theatre company and recorded Greek voices to go inside her head. After a week of experimenting with these voices they told me theyÕd failed, but they hadnÕt dared tell me they were doing it because they knew it would enrage me. I said, ÔDid you know that ancient Greek is different from modern Greek and the voices you were putting on were modern Greek?Õ They said, ÔNo, we didnÕt know that.Õ I said, ÔThen itÕs lucky you didnÕt put them on.Õ
The prologue of the lm Ð and the epilogue, looping back to the day of VirginiaÕs death, which isnÕt in the book Ð do, however, contain the only voiceover in the lm: her suicide letter to her husband.
I donÕt really think of a letter as voiceover.
Going back to what you said earlier, that the screenplay is totallyfaithful to the book; Scott Rudin may have disagreed, but actuallyI think it is totally faithful Ð as much as any screenplay can be.
Yes, I do, too.
Most scenes in the book found their way into the lm in one form or another, and thereÕs only one character of any signicance who didnÕt make it, the writer Walter Hardy, whose scenes in the book feel almost satirical.
ThatÕs true, and I wasnÕt interested in satire. Michael is deeplyembedded in the New York literary world, and is unapologetic aboutit. He reads all the novels that come out, he knows all the authors, heÕs a diligent and hardworking member of PEN. IÕm totallyuninterested in the New York literary world. I think it was explained to me who that character was Ômeant to beÕ and thatÕs not who IÕm making the Þlm for. IÕm making the Þlm for a general audience. I donÕt want it to be self-referential.
The female supporting characters are almost as rich as the leads:Miranda Richardson, Toni Collette, Claire Danes Ð and Allison Janney, playing ClarissaÕs partner, a relationship which youbrought ever so slightly more into the foreground. What was yourthinking there?
I think because otherwise Clarissa can seem rather haughty. Both in Mrs Dalloway and in The Hours, thereÕs a danger of ClarissaÕs isolation being a bit alienating, so I wanted her to be in a fullyengaged relationship. I also wanted a lesbian relationship to be credited in mainstream American cinema and treated as completelynatural and not exceptional. Stephen and Scott and I all felt verystrongly about this, and in fact the commercial red ags that would normally go up at that time around the representation of those relationships didnÕt go up on this Þlm. We felt weÕd somehow get this Þlm into and accepted in the multiplexes, and we did, and that was great.

CASE STUDY: THE READER
US/Germany, 2008 ¥ Directed by Stephen Daldry ¥ Produced by Anthony Minghella, Sydney Pollack, Donna Gigliotti, Redmond Morris ¥ Screenplay by David Hare, based on the novel byBernhard Schlink ¥ Cast: Kate Winslet (Hanna Schmitz), RalphFiennes (Michael Berg), David Kross (Young Michael Berg), LenaOlin (Rose Mather / Ilana Mather), Bruno Ganz (Professor Rohl)
Anthony Minghella originally intended to write and direct The Reader, but he ultimately produced it alongside Sydney Pollack. Do you have any sense of how di erently he would have adapted it?
I read the book not long after it came out and said to my agent, ÔI want to do this. I know exactly how to write a Þlm of it.Õ And myagent said, ÔYou canÕt. Anthony MinghellaÕs got it.Õ Minghella had the rights from the moment the book came out, and whenever I saw him I said to him, ÔCome on, Anthony, give me that book.Õ And Þnally he rang me and said, ÔI donÕt have time to do The Reader. You do it.Õ So when we set about it, this time with Stephen Daldry on board from the beginning, Anthony was more like a benign patron. The Þrst thing to say is that the book is written in the Þrst person, and thatÕs a convention which is accepted in literature: the narrator has written this book in order to tell us something. ThereÕs no equivalent in Þlm Ð unless you do it as a documentary made by Michael. So you start withthe basic philosophical problem: why is he telling this story? I decidedhe must be telling it because itÕs hitherto been secret and he now wishes it to be known, therefore heÕs got to have someone to tell it to Ð and thatÕs where the narrative structure with his daughter comes from. Now, Anthony said to me, ÔYouÕll shoot that, but itÕll never appear in the Þnal Þlm, because itÕs not necessary.Õ And I said, ÔItÕs absolutely essential. ThereÕs no Þlm without it, because unless youknow why the story is being told, thereÕs no point in telling it.Õ To me, Michael Þnally achieving an act of honesty with his daughter is whatmakes the Þlm work, but Anthony always thought that this structure would fall away Ð and he died before he knew that it would stay in.
ItÕs absolutely essential for another reason, too. Without it, the
lm would be completely pitiless. ThatÕs interesting.
The lm becomes about truth and reconciliation: Michael is reconciled with his daughter by telling her the truth about Hanna,but Hanna is reconciled with no one because she has no understanding of what the truth is.
ThatÕs right. There were two lines of attack on the book, and therefore on the Þlm. The Þrst was the presumption that the act of reading is an awakening of her moral as well as her imaginative faculty Ð and it is not. The Þlm would not dare, and I would not dare, and Bernhard Schlink would not dare, to offer any sense of redemption to the character of a concentration camp guard. That would be a huge insult to people who suffered at the hands of the Nazis. We would have no moral right to do that, and if we were doing that people would have a right to be angry Ð but we are not. It is a deliberate misreading of the book to presume that because she is read to she develops a moral faculty whereby she understands the iniquity of what she has done. She doesnÕt. She doesnÕt in the book, and she doesnÕt in the Þlm.
In the lm, Michael asks Hanna what sheÕs learned, and she says simply that sheÕs learned to read.
Exactly. She remains morally ignorant. Their relationship has not redeemed her. SheÕs with a young boy because heÕs unformed and has no real sense of history: he doesnÕt know what was done and isnÕt concerned with what was done. He thinks the relationship is mutual,but itÕs almost entirely on her terms. ItÕs a relationship of control.
You mentioned two lines of attack on the book. What was the second?
The second line of attack was, is it autobiographical? From SchlinkÕspoint of view, he was damned if it was and damned if it wasnÕt. If itÕs true, ÔOh, itÕs just a story about your life.Õ If itÕs not, ÔOh, itÕs justsomething you made up.Õ At the end of it all, I have no idea. I never asked him and was never curious to Þnd out, because it seemed to me that we had to work as if it were a true story Ð rather than as a true story. When IÕd written it, Sydney gave me the best note anyone has ever given me. He said, ÔBooks get away with things that Þlms donÕt, because Þlm is a literal medium. Even if you stylise them, whatÕs always being tested in Þlms is plausibility: do I believe this?Õ So, he has an affair with an older woman when heÕs a student, then later goes to a trial and sees that same woman and realises she was a war criminal. He said, ÔYouÕve got to believe in that coincidence. ThatÕs your test. I can give you a hundred notes on this script, I can go through it scene by scene, but if at the end the audience donÕt believe that idea, youÕre fucked.Õ What a great note.
How did you deal with it?
Stephen dealt with it in the way he handled that moment. ThereÕs Kate Winslet in the dock, and in walks Michael and sees her. ItÕs not a spine-chilling moment, because the audience sort of knows it Ð theyÕveread the publicity, they know somethingÕs up Ð itÕs just an incrediblydifÞcult moment to handle, so we spent a lot of time talking about that.
In the introduction to the published screenplay, you quote Sydney PollackÕs repeated question: ÔWhat is the metaphor of reading in the lm? What is the function of literature?Õ What was your answer?
The Þlm is about a woman who has lived an unremittingly ugly life, then glimpses, too late, another world Ð a world which can no longer operate as it might have done if it had been opened up to her when she was illiterate. Reading is her attempt to climb out of a mud of incomprehension.
ThereÕs a passage in the book: ÔI thought that if the right time gets missed, if one has refused or been refused something for too long, itÕs too late, even if it is nally tackled with energy and received with joy. Or is there no such thing as Òtoo lateÓ? Is there only ÒlateÓ, and is ÒlateÓ always better than ÒneverÓ?Õ The ending of the lm answers that question: itÕs too late for Hanna, but not too late for Michael.
Yes. Michael always seemed to me the central character in the Þlm. Hanna is the ashier part, and generally people noticed Kate Winslet more than they noticed Ralph Fiennes. But actually itÕs RalphÕs performance that holds the Þlm together, because Michael is doingsomething unbelievably brave. He has lived an effectively frozen life in which he has emotionally shut down, and the Þlm represents the moment at which he begins to come out of that. Not to take anythingaway from Kate, because obviously Kate is astonishing in the part, butthe underlying heart of the Þlm is held by Ralph. I feel there was a tension in the Þlming, which was that the producers and Stephenlocated the energy of the project in the Þgure of Hanna and I located itin the Þgure of Michael.
You feel that in retrospect, or you felt it at the time?
Oh, at the time. I felt that I was defending Michael, and was therefore defending the actor, Ralph Fiennes. Stephen would feel instinctivelythat exploring the character of Hanna was the dramatic thing to do, whereas Michael was downbeat, repressed, secretive, hidden Ð and, because of that, a less overtly dramatic character.
Another job that the framing narrative does Ð meeting his daughter, talking to his daughter, taking her to HannaÕs grave Ð is to make the older Michael more proactive. More dramatic, in fact.
Precisely. ThatÕs exactly what I was doing. HannaÕs story could never be fulÞlling. His story can. The story isnÕt really about wartime Germany, itÕs about post-war Germany and how the country pulleditself together and remade itself and dealt with what had happened inthe past. For that reason, I strongly resisted ashbacks to the war. I have a horror of what I call Ôstriped-pyjama moviesÕ, Þlms in which well-fed actors gather together to re-enact the fate of people during the Holocaust. *Denial* isnÕt a striped-pyjama movie, and The Reader isnÕt a striped-pyjama movie. Many people choose to project their moral anger on to The Reader for its supposed redemption of a Nazi war criminal. I Þnd it inÞnitely more offensive when the clichs of Þction are put on top of this experience to turn it into soapy drama. MunkÕs Passenger is great, and ResnaisÕ Night and Fog is great, and SchindlerÕs List is obviously great, because theyÕre done with complete integrity and are totally convincing and seem to look the event full in the face, but I donÕt think you should ever use that event to hitch a ride. As a screenwriter, the toughest thing is holding the line: saying, ÔYou can do that, but it wonÕt be my screenplay.Õ I think Christopher Hampton said, about screenwriting, that you only have power if youÕre willing to walk away. If youÕre the prisoner of a project,meaning that you go, ÔIÕve put so much into this that I must stayregardless,Õ you lose all your power. If anyone had tried to shoot the war material, I would have walked away.
Going back to the point that books get away with things that lms donÕt, HannaÕs illiteracy is a good example. In the lm, you have to maintain a tension between giving the audience enoughinformation so it makes sense when they nd out, but not giving them so much that they gure it out too soon.
When I said earlier that screenwriting is about conveying information,I should also have said that itÕs about concealing information Ð without the audience feeling theyÕre being cheated. What is a cut, when youÕre editing a Þlm? A cut is a jump, and itÕs a jump which leaves out a lot of stuff. Now, if the audience begins to feel that youÕre jumping over stuff which they ought to know but youÕre not tellingthem, they start to Þnd the Þlm dishonest. You canÕt just avoid the difÞcult scenes, you should confront them. ThatÕs another fundamental principle of screenwriting, it seems to me.
Your screenplay had already left out a lot of stu: the draft that I read was a very tight 81 pages.
The Schlink material was so rich, and there were so many different scenes, that a lot of them got left out. And I wanted anyway to focus in on the shocking contrast between how Hanna seemed to Michael when he didnÕt know who she was and what sheÕd done, to the totally different person that he looked at with complete mystiÞcation so many years later. I also wanted the whole thing to have the feeling of a fable, like the cinematic equivalent of a novella. And above all I wanted it to be eet. I think I failed as a Þlmmaker in the eightiesbecause my Þlms were self-consciously arty. Although Wetherby was hugely acclaimed, it was very much an ÔArt FilmÕ, and I didnÕt want to work in that genre. I wanted, with Stephen, to make a genre of our own, which wasnÕt weighty and didnÕt linger.
Not only did you leave scenes out, but you also added scenes in: for example, when Hanna and young Michael go into a church sheÕs transported by the sound of a choir singing Bach, which carries over into the framing narrative as older Michael listens to the same piece on his car stereo.
Again, thatÕs a glimpse of a world which is completely unattainable for her Ð and thatÕs the tragedy. Bernhard Schlink is asking a verybrave question, which I think he deserves credit for asking, which is what was the experience like for those on the side of the oppressor? And people who have a stake in the crimes which were committed have a perfect right to say, ÔI donÕt care. IÕm totally uninterested in what the perpetrators thought and felt.Õ But there was an element in the reception of both the book and the Þlm in America which seemed to me astonishingly self-deceiving, particularly people who said that the Germans were bad because they didnÕt stand up to Hitler and thatif theyÕd been there they would have resisted. Anyone who knows thehistory of Germany in the 1930s will know that what those people are effectively saying is, ÔI would have been willing to die.Õ And, signiÞcantly, this criticism came from people who had no experienceof war at all, people who had led entirely peaceful lives. ItÕs the moral equivalent of Donald Trump saying that the man who failed to go into the school shooting was a coward when he himself had dodged the Vietnam draft Þve times. I donÕt believe these people who say that theywould have behaved so brilliantly in Nazi Germany Ð and Europeanswho experienced occupation will know that all these things are shades of grey.
Did Bernhard Schlink like the lm of The Reader as much as Michael Cunningham liked The Hours?
I donÕt know what Bernhard Schlink thought of the Þlm. I never discussed it with him. In fact, IÕve not spoken to him since. I know Michael quite well, but Bernhard Schlink never became part of my life in the same way. I would regularly go through the script and talk scenes over with him, and he came to the shoot and was there when the Þlm premiered in Berlin. He seemed perfectly at peace with the Þlm, but whether he feels it represents the book or was somethingdifferent from it, I donÕt know.
It strikes me that Sydney PollackÕs question Ð ÔWhat is the metaphor of reading in the lm? What is the function of literature?Õ Ð could also be asked of The Hours. Again, what would your answer be?
So, literature exists to describe the most fundamental, elemental, profound human impulses which canÕt otherwise be reached exceptthrough religion, music, art. [Long pause]. ThatÕs about it, isnÕt it?


Olivia Hetreed
Olivia Hetreed was born in 1961 in Wells, Somerset.
Her screen adaptation credits include: The Treasure Seekers (1996, novel by E. Nesbit); The Canterville Ghost (1997, story by Oscar *Wild*e); What Katy Did (1999, novel by Susan Coolidge); *Girl with a Pearl Earring* (2003, novel by Tracy Chevalier); Canterbury Tales Ð The Man of LawÕs Tale (2003,after Chaucer); *Wuthering Heights* (2011, novel by EmilyBront); and three novels in Caroline LawrenceÕs The Roman Mysteries series (2007).
*Girl with a Pearl Earring* was BAFTA-nominated for Best Adapted Screenplay.
APPROACHES TO ADAPTATION
Do you prefer to adapt material which chimes with your own work, or material which is completely di erent and gives you a chance to try out new things?
The opportunity to get an insight into something different is deÞnitely part of the attraction of an adaptation for me. On the other hand, there are things IÕve looked at where IÕve thought, ÔI donÕt have anything to add to this world.Õ YouÕre quite bound in by the material and whatever research you do beyond it, so itÕs a question of how tight that world feels. I did an Indian adaptation recently, set at a particular moment in recent Indian history, and the book is so fantastic and expansive that I could simply go into the writerÕs world and it was all there. If it had been a much sparer story I would have been quite anxious about it, but the book was a brilliant guide to what I was trying to do. You can also sometimes tell a story because itÕs an adaptation that you wouldnÕt necessarily be allowed to tell if it were just a standalone piece. IÕm thinking particularly of an adaptation I did of The Man of LawÕs Tale from The CanterburyTales. That gave me permission to put a story about Nigerian refugees on BBC primetime which never would have got there if it wasnÕt pretending to be Chaucer.
Do you think adaptations involve a completely di erent set of creative gears to original screenplays?
No, I think itÕs remarkably the same. ItÕs like building a house. If youÕre doing an adaptation, there was already a house there and youknock most of it down and rebuild it. If youÕre doing an original, you have to dig the foundations and put in the services and then build the house. So that initial work is very different because youÕre trying to Þnd the story Ð and sometimes thatÕs an all-encompassing, totally created world from a novel, and sometimes thatÕs just a newspaper story Ð but then the actual process of writing the script is not that different.
Do you always agree a mission statement or direction of travel with whoever has commissioned the adaptation?
More and more. I didnÕt always realise the need for that, but itÕs incredibly important to know that you both want to make the same Þlm, and IÕm very keen on doing lots of preparatory work to make sure that we are, so that when they get the script theyÕre not astonished and go, ÔWe didnÕt think it would be like this.Õ WeÕve had all the difÞcult discussions beforehand about whatÕs in and whatÕs out and what perspective weÕre using, and thereÕs already an outline and a treatment as a result of that, and the surprise I want them to have reading the script is how thrilling and exciting it is compared to thoseslightly dull documents, not, ÔI never thought the story was going to be told this way.Õ
Have you ever had any radical disagreements with producers?
I wouldnÕt say IÕve had radical disagreements. ThereÕs been a couple of times where IÕm leaning one way and theyÕre leaning another way Ð particularly, interestingly, about female characters, where I feel like theyÕre not giving that character their due Ð but IÕve never backed out of something because of creative differences. I did have a projectwhere they didnÕt know where they were going. WeÕd set off in one direction and theyÕd say, ÔCan you go the other way?Õ And then weÕdgo the other way and theyÕd change their minds again. ThatÕs the only time IÕve been in development hell, in that you could have gone on inÞnitely producing different versions.
If the author of the source material is living, do you nd it useful to have their input on the script?
Certainly. ItÕs completely up to them how much input they have, and italso depends on what kind of author they are. Tracy Chevalier, who wrote the novel of *Girl with a Pearl Earring*, was very clear that she didnÕt want to be the screenwriter, so it was very easy to work with her because we were just two writers working together on the best possible version. She was very open to me asking her questions aboutwhere she got this and that from, but I wasnÕt asking her to help me write it and she wasnÕt insisting on helping me write it. Whereas IÕve also done true stories, and thatÕs a very different kind of relationship. ThatÕs someone who happens to have a really interesting story to tell, but isnÕt themselves a writer and hasnÕt been through this processbefore and doesnÕt have a professional detachment. ItÕs their story, or the story of someone they know and love, so for me thatÕs much more about them being comfortable with whatÕs happening. Not that IÕm going to tell the story to make them look wonderful all the time, but that weÕre going to be as truthful as we possibly can about what happened and that they understand the process as we go along. IÕve deÞnitely got better at doing that, and I think itÕs a really complicated thing to do, because itÕs a huge moral undertaking to do that with someoneÕs life. Their story will inevitably be simpliÞed and made more exciting and characters combined and so on, and I need to feel comfortable that they really understand the whys and wherefores of that Ð so that when it comes to asking them difÞcult questions, or saying I need to talk to someone who they feel angry with or whatever, they trust that thatÕs okay, that itÕs part of the process.
Is it easier to navigate notes from directors, producers and scripteditors when you have a piece of source material to measure the screenplay against?
I suppose itÕs a security blanket for everyone. You all know that this bit works in the book or that people already love this story, so theyÕre not questioning whether anyone would be interested in the story of a girl who cleans a painterÕs house, but doesnÕt do any painting herself. Without the book, that would have been a much harder sell and youÕd have been constantly defending it against people going, ÔSurely the story should be about Vermeer and not about the maid?Õ So itÕs incredibly useful for that.
Have you ever started work on an adaptation and found it harder to adapt than you anticipated?
Absolutely. I must be very dim, but I often donÕt realise the pitfalls and then think, ÔHow did I not see this?Õ Ronald Harwood tells a storyabout adapting The Diving Bell and the Butterand not realisinguntil he sat down to write it how impossible it was to be inside the head of someone who couldnÕt say or do anything, and I was veryheartened by that. YouÕre seduced by the story and the glory of it, andthen you come down to the hard graft of, ÔWhere does this go?Õ and, ÔIf we take out this enormous chunk of the story how does the rest of it work?Õ IÕd like to think IÕve got better at assessing that as IÕve done the work over and over again, but IÕm sure IÕll still continue to make that mistake.
Have you ever been o ered material to adapt which you felt couldnÕt, or shouldnÕt, be adapted?
Yes, and IÕve been right and wrong about that. IÕve been offered things to adapt and thought, ÔThis isnÕt going to be a good adaptation because the thing thatÕs interesting about it isnÕt the story, itÕs the way itÕs written or itÕs the witty asides or itÕs the 500 peripheral characters that our main character meets Ð which would be a terrible movie.Õ IÕve also thought that a book wasnÕt going to be a good adaptation because the book itself wasnÕt very good, but then IÕve seen the Þlm and theyÕd achieved things with it that the book simply hadnÕt done successfully. I didnÕt feel sad or jealous or angry, I just thought, ÔHow wonderful that someone has taken that unpromising material and found something so fantastic in it.Õ
Are there any screen adaptations which you think are especially good or you particularly admire?
I think all of James Schamus and Ang LeeÕs adaptations are reallywonderful. *The English Patient*, and Anthony MinghellaÕs writing in general Ð though I would make an exception for Cold Mountain, which for me is an example of the thing we were just talking about. Even in the hands of a master, the things that make the book interesting werenÕt in the Þlm and the things that were in the Þlm were somehow trivial compared to the book. So even the greatest of us can get it not quite right. But I think it was fantastically interesting to see his version of Ripley when there were at least two other versions of that character which were also really good and interesting. Not only can you take something and make a great thriller of it, but you can take something that other people have already had a go at and still produce something really characteristic of that Þlmmaker. Possibly my favourite Þlms in the world are both adaptations. One is Cabaret, byJay Presson Allen, which I think is a awless adaptation Ð again of material thatÕs been adapted in all sorts of different ways. ItÕs adaptedfrom the musical, and the musical was already an adaptation of a book, but the Þlm is so integrated that you never think about the script, you just feel itÕs one beautiful piece of machinery that works. And the other one I love is The Right Stuff, Philip KaufmanÕs adaptation. William Goldman, who was originally writing the screenplay, said, ÔYou have to choose either the space story or the Yeager story,Õ and Kaufman went, ÕNo, we need to do both,Õ and Goldman was right Ð you know, thatÕs the correct, orthodox answer Ð but actually thereÕs something so fabulous and epic and all-American about that Þlm. I love it as an example of breaking all the rules and still making it work.
ADAPTING FICTION
Some writers try to include as much of the novel as possible by boiling scenes down to their essence. Others are more ruthless in editing it down to a sort of greatest hits, but being true to the spirit of it. Do you favour either of those approaches?
There are probably always going to be scenes, certainly in a classic or much-loved book, that have to be in the story no matter which way you slice it, but other than that I think itÕs my job to be absolutelyruthless with the book. IÕm not there to present an audio-visual version of the book, IÕm there to make the best possible Þlm from the material Ð and at that point everything is up for grabs. I can take out what I want, I can add in what I want. I can do anything, because itÕs my version.
If a novel has an unusual structure, would you try to reproduce that in adapting it?
Not necessarily. IÕd look at it and go, ÔIs this central to telling the story? Is this what makes the story interesting?Õ If it is the thing thatmakes it interesting, how do I Þnd the visual equivalent of that? And ifitÕs not going to be exactly the same as the book, whatÕs my guiding principle in deciding when to deviate from it? I might say to myself,ÔIÕm going to follow this structure except for this point, where actually we need to know lessÕ Ð because a Þlm sometimes shows you a lot more than a book does; you can withhold information in a book in a way you often canÕt in a Þlm. IÕd have to have a really clear idea what my rules of engagement were so that it didnÕt become arbitrary. The worst answer to, ÔWhy was that scene there?Õ is always, ÔBecause it was there in the book.Õ ItÕs like with true stories: ÔWhy did this unbelievable thing happen?Õ ÔBecause it happened in real life.Õ ItÕs justdreadful.
Do you try to avoid voiceover in adapting a rst-person narrative, or do you see it as another tool in the toolbox?
I assume that I wonÕt use voiceover, but I wouldnÕt say IÕm never going to use it. It has to be on a case by case basis. One of the earlydecisions I made on *Girl with a Pearl Earring* was not to use voiceover, even though the novel is written in the Þrst person. I knew it would put a gap between her situation and her recall of it, which would immediately make you feel safer, whereas without it you were in the situation with her. Sometimes you can play a game with that gap, where the voiceover has a different point of view to the personweÕre seeing, and that seems to me a really interesting and energetic way to use voiceover. IÕm doing an adaptation at the moment of a true story and a lot of it hangs on a diary, and as you hear excerpts of the diary you realise that thereÕs a divergence between what weÕre seeing played out and what the diary is telling us happened. The voiceover becomes a character in the drama of ÔWhoÕs telling the truth here? What version of things is this?Õ
Do you use the language of the novel in writing the dialogue, or do you put the whole thing in your own words?
It varies. Until quite recently I would have said that very few novelistswrite dialogue you can use, that all novel dialogue is just rhythmicallydifferent. And I would certainly say that about anything written pre.cinema. But when you read contemporary novels you often get a sense of the author writing down the movie theyÕre seeing, so itÕs much closer now. It also depends how far away from my experience or expertise the dialogue is and what I think it needs to be achieving.With the Indian story I thought the dialogue in the novel was reallyrich and interesting, and I had to do a forensic examination of it to understand what the cultural and political references were. Once I understood what someone was saying, then I could make a decision as to whether they should say it in that way, or whether I should put it in some sort of context Ð because if I didnÕt understand it, another Western audience member wouldnÕt understand it either. But the things they were saying were so great that I was keen to hold on to them if I could.
Do you keep the novel beside you throughout the adaptationprocess, or do you try to internalise it and set it aside somewherealong the way?
I very much set it aside. Trying to write with the novel in your hand doesnÕt work for me at all, itÕs really inhibiting. But when IÕm revising the script, particularly if I have a scene that isnÕt working well or isnÕt as good as it should be, IÕll go back to the novel. There may not be an exact equivalent scene, but IÕll look at the area of the novel which deals with that event or that character and go, ÔWhat is it IÕve missed out?Õ or, ÔWhat is it IÕve done wrong?Õ and try to reconstruct the scene in a better shape. And then before I deliver the script, particularlybefore I deliver the Þrst draft, IÕll sometimes go back and re-read thenovel more generally and see if there are any other bits I can steal to build my house. And when the script is Þnally free-standing, the novelbecomes an incredibly useful reference point.
How much pressure do you feel when adapting a well-known andmuch-loved novel, knowing that you wonÕt be able to please everyone with the nished product?
I feel anxiety beforehand Ð ÔIs it wise to take this on?Õ Ð but once IÕve decided IÕve got something to bring to it thereÕs no point in beinganxious about it. On *Wuthering Heights*, it really didnÕt bother me, because I had the version I wanted to tell and I thought it was truthful to whatÕs in the book and I felt it hadnÕt been told before, so I was really clear about what my mission was in doing it.
