If I wasnÕt clear about why I was doing something, or I wasnÕt sure what I was bringing to it, that would be a bigger cause for anxiety. But as for worrying about whether people will be cross about it, yes of course sometimes they will be Ð but they can just read the book, you know?The book isnÕt going to be damaged by what IÕm going to do to it.

CASE STUDY: *GIRL WITH A PEARL EARRING*
UK/US/Luxembourg, 2003 ¥ Directed by Peter Webber ¥ Produced by Andy Paterson, Anand Tucker ¥ Screenplay by Olivia Hetreed, based on the novel by Tracy Chevalier ¥ Cast: Colin Firth (Vermeer), Scarlett Johansson (Griet), Tom Wilkinson (Van Ruijven), Judy Par tt (Maria Thins), Cillian Murphy(Pieter), Essie Davis (Catharina), Joanna Scanlan (Tanneke), Alakina Mann (Cornelia)
What attracted you to adapting Tracy ChevalierÕs novel?
I read it in proof because I have the same agent as Tracy, so I didnÕt have any preconceptions about it, except for my agent saying, ÔYou should read this. YouÕd love it.Õ And I did: I found it a thrilling read. Ifelt I completely understood that girl and I knew I could tell that story.IÕve rarely felt more conÞdent about that. Then it was just a questionof persuading everyone else, because I had no feature credits. But I was really sure I could tell it, and I was really sure it was a thriller. People kept going, ÔNothing really happens,Õ and ÔItÕs all so small,Õ but it thrills you because those small things matter so much. It matters that Griet moves the chair in VermeerÕs studio. It matters that she puts the earring in her ear. These things have very high stakes in this world. You asked earlier whether I see the complications and difÞculties in things, and I absolutely didnÕt see the complications and difÞculties of it being a subjective story and of her being an unreliable narrator; I felt those would be very easy to overcome. In terms of writing theywere, but in terms of other peopleÕs perceptions they werenÕt.
In what way is Griet an unreliable narrator?
SheÕs unreliable in that she thinks Vermeer is great so she gives him a lot of leeway, and dislikes his wife and always gives her short shrift, and covers up her own motives. So then you pull it apart and go, ÔButwhat are these characters actually doing?Õ It was a pleasurable thoughtexperiment to go, ÔIÕm Catharina, this girl has arrived and is closeted upstairs with my husband, theyÕre doing this painting that IÕm completely excluded from Ð how do I feel about that?Õ Expanding on those things was a really interesting part of exploring the book. Sometimes you feel like youÕre a butcher chopping the whole thing up
Ð IÕve got this much steak and this many sausages Ð and when youchop it up you just feel like youÕve got a shambles. But this was one of those books where you chop it up and it all falls beautifully into theright pieces. Tracy had done such a brilliant job of constructing a narrative from the very few known facts about Vermeer and his family that it was a complete pleasure to do the dismantling.
And what was it you understood about Griet?
I understood about being a teenager and feeling a disconnect between your intellect and your circumstances; that youÕre being treated one way, but you feel youÕre something else. SheÕs a maid, but she feels she has something to contribute and thatÕs impossible in the world sheÕs living in. Also, IÕd always vaguely known that my family history was Dutch, but IÕd never really thought about it, so when I went to Holland to do the research I had the feeling of being an adopted child who meets their birth family. I felt extraordinarily at home. Obviously the book wasnÕt written by a Dutch person, but there was somethingabout VermeerÕs reticent Dutch attitude to life that I found incrediblypersonally resonant. I just thought, ÔThis is so much my story.Õ
With the exception of the moment where she moves the chair in the studio, Griet could be perceived as a rather passive character,which is often seen as being a problem for a lead character in a
lm. Did that immediately occur to you?
The thing that sold the story to me is the moment where she slapsCornelia, the wilful child. She thinks, ÔIf I let this kid get the upperhand here, IÕm stuffed.Õ So she makes a choice and she slaps that child and she deals with the consequences, and I loved that choice. She proves how much sheÕs not a passive character in that moment. Her situation is passive, but sheÕs actually not. She doesnÕt wait for things to happen, she keeps moving forward to meet them. She forces the issue with Vermeer. She takes control of the relationship with Pieter. It was another brilliant thing that Tracy had done, to make her have power and agency without her ever stepping out of her properhistorical position. So I really liked that about her character: she was secretly much more active than you think.
The script is very tight: 87 pages. The lm is equally tight: 95 minutes. Almost an object lesson in how little exposition and dialogue is actually needed to tell a story on lm.
If the room is very quiet you donÕt need to speak loudly to be heard, and I think thatÕs what the Þlm does. I come from a Þlm editingbackground, so I really like things to be sparse. Through the energy of a cut or the presence of the actors you understand so much and need to say so little. What I discovered to some extent in writing that script, and certainly subsequently, was that you sometimes have to write things for people to read because they canÕt see what theyÕll see in the Þlm. You need to explain more in a script and trust that you can let it fall away in the process. IÕve experimented with writing dialogue andgoing, ÔYou donÕt need to say this, but this is what youÕre thinking,Õ because if thereÕs no dialogue there then the actor may not know whatÕs going on in the scene, and having it editorialised for you on the page is quite unsatisfying. Whereas to write the dialogue and then go, ÔLetÕs try playing the scene without actually saying those things,Õ can be really useful and really fruitful.
You establish a great deal very quickly in the rst ve pages: the class and religious di erences between the Vermeers and GrietÕs family; her fatherÕs incapacity; and VermeerÕs identity. How important is that set-up, especially with a period drama where the audience may not know that world well?
It can be very tempting to do lots of set-up: ÔYou donÕt know this world. Let me give you a guided tour.Õ As a writer, IÕm quite keen on that stuff and you have to go, ÔAs a viewer am I keen on that stuff? No: I want you to get on and tell me the story and IÕll Þnd out the world as I go.Õ I think one of the things thatÕs really successful in thestory is that you understand her situation without anyone ever having to explain it to you. You can see where she is in the hierarchy of the family and how she relates to the kids and so on because these things just happen in front of you. There was the underlying question of how much it mattered that VermeerÕs family are Catholic, and whether you have to understand seventeenth-century Dutch politics Ð and obviously you donÕt. You just have to know that thereÕs some kind of difference between his family and GrietÕs, and that happens to be represented by religion: theyÕre different from us, theyÕre richer than us, so be careful. Then youÕve done your job.
ItÕs a little way into the lm before Griet and Vermeer meet for the rst time, and then only briey and wordlessly. Their scenes together have been rearranged from the script so they become
longer and more intimate, and the development of the relationship between Griet and Pieter is more precisely mirrored.Did that progression emerge during the edit, or at the shooting script stage?
When I originally wrote the script, Mike Newell was attached to direct and MikeÕs view of the story was that the household was this little microcosm of society, and the painting emerged from all these different pressures of all these different characters on each other. It was more of an ensemble than the Þnal Þlm. When Peter Webber came on as director, he said, ÔThis is a love story, the painting is the consummation of the love story, and we need to question anything that isnÕt driving that forward.Õ So there was quite a big change of emphasis at that point, and then in the edit he was pursuing that lineeven further. I was seeing cuts of the Þlm as they went along, and there was one version where there was practically nothing left of it at all, and he did bring it back from that a bit.
There was one interesting change between the script I read and the lm. In the script, Vermeer and his wife come to GrietÕs house before she starts working for them, and he comments on her arranging the chopped vegetables in the kitchen in colour order, and you understand that she has some sort of artistic sense. In the lm, that scene has disappeared and their meeting is hence delayed.
I wish IÕd thought of that in the script, but actually we all felt it was Þne. Then, on the day Peter shot it, he said to me, ÔWeÕre never going to use this, because as soon as you put them in the same room and he says that to her youÕve told the whole story.Õ So we were alreadythinking about where they would meet and the subsequent effects of that, and one of the great things about me being on set was that we could go, ÔIf weÕre not introducing them here, what are we going to do further on?Õ rather than going, in the edit, ÔHow can we cobble this together to tell the story in a different way?Õ
ThereÕs much less of GrietÕs family in the lm. Why did you reduce her mother and father and brotherÕs roles Ð and remove her sister completely, who dies of plague in the novel?
We wanted to isolate her as much as possible so that she felt more vulnerable. She needed to have just enough family to feel the loss of them, but not so much that she was safe and surrounded all the time. That was a very clear decision by Peter to tell a more disciplinedversion of the story.
On the other hand, the character of VermeerÕs patron, Van Ruijven, has been considerably expanded from the novel. Did youfeel that the drama needed a more overt villain?
ThatÕs pretty much exactly what I thought. I also felt like he was fun and the story is a little bit light on fun. Everyone else is very seriousand earnest, so when he comes along he gets to shake things up, whichseemed like a good thing in terms of the energy of the story.
The script is very deft at combining di erent scenes from the novel for greater dramatic e ect: for example, the birth feast for VermeerÕs son and the unveiling of the painting of Van RuijvenÕs wife. Do you deliberately look for those moments?
One of the things that became apparent once I started taking the book apart was that the story was an accumulation of hundreds of tinymoments, and that really wasnÕt going to work on Þlm. So I was veryconscious of looking for scenes in which you could pull those moments together, but that speciÞc combination happened when Peter came on board. I did have those two scenes in an earlier draft, but they were doing slightly different things, and he said, ÔLetÕs have one big party scene in which all the issues are aired.Õ That was a reallyuseful note. Another combination of different bits is VermeerÕs neighbours being thrown out of their house. ThatÕs a strong visual moment and we see what Þnancial ruin looks like; we donÕt need it explained to us. Out goes your stuff on the side of the canal and thereÕs no social services coming to help you. The other thing that Tracy does in the book is jump around in time Ð you think youÕre in a simple narrative, but actually, chronologically, itÕs quite complex Ð and I chose to make it completely linear. So it was a question of combining this event that happens in the present and this emotion that started it three months earlier into one moment, so we get the effect of the nonlinear narrative within the linear structure.
Do you ever feel that there are moments Ð not just with this novel, but in adaptations generally Ð where lm enables you to do something better than the book?
*Girl with a Pearl Earring* is such a strong example of that, because the whole story is about looking and seeing, all of which you can do better on Þlm. There are some lengthy passages in the book about her becoming aware of the effects of light and we were able to Þnd visual ways of showing that, like the opening of the shutters in his studio and the reection on the tray she polishes. So much of the script is actually description Ð much more than youÕre supposed to do, and much more than IÕd normally do Ð because itÕs telling you what youÕre going to see. My intention when you read a script is always that you see the Þlm, but usually I write just enough for you to see whatÕs essential and preferably not a word more. But with this, it was so important that the reader understood the signiÞcance of certain objects and was conÞdent that the silences in scenes would be explicable.
In the novel, Griet says, ÔI never got to have a proper look at the
nished painting.Õ In the lm, she does see it and her reaction is, ÔOh. You looked inside me.Õ Why did you make that change?
Probably one of the biggest changes was that Vermeer was a less mysterious character than he is in the book, therefore she had to understand what the transaction between them had been in a more explicit way, rather than coming to that realisation years later as an adult woman looking back on it. It was really important that everything got dealt with at the end of the story.
The pearl earring itself is also put to slightly di erent use at the end of the novel than in the lm. Just as the opening of the lm is very concise, so is the ending: a 17-page epilogue is reduced to little over a page of script. Tanneke brings the earrings, a gift from Vermeer while heÕs alive rather than a legacy after his death
Ð in other words, I have feelings for you that I canÕt do anything about, so here are the earrings instead. Then you cut to the painting. In the novel, she actually sells the earrings, which pays
o VermeerÕs debt to Pieter the butcher, so they and she become part of a nancial transaction. So again, a deliberate shift into something more emotional and elegaic.
In the book, you never forget the Þnancial transactions underlyingeverything. In the script, that was very focused around Maria Thins, the mother-in-law. She became the person who was always thinkingabout money, rather than everyone being *drive*n by it. But you do have to be simpler in a Þlm. You have this incredible visual richness, and there are all sorts of things youÕre saying in the way you frame and edit things, but those ambiguous moments can be a little underwhelming. Whereas with a book you sit there and savour it, with a Þlm you want a more immediate kick.
ThereÕs a scene in the lm where Griet pauses outside VermeerÕs studio before leaving for the last time, and she canÕt go to him and he canÕt go to her, but theyÕre separated only by a door. Did you want to make it clearer that he loved her? Because in the novel it seems more ambiguous, at least in GrietÕs mind, whether he actually cared more for her or for the painting.
ThatÕs a good question. I think in the end we felt Ð and IÕm saying ÔweÕ because it was very much Peter and I together Ð that you need to believe this love was real. You know that nothing will come of it, that they wonÕt physically love each other, but there really was that feeling between them. It would be very cold and frustrating for us to think that she loved him, but he just wanted a painting. ThatÕs just too difÞcult. YouÕd feel cheated by it.
Dicult in that it would have narrowed the gap between Vermeerand Van Ruijven?
Exactly. TheyÕd both have been using her. In that respect, it was a conscious decision not to completely follow the truth. Obviously I have no idea what Vermeer really thought, and this story isnÕt how that picture really got painted, but my reading of his paintings is thathe was quite a remote person who had difÞculty relating to women.
He doesnÕt paint women in a way that suggests he was comfortable with them. He had to be in the room with them, but he puts up as many barriers as possible. In most of his paintings theyÕre kept at a distance and framed in doorways and protected by props. And then suddenly thereÕs this one girl staring at you and challenging you, much more intimate and physically present. It felt like there was a reallyimportant difference between her relationship with him and all the other relationships he might have had Ð and that was the story we were interested in telling.
You added another scene, too, where Van Ruijven nally gets his painting of Griet, but it gives him no pleasure. What was your thinking there?
It was really important to get across the sense that she had escaped allof them. He takes possession of the painting, but not of her.
And it feels absolutely right to end on the painting, because thereÕs something so direct about her gaze that after several centuries it still endures.
Exactly. Somehow this girl still speaks to us, and even though we donÕt know who she was, this version is sufÞciently credible that whenyou look at that painting, you go, ÔMaybe that was her life.Õ

CASE STUDY: *WUTHERING HEIGHTS*
UK, 2011 ¥ Directed by Andrea Arnold ¥ Produced by Robert Bernstein, Douglas Rae, Kevin Loader ¥ Screenplay by Andrea Arnold and Olivia Hetreed, screen story by Olivia Hetreed, based on the novel by Emily Bront ¥ Cast: Kaya Scodelario (Cathy), James Howson (Heathcliff), Solomon Glave (Young Heathcliff), Shannon Beer (Young Cathy), Steve Evets (Joseph),Oliver Milburn (Mr Linton), Paul Hilton (Mr Earnshaw), Simone Jackson (Nelly), Lee Shaw (Hindley), James Northcote (EdgarLinton), Amy Wren (Frances), Nichola Burley (Isabella Linton), Michael Hughes (Hareton)
Emily BrontÕs novel has sharply divided readers since it was rst published: love it or hate it. I assume you love it?
I do love it, but I Þrst read it as a teenager and read it as a very twistedbut deeply romantic story, and felt as the protagonists do that love and death were adjacent and almost equally attractive. When I came back to it many years later, and saw what an extraordinary catalogueof violence and abuse and perverse relationships is laid out for you, I was really astonished Ð and really impressed, because itÕs very unusual in classic literature to Þnd a story thatÕs so uninchingly honest about being a teenager. Although some of the main characters in great Þction are teenagers, theyÕre often not teenagers as we would recognise them. The Bennet sisters are teenagers, but they donÕt behave like anyteenager youÕve ever come across. Whereas Cathy and Heathcliff are absolutely in the throes of hormonal chaos, quite apart from all the other chaos thatÕs visited upon them, and it felt like that story hadnÕt really been translated into Þlm. I didnÕt watch all the previousadaptations but IÕd seen various versions over the years, and theyseemed to me not very representative of what was most interesting in the book.
Your script is considerably stripped back from the novel, and the
lm is further stripped back from your script. What would you saythat you, Andrea Arnold and Ecosse Films had in mind in tacklingthis story?
I suppose, as a producer, you look at a book like *Wuthering Heights*, and its history and heritage make it attractive in the Þrst instance, andthen you think, ÔWhat would a version made now look like?Õ So they proposed it to me and I came back to them and said, ÔI think this is a story about teenagers, I think this is a story in which Heathcliff is clearly not white, and I think this is a story about the damage done to you in childhood and how you can never escape that.Õ That isnÕt the way *Wuthering Heights* has usually been told, and they were reallyenthusiastic about that. I think part of their enthusiasm was that the Twilight Þlms had just started and the Þrst Twilight book is based on *Wuthering Heights*, so when I came in saying, ÔThis story is about teenagers,Õ they went, ÔTeenagers. Fantastic.Õ Their Þrst intention was a very commercial Þlm, and even though we had a good time working on the script there was a certain pull towards more explanation and more romantic love than I wanted. As a screenwriter you only have a limited number of shots in your gun and you shouldnÕt Þght battles any sooner or harder than you have to. You need to wait until youreach a point where you go, ÔNo: if we do this then the whole thing is pointless.Õ So I felt we would sort out those things in due course. But once it came to the casting, that starting point Ð itÕs about teenagersand Heathcliff isnÕt white Ð instantly became problematic. Theywanted to get the Þlm made and to support the budget that the Þlm needed to be made at, but they felt that the casting required to achievethat budget didnÕt allow those things to be paramount. It was onlywhen Andrea came on board, and we suddenly had a much lower budget and more creative freedom, that we were able to cast teenagers and a non-white Heathcliff. Although the irony is that by casting teenagers and having Andrea direct, it became a Þlm that wasnÕt for teenagers Ð apart from very political, Gothic teenagers, who love it.
Bront isnÕt precise about Heathcli Õs origins, but he is described as Ôa dark-skinned gypsy in aspectÕ. Why was his ethnicity so important to your pitch, and how did it a ect your approach to the novel?
So much in the story is about prejudice, not being given a fair chance, being treated differently for who you seem to be, in looks or class. And the reaction of almost every character to Heathcliff is some comment about his Ôdark-skinned looksÕ: that heÕs not one of them, that heÕs strikingly different and therefore suspicious. I was struck in researching the writing of the book that Emily Bront had set it at a moment when a great many former slaves were turned out of their mastersÕ houses because slavery was no longer permitted in Britain and there was a racist panic about black vagrants, which seemed to me to resonate very clearly with the contemporary panic about ÔhordesÕ of migrants. So it makes absolute sense that she had written this interloper, picked up off the streets of Liverpool, one of the great slave trade ports, as a person of colour. But no one who meets him in the book has any real reference for that, so they hazard a guess: gypsy, Lascar Ð an East Indian sailor Ð Chinese or plain Ôblack devilÕ. And once you see that, and get away from Laurence Olivier and Byronicdark eyes, itÕs hard to unsee it, and it informs all the prejudice and badtreatment that he endures and reacts against.
Would you describe the novel as a melodrama, and if so did you set out to embrace or resist that?
I think itÕs a combination of intense realism and Gothic melodrama, and for me the Gothic melodrama wasnÕt the most interesting thing about it. I know there have been versions which have gone quiteGothic and I didnÕt want to do that; I wanted to do something that felt very real. Some of the strongest passages in the book are her descriptions of the farmhouse and the people in it, the close relationships you have in a small household like that and how unservile the servants are towards their so-called masters. All that felt very convincing, so it was quite easy to jettison anything that seemed overblown. The dialogue is unspeakable: they never utter a singlething that you could actually say in real life Ð particularly Heathcliff, who carries on talking a great deal of high-own stuff, even when heÕs supposed to be reduced almost to animal noises. The other Gothic element is the various layers of narration: Lockwood telling the storyand Nelly telling the story and so on. Again, those have been signiÞcant elements in other versions, but they werenÕt the story I was interested in telling. They might be there partly because it was the Þrst and only novel she ever got to write, so sheÕs writing in the styles of novels sheÕs read and working out a way of doing it, but to me theyÕrejust Gothic trappings that arenÕt helping you towards a better understanding of the story.
Cathy and Heathcli arenÕt particularly sympathetic characters: they donÕt treat each other or anyone else very kindly. So why should we care about them?
I hope we care about them because we understand why they behave so appallingly. One of the things I found really interesting as I took the book to pieces was that Heathcliff is constantly referred to as devilish and diabolical and is always swearing terrible curses on people Ð and he does do some bad things, for sure Ð but mostly he just puts on a bad front and goes out of his way to make the people around him dislike him because heÕs such an angry, damaged person. I Þnd something beguiling about that. Emily Bront deals with psychologicaldamage in a way that feels very modern and truthful, and itÕs obviously a book thatÕs very heavily inected by her own familyexperience of what itÕs like to live with someone very damaged, someone who you love but also hate. Cathy was also really interesting to me, because for most of the time I was working on the script I was the only woman in the room Ð and even though the men around me were very nice and sympathetic, they all disliked Cathy intensely andfound her really irritating and wrongheaded. The thing with Cathy isthat she has a boyfriend whoÕs sweet and blonde and respectful Ð the kind of kid you could bring home to meet your parents, if you had any Ð and she has this other boy whoÕs *wild* and dreadful and unacceptable, and she wants both of them. If that were a man and he had a sweet blonde girlfriend and a *wild* sexy wicked one, then it would be Þne: ÔOf course. What a dilemma. What can the poor boy do?Õ But because itÕs a girl we go, ÔFor GodÕs sake, make up your mind, stop putting everyone through hell.Õ I was absolutely fascinated by how difÞcult that story still is. SheÕs stuck in a horrible mess with a terrible brother; her only way out is to marry someone. She canÕt marry Heathcliff because heÕs made it impossible, so she marries Edgar. HeÕs a decent person, itÕs a sensible choice. ItÕs not romantic, but itÕs sensible. Then Nelly says, ÔHe wonÕt want Heathcliff hanging around,Õ and Cathy goes, ÔIf he doesnÕt take Heathcliff heÕs not getting me.Õ SheÕs not saying, ÔIÕm going to have an affair with him,Õ she just means, ÔI need to get Heathcliff out of this awful place and rescue him as well as me, and then we can carry on as before.Õ And of course itÕs ridiculously nave of her, but I donÕt think itÕs wicked. TheyÕre just kids, and sheÕs trying to do the best she can under the circumstances, but the men wonÕt have it. They wonÕt share her, so they tear her apart.
BrontÕs dialogue is dense and orid. Yours is sparser but elegant. ArnoldÕs is sparser still and more colloquial Ð and more contemporary. It feels like the source material has gone on a real journey there.
When the book Þrst came out people were horriÞed by it. Theythought it was brutal and ugly, and that the language of it was disgusting, and I think the Þlm has some of that too. ThatÕs not to say the Þlm is brutal and ugly, itÕs beautiful in lots of ways, but itÕs a hard story and bad things happen in it. ItÕs not bonnets and frocks, itÕs really got away from that, and I think Andrea is one of the few Þlmmakers who could have achieved that. You feel youÕre in that place, living that life Ð and I love that.
ItÕs very much a northern, working-class telling of the story: both the characters and their houses are less grand than I imagined Ð *Wuthering Heights* is a farmhouse, for example, rather than a manor house.
Film often tends to push things up. You get people who are supposedly 20-something living in a palatial at and you think, ÔHow the hell are they achieving that?Õ I wanted things to be a bit more real. ItÕs a story that feels incredibly rooted in a particular place, and whatÕs extraordinary about going to that place now is that itÕs remarkablyunchanged. The feeling in the book is that the moor is a planet of its own. You have no sense of the outside world at all. And that enclosed world is clear in the Þlm as well.
The tagline on the poster was ÔLove Is A Force Of NatureÕ, and nature certainly plays a big part in the lm: the landscape, the elements, animals dead and alive. At times it feels like a collaboration between Ken Loach and Terrence Malick. How did you envisage it when you were writing it?
I just see it and write it down. ItÕs that simple for me. I try to write down the movie I see in my head, and when the movie isnÕt there, IÕm thinking, ÔWhy am I not seeing this?Õ Not being able to see a scene is a good indicator that something isnÕt quite right and I need to think differently about it. So I had a really clear idea of what it looked like. IÕve been to the places where it happens. IÕve been to the rock that they sat on, which is a real rock overlooking a real moor. And Andrea has this fantastic ability to be visually subjective, to put you right inside the sensory perceptions of the main characters. Her visual storytelling is really, really strong.
The novel is in two volumes. Your script and the lm mainlydramatise the rst volume, with elements of the second. In other words, a couple of hundred pages are dropped completely Ð including the framing narrative involving the older Nelly and the new tenant of Thrushcross Grange; Heathcli Õs plot to take ownership of the Grange by marrying his and IsabellaÕs sick son to Cathy and EdgarÕs daughter; and her ultimate engagement to her cousin Hareton. Were those immediate and obvious cuts for you?
It was the most agonising thing for me. I really wanted to tell that second half of the story, and the way I found to do that was to bookend the Cathy and Heathcliff story with the Catherine and Hareton story. With great toil and strain I got it down to a 24-hour framing device, in which Catherine comes to *Wuthering Heights* to getthe doctor because her father is dying, and Heathcliff kidnaps her as he does in the book and says, ÔYou canÕt leave until you marry my son,Õ and while sheÕs trapped there he effectively tells her the story. She says, ÔWhy do you hate us?Õ and his story is the explanation of why,then at the end of the Cathy and Heathcliff story you come back to Catherine and Hareton, who helps her escape and get back to her father Ð and in that help is implied an improved relationship which will end up with them being together. I thought that was hugelyimportant, because the end of Cathy and HeathcliffÕs story isnÕt really an end, itÕs just a cessation. The end, the satisfying way of dealingwith all this generational damage, is Catherine and Hareton beginning to forgive each other and being decent human beings, so you go,ÔThank God. ThereÕs hope at last. These two people can overcome all this this hatred and misery.Õ But what actually happened was that everyone would read the script and go, ÔIt doesnÕt get started until we get to the Cathy and Heathcliff story.Õ Peter Webber was originallygoing to direct the Þlm, and Peter is great on script, and he said, ÔI know how important this is to you, but at the moment itÕs not working. Why donÕt we just set it aside, work on the main story and put it back later?Õ Of course we were never going to put it back later, but it was a way of getting me off my high horse. So my feeling about my script and the Þnished Þlm, is that it doesnÕt have an ending. I didnÕt Þnd a way of solving that problem before I handed the script on and I really regret that.
The novel is about hate as much as love, and how hatred corruptsand destroys from generation to generation. ThereÕs a hint of that at the end of the lm, when Hareton casually hangs a dog in the same way that Heathcli did. But at the end of your script thereÕs a sense that Heathcli may treat Hareton better because of the way he was treated, rather than repeat the cycle of brutalisation. Again, can you talk me through that journey from novel to script to lm?
As I said earlier, Heathcliff is constantly described as diabolical and devilish, but actually his parenting of Hareton in a practical sense is not so cruel and abusive. The thing heÕs done is displaced him, in the same way that he was displaced, and itÕs obviously a terrible injustice to Hareton that heÕs taken away his birthright Ð but he didnÕt steal it from him, he got it from his useless dad, and thereÕs actually a kind of affection for him. I think at some point Heathcliff says, ÔI really try to hate him, but IÕm not as awful as I intend to be,Õ and I thought that was quite helpful, that youÕre not just left with this feeling of utter bleakness. Characters who are relentlessly bad arenÕt as interesting as characters who are contradictory and mixed up, like most of us are.
You received a screen story as well as a co-screenplay credit, which seems appropriate given that the lm often follows the contours of your script, and that you chose to dramatise parts of the story which Bront skated over. Did the story feel as much yours as BrontÕs by the time you nished the script?
ItÕs a strange hubristic thing you do in an adaptation. You do gradually take possession of it, and if you donÕt take possession of it thatÕs problematic Ð for me, at least. Without denying for one instant that every bit of it is created or inspired by Emily Bront, IÕm saying, ÔThis is my version. This is what I think is important,Õ and I need to have a sense of ownership of the material to do that. And then, of course, it becomes the directorÕs, and there are further versions beyond mine.
The story you told, coupled with Andrea ArnoldÕs naturalistic shooting style and the direction she took the dialogue in, makesthe lm feel faithful to the spirit of the novel without being over-reverent to the letter of it. To put it another way, itÕs a compelling adaptation, but not a conventional one. Is that fair?
I think that is fair, and thatÕs very much what IÕd hope for it. With almost all period Þlms, but certainly with adaptations, you get a strong sense of the time in which the Þlm is made layered on to the original material. So when you watch a period Þlm which was made inthe 1960s, it speaks to you strongly of the 1960s. I think this Þlm speaks equally strongly of the period in which it was made, and I donÕt think thatÕs a bad thing.


Nick Hornby
Nick Hornby was born in 1957 in Redhill, Surrey.
His screen adaptation credits include: *An Education* (2009,memoir by Lynn Barber); *Wild* (2014, book *Wild*: From Lost to Found on the Pacic Crest Trail by Cheryl Strayed); *Brooklyn*(2015, novel by Colm Tibn; Love, Nina (2016, book Love, Nina: Despatches from Family Life by Nina Stibbe); and a dramatisation of his own memoir, Fever Pitch (1997).
Fever Pitch was remade in the US in 2005 (released in the UK as The Perfect Catch). Several of his novels have also been adapted for lm and television by other screenwriters, including: High Fidelity (lm 2000/TV series 2020); About a Boy (lm 2002/TV lm 2003/TV series 2014Ð15); A Long Way Down (2014); and Juliet, Naked (2018). He recently scripted the Sundance TV series State of the Union (2019).
*An Education* and *Brooklyn* were both Oscar- and BAFTA-nominated for Best Adapted Screenplay.
APPROACHES TO ADAPTATION
Do you prefer to adapt material which chimes with your own work, or material which is completely di erent and gives you a chance to try out new things?
I donÕt think the nonÞction things IÕve done have chimed with my own work. They chime through an emotional outlook or an interest in material that IÕm unlikely to generate myself. When you set out on a piece of original writing, you often think, ÔThis is different from anything IÕve done before,Õ then eventually you reach a point, a bit like Jim Carrey in The Truman Show, where youÕre sailing towards blue skies and Ð clunk! Ð you hit the inside of your skull: ÔOh, my God, IÕm stuck here again.Õ But with the things IÕve adapted, IÕve found a little door out of my skull and into someone elseÕs, and it feels incrediblyrefreshing.
Do you think adaptations involve a completely di erent set of creative gears to original screenplays?
An adaptation doesnÕt seem to me to require a different set of gearsfrom writing a novel. ItÕs just writing. I donÕt know how youÕve foundit with your novel and your screenplays, but I donÕt feel like I become a different person when IÕm doing one as opposed to the other. IÕm using a broadly similar set of skills. The differences between an original and an adaptation are more practical, I think, and for all sorts of reasons connected with the industry, adaptations tend to come to the fore.
Do you always agree a mission statement or direction of travel with whoever has commissioned the adaptation?
I wouldnÕt put it as grandly as that. ThereÕs never anything written down. I wrote *An Education* on spec and my wife produced it, so bythe time people got to see it we could pretty much say, ÔThis is what it is. You can see where itÕs going and what it wants to do, and youÕre either interested in it or youÕre not.Õ With *Wild*, I did go to America and talk Ð I wouldnÕt say pitch Ð to Reese Witherspoon and her producer, and I did have a couple of ideas about chronology, but I think they just wanted the book adapted, really. Can we turn this book into a good Þlm? That was our mission statement.
Do you usually produce an outline or treatment before you start writing the script?
No. IÕve tried to do it a couple of times, but itÕs really difÞcult if youÕre a prose writer, because itÕs deeply ingrained in you that your prose counts. I Þnd it very hard to sit down and think, ÔThis is a piece of writing that will never be published, and no one is ever going to think about it in terms of its prose.Õ So even though IÕve thought IÕll muster up an outline, IÕve given up after two paragraphs.
If the author of the source material is living, do you nd it useful to have their input on the script?
ItÕs different from case to case. I donÕt think Cheryl Strayed had a great deal of input into *Wild*, but she was Þercely loyal to the Þlm Ð and loved it, as far as I know. With *An Education*, Lynn Barber seemed quite surprised and tickled by the whole thing in terms of it being a movie project, because what I adapted was six pages in Granta. We went out for lunch a couple of times to talk about it, and Iremember her being helpful on period detail. The thing about adapting memoirs is I donÕt want to piss anyone off. ItÕs an agonisingenough process for the authors anyway, and if it makes them feel exposed or uncomfortable in a way the books didnÕt, then IÕve done something unhelpful or wrong Ð and IÕm happy to talk to them about that, or disguise characters, or miss out a piece of the plot.
Is it easier to navigate notes from directors, producers and scripteditors when you have a piece of source material to measure the screenplay against?
The big capital letters answer to that is YES, both for Þction and nonÞction. One of the big problems with an original screenplay is that it only exists as a Þlm, as it were. ItÕs built on sand. And if it doesnÕt become a Þlm, it isnÕt anything, really. So if a director says, ÔWhy doesnÕt this take place in the twentieth century instead of the nineteenth?Õ or, ÔWhy isnÕt the protagonist a woman instead of a man?Õ you have to think about that. But if youÕre adapting Dickens, ithas characters, it has a shape, it has a tone, it has somewhere itÕs going, and those things are all enormously valuable in your battle to protect what you want to do. When itÕs all being spun out of the air, everyoneÕs got equal rights to chip in.
Have you ever started work on an adaptation and found it harder to adapt than you anticipated?
Not yet. I never think anythingÕs going to be easy, so IÕm not often shocked or thinking to myself, ÔMy God, why am I taking this on?Õ
Have you ever been o ered material to adapt which you felt couldnÕt, or shouldnÕt, be adapted?
There are some very basic things for me, like the length of the book. If somethingÕs 800 pages long, itÕs probably not a Þlm Ð though it might be a very good TV series. You have to lose something like three-quarters of a book in adapting it, and at 800 pages youÕd simply be losing too much to do it any kind of justice. I also donÕt want to writethings where you have to recast the central character several times because of age. And there are lots of things IÕve been offered that are just too internal, where youÕre reading the book and the question in your mind is, ÔWhatÕs actually happening here for us to look at?Õ and the answer is, ÔSomeone walking around an art gallery thinking aboutthe past.Õ I tend to shy away from all those things.
If someone wants to option a book of yours, how do you decide whether to adapt it yourself Ð and if someone else adapts it, how involved would you be in the process?
I wanted to do Fever Pitch myself because it was a memoir, and IÕd rather that I messed it up than someone else. But thereÕs also a verybasic reason why I adapted Fever Pitch, which is that when I was asked, in 1992 or 1993, I was just starting a family, I hadnÕt written a novel and I wasnÕt in the business of turning down work. So when theyoung director who wanted to do it said, ÔDo you want to have a go at adapting it?Õ I said, ÔSure.Õ So someone paid me to do a draft, and we just kept doing drafts until someone eventually said, to mysurprise, ÔOkay, this is ready, weÕre going to make it.Õ By the time the Þlm came out, in 1997, IÕd written High Fidelity and just Þnished About a Boy, and the Þlm rights for High Fidelity were sold and then the Þlm rights for About a Boy, and I thought, ÔThis might keep happening, and IÕm fucked if IÕm going to spend two years writing a book and another Þve years taking it apart again.Õ While I have an idea in my head to do something else, IÕm done with the last thing and I donÕt want to go back to it. ThatÕs just never ever appealed to me, and IÕve not regretted it for a moment.
What inspires you to swap the authorial autonomy of novels for the collaborative constraints of lm and TV?
There are endless frustrations, as you know, and a great deal of despair, but IÕve made very good working relationships throughscreenplays with people I didnÕt think IÕd get a chance to work with. I like the rhythm of it: I can do a draft in a few weeks. I canÕt do a draft of a novel in a few weeks, so I like the sense of having accomplished something that quickly. And then I like meeting to talk about it with people whose company I enjoy Ð and if IÕm not enjoying their company, it tends not to go on too long.
Are there any screen adaptations which you think are especially good or you particularly admire?
Moneyball was really good; I was very admiring of Steven Zaillian and Aaron SorkinÕs work on that. The Godfather, obviously, is really good. And Peter Straughan and Bridget OÕConnorÕs adaptation of Tinker Tailor Soldier Spy was really good.

ADAPTING NONFICTION
When adapting a book, ction or non ction, do you rely on the authorÕs research, or do you conduct your own?
I donÕt think thereÕs a consistent answer to that. With *An Education*, there wasnÕt enough in LynnÕs piece for me not to conduct the research. With *Wild*, there was no way I was trekking off on some thousand-mile journey, so I completely relied on Cheryl. I talked to her and I watched things on YouTube, but I didnÕt go anywhere near the place.
Do you approach a non ction adaptation in the same way as
ction, or does the strangeness of real events give you licence toexperiment with, say, structure?
Again, IÕm not sure it makes much difference. Something either works as a screenplay or it doesnÕt, and the fact that things actuallyhappened doesnÕt really help you in any way. I donÕt think it helps you to be more experimental.
Have you ever encountered events in a non ction book so strange that you couldnÕt use them in the adaptation because audiences wouldnÕt believe they were real?
No, but I can completely imagine that happening. The thing youÕve got to watch for is randomness. If a narrative development feels completely random, even if it happened in real life, thatÕs not a goodenough excuse to put it in a screenplay, which has to be organicallyconstructed and psychologically convincing. So I can absolutelyimagine chucking something out on those grounds.
How much responsibility do you feel towards historical accuracy when dramatising the lives of real characters?
It depends on who they are. Lynn isnÕt a character from history, sheÕs a well-known journalist, and I was telling her story because it seemed meaningful and resonant about a particular time and place. I was using it as a way of writing about Britain at a certain point in its socialhistory, and I felt I could write freely about what had happened to her.At the moment IÕm doing some research on rock stuff. I donÕt know if anything will happen with it, but with that I think I have a duty to be accurate. Why not be? The facts are extraordinary enough.
How do you set about nding the voices of real characters?
With Lynn, I had perfect liberty to imagine the voice of a bright teenager in 1961. With Cheryl, I had a very strong voice in the memoir. And on the one IÕve been doing in the last year Ð another memoir about an extraordinary set of circumstances Ð IÕve been talking to the young woman concerned on an almost daily basis. But I think to a certain extent voice comes from experience. Once you have the narrative events and the character has made certain choices, then their voice starts to emerge Ð because itÕs going to be a certain kind of person who does this at this point, instead of that at that point, and you can start to Þll in the corners of their soul in that way. But when IÕm doing these Þlms, IÕm often asked, ÔHow did you get inside a womanÕs head?Õ And I say, ÔI didnÕt have to get inside a womanÕs head. I adapted a book that a woman has written. ThatÕs the womanÕs head.Õ You know? Why do I have to recapture the way she thought when shewrote a book about it? ThatÕs not my job.
Do you try to avoid voiceover in adapting a rst-person narrative, or do you see it as another tool in the toolbox?
ItÕs a tool in the toolbox. If a Þlm hasnÕt turned out well, people will point to the amount of voiceover and say itÕs the oldest fallback in the book. If they love the Þlm, they probably wonÕt even mention it. But itÕs good to make sure your screenplay is structurally sound before you start thinking about any voiceover that might be needed. The voiceover should be a ourish rather than something thatÕs taking any narrative weight.
Do you use the language of the book in writing the dialogue and stage directions, or do you put the whole thing in your own words?
Stage directions, no. Dialogue, not immediately. YouÕre always glad to copy something out, but it doesnÕt work like that most of the time. One of the reasons I do adaptations is that there are scenes where you think, ÔThe dialogue here is a gift,Õ and I get excited about the opportunity to use that. But if youÕre doing your job properly, actually you have very little choice about what language you use. You canÕt write Jane Austen dialogue when someoneÕs had a brief period as a heroin addict. So the language sorts itself out, I think.
Do you keep the book beside you throughout the adaptationprocess, or do you try to internalise it and set it aside somewherealong the way?
I think youÕre always going to need it, even if itÕs just for someoneÕs name or Ð with *Wild* Ð where they were on their journey. For that, I had a map on my pinboard to organise the material. There did come a point with *An Education* where the article was no longer any use to me. And the one IÕve done recently is interesting, because her memoir isnÕt out yet and sheÕs been editing it. I know sheÕs made a lot of changes to the book since I Þrst read it, but IÕve stuck with her original version because I donÕt think whatever sheÕs changed is going to make any difference to me in terms of what I see the essential story being. So I havenÕt had that beside me quite as much. When I Þrst saw the Þlm of High Fidelity, the characterÕs name was Rob Gordon, not Rob Fleming as it is in the novel, and I said to D.V. DeVincentis, who co-wrote it, ÔAs a matter of interest, why did you change it?Õ and hesaid, ÔI couldnÕt Þnd the book.Õ
How much pressure do you feel when adapting a well-known andmuch-loved book, knowing that you wonÕt be able to please everyone with the nished product?
I felt it a little bit with *Brooklyn*. I certainly felt it with *Wild*. That was such a phenomenon in America Ð number one bestseller for quite a while Ð and meant so much to so many women that obviously therewas pressure on me because I was a bloke and I had to honour Cheryl.But whatever youÕve adapted, youÕll meet someone whose favourite part was missing. ThereÕs a bit in *Brooklyn* which is about race, and in quite a few of the interviews I did, people who had read the book would go, ÔYou copped out of the race scene.Õ And we didnÕt, we Þlmed it. But it was a self-contained scene and when youÕre overlength the Þrst thing you do is take out the self-contained scenes, especiallywhen theyÕre tonally jarring. It had absolutely nothing to do with race or controversy or wanting it to be a certain kind of Þlm, it was reallyjust the storytelling logic of it.
Of your produced screenplays, four are non ction Ð includingyour adaptation of your own memoir, Fever Pitch Ð and only one is
ction: *Brooklyn*. Do you prefer adapting non ction?
I havenÕt ever analysed it. Now that you say it, thatÕs probably right. ThereÕs a social history side of me thatÕs awoken by a certain kind of book. I read quite a lot of nonÞction and thereÕs pretty much no nonÞction book that wouldnÕt make a fantastic Þlm, even if itÕs just a three-page section from it. So ideas for movies do come to me more readily from nonÞction.
Do you think that being based on a true story in some way gives aproject more weight?
Not to me. IÕm slightly alarmed by the number of people who say, ÔOh, itÕs based on a true story, isnÕt it?Õ as if that somehow must mean itÕs worth more or is more interesting. Generally, to my way of thinking, it doesnÕt give it any more weight at all.

CASE STUDY: *AN EDUCATION*
UK/US, 2009 ¥ Directed by Lone Scher g ¥ Produced by Finola Dwyer, Amanda Posey ¥ Screenplay by Nick Hornby, based on the memoir by Lynn Barber ¥ Cast: Carey Mulligan (Jenny Mellor), Peter Sarsgaard (David Goldman), Alfred Molina (JackMellor), Rosamund Pike (Helen), Dominic Cooper (Danny), OliviaWilliams (Miss Stubbs), Emma Thompson (Headmistress), Cara Seymour (Marjorie)
Did you learn anything from adapting Fever Pitch which you were able to apply when adapting other peopleÕs memoirs, starting with Lynn BarberÕs?
No [laughs].
Because the stories are so di erent?
Yes. And because the lengths of the books were so different, and the timescales were so different, and both became Þctionalised. So I guess I learned that you could Þctionalise memoir, which was probably a helpful thing to know. To free myself to think, ÔJust because itÕs a memoir, doesnÕt mean I have to stick to it.Õ
As you said, *An Education* was rst published in Granta. If a non ction book is equivalent to a novel, then a magazine article is probably more like a short story. How did the length of the source material a ect the work of adapting it?
*An Education* was really partly an adaptation and partly an originalscreenplay. The six pages which Lynn wrote provided me with a skeleton, but there was no esh on that skeleton. So I had a rather nice time imagining characters more fully and inventing plotdevelopments that Þtted in with them. For example, the Peter Sarsgaard character being a cheat and a liar meant that you could make him cheat and lie about other things. I enjoyed doing all that.
In her introduction to the lm tie-in edition of her expanded memoir, Barber says that the rst draft of your script stuck very closely to her story Ð and even in its nal form, *Wild* is very faithful to Cheryl StrayedÕs story. Is that level of delity always your rst instinct?
ItÕs not so much about Þdelity, itÕs that the shape of the story is usually whatÕs appealed to me. The ending of a book, or the midpoint crisis, has struck a chord in some way Ð and if IÕve gone away from that, itÕs no longer the thing I chose to adapt. With *An Education*, it took a few drafts. The Þrst draft did stick closely to the piece, not just in terms ofnarrative, but in terms of LynnÕs voice. Lynn is a inty woman in her late sixties, and in the Þrst draft Lynn was a inty young woman in her teens, and you think, ÔThis isnÕt going to work, because she knowseverything.Õ So youÕve got to chip all that away otherwise you miss the whole point of the story, which is that sheÕs nave enough to believe this man. But as it went on, it emerged that what I was interested in was suburban alienation and being excited by culture and wanting to get to the big city Ð which is also what Fever Pitch is about, to some extent. So I started to feel quite personally connected to the material, and felt passionately about that on JennyÕs behalf. I knew exactlywhat it was like, that feeling of being alive because youÕre going to the theatre or to concerts or to clubs. But it comes at a cost for her, because sheÕs taking a shortcut that really doesnÕt exist. And sheÕs rescued by education, which is something else I probably believed.
Lynn and her older lover Simon became Jenny and David in the
lm, whereas their friends Danny and Helen retain the names they had in the memoir. What was your thinking there?
As I remember, it was entirely dependent on who was still alive and might recognise themselves and sue us Ð but it also gave me permission to go further away from the facts. I did the same thingwith Fever Pitch, where the lead character became Paul in the Þlm. ItÕs a very simple thing, but the sense of freedom it brings you is much greater than the tiny change youÕve made.
In the lm, JennyÕs mother is portrayed more or less as a housewife. In reality, BarberÕs mother was an English teacher at a girlsÕ school. There is an English teacher in your script, but it isnÕt her. Why did you change and split that character in that way?
My feeling was that having her mother working, and being an Englishteacher, wouldnÕt necessarily inspire her to feel that women could work, because everything her parents did would be dead, as my kids would say. Giving her an English teacher whoÕs funky and inspiringand cares passionately about the arts seemed to me to do her and the script more good. And, of course, it was a time when lots of middle-class women were housewives, and I felt that young women todayought to know that often women did nothing and were very frustrated doing nothing and had to live through their kids. So the Olivia Williams character becomes an important, and very moving, example of something you can do if you go and get your education. I donÕt think youÕd have found that if youÕd used the mother.
The rst half of the script is light and funny in a way that the memoir isnÕt, partly because the stage directions and dialogue are written in your distinctive tone. Were you aware, rst, of superimposing your voice over BarberÕs, and second, of wanting to seduce the reader and viewer with charm and humour in the same way that David does to Jenny?
Things like stage directions are almost nothing to do with me. IÕve got to write them and my voice is unavoidable. I never think, ÔIÕd better try and do these in the voice of the author.Õ In terms of tone, IÕm always looking for funnyÐsad, and there isnÕt that much of it out there. IÕm not very interested in adapting comedy stuff, and thereÕs an awful lot of literary Þction that hasnÕt got any jokes, so if I see an opportunity to create that contrast, thatÕs a good start for me with material. The things I love most do that, where you turn on a sixpenceand suddenly youÕre in different emotional territory. The Þrst half of any Disney movie is light, and then it goes very dark in the second half. When you come out of those Þlms as a kid, you feel like youÕve come a long way and the emotional territory youÕve covered is considerable. ItÕs hard to do in a screenplay, especially over a relativelyshort period of time like 90 minutes, and feel youÕve gone somewhereby the end of the Þlm thatÕs different from the place you were in at the beginning Ð but I think tone is one of the ways you can do that. If thereÕs some kind of emotional complexity in the tone of the Þlm, youdo feel youÕve been further.
ItÕs quite a novelistic screenplay in some ways, as is *Wild*: precisedescriptions of people and places, and of what people are feeling at any given moment, and even authorial comments like, ÔWe know now that her life can never be the same again.Õ
I think it causes me problems with directors. Actually, I think it makes it harder to Þnd directors. YouÕve got to Þnd a director who thinks theyÕve still got something to do, even though that kind of stuff is in there. I donÕt think thereÕs anything I can do about it; itÕs what keeps it interesting for me. The ones that have worked have worked, and the right director has been found. I just feel that when IÕm presenting a screenplay IÕd like it to be read properly, rather than relying on enormous amounts of imagination from the people who are trying to think about it as a Þlm.
Barber describes Simon as Ôrather short, rather ugly, long-faced, splay-footedÕ, which clearly isnÕt Peter Saarsgard, and Jenny certainly seems to fancy David more than Barber says she fancied Simon. Was that designed to make JennyÕs interest in him more plausible and understandable?
I always found LynnÕs description of him rather mystifying. I donÕt know how many 16-year-old girls would look at that character and think, ÔWhoa, yeah, IÕm going to get involved in this!Õ It seemed to add a whole level of complexity to the screenplay that didnÕt get us very far: youÕve got to spend a long time explaining why a short, ugly, splay-footed person has seduced a pretty young girl. So thatÕs the Þrst thing: it just simpliÞed matters. HeÕs got charm and that helps Ð and I donÕt think the Þlm is a lesser thing as a result of that. The second thing is a very basic thing, which is that actors are better looking than ordinary people Ð thatÕs why theyÕre actors. Even if theyÕre really greatactors, theyÕre also good-looking people. So every time you imagine a character being ordinary, or ordinary-ish, the person who ends upplaying that part will be better looking than that, if itÕs a lead. If itÕs not, Þne, but if you want your Þlm funded, and you want to cast a short, ugly person in the lead, itÕs going to take a while, not least because youÕre trying to cast someone who by deÞnition is not veryfamous. I mean, itÕs sort of a joke, isnÕt it? The relatively ordinary guy in Fever Pitch turns out to be played by Colin Firth. The relativelyordinary guy in High Fidelity turns out to be played by John Cusack.ThatÕs all youÕve got to work with, basically.
Similarly, Barber describes herself as having simply Ôhopped inÕ to SimonÕs car when they rst met, whereas Jenny puts her cello in rst and walks along beside DavidÕs car to start with. Again, did you feel that Jenny would otherwise seem too silly and trusting from the point of view of a modern audience?
Yes. If youÕre trying to persuade people that this is a bright, complex young woman and she behaves like an idiot, even though sheÕs only16, then your cause is damaged. The mores of audiences have changed as well. It was interesting talking to women over 40 who saw *An Education*, because they all had an equivalent story Ð ÔHe said, ÓWould I like to get in his car?Ó and I didÕ Ð and I donÕt think youcould show that anymore, not without an explanation. WeÕre so sensitive about the protection of young people now, quite rightly. So we had to be careful with that and Þnd an interesting way of delaying her getting into the car. In fact, she might have played the cello as opposed to the violin because of that scene.
BarberÕs headmistress refused to make her a prefect because she was a ÔtroublemakerÕ, but the lm gives the impression that she was initially a model pupil. Was that to emphasise the negative e ect that David has on Jenny?
It was, and I also wanted her to have this hunger for culture, and itÕs slightly more complicated if the person with the hunger for culture isthe person whoÕs also kicking up hell at school. She wants this stuff, but itÕs coming too slowly and all the people around her are dim, so David becomes the conduit for things she was thirsting after as a smart, positive pupil.
At times, the memoir is more expansive than the lm: Simon took Barber to Amsterdam and Bruges as well as Paris. Did you con ne it for practical reasons of time and budget or, again, for plausibility?
I couldnÕt make head nor tail of any of that in terms of plausibility. You can imagine a huge amount of planning going into one weekend, but the idea that she jumps out of her uniform on Friday, they y off somewhere and she comes back on Sunday Ð I couldnÕt think how I was going to sell that to a contemporary audience.
In fact, the rst big trip she takes with him in the lm isnÕt in the memoir: they go to Oxford, which is a handy way of dramatising the life sheÕs aiming for.
Yes, and of dealing with why her parents were so credulous. HeÕs saying, ÔIÕve got an in with this old college professor, but never mind, next time,Õ and theyÕre going, ÔOh, shit, sheÕs going to miss out on a huge opportunity here.Õ ItÕs hard work setting it up so that theyÕre in a position where they believe it, or theyÕre allowing it to happen, but once I started to see it through it turned out to have a lot of legs.
There are some scenes in the memoir youÕve bumped up and some youÕve downplayed. For example, BarberÕs discovery that Simon was married was less dramatic in real life, whereas her fatherÕs reaction was more dramatic Ð he went outside and kicked SimonÕs car.
That seemed a little absurd in terms of the way the dad had been written up to that point, and in terms of Alfred MolinaÕs performance.
I was worried it would tip over into comedy, when the best way to play it was to show their heartbreak because theyÕve been conned too.
Equally, SimonÕs wife never spoke to Barber in real life Ð she talked to BarberÕs mother, whereas Jenny sees her and talks to her directly.
I wanted Jenny to own what sheÕd done. I wanted her to have a veryhard moment, the sort of thing that helps you grow up quick, and I didnÕt want her to be protected by a parent at that point.
Barber describes how, when all this was over, her parents would simply say to her, ÔYou know best.Õ But, of course, the lm has the cathartic moment when her father apologises outside her bedroom. How do you reconcile the truth of real life, the lack of catharsis, with commercial cinemaÕs hunger for tidy, redemptive endings?
I donÕt see the point of asking people to pay money to go to the cinema if theyÕre left unsatisÞed. *Brooklyn* was an interesting one, because a couple of people said, ÔWhy did you change the ending?Õand we didnÕt change it, we extended it by about two minutes. SheÕs made her decision. ItÕs clear at the end of the book that sheÕs goingback to America. What we did was get her to embrace that decision as a positive thing. But there is a certain type of literary audience that prefers miserable ambiguity. Then lots of people went to see the Þlm, because audiences were coming out in tears, grabbing people and saying, ÔYou have to go and see this Þlm.Õ IÕd rather have the second response, to be honest. I want to make it as emotionally complex as I possibly can, and I donÕt like cheesy redemptive endings that bear no reference to whatÕs gone before, but I donÕt think the things IÕve been involved in have done that, actually. I wouldnÕt say they have happy endings. IÕd say they end with some kind of doubt, or a doubt has been placed in the minds of the audience before that point.
Barber writes, again in her introduction to the lm tie-in edition of the book, that in sticking very closely to her story your rst draft Ôcruelly exposed the fact that it had no proper ending Ð it reached a dramatic climax and then dwindled awayÕ. What would
you say the dramatic climax of her story is, and how did you set about creating your own ending in subsequent drafts Ð and in the
nished lm, where the scripted ending was itself changed?
Several things happened, really. One is that we always presumed we would end with David turning up at Oxford to see Jenny, and it didnÕt work out. We did Þlm it at the end of the shoot, but neither Peter nor Carey had their best day, and the big problem with that ending is thatit was dramatically inert. YouÕre just restating everything thatÕs happened before, so it doesnÕt go anywhere. So it went. Another thing was that I donÕt think any of us expected the scene with Jenny and her father at her bedroom door to be as emotionally powerful as it was. Carey and Fred both killed it. When youÕre looking at it you think, ÔOh, thatÕs probably the dramatic climax of the Þlm, and everythingfrom this point is a coda.Õ To be honest, we cobbled together an ending which we hoped worked because the thing that we hoped was going to be the ending didnÕt work. As I remember it, weÕd been invited to Sundance and we didnÕt have the last scene of the Þlm, and the idea of showing it at Sundance was to try to Þnd an American distributor. So I wrote that Þnal piece of voiceover and Carey came and did it, then we went to Sundance and the Þlm was the subject of a bidding war Ð and Sony, who bought it, literally said, ÔCarey, Best Actress nomination. Nick, Best Adapted Screenplay. And if we work, we can get Best Picture.Õ WeÕve gone from not having an ending to thatin about four or Þve weeks. This is a long way of saying that it must have worked as an ending because those responses wouldnÕt have been possible if it hadnÕt. But it was only through a mixture of bad luck, happenstance and being resourceful with the time and money we had available that we were able to come up with something which felt like an ending. ThatÕs Þlm, isnÕt it? With a novel, if you havenÕt got the right ending, you just go off and rewrite it until you do. With a Þlm, youÕve got a day of reshoots and an actressÕs voice, so you put that in a pot and see what you can do with it. ItÕs not a perfect medium, itÕs a collaborative medium, and you have to alter things as you go. The thing we probably did right was recognising that weÕd be ogging a dead horse to try to Þnd a big, emotional moment after the door scene. ThatÕs when everyone cries. So you make a virtue of that, accept that itÕs your climax, and work out how youÕre going to negotiate the rest of the story without letting people down.

CASE STUDY: *WILD*
US, 2014 ¥ Directed by Jean-Marc Valle ¥ Produced by Reese Witherspoon, Bruna Papandrea, Bill Pohlad ¥ Screenplay by Nick Hornby, based on the book *Wild*: From Lost to Found on the Pacic Crest Trail by Cheryl Strayed ¥ Cast: Reese Witherspoon (Cheryl), Laura Dern (Bobbi), Thomas Sadoski (Paul)
You said earlier that there was no way you were trekking o on some thousand-mile journey before you adapted *Wild*. So what drew you to it as a project?
I read a review of it in the New York Times by Dwight Garner, whose taste I trust, and the review was so amazing that I ordered it from Amazon and didnÕt really read what it was about. I just came to a paragraph where he said, ÔIf you Þnd yourself in tears on the subwayÉÕ and I thought, ÔIÕve got to buy this book.Õ Then, when it turned up, thereÕs a pair of hiking boots on the front cover. I thought,ÔWalking? Nature? Bollocks!Õ And then I opened it and read a bit and thought, ÔOkay, this has got something.Õ This is almost too nerdy to explain, but IÕm a big Bruce Springsteen fan, and to me the whole thing read like the live shows of his 1978 album Darkness on the Edge of Town: kind of *wild* and intense. I thought, ÔI love this book, I want to adapt it and I think I know how to do it,Õ so I called my agent to Þnd out about it and she said, ÔReese Witherspoon owns it.Õ IÕd met Reese during award season for *An Education* and we had a very lovelyand surprising connection Ð she was very warm about my work and particularly loved a short story sheÕd read in a charity anthology Ð and we talked about working together and nothing happened. So I emailedher and said, ÔI love this book,Õ and she said, ÔI wish IÕd known, weÕve just asked someone else to do it.Õ So I was a bit disappointed. Then about a month later she said, ÔWe canÕt get a deal done with this person, will you do it?Õ
In her authorÕs note, Cheryl Strayed writes, ÔThere are no composite characters or events in this book. I occasionally omitted people and events, but only when that omission had no impact on either the veracity or the substance of the story.Õ Did you try to follow the same principles in adapting it?
It got boiled down one stage further, so there were actually composite characters. But yes, pretty much the same principles. When IÕd been given the gig I read the book again and was underlining stuff, and when I looked back at what IÕd underlined I realised I could get a really powerful, dramatic movie out of that stuff without goinganywhere near the trail, because thereÕs so much in that book. It was like, how can I organise this material so that it reects the walk and the backstory? Then the next level was, how can I organise this material so that you get the walk, the backstory and the sense of complete solitude that the walk gave Cheryl? Because what youÕredrawn to in adapting the book is incident, and quite often the incidentinvolves other people on the trail, but thatÕs not the point of the walk. The point of the walk was that she did it on her own, and she was frightened, and there were lots of things to be frightened of. So there was a third level to squash in, which was her silence and solitude and fear.
*Wild* could be called a road movie. Did you have that genre in mind, and did the fact that CherylÕs journey had a timetable and agoal help give the script a tension and a *drive*?
Anything that involves movement and meeting people along the way is a road movie, so I couldnÕt not have thought of that, but I wasnÕt paying particular homage to anything. IÕm also very suspicious of things that have timetables and goals. ItÕs the oldest trope in Þlm. YouÕve got 12 minutes to do this, or youÕve got 12 days to do this. Why have you? Bollocks you have. So I donÕt think that helps unless all the elements are right.
The book starts where the lm starts, with Cheryl on the trail, losing one of her toenails Ð then losing one of her hiking boots, then basically losing her shit. ItÕs an inherently dramatic, cinematic opening.
ThatÕs the bit that got me into it. But when I went to see Reese I spunthe order of it, starting with the walk and spinning backwards into theheart of her crisis, because I didnÕt see how you could start with the toenail scene and then the motherÕs death, which is pretty much where the book starts. If the central dramatic moment happens in the Þrst Þve minutes of the Þlm, youÕre going to struggle to maintain the intensity that the book had. The image I had in my head was peeling an onion. The trail is one layer, divorce is another layer, drugaddiction is another layer, promiscuity is another layer. And then right at the centre of it is this trauma of the motherÕs death.
And it is literally at the centre of it. Chapter 1 of the book, describing the sudden illness and death of CherylÕs mother Bobbi, becomes a series of long ashbacks in the lm, culminating in a scene where she nds herself standing on the trail in the snow with only a watchful fox for company shouting ÔmomÕ over and over. That scene falls exactly in the middle of both the book and your script. ItÕs as if your adaptation is walking on the same path, sometimes behind, sometimes ahead, but always ultimately meeting up.
Her emotional beats are brilliant in the book, but knowing I couldnÕtdo that because of the decision IÕd made about the chronology, I had to Þnd a parallel way. The Þlm is probably more literal-minded, because the fox scene comes closer to the motherÕs death than it does in the book. Given the kind of adaptation I wanted to do, I didnÕt know how much spooky spirituality I could get away with, but I thinkit has a certain weight to it.
One of the few signicant changes to the story Ð and one of the few deviations from CherylÕs strict point of view Ð is when she phones her ex-husband Paul from her motel room in Mojave, something which she had the urge to do in reality at the start of her journey but resisted. Why did you feel it was important to do that and to show Paul in his apartment with a female friend, a scene which Cheryl could have imagined, but wouldnÕt have seen?
The way I chose to structure the script meant that the Þrst few minutes of the Þlm had to do a bit of work in terms of explaining why any woman would do this, and quite a lot of that is conveyed in that scene: a relationshipÕs ended, the guyÕs moved on, thereÕs a great deal of pain involved. Him saying, ÔYou donÕt have to do this,Õ suggests the whimsicality of the project and stops you having to come up with a proper reason, other than her desperation, that makes her go on the walk. I thought, ÔThere wonÕt be many opportunities to switch point of view from this point on, so I should make the most of this one,Õ and that conversation does explain things, even though what they say is quite elliptical.
Her diary, poetry extracts, snatches of songs, are all part of whatshe calls in the book, and you call in the script, the mix-tape radioin her head. You obviously need that because sheÕs so often on her own, but what determined when you used it?
It was another thing I went to Reese with: the idea that there has to bethis jumble. I didnÕt want to overuse it, but we had to Þnd a way of externalising her thoughts without a voiceover: ÔDear Diary, today I went to blah, blah, blah.Õ I donÕt know if anyone noticed how brilliantthe sound design is in the Þlm. They found a level for that hum in her head which is between internal and external. ThereÕs a static-y quality to it which I think is great. Of course, a lot of the time, something she hears draws her back into her memory, so it was choosing the rightpoints for the ashbacks as well.
The lm starts with that moment in the middle of her journey, then ashes back to the beginning of it Ð then, when her journey is under way, ashes back to what motivated her to make it, even ashing back further within those ashbacks. Were your decisions about where to position the ashbacks Ð and what
sounds and images you would use to link past and present Ð instinctive and immediate?
Yes, because we were on the clock. Reese said, ÔI have this window next year and IÕd really like to hit it,Õ and I said, ÔI think I can do that,Õ but I came back in a sort of heightened state, thinking, ÔOkay, this is serious.Õ IÕd never worked with that kind of deadline before Ð usuallythere is no deadline and no one gives a shit: if itÕs not next year, it might be the year after that Ð but this was, ÔIÕm doing this, and IÕm doing this, and IÕd like to do this in-between.Õ I wrote the Þrst page of the screenplay around ten months before they started shooting, so I did think more about structure than I had for previous scripts simply because it was needs must. I needed to know what I was doing before I started.
The lm ends quickly once Cheryl has reached her destination, the Bridge of the Gods. You get to the point, make it, and then get out.
That was also clear to me before I started, and we knew it when we saw the cut of the Þlm. ThatÕs the big moment.
You were known initially as a writer of men Ð Fever Pitch, HighFidelity, About a Boy Ð but your novels since have focused equally on women, and your produced screenplays have all been from a female perspective. What brought about that change?
*An Education* was a big shift in my career and my thinking. The truth is, in the urban world I write about there isnÕt an awful lot to stopwhite men doing what they want to do. ItÕs their internal life that stops them from doing what they want to do, which is pretty much true of most of the male characters in the Þrst three books. Women have proper obstacles, especially if itÕs a period piece. Historically, traditionally, theyÕve not been allowed to do what they want to do, and there have been real barriers to stop them achieving what they want to achieve. So that was one thing: women just seem more dramatically alive to me. The second thing is the unbelievable quality of actress available to you, who donÕt get sent decent parts. When we cast Carey Mulligan, it quickly became apparent that we had the best actress of that age in the world. If it had been about a 25-year-old man, we absolutely would not have got the best actor in the world, because the actor would be getting paid $20 million to put on a superhero suit and would say, ÔI like your little drama, but what can I do?Õ It makes things harder to fund and the Þlms tend to be less commercial, but in terms of the art of it, getting to work with talent ofthat magnitude has got to be a huge *drive* for a screenwriter.


Deborah Moggach
Deborah Moggach was born in 1948 in Bushey, Hertfordshire.
Her screen adaptation credits include: Goggle Eyes (1993, novel by Anne Fine); Love in a Cold Climate (2001, novels The Pursuit of Love and Love in a Cold Climate by Nancy Mitford); *Pride & Prejudice* (2005, novel by Jane Austen); and *The Diary of Anne Frank* (2009).
She has also adapted ve of her 19 novels for lm and television: To Have and To Hold (1986); Seesaw (1998); Close Relations (1998); Final Demand (2003); and Tulip Fever (2017).Her novel These Foolish Things was the basis for The Best Exotic Marigold Hotel (2011).
*Pride & Prejudice* was BAFTA-nominated for Best AdaptedScreenplay.
APPROACHES TO ADAPTATION
Do you prefer to adapt material which chimes with your own work, or material which is completely di erent and gives you a chance to try out new things?
I think I slightly prefer something different. Because IÕve been workingfor so long Ð 40-something years Ð I can get stuck in a groove,particularly a groove of domestic drama, stuff about families and children and so on. If itÕs something completely different, itÕs a bit of a stretch, and I think thatÕs what I need at this stage.
Do you think adaptations involve a completely di erent set of creative gears to original screenplays?
No, I donÕt. I think that when writing scripts one needs completelydifferent gears, and itÕs quite unusual to do both Þction and screenplays Ð though obviously David Nicholls and Nick Hornby andother people do it Ð but I donÕt think adaptation is by its nature that different to making up stories, itÕs just easier. YouÕre moving into a house that someone else has built, and you might shift the furniture around and knock down a wall or two, but theyÕve made the house, theyÕve made the plot, and itÕs much easier rearranging someone elseÕs plot than making up a plot of your own.
Do you always agree a mission statement or direction of travel with whoever has commissioned the adaptation?
Oh, God, no. With *Pride & Prejudice*, for example, I had no mission statement at all. Working Title just said it was time for a movie of it Ð it hadnÕt been done as a Þlm since Aldous Huxley adapted it many years ago Ð and they didnÕt say they wanted a feminist one or a revisionist one or whatever. So I had to make up my own mind.
