Sometimes people do say what they want, and sometimes I agree andsometimes I donÕt, but itÕs their project, theyÕre the ones forking out the money and making the big commitment, so you should be in tune with them before you start. But a surprising amount of times theyhavenÕt got a view; they want you to give your view, which is quitenice in a way.
That is surprising, given how much money they may have spent to option the material.
I know. But so far as outside help goes Ð and obviously itÕs a collaborative process, so itÕs different from writing novels Ð if theyÕreintelligent and theyÕre in tune with the project and youÕre in tune withthem, a good script meeting can be a wonderful thing. ItÕs such fun, because youÕre galloping together. In a bad script meeting, on the other hand, of which IÕve had many, they donÕt get it at all.
Do you usually produce an outline or treatment before you start writing the script?
Yes, which is loathsome. IÕm sure everyone says that.
Almost everyone has said that.
TheyÕre ghastly. I donÕt know how people do it. ThereÕs a certain technique in reducing something to three pages or a page-and-a-half or whatever, and I donÕt have it. I canÕt boil it down. Most plots, if you reduce them to a page-and-a-half, sound banal Ð even Anna Karenina Ð so you Þll it out because itÕs a sales document and you want it to sound interesting. If I do a treatment itÕll be 40 pages, andIÕll put in bits of dialogue as well because I want it to be readable. Andby that time, you might as well write the thing. It hardly takes longer to write a script than to write the treatment. Everyone hates them, basically, but producers have got to have them because they need something to raise the funds off. Maybe if I was very famous my name would be enough, but it certainly isnÕt.
If the author of the source material is living, do you nd it useful to have their input on the script?
ThatÕs jolly interesting. Most of mine have been dead. Jane Austen. Nancy Mitford. And Anne Frank, obviously, unfortunately. But there was a thing called Goggle Eyes that Anne Fine wrote, about a woman whoÕs got children and thereÕs a new man in her life, and the daughterÕs difÞcult relationship with her stepfather. I read the book, and it was so much what was happening in my life that I rang upAnne Fine and said, ÔIs anyone doing this for television?Õ and she said,ÔItÕs been optioned, here are the people who are doing it, will you ringthem up?Õ So my agent rang them up and I got the option and I did it,and that was wonderful because Anne and I got on really well and she loved what I did and it was very encouraging to know she was there. The advantage of adapting my own work, or adapting AnneÕs in that case, is that you have a deeper knowledge of the characters and you can be bolder with them Ð and they can do more surprising and interesting things. If you just read the characters in a book of course you can adapt it, but itÕs really good to talk to the author about them because theyÕll have lived with them.
Is it easier to navigate notes from directors, producers and scripteditors when you have a piece of source material to measure the screenplay against?
Yes.
Have you ever started work on an adaptation and found it harder to adapt than you anticipated?
Moll Flanders. Absolutely impossible. Because Moll Flanders is impossible to understand. SheÕs very contradictory. One minute sheÕs weeping because her child has been taken away, the next minute sheÕs forgotten all about it Ð and Defoe seems to have forgotten all about it, too. So you canÕt ever get a handle on her. ItÕs very picaresque as well: itÕs just one damn thing after another, it doesnÕt actually have a shape.Andrew Davies did it in the end, and he probably did a very good job, but I had to give up. I couldnÕt do it.
Are there any screen adaptations which you think are especially good or you particularly admire?
Robert Altman and Frank BarhydtÕs Short Cuts, from the RaymondCarver short stories. Absolutely brilliant. They made one up and the others they wove together. Harold Pinter: good playwright, greatscreenwriter. His version of The Go-Between. A wonderful book, too. You may be talking about that later: whether itÕs easier to work with a good book, or with total crap.
ThatÕs a really good question, which IÕm now wishing IÕd asked everyone. WhatÕs your answer to it?
My answer to it is that you want a good book. If the characters are cardboard you canÕt put them into drama because theyÕve got no life anyway. But if theyÕre wonderfully observed characters, like a goodbook has, then theyÕre living, breathing human beings. TheyÕll change,because theyÕre going to become creatures of Þlm rather than creaturesof a novel, but they already have a fullness and a life which gives them movement. TheyÕre not mannequins.
ADAPTING FICTION
Some writers try to include as much of the novel as possible by boiling scenes down to their essence. Others are more ruthless in editing it down to a sort of greatest hits, but being true to the spirit of it. Do you favour either of those approaches?
It certainly shouldnÕt be greatest hits, because a screenplay should have a narrative and an integrity of its own. And itÕs not really a question of just boiling stuff down: you may expand stuff, you mayget rid of it, you may reshape it. I think itÕs just a different creature. Another wonderful screenplay is Emma ThompsonÕs for Sense and Sensibility. SheÕs so brilliant. SheÕs got Jane Austen in her bone marrow. And what she did with that was dismember the baby and put it back together again with huge love and reverence. I learned a lot from reading that screenplay.
If a novel has an unusual structure, would you try to reproduce that in adapting it?
I donÕt think you can generalise. YouÕd have to see with each one. There are some novels, like The Virgin Suicides, where you think, ÔNo one can adapt this,Õ because either nothing happens, or itÕs too weird, or itÕs too inconsequential. But a really clever screenwriter will justÔgetÕ it and come at it from some interesting angle: ÔIÕll do it all in voiceover,Õ or, ÔIÕll stick in an alter-ego,Õ or, ÔIÕll turn them into dogs.Õ
Do you try to avoid voiceover in adapting a rst-person narrative, or do you see it as another tool in the toolbox?
Some people say itÕs a sign of weakness and that youÕre bailing yourself out. Other people, including me, think that some of the greatest movies of all time have had voiceover, from All About Eve onwards. You obviously donÕt want voiceover that just describes whatÕs happening, but itÕs interesting to use it if it cuts against whatÕs happening.
Do you keep the novel beside you throughout the adaptationprocess, or do you try to internalise it and set it aside somewherealong the way?
I throw it away, practically. I read it Þrst with my adapterÕs hat on, watching for dramatic scenes, visual scenes Ð maybe underline them, maybe not Ð I do the Þrst draft, which may be very literal, and then Ialmost physically chuck the novel away, because the Þrst draft is whatIÕll then be working from. If you go back to the novel at that stage it will be regressive and will probably hit the cutting-room oor. The sea change, hopefully, will happen after the Þrst draft: each draft will thenfeed off the one before and become more and more of a movie Ð or TV.
How much pressure do you feel when adapting a well-known andmuch-loved novel, knowing that you wonÕt be able to please everyone with the nished product?
Well, I had two of the most-loved books of all time: Pride and Prejudice and *The Diary of Anne Frank*. ItÕs a huge challenge and responsibility, and people are very proprietorial. So you do feel the weight of peopleÕs expectations, and you also feel the weight of all theprevious adaptations. With Pride and Prejudice, youÕve got the wet shirt scene, which set female bosoms heaving the world over. And with*The Diary of Anne Frank*, there were also other adaptations, mostly rather sugary.
If someone wants to option a novel of yours, how do you decide whether to adapt it yourself Ð and if someone else adapts it, how involved would you be in the process?
I try to do it myself. ItÕs poacher turned gamekeeper, but the feeling of someone else adapting your work is like being out of your house while your underwear drawer is being ried through Ð very intrusive and intimate. I donÕt like it at all. I also think I could do a better job! IÕm very ruthless with my own work, because I donÕt want to be thought of as precious and retentive, and I think I have the necessary distance to treat it as drama. But I may be wrong and they may not want me, and in each case where thatÕs happened I havenÕt been involved at all because they havenÕt wanted me to be. IÕd probably like to be, but someone else would come in and IÕm out of it. And I really hate that.
What inspires you to swap the authorial autonomy of novels for the collaborative constraints of lm and TV?
Collaborative is the word. Writing a novel is very solitary, and IÕm rather sociable Ð and, as I said, I love a good script meeting. I love talking about work, and if you write a novel youÕre completely silent for a year or two years or three years. You canÕt talk to anyone about it, because if you let the sunlight into the room the pictures fade. But with a screenplay, because itÕs collaborative, youÕve got these other voices in your head Ð the director and the producer and the scripteditor Ð and if theyÕre in tune with you, and you with them, itÕs reallyinteresting. So I like that. It suits my personality.

ADAPTING NONFICTION
When adapting a book, ction or non ction, do you do research beyond the book, or do you rely on the authorÕs research?
I do some research beyond the book, but IÕm very lazy so I try to get a nice researcher to do it. But IÕve researched the Crimean War and World War II and all sort of things.
How much responsibility do you feel towards historical accuracy when dramatising the lives of real characters?
In the case of Anne Frank there was a huge responsibility, because theywere not only people who really lived but people who died horribly, sothe responsibility was to respect that, but not to sanctify them. Sanctifying them turns them into creatures of history and I wanted to keep them as human beings. They werenÕt nicer or nastier than anyone else; they just happened to die in a horrible way. Young girls and boys wouldnÕt be able to identify with Anne Frank if she was simply a goody-goody. In fact, she was a typical stroppy teenager: Þghting with her mother and obsessed with boys and all that. So that was a tightrope I had to tread: bringing to life people who actually existed and giving them a voice again without making them too cloying. In the diary, Anne is very beady and listens to people and you can use a lot of that, but some of it I had to make up. There were Þve episodes and I had to shape each episode, and that would mean creating stuffand having it pay off by the end, putting words into peopleÕs mouths and making up conversations. That moral responsibility towards realpeople who lived and died was very interesting to me Ð and veryworrying as well. If you were adapting another nonÞction book youwould to some extent have the same responsibility, but with Anne Frank it was particularly acute.
Have you ever encountered events in a non ction book so strange that you couldnÕt use them in the adaptation because audiences wouldnÕt believe they were real?
All the time. As we know, thereÕs nothing stranger than life Ð but itÕs not as shapely as Þction, so you need Þction to shape it up.
How do you set about nding the voices of real characters, especially if there is little or no written or audio-visual research material in their own words?
I was doing a script about this chap Bernie Jordan, who escaped from his old peopleÕs home to join the D-Day celebrations. So heÕs a real person Ð he only died a couple of years ago Ð and I had to get a handle on him. But when I interviewed the residents of the home, no one said anything interesting about him at all, except that he liked Hobnob biscuits and wore two watches. So I put that in. But actually, in the end, we got rid of the two watches because it just made him seem odd. HeÕs wearing two watches because heÕs obsessed with time, as he was in charge of a vessel that let out the troops on to the beaches and he was worried that the time wouldnÕt be right Ð or something. It justdidnÕt work. And the biscuits I put in, but they seemed a bit feeble. So I had to get a handle on him in another way Ð in other words, I had to create him. His wife was also in the old peopleÕs home with him, and I didnÕt know anything about her attitude towards him, and whether they were in cahoots that he was stealing away. She was very passive in the Þrst draft and people said, ÔSheÕs not interesting enough. WhatÕsher story?Õ So I had to go back and make up a story, but by this time I was losing the real people. In my version, she used to have a hairdressing salon, and her husband was actually traumatised by the war but he kept this down in an English way, and there will be a young helper at this place and the wife will tell her this. So I started building them up into my creatures, which is what you have to do, and they were pretty well unrecognisable by the end. And then youhave the actors come in and theyÕll change it anyway.
Do you think that being based on a true story in some way gives aproject more weight?
Yes.

CASE STUDY: *PRIDE & PREJUDICE*
UK/US/France, 2005 ¥ Directed by Joe Wright ¥ Produced byTim Bevan, Eric Fellner, Paul Webster ¥ Screenplay by Deborah Moggach, based on the novel by Jane Austen ¥ Cast: Keira Knightley (Elizabeth Bennet), Matthew Macfadyen (Mr Darcy), Brenda Blethyn (Mrs Bennet), Donald Sutherland (Mr Bennet), Tom Hollander (Mr Collins), Rosamund Pike (Jane Bennet), JenaMalone (Lydia Bennet), Carey Mulligan (Kitty Bennet), Talulah Riley (Mary Bennet), Simon Woods (Mr Bingley), Kelly Reilly (Caroline Bingley), Rupert Friend (Mr Wickham), Judi Dench (Lady Catherine de Bourgh)
What attracted you to adapting Pride and Prejudice, and what did you think you could bring to it?
I wanted to adapt it because I love it Ð though I donÕt love it as much as some people do Ð and I thought I could bring a modern sensibility to it. ItÕs reinvented for every generation because itÕs so loved, and I thought I could give it some freshness and wit. IÕd just adapted Nancy Mitford and sheÕs got a lot in common with Jane Austen Ð funny, lots of girls, countryside, eccentric father, animals and forensic social comedy Ð so I thought I could bring that. But what I most wanted to do with it was to show that it all really mattered. I didnÕt want it to be a frothy comedy at all. I wanted people who watched it to realise thatthis was pre-education, pre-Welfare State, and if one of those girlsdidnÕt marry well Ð because the estate was entailed Ð then theyÕd be out in the mud. I called it Ôthe muddy-hem versionÕ. I wanted them tobe in the same dresses day after day. I wanted them to be really young with red noses and no make-up. I wanted us to feel that outside their admittedly quite nice country home was real deprivation, and that those Þve young girls were going to go into a life of dependence and poverty. Maybe theyÕd become a governess if they were lucky;otherwise theyÕd be making jam all their lives. So I wanted to make it really matter that their mother had to marry one of them off Ð sheÕs not just an airhead, sheÕs actually working very hard for her girlsbecause she knows that their period of bloom is so short Ð and I wanted to get inside Elizabeth BennetÕs head because sheÕs reallyinteresting. ThatÕs why everyone loves the book: because it shows you donÕt have to be gorgeous to get the richest, most desirable man in Britain, you can just be funny and intelligent and true to yourself, andthat gives hope to women everywhere.
You slightly lose the potency of that when you cast an attractive actor like Keira Knightley.
I know. ThatÕs another question. Casting just presumes that the heroine is going to be beautiful. But sheÕs got very modern good looks, and Rosamund Pike, who played Jane Bennet, was much more the Jane Austen model of reÞned beauty. Anyway, those were some of my thoughts. I also thought that the way to get to grips with Mr Darcy, who IÕd always disliked, was that heÕs really homesick. HeÕs got this huge place and his darling sister, but heÕs always being hauled around to these house parties like a prize bull, having vapid girls thrust at himby their desperate parents. So heÕs not just a total snob. I also wanted to make the Bennet family as hoydenish and Hogarthian and old-fashioned as possible, because then when we move into the HighRegency world of NetherÞeld, the world of Mr Darcy and Mr Bingley, we too would feel a bit inhibited. I wanted us to feel that the Bennets, despite their big house and their carriage, could actually be looked down on by the real posh people.
The novel could be described as a romcom. Did you ever think of it in terms of genre?
No. I tried to cut against that. But it is the archetypal romcom, the template for all that followed. ThereÕs Jane Austen, a virgin, never really been in love, and she understood the friction thatÕs the basis of sexual attraction better than anyone. Two people think they loathe each other but really misunderstand each other; theyÕre just about to get it together when a whole lot of obstacles are thrown in their way; and we know long before they know that theyÕre madly in love and simply in *denial*. ItÕs the perfect romcom Ð and the perfect plot. The plot of Pride and Prejudice is a beautiful thing, beautifully structured.I did have to mess about with it, but not a great deal.
Lizzie and DarcyÕs character arcs are perfect for mainstream cinema, but most of the other characters Ð Mr and Mrs Bennet, Lydia and Wickham Ð donÕt change at all during the story. Did you try to give any of them more of an arc?
Not really. I concentrated entirely on Lizzie and her interior life Ð though a screenplay canÕt do that in the same way a novel can. A novel can do hopes, dreams, fantasies, whatever. A screenplay canÕt. A screenplay is like a verb and a novel is like a noun, and the verb of the screenplay has to push the story along. That interior life you will onlyreally get through watching the actors. In the scene where Lizzie bumps into Darcy at Pemberley near the end, the screenplay is completely banal. He says, ÔFancy seeing you here,Õ and she says, ÔIÕm on holiday with my aunt and uncle,Õ and he says, ÔAre you having a pleasant trip?Õ and she says, ÔYes, we went to Matlock yesterday.Õ ItÕs so boring you can barely say it, but what their faces are saying is, ÔI completely misjudged you. I love you so much I can hardly breathe. I could be living in this house with you if I hadnÕt been so blinkered and snobbish and IÕm so unhappy I want to die.Õ ThatÕs what a good screen actor is paid for: the changing weather on their face tells you their interior life.
Were there any scenes or characters which you decided to cut right away, and were there any which you had to cut reluctantly?
I wasnÕt reluctant to cut any of them. I cut any scene that Lizzie Bennet wasnÕt in, so thereÕs no Jane in London, thereÕs no Lydia in London. It was all through LizzieÕs point of view.
The novel proceeds at a fairly steady pace. The lm ebbs and
ows more, from busy, bustling social scenes to moments of quiet, private reection. Did you bring that to it, or did it emerge in the shoot and the edit?
I did several drafts, then other people came on Ð Emma Thompson did some, uncredited Ð then they called me back in and I did some more work on it. So I wasnÕt terribly involved in the shoot. But I am conscious when IÕm writing a screenplay, whether itÕs an adaptation or an original, of trying to bring a rhythm to it, of trying to get light andshade and fast and slow and funny and serious. A screenplay shouldhave a rhythm to it. It should also be a fun document to read. People love Andrew DaviesÕs scripts because heÕll write something like, in Vanity Fair, ÔBecky thinks, Òfuck itÓ.Õ And Callie KhouriÕs script for Thelma & Louise, writing about Geena DavisÕs husband: ÔPolyester was made for this man.Õ ThatÕs a good screenplay. The actors get it. The people buying it get it. ItÕs totally on the button for what that character is. And it makes you laugh. ThatÕs going slightly off yourquestion, but anyway.
Although the dialogue has been substantially trimmed, the wordsthat the characters do say mostly come from the novel, with all the elegance and oridity of the period. Were you ever tempted to try a more modern style of dialogue?
No. ThereÕs only one line I wrote that wasnÕt somewhere in the book, which is when Mr Darcy says to LizzieÕs uncle at Pemberley, ÔThe Þsh in the lake have been left alone for too long.Õ I made that up.Otherwise I just pulled a comb through Jane AustenÕs wonderful dialogue.
The lm has a sunny, leafy, windy feel Ð much more outdoorsy than the novel, which seems more at home in houses than landscapes. Did you deliberately look to open it out in that way?
No, I mostly put scenes indoors because I know theyÕre cheaper and wonÕt depend on the weather. Sometimes I would put scenes outdoors, but I do think about what would be easier to shoot. So they did that, and found all those lovely landscapes. They were going to have a tavern scene in which I was going to be an extra, but they found thiswonderful wood of gnarled oaks and decided to put the scene there. I was a bit disappointed by that, because I like being in my own work!
The novel opens with a static dialogue scene between Mr and Mrs Bennet. The lm opens in a whirl of action and motion, introducing the entire Bennet family at their most typical, and setting up the equally lively dance scene that follows. Were you keen to get things moving and keep them moving?
The Þrst opening I had was of NetherÞeld coming to life and all the dust sheets being pulled off, but of course itÕs not about a house, itÕs about people, so they didnÕt like that. Then I had Lizzie walking along a wall reading a book and going into her house, and they ran with that. The interesting thing about screenplays, which IÕm still not sure I manage to do, is that you want to suggest how scenes might look or be shot, but you donÕt want to lay down the law, so you just have to hint at it. ItÕs quite an art, that. If itÕs too technical it feels too bossy, telling people their jobs Ð and again, it makes the screenplay less fun to read.
The dance scene has its own three-act structure: from the entrance of Darcy and Bingley, to DarcyÕs dismissal of Lizzie, to LizzieÕs riposte and exit. Do you consciously structure all scenes like that, or just the larger set-pieces?
The larger set-pieces are awfully difÞcult to write, because you donÕt know whether to do a great list of characters at the beginning, or feed them into the scene, or introduce them when they speak. I still donÕt know when to put their names in capitals; I can never understand the logic of that. I just Þnd them really difÞcult. I donÕt think that was consciously a three-act thing, but youÕre always trying to shapeeverything, and that scene would need a beginning, a middle and an end. But Jane Austen does a lot of the shaping for you because sheÕs so clever.
The implication in the novel is that Mr Bennet made a mistake in marrying Mrs Bennet, and therefore leaving the raising of their daughters to her was a dereliction of duty. But in the lm he comes across as an a ectionate, amused, comical character. Was that deliberate?
Yes, but thatÕs also the performance of Donald Sutherland, who was marvellous, and what he brought to it was much tenderer. He Þnds his wife attractive, and thereÕs a scene where theyÕre on the bed and it looks as though theyÕre about to have sex, and I think it makes them more human Ð and we respect him, and indeed her, more. SheÕs still a bit silly and he still retreats to his study Ð and of course we have to have his closeness to Lizzie because sheÕs so bright Ð but heÕs not an intelligent man who looks a fool for marrying someone ghastly.
The UK ending Ð Mr Bennet sitting in his study, laughing and crying at LizzieÕs engagement to Darcy Ð is less romantic but more a ecting than the US ending: Lizzie and Darcy exchanging endearments and kisses on the terrace at Pemberley. Which do you prefer?
ThatÕs jolly interesting. I didnÕt want to have a big wedding because itÕs too corny and triumphalist, so I wrote an ending where the wedding is going on, but theyÕve stolen away at night and theyÕrelooking down the hill at the lights and they have a little conversation. But they didnÕt like that, and it might have been Emma Thompsonwho wrote a really lovely ending: itÕs the morning after the wedding, theyÕre still in their night-clothes, and they steal away and he sayssomething like, ÔHow do you feel this morning, my dear?Õ and she says, ÔOh, please donÕt call me that, my father calls my mother that when heÕs cross with her,Õ and he says, ÔWhat shall I call you, then?Õ and they have a very tender exchange, and the dewy morning echoes the beginning of the Þlm. Now for some reason they shot that at night, which didnÕt make sense at all. You couldnÕt work out whether theyÕd spent the night together, which is very important, and all thoselights made it look very grand and not informal. It just didnÕt work. So for the English version they cut it Ð and ended, I think, rather well.
I think that ending is delightful. ItÕs almost anti-climactic Ð except itÕs not, because itÕs so emotional.
Exactly. ItÕs heavenly. I think lots of Þlms should have the last scene cut! But Americans have a sweeter tooth, allegedly, so they stuck the other ending on for them.

CASE STUDY: *THE DIARY OF ANNE FRANK*
UK, 2009 ¥ Directed by Jon Jones ¥ Produced by Elinor Day ¥ Screenplay by Deborah Moggach, based on the book The Diary of a Young Girl by Anne Frank ¥ Cast: Ellie Kendrick (AnneFrank), Iain Glen (Otto Frank), Tamsin Greig (Edith Frank),Felicity Jones (Margot Frank), Geoffrey Breton (Peter Van Daan),Ron Cook (Hermann Van Daan), Lesley Sharp (Petronella Van Daan), Nicholas Farrell (Albert Dussel), Kate Asheld (MiepGies)
*The Diary of Anne Frank* aired on TV in ve 25-minute episodes, which is a relatively unusual format for drama. Was that your decision, or the BBCÕs?
It was the BBCÕs decision, because they wanted an early evening drama to get families to watch it Ð because it was such an importantsubject. They also wanted us to follow it over one week so weÕre involved very intimately with the story. ItÕs not an episode, a gap of a week, then another episode Ð weÕre living it with them. We all know what happened at the end, but weÕre still rooting for them.
The diary is inevitably episodic and discursive. How did you set about shaping it into a dramatic ve-part narrative?
Each episode had its theme and its arc. The Þrst one was the Franks going into the annex and the arrival of the Van Daans. One was the Franks falling out with the Van Daans. One was about religion Ð Anne losing her faith. One was AnneÕs romance with Peter. And then in the Þnal one, we built up towards intimations of people knowing they were there. And all the way through I had stories going on among thegrown-ups that Anne, however beady she was, didnÕt always clock. So you have the rather reÞned Franks and the more earthy, less sophisticated Van Daans, who in some ways were more fun than the Franks Ð certainly more than Mrs Frank, who was suffering hugely, and AnneÕs sister Margot, who was frozen with misery and subsumedherself in her studies. And you have Mr Dussel, who Anne shared thatlittle room with and who was incredibly homesick and lonely, which issomething Anne didnÕt get at all. Families were nearly always split up, but the Franks and the Van Daans were together there, which Mr Frank was very optimistic about, and I wanted to show that Mr Dussel was the only one who didnÕt have his loved ones with him.
So each episode had a theme, each episode had an arc, each episode ended on a cliffhanger. I also wanted to bring in different threats all the way through, because there were so many ways they could have been discovered: the threat when the lavatory gets blocked that the plumber will come upstairs; the threat when the building is sold that the new owners will come in; the threat when Anne falls for Peter that she will get pregnant Ð because she very nearly slept with him, she was very highly sexed for her age. And I needed to darken it towards the end, when they all start realising, in their different ways, that this is likely to be it for them Ð although always with humour, because the humour is heartbreaking, and also they werenÕt just sitting there terriÞed all the time, they were like normal people quarrelling and so on. So in each one we had a break-up and then we had them making up in some way Ð and at the end Anne makes her peace with Mr Dussel when her hands are trembling with fear and he helps her with her sandal strap.
The initial antagonism between Anne and Peter is less pronounced in the diary. Was that part of a strategy to enhance the con ict in the adaptation?
I manipulated their romance quite a lot, because I had to make sense of the fact that in the diary Anne picks Peter up and falls for him quiteheavily, and then after a while doesnÕt mention him anymore. My take on it is that although she despised him for being dull and not veryclever, she realised that he was as good as she was going to get. ItÕs a horrible thought, but I think she saw that sheÕd better go for it, so she cobbled together this love affair with him. Then when the Dutch government in exile issued an edict, which they heard on the radio, that everyone in Holland should keep writing diaries and letters so that the world would know about their sufferings under the Nazi occupation Ð and her father looks at her and says, ÔHeÕs talking to you,Õ takes his stuff out of his briefcase and gives it to her for her diaries Ð that turned Anne into a writer and Peter became irrelevant. We talked to Miep, the secretary of Mr Frank who very courageously used to come and deliver stuff to them, and she said she once came in when Anne was writing and when Anne looked up she didnÕt recognise her. Her face was completely different, she was so absorbed in this diary. She wrote like a fury because she thought they might die,and she rewrote lots of it again and again.
One of the most striking aspects of the diary is the extent to which Anne, despite the extraordinary circumstances, remains what you described as Ôa typical stroppy teenagerÕ. You seem to have embraced that.
I embraced it because, as I said, I wanted teenage girls to sympathisewith her and not consign her to history. I wanted them to think, ÔWhatmust that be like?Õ and, ÔHow would I react?Õ I wanted her to be a real person. We went to visit her cousin, Buddy, and watched some of it with him, and he said it was the most truthful portrayal of her Ð because he remembered her very well. I watched other versions and she was this cheeky, mischievous girl, and it was just ghastly. The word ÔteenagerÕ, I think, was only invented in the 1940s, and she was a modern teenager Ð and I wanted to show that.
Given the rst-person nature of the source material, was it a given that you would use voiceover?
SheÕd have to have a voiceover, because there are so many layers.YouÕve got the grown-upsÕ stories. YouÕve got AnneÕs thoughts. And youÕve got what Anne says, which is sometimes different from her thoughts. YouÕve got a real stew of human emotions there. And I found it easy to write her voiceover because I felt very in tune with her
Ð because I could remember what it was like being a teenager.
Apart from some brief scenes at the start, showing the FranksÕ
ight from their house, the whole of the series takes place in the annex. Did you ever consider opening it out?
No. I wanted it to be claustrophobic. They reconstructed the annex inch for inch. The camera crew was stuck in there with the actors and everyone was sweating like pigs and tripping over wires. We wanted it to feel like that. ThereÕs no point in opening it out. The diary is aboutwhat happens in the annex, and if you open it out you dissipate that Ð and anyway, there was enough going on with the people in that annex for something ten times as long. We didnÕt extend it, either. Some adaptations take her to the camps, but of course the diary stops whentheyÕre discovered. I actually stole the end from another adaptation Ð where theyÕre all coming down the stairs one by one and we freeze-frame and it says what happens to them Ð because itÕs such an emotional moment to have this bald little caption. ÔDied of typhusÕ. Itbreaks your heart.
The last line of her voiceover is a phrase from towards the end ofthe diary: ÔI still believe, in spite of everything, that people are truly good at heart.Õ Was that the underlying theme of the piece for you?
No, it wasnÕt. I think my underlying theme was the resilience of the human spirit. ItÕs hard to talk about people being good at heart when the Nazis are murdering six million people. That was AnneÕs thought,it wasnÕt mine Ð but she did mature into an extraordinarily thoughtful young woman. That was also a theme: her maturing. She starts as this quite manipulative, difÞcult young girl, and she grows into someone much wiser and bigger-hearted. It was two years they were there Ð formative years for a young girl Ð and she was going through greatadventures of the spirit and growing up. So although the others donÕtchange that much, Anne does, I think.


David Nicholls
David Nicholls was born in 1966 in Eastleigh, Hampshire.
His screen adaptation credits include: Sympatico (1999, play by Sam Shepard); Much Ado About Nothing (2005, after Shakespeare); *And When Did You Last See Your Father?* (2007,book by Blake Morrison); Tess of the DÕUrbervilles (2008, novel by Thomas Hardy); *Great Expectations* (2012, novel by Charles Dickens); Far from the Madding Crowd (2015, novel by Thomas Hardy); *Patrick Melrose* (2018, novels by Edward St Aubyn);and dramatisations of his own novels Starter for 10 (2006), One Day (2011) and Us (2020).
He wrote the BBC drama The 7.39 (2013), created and wrote the ITV and BBC series I Saw You and Rescue Me (both 2002) and contributed to the third series of Cold Feet (1999). His most recent novel is Sweet Sorrow (2019).
*Patrick Melrose* won the 2019 BAFTA Television Craft Award for Best Writer: Drama.
APPROACHES TO ADAPTATION
Do you prefer to adapt material which chimes with your own work, or material which is completely di erent and gives you a chance to try out new things?
The latter, I think. ItÕs changed, because my own work has changed.When I Þrst started writing, my own material was broadly romantic comedy and relationship drama, and I got sent a lot of books in that vein to adapt. It was easy to say no, even when I could see their commercial appeal, because itÕs less interesting to me if thereÕs an overlap with material that I want to deal with in my own way. What I like about adaptation is that it coaxes me into writing about thingsIÕm not necessarily conÞdent enough to write about in an originalform. To begin with, I was quite scared of original material that was outside my usual style. I would have been scared of the melodrama ofTess of the DÕUrbervilles, which was the Þrst thing I adapted, or the darkness of *Patrick Melrose*, or the emotional rawness of *And When Did You Last See Your Father?* Material thatÕs different from mine serves as a sort of scaffolding which has elements of my own work, but gives me some parameters. But it would feel a little pointless to adapt a book that was too similar in style to, say, Starter for Ten or One Day.
Do you think adaptations involve a completely di erent set of creative gears to original screenplays?
Yes. I can adapt while IÕm writing original work, but I canÕt write two original works at the same time. I wouldnÕt be able to hold two different sets of original voices and situations in my head. I get veryemotionally engaged writing original Þction, because IÕm drawing on my own concerns and preoccupations. Even though my books arenÕt directly autobiographical, theyÕve all originated in an experience IÕve had, or a set of emotions IÕve gone through. With adaptation thereÕsusually an element of that, something in the source material which IÕveresponded to, but the story IÕm telling isnÕt my own so I feel a little more distance. ItÕs much more of an editing job, a technical, structural process.
Do you always agree a mission statement or direction of travel with whoever has commissioned the adaptation?
I think so. I mean, thereÕs always a pitching element involved. When I took on *Patrick Melrose*, I knew there were other writers in the frame, and I had to go and meet the producers and say what I loved about the books, and what I wanted to keep and take out, and how I might augment that. Likewise, I remember my pitch for Far from the Madding Crowd, and how it would differ from the John Schlesinger/Frederic Raphael version. That pitch doesnÕt necessarily come through in the Þnished product, you donÕt always quite hit it, but the ones where my vision of the material has matched the Þnal Þlm are the projects that IÕm happiest with. I used to work as a scripteditor before I was a writer, so IÕve been at the other end of it and IÕm able to speak that language. ThereÕs a certain amount of smoke and mirrors in pitching, because you often wonÕt Þnd out what the source material is really about until youÕve spent some time with it.
Do you usually produce an outline or treatment before you start writing the script?
No, I donÕt, but thatÕs probably to do with having some experience in all Þelds of writing. When I started out as a novelist and as an originalscreenwriter, I did do them and got notes on them and went back and did them again. Now, once IÕve made the pitch, IÕll go my own way. I think IÕm quite a safe pair of hands, in that I do what I say and wonÕtsubmit something crazy. I wonÕt suddenly change the ending withouttelling anyone, IÕll talk my choices through with the script editors andproducers and so on. So apart from verbally stating my intentions, IÕm allowed to do what I want. The exception to that was the latter episodes of *Patrick Melrose*. As the books go on, they become less and less story-*drive*n and more like montages of memories. ThereÕs nothingyouÕd call a plot in the last book, really. So with those there was a certain amount of planning and cards on the wall and storybreakdowns, but thatÕs the least story-led material IÕve ever worked on. With everything else thereÕs been a great deal of discussion, but not a lot of pre-planning, except in my head.
If the author of the source material is living, do you nd it useful to have their input on the script?
ThatÕs a really interesting one. No screenwriter thinks, ÔI wish I was getting more notes.Õ So I would expect them to have input, I justwouldnÕt crave it coming directly to me. IÕd want them to see everydraft and feel free to give notes, but IÕd also want those notes to be put into a pot and mediated. What youÕre doing is an act of vandalism. YouÕre chopping things out and conating characters, andundoubtedly theyÕre going to read it and go, ÔThatÕs not what I had in mind.Õ On *Patrick Melrose*, I had a meeting with Edward St Aubyn,whoÕs a writer I massively admired and, as a different kind of novelist to me, was rather intimidated by. But heÕs a very charming man and we had a lovely lunch; I asked him a certain number of questions and at the end he said, ÔWell, we can either talk every day, or we need never talk again. ItÕs up to you.Õ He completely understood either approach. If youÕre reading the books Ð and this applies to Blake MorrisonÕs book as well Ð youÕll inevitably think, ÔWe really need this scene and it isnÕt in the book so IÕm going to have to invent it,Õ and the most useful question to ask the author at that point is, ÔWhat reallyhappened?Õ But in a way thatÕs also the least useful question, because theyÕve already given their experiences a shape which wasnÕt necessarily there in real life, so to ask them ÔWhat really happened?Õ isa step backwards. It ruins the shape.
Is it easier to navigate notes from directors, producers and scripteditors when you have a piece of source material to measure the screenplay against?
I have a rule that no one is allowed to say, ÔBut in the bookÉÕ, because adaptation means change. And of course itÕs a rule thatÕs constantly broken and so not really a rule at all, but if you just go through and highlight the dialogue and copy the story, itÕs probably not going to work. You all have to be in agreement about what youthink is important in the book, but the success of an adaptation is notnecessarily how much of the book you manage to cram in, so there arelimits to the extent you can use the book as evidence for your point of view. IÕve been on productions where we were not allowed to talk about the book, and IÕve been on productions where the book has been on set and people pick it up and ick through it and refer to it. IÕm not crazy about them doing that, but itÕs okay if the adaptation isbroadly faithful and weÕre not trying to do something different with it.I do get a little prickly if an actor says they preferred something in the book, because theyÕre usually, quite naturally, looking at their own scene, theyÕre not taking into account, say, the directorÕs view or the style of the production. You might adapt Dickens or Hardy and want the dialogue to feel very up-to-date and real, and if an individual actor goes back to the book and likes the more verbose, old-fashioned version of a line, that might be more fun for them to say, but it mightnot Þt in with the overall tone. So I am wary of the book being used as a weapon.
Have you ever started work on an adaptation and found it harder to adapt than you anticipated?
The hardest was *Patrick Melrose*, but I knew that was going to be a tough one. IÕve had the opposite experience, which is not that itÕs easier, but that it falls naturally into a certain structure. On Tess, for instance, we were doing four episodes and the book fell neatly into four narrative chunks with strong cliffhangers. Hardy actuallyoriginated the term ÔcliffhangerÕ, and books written for serialisation adapt particularly well to serial television. Weirdly, I found my own book, Starter for Ten, much harder than expected. It was inspired by a lot of Þlms, and it had a three-act structure and a protagonist with wants and needs and various obstacles along the way, so I thought it would just be a case of picking the best scenes Ð but it was extremelyhard to do that because of the loss of the narratorÕs voice. Things that were absolute highlights on the page didnÕt work without the irony of the narratorÕs point of view, so it was difÞcult to Þnd the comedy in it.Actually, I think the hardest things IÕve had to adapt have been writtenin Þrst-person voices Ð or what we call an indirect third person, like the Melrose books, where itÕs technically in the third person, but very much from a particular characterÕs point of view Ð because youÕreconstantly looking for ways to put thoughts into dialogue without slathering it in voiceover.
Have you ever been o ered material to adapt which you felt couldnÕt, or shouldnÕt, be adapted?
Yes, I have, and IÕve often read books that IÕve loved and thought,ÔThis is unadaptable.Õ IÕve adapted several of my top-ten favourite books, but there are some in there that I wouldnÕt touch. I wouldnÕt touch anything by J.D. Salinger. IÕd have been very wary of The Great Gatsby. IÕve been approached more than once about Ford Madox FordÕs The Good Soldier, and I think thatÕs unadaptable unless you throw away the point of the book, which is that youÕre not sure who the narrator is and what really happened. Books with unreliable narrators, books which rely on ambiguity, books where thereÕs no objective truth, books where thereÕs very little narrative Ð so a lot of modernist Þction Ð are all very hard to adapt. But IÕve been proved wrong many times. You think something can never be done, then someone does it really well.
Are there any screen adaptations which you think are especially good or you particularly admire?
There are lots. All the PresidentÕs Men, by William Goldman, is a wonderful adaptation, probably one of the best, because it approaches Watergate in an entirely unexpected way. The Wings of the Dove, byHossein Amini, is also a fantastic adaptation, of a novel IÕd be veryscared to adapt. I thought it was amazing that someone could deal with that material in that tone of voice. There are also celebrated adaptations that IÕm not crazy about. IÕm not crazy about the David Lean versions of Dickens, even though IÕm absolutely sure his version of *Great Expectations* is better than ours. I love David Lean, but I donÕt love those.

ADAPTING FICTION
Some writers try to include as much of the novel as possible by boiling scenes down to their essence. Others are more ruthless in editing it down to a sort of greatest hits, but being true to the spirit of it. Do you favour either of those approaches?
It depends on the project. With *Patrick Melrose*, because we were doing 800 pages of Þction in Þve hours, it clearly wasnÕt going to be a distillation of everything that happens. If you want to put things in because theyÕre great incidents or great observations or great lines of dialogue, itÕs very unlikely theyÕll be essential to telling the story. So it was about selecting material and discarding upwards of 50 per cent ofthe action. The Þrst three episodes are pretty faithful to the books, but in the last two there are great chunks missing and a story is teased outthat wasnÕt necessarily so prominent on the page. Other times, because IÕve loved the material so much, IÕve tried to put it all in. I did that with *Great Expectations* and it was probably a mistake: I think people who have taken bigger liberties with that book have had a more successful time. ThereÕs a massive storytelling problem in the book, which is that a lot of it has happened in the past. Now you could go into ashback and dramatise it, but thereÕs something weird about that with Dickens. Flashbacks are part of a cinematic language that sometimes feels a little strange in a nineteenth-century story. So the other option is tell the backstory in dialogue Ð monologue, most likely
Ð and it starts to look like a great big slab of exposition. David Lean was much more ruthless in simplifying the plot, but I think the plot of*Great Expectations* is fantastic, so I kept a lot in. The things I chose to take out, though, I think were the right choices: a lot of the comic set pieces got thrown away because theyÕre primarily about the tone of DickensÕ prose, the wry, observational voice, and thereÕs no way to putthat on screen except using voiceover.
It also depends on the time you have available. People have said that the ideal form to adapt for a feature Þlm is a novella or a short story, and if you try to cram a 400-page novel into two hours youÕre going to have difÞculty. I think thatÕs true. In a two-hour Þlm youÕre either disappointed that somethingÕs not there, or everythingÕs there but nothing really lands. The option that we didnÕt have open to us, even Þve or six years ago, was TV. When a big book came out, it used to be, ÔTheyÕve sold the Þlm rights.Õ Now theyÕll go after the TV rights, and screenwriters, and probably novelists too, are extremely pleased that they have this other, longer, equally ÔcinematicÕ form for adaptations of their work, where the primary job of the dramatist isnÕtcutting stuff, itÕs arranging it in Þve or six or eight lovely chunks of material, allowing the story to breathe in the way it does on the page. If I was doing One Day now, and I was getting offers from Þlm and TV companies, I would deÞnitely go for the TV version, because it would Þt much more naturally into that longer form.
If a novel has an unusual structure, would you try to reproduce that in adapting it?
ThatÕs very interesting. I did a screenplay of Tender is the Night, which is my favourite novel, and that has a very confusing structure for a cinemagoer. It starts from the point of view of someone who you think is the main character and they turn out to be a subsidiarycharacter. Then it goes into an extended ashback in the middle that explains everything you saw in the Þrst third and gives it in an entirely new context. When I adapted it, I clung to that structure and people inevitably said, ÔWhat would happen if you told the storychronologically?Õ The book was actually published in two versions, the ashback version and a chronological version, and although the chronological version would probably make more sense to a cinema audience Ð clear central characters, forward momentum Ð for me itÕs blasphemous. The ashback structure is one of the things I love aboutthe novel and I would Þnd it very hard to step away from that.
Do you try to avoid voiceover in adapting a rst-person narrative, or do you see it as another tool in the toolbox?
I might use it as a way in and a way out. I tend not to use it all the way through because, to continue the tool analogy, itÕs a bit of a blunt instrument. Voiceover rarely lands as powerfully as dialogue and gives scenes an archness that isnÕt always appropriate. YouÕre alwaysasking, ÔWhoÕs telling us this and why, and are they telling us in the moment, or are they telling us looking back?Õ If you think about voiceovers that really work, theyÕre often at odds with what youÕrewatching. ThereÕs a deliberate irony to them, or the character doesnÕt realise what we realise. I think thatÕs a justiÞcation for using voiceover.But if youÕre using it for exposition, or to cherry-pick the best jokes or your favourite bits of internal monologue, or just to give us a taste of whatÕs on the page, why not have a voiceover in every adaptation? With *Patrick Melrose*, we only used voiceover to suggest an altered state of mind, and that felt valid. Once he stopped taking drugs, no more voiceover. IÕm a huge admirer of Billy *Wild*er and he uses voiceover in nearly all his Þlms, but he uses it rather like a title card at the beginning of the Þlm, as a way into the world, and then drops ituntil the end, which to me is also a valid way to use it.
Do you use the language of the novel in writing the dialogue, or do you put the whole thing in your own words?
When IÕve adapted Hardy, IÕve stepped away from the verbosity of thedialogue in the books. I love Hardy, but if you speak the dialogue offthe page itÕs quite stilted and stiff, so I donÕt mind if the version in the adaptation isnÕt Hardyesque. There are little phrases and lines that are beautiful and memorable and of course you want to use them, but thereÕs a verbosity, sometimes a pomposity, that I tend to simplify and condense. On Far from the Madding Crowd, in the scene where Boldwood proposes to Bathsheba, IÕd written in the script that he says, ÔWill you marry me?Õ and she says, ÔMr Boldwood, I cannot.Õ The scene was really good in rehearsal, but Carey Mulligan found theline in the novel, which was something like ÔMr Boldwood, I do not Þnd the emotions in my heart that would allow me to say yes to your proposal.Õ IÕd tried to simplify it, but Carey spotted that there was something in the awkwardness of it that she could use, and she did. But for the most part, I wrote a more straightforward version. Dickens, on the other hand, writes wonderful dialogue. ItÕs also verbose and very stylised, but itÕs Dickens so it works. In the case of *Great Expectations*, 70 per cent of the dialogue was from the book, condensed or edited slightly, but retaining that ornate Dickensian quality.
Do you keep the novel beside you throughout the adaptationprocess, or do you try to internalise it and set it aside somewherealong the way?
It varies from book to book. My own books I generally know off by heart anyway. BlakeÕs book I knew well enough not to have by my side. ItÕs quite a short book and thereÕs not a lot of dialogue or what youÕd think of as conventional scenes, so I read it Þve or six times, made notes and then just wrote the script, because it was a looser adaptation. With *Patrick Melrose*, I learned the books. I went throughthem and annotated them, I found images in the prose that might beuseful on screen, I walked around London with the audio books playing in my head. IÕve done the same with Dickens and Hardy. There are celebrated dramatists who read the book once and throw it away, and IÕve never understood that. My motive for taking on the job is a real love of the material, so I want to completely immerse myselfin it and communicate that passion. The downside of this is that you can quickly grow to hate the original. I would still say *Great Expectations* is one of my favourite books, but reading it now bringsback feelings of regret about the things you cut or didnÕt think were quite right on screen, and because youÕve watched scenes over and over again you canÕt help hearing the actorsÕ voices in your head. Soadaptation kills the thing you love.
How much pressure do you feel when adapting a well-known andmuch-loved novel, knowing that you wonÕt be able to please everyone with the nished product?
More and more I feel the answer is not to adapt books that are really, really well-loved, because people donÕt tend to watch them and think, ÔThe dramatist has made an interesting choice here,Õ they just think, ÔSacrilege!Õ and thatÕs quite inhibiting when you sit down to write the script. YouÕre starting from a position where, if you change the material, people who love it will be disappointed, and if you stick to it, people who donÕt know it will be confused. The hardest experienceIÕve ever had as a screenwriter was One Day, where the book became very much talked about while I was still writing the screenplay. So instead of thinking, ÔHow am I going to turn this book into a Þlm?Õ youÕre thinking, ÔHow am I going to turn this book into a Þlm withoutpissing people off?Õ YouÕre no longer free to make bold choices for fear of offending loyal fans, nor are you necessarily making the best choices for people who donÕt know what all the fuss is about. *Patrick Melrose* was the perfect project because the books were celebrated, but we all felt they should be better known. You could introduce people to the books via the drama in a way that was good for everyone, rather than annoying Þve million readers.
If someone wants to option a novel of yours, how do you decide whether to adapt it yourself Ð and if someone else adapts it, how involved would you be in the process? Nick Payne, for example, adapted your novel Us for TV, but you have also written a draft of the series.
I feel a bit embarrassed about that; itÕs like back-seat driving. Giving it to someone else and saying, ÔHere you are, run with it,Õ is reallyappealing, and Nick came in and did a fantastic job. But he has his own voice and his own take on the material, and actually I found it hard to let go of it, so IÕve now done a pass on his scripts. IÕve worked on scripts for all my books and theyÕre the jobs IÕve enjoyed least, partly because when the book comes out IÕve told the story and itÕs hard to keep it fresh, partly because IÕve got a vision of the book in my head which makes it hard to invent new material. When I worked on One Day, people kept saying, ÔI think we need this character, I think we need to see this scene,Õ and IÕd already made those editorial decisions and it was hard for me to unpick them Ð and IÕm ashamed to say that everything new I added which wasnÕt in the book didnÕt really work; EmmaÕs parents were in it at one point, played by brilliant actors, but it was all superuous. I lack the callousness, really, or the objectivity, to adapt my own work. At the same time, because youÕve invested so much, and because a truth lies behind the Þction that youwant to put across, if you have experience as a screenwriter itÕs easy to succumb and think, ÔI know IÕll have to cut stuff. I know the casting might not be what I had in my head. I know the person doing the score will have different ideas to me. But IÕm a screenwriter, I know how ruthless the process is, I can do it.Õ And every time, even when the results have been okay, IÕve found it very, very hard.
What inspires you to swap the authorial autonomy of novels for the collaborative constraints of lm and TV?
I saw my book agent the other day and he said, ÔWhy do you do it?ÕThe novels, generally speaking, have gone quite well. There have beenups and downs, but the uctuations have been less extreme than with screenwriting. In Þlm, the Þnancial risks are huge and there are so many people involved and it can seem like a much less genteel world than the world of publishing. So why do I do it? I really like actors. I donÕt spend a lot of time with them on set, but I love seeing what theybring. I love the read-through. When you see something extra brought
to the dialogue, or to a scene that wasnÕt really working on the page, that can be really exciting and gratifying. I love the fact that itÕs social
Ð
until I donÕt love the fact that itÕs social. By which I mean, I love thecompany and I love the collaboration until the point where discussion turns into an argument. Fiction is much less scrutinised and pickedapart, whereas every line of a screenplay is examined. If I write a pageof dialogue in a novel it might get one or two passes, but chances are the Þnished version will broadly be the same as the Þrst draft. With a screenplay, I know thereÕs going to be ten or 15 drafts, and the director and the actors will have a view, and in the edit youÕll still discover lines that were boring and unnecessary. ThereÕs a rigour in screenwriting that there really isnÕt in Þction, and thatÕs both maddening and challenging. I write with great care, but I know that in every novel if I apply my edit-suite eye, there are things that can be cut. As long as the bookÕs not dull, thatÕs Þne Ð the writing is the point

Ð
but I like the ruthlessness and rigour of screenplays. I like peoplesaying, ÔDo we need this scene? Is there a better way of doing it?Õ And other times I hate that. So thatÕs really not a very good answer at all, is it?



ADAPTING NONFICTION
When adapting a book, ction or non ction, do you rely on the authorÕs research, or do you conduct your own?
The headline answer to this is that I almost instantly say no to biopics,or adaptations of biographies, particularly of famous people, because Iknow you ought to do research and explore the background and Þndout what really happened Ð and IÕm not very interested in that. YouÕre going to have to change things, and thereÕs going to be a controversyabout it Ð Churchill didnÕt go on the Tube, and Mary and Elizabeth didnÕt meet Ð and I understand why that debate happens, but as a screenwriter it isnÕt a debate I want to have. So whenever IÕve been offered something based on true events, IÕve nearly always turned it down. I donÕt want to research it, I donÕt want to Þght about ÔtruthÕ, IdonÕt want to have an argument with a producer saying, ÔI know thatÕs not what happened, but it works better dramatically.Õ I also donÕt feel that life has a three-act structure: that you have antagonists and a series of hurdles you have to overcome. If youÕre Winston Churchill or Mary, Queen of Scots, do you feel that? No, of course you donÕt. All of that is going to be artiÞcially stamped on to lives that were far morecomplicated and ambiguous and unresolved, and it can be done really well, but it isnÕt a form I love. In the case of BlakeÕs book, the real events were certainly complicated and we had to esh out the story more than it is on the page, but I didnÕt go through photo albums and meet the real-life versions of the characters. That may be laziness on my part, but I didnÕt know what I was going to gain from it. IÕd chosen the material because it was great; not because it was simplistic or easy, but because it worked beautifully. What was powerful about it was what Blake had already done to it, which is that heÕd brought a novelistÕs eye to biographical events. It isnÕt a biopic, itÕs a memoir, and memoirs often have a novelistic quality. ThatÕs why someone was compelled to write them, because they already felt like a story. So for me, the best approach, in fact the only feasible approach, was to treat it as a work of Þction and not dig around in it for further complications. ThereÕs something to be said for that kind of detective work, but it didnÕt feel appropriate in this case.
Have you ever encountered events in a non ction book so strange that you couldnÕt use them in the adaptation because audiences wouldnÕt believe they were real?
The opposite, if anything. There were some really strange things in BlakeÕs book that we kept in and maybe we shouldnÕt have. There was a lot of debate about the scene where he masturbates in the bath while his father is dying in the next room, and when I read that on the page I thought it was very striking and had a confrontational truth to it. IÕd never written anything as shocking as that, and describing it now it sounds quite extreme, but at the time it felt right to keep it.
How much responsibility do you feel towards historical accuracy when dramatising the lives of real characters?
I feel a certain amount of Þdelity to the book but Ð and perhaps this is irresponsible Ð I donÕt necessarily want another personÕs take on it. I want it to ring true, but I also want to be true to the character Ð and character is a very subjective thing. *Patrick Melrose* isnÕt Edward St Aubyn, but the thing Teddy always has to talk about when he talks about his work is whether or not Patrick is him. In episode three thereÕs a scene where Princess Margaret comes into the room, and I wrote a stage direction that said, ÔPatrick remains seated.Õ TeddyÕs note was that Patrick would never do that, and I think underlying that note was, ÔI would never do that,Õ because we were trying to impose a rebellious Republican spirit on someone who doesnÕt actually share the audienceÕs antipathy to royalty and privilege. There was a similar example in *And When Did You Last See Your Father?*, which was one of the times when I was aware of the presence of a living writer. Blake was incredibly supportive during the process and I think he liked the Þlm, but thereÕs a scene where his father is being awful and youngBlake goes into the next room and says out loud to himself, ÔI hate him.Õ Blake felt very strongly that he would never have done that, that even when his father was at his most maddening he was always aware that he loved him, but within the structure of a Þlm you want a moment of pure antagonism, you want to ramp things up and givethem somewhere to go. There were examples in *Patrick Melrose* where we went the other way. In the books, heÕs unrelentingly vicious and vitriolic about his mother. But on screen, because you have a great actor playing her, and because thereÕs a pathos in their scenes thatÕs different from the emotion on the page, that relationship is perhaps a little more empathetic. Sometimes you have to adjust the pitch to make it work dramatically. So I feel a degree of responsibility to reality, and I wouldnÕt want people to feel their experiences had beenmisrepresented, but there will inevitably be adjustments.
Do you think that being based on a true story in some way gives aproject more weight?
I think it does commercially. ItÕs a great hook to hang the publicity on. But I must admit I do dislike biopics and Ôtrue storiesÕ. All I see are the wigs and the prosthetics and a sense of manipulation. IÕm suspicious. I really admire The Crown, for instance, and Peter Morgan is a greatwriter, but I think, ÔAre these people really that eloquent? Is the Dukeof Edinburgh really that complex?Õ Maybe he is. Maybe IÕm not beingfair. There are a few exceptions. All the PresidentÕs Men is obviously based on true events, and its great strength is its authenticity and its messiness, the fact that you donÕt get a pay-off and that it stops just as the story is getting started. If you made that now, people would say, ÔWeÕve got to do the next bit, not this bit. Why are we taking so much time to tell the beginning of a much bigger story?Õ But thatÕs whatÕs brilliant about it. Or Zodiac, which gives a real avour of what that investigation must have been like and how frustrating it was, and communicates that frustration to the audience by not providing an answer to the case. A true story which ends with, as it were, punching the air, makes me think, ÔI donÕt believe this.Õ Whereas with Þction I donÕt mind that. With Þction thereÕs a permissible element of escapism, a sense of resolution that doesnÕt ring true to me in a true story.

CASE STUDY: *AND WHEN DID YOU LAST SEE YOUR FATHER?*
UK, 2007 ¥ Directed by Anand Tucker ¥ Produced by Elizabeth Karlsen, Stephen Woolley ¥ Screenplay by David Nicholls, based on the book by Blake Morrison ¥ Cast: Jim Broadbent (Arthur),Colin Firth (Blake), Juliet Stevenson (Kim), Gina McKee (Kathy),Sarah Lancashire (Beaty), Elaine Cassidy (Sandra), Clare Skinner(Gillian), Carey Mulligan (Rachel), Matthew Beard (Teenage Blake)
*And When Did You Last See Your Father?* was almost like a non ction dry run for *Patrick Melrose*: a life-and-death-spanning story of fathers and sons. Do you think they have any thematicsimilarities, and were you attracted to both projects for any of thesame reasons?
They do share similar themes of redemption and forgiveness and coming to terms with the past. But my response to BlakeÕs book was that it was very hopeful and humane Ð and very touching. Whereas my initial response to the Melrose books was shock and laughter Ð although I also Þnd them moving, despite the hate and venom. I think theyÕre both faithful Ð most of whatÕs said and done on screen was there on the page Ð but I probably did introduce moments of pathos and humour in *Patrick Melrose* that arenÕt in the books, and the adaptation of *And When Did You Last See Your Father?* is less episodic and more eventful than the original because of how I selectedand shaped the material.
The script does have a very British bittersweet tone which is all your own Ð which translates through to the lm. Did you realise the extent to which your voice, both as a screenwriter and a novelist, had become superimposed over Blake MorrisonÕs?
IÕm aware of that with everything IÕve done, really, but IÕm also waryof knocking the edges off things, of not being as tough as the original material is. I like writing romantic comedy and I like writing social awkwardness, and I worry that I select scenes in the source material that will allow me to do those things and perhaps neglect stuff that IÕm not so good with myself as a dramatist. As I said, IÕd never reallydealt with darker material before I adapted *And When Did You Last See Your Father?*, so the book gave me a leg up into writing about things that I felt a little nervous about.
The book alternates between chapters set in the past and chapters set in the present Ð until ArthurÕs death, when past and present largely converge, structure quite literally giving way. Were you aware of that e ect, and did you try to mirror it in any way in your adaptation?
I was, and I think we did Ð but again we had to select the material, particularly in the past. ItÕs a wonderful book, but youÕre always looking out for beats that are repetitive: is this yet another example of his father being a stick in the mud, or a bit embarrassing? There needs to be a cumulative quality to it, and both in the book and the Þlm that quality comes from young Blake getting older: growing into a man himself and becoming sexually aware and wanting to move away. ThatÕs the arc in the past and you donÕt want to jump about, you want it to be chronological. The treatment of the past in *Patrick Melrose* was much more of a collage Ð dreamlike, an image from here, a line from there, something which illustrates this aspect, something which illustrates that Ð but with *And When Did You Last See Your Father?*, both story strands need to move towards a letting go of the past.There was a lot of good stuff in the book about him bringing back girlfriends from university, but that would have required a mid-stageBlake, which would have confused the whole issue of young and old.
The lm starts where the book starts: with Arthur jumping the queue to enter a motor-racing event. But in the script, young Blake is described as Ômortied and ashamedÕ, whereas in the
lm he looks at Arthur in childlike wonder and says, as an adult invoiceover, ÔMy father seemed to me infallible, invincible Ð immortal,Õ which sets up both his teenage disillusionment and adult grief. Can you talk me through that transition from book to script to lm?
ThatÕs interesting. That feels like a response to a note. IÕm almost certain that in the Þrst draft the scene would have been as it was in the book, and the revision was probably to do with there being a journeyfor Blake. If we start with his father being an embarrassment to him and continue that way for 90 minutes, thereÕs nowhere to go. ItÕs not about imposing an arc, but about introducing variety, a change in the charactersÕ attitudes rather than a reiteration of them.
When heÕs dressing for his awards dinner near the start of the
lm, BlakeÕs wife Kathy jokes, ÔI hope IÕm going to get a mention.Õ In fact, sheÕs only mentioned twice in the book, in passing. Why did you decide to make her more of a presence in your adaptation?
The book is very male. I donÕt mean itÕs a macho book, but itÕs prettymuch a two-hander Ð or a four-hander, if you count their youngerselves Ð about the father and son, then and now. At the same time, youÕve got these great actresses Ð Juliet Stevenson as BlakeÕs mother, Gina McKee as his wife, Carey Mulligan as the young woman teenageBlake meets on holiday Ð so of course you want to give them something to work with. And itÕs necessary for the scenes to feel like scenes, for there to be more back and forth, more push and pull than there is on the page. ThatÕs not a failing of the book, because itÕs not the story Blake is telling, but in the book heÕs extremely solitary andinternal, and expanding the other roles gives you a chance to vocalise that. Also Ð and this probably sounds pompous, but IÕll say it anyway
Ð there are sometimes female voices in source material that I feel are underserved and I want to give more of a voice and an attitude to them. In *Patrick Melrose*, he gets a lot more pushback from the female characters than he does in the novels. With Tess of the DÕUrbervilles, I made a conscious effort to give her a wit and a curiosity that is presentin the novel, but missing from previous adaptations. And with *And When Did You Last See Your Father?*, I really liked writing Carey MulliganÕs scenes and the scenes with BlakeÕs old girlfriend, played byElaine Cassidy, and Þlling them out in ways that werenÕt necessarily inthe book.
You moved the dinner from the mid-eighties to just before ArthurÕs illness, and show Arthur helping Blake move house at the start of his illness, rather than towards the end of it. Did you want to show Arthur t and healthy, at his most selsh and bumptious, before the illness kicks in?
Yes. I also wanted as tight a timeframe as possible, a sense of pace and momentum. We did the same thing in *Patrick Melrose*, particularly in episode four, MotherÕs Milk. The novel covers four years; we condensed it to one month. IÕd rather there be an artiÞcial quality to the decline in his motherÕs health or the speed of PatrickÕs adulterythan explain time leaps.
At the dinner, nursing his sorrows at the bar, Blake calls Arthur a Ômean-spirited, sanctimonious, narrow-minded old sod.Õ ThatÕs echoed later, in the ashback we talked about, where youngBlake retreats to the toilet and says to his reection in the mirror,ÔI fucking, fucking, fucking hate him.Õ Those moments donÕt appear in the book, and are presumably there in the lm to establish and maintain the con ict between them in a clear and dramatic way?
Absolutely. Blake found the Ôfucking hate himÕ scene quite upsetting Ð and not necessarily truthful Ð and itÕs pretty unlikely he would have said the ÔsanctimoniousÕ thing either. ThereÕs two issues here. One is the age-old problem of how you express charactersÕ thoughts. If youjust write, ÔYoung Blake is furious,Õ someone reading that is likely to skip over it, so you Þnd yourself reaching for things that are rather coarse Ð and sometimes you need them on screen, and sometimes, because of the things an actor can do, you donÕt. So one issue is the craft of it. The other issue is the truth of it. The book is very much about unexpressed feelings and the way they become a disease in themselves, and itÕs hard not to express that in some kind of catharsis on screen, not just for the characters, but for the audience. ItÕs not very dramatic to watch someone getting pissed off, then getting a bit more pissed off, then getting a bit more pissed off, and not saying or doing anything about it. So I probably would have said to Blake, ÔI know itÕs not true, but itÕs necessary. ItÕs true for the character in the moment, and there needs to be some expression of fury.Õ You need it because thereÕs no prose to convey his degree of emotional frustration.
You actually introduced quite a lot of con ict: in the rst visit to his fatherÕs bedside, for example, Blake is at odds with both Arthur and his sister Gill. In the book, Blake and Gill are more a ectionate, and sheÕs being shielded from the extent of ArthurÕs illness. Did you ever hesitate about changing things to that degree?
I talked to Blake about it, and I think he talked to Gill about it Ð that it would be a different Gill, a Þctional Gill, who didnÕt necessarily feel the way the real Gill felt. ThatÕs why you sometimes changecharactersÕ names: because youÕve written about things in a way thatÕs partly true and partly not, and you need a character to represent an attitude or a state of mind that wasnÕt the case in real life.
One e ect of KathyÕs increased presence in the lm is to emphasise BlakeÕs misguided attempt to rekindle his sexual relationship with Sandra: it makes him seem more like his father, trying it on with another woman despite having a wife and children, but it also risks making him seem unsympathetic. Whatwas your thinking in terms of balancing those things?
We talked a lot about that. I felt it should be less, but the director, Anand Tucker, felt it should be more, and Anand won that one Ð thatÕs my memory, anyway. I thought a irtation was okay, but there was a feeling that we might need to turn up the dial a bit if it was to be a meaningful scene. It deÞnitely goes further than it does in the book, and IÕm sure that would have been a discussion with Blake, because you wouldnÕt want to suggest that this was more truthful than the book. The overall tone of the book is rather melancholy and ruminative, and of course you want to get that, but you also want to feel that thereÕs some real drama going on. We also had a veryattractive actor with great charm, which perhaps takes the curse off. If weÕd cast someone else, we might have got to the edit suite and thought, ÔShit, I wish weÕd stuck with irtation.Õ We talked about the same thing on *Patrick Melrose*. Is it clearly a midlife crisis provokedby grief and anger, or is it just a lot of excuses for shitty behaviour? Can he really go to bed with another woman while his wife and children are asleep next door? Those are valid questions, but others are: what adjustments do you make to the sympathy dial depending on the casting, and how much charisma, warmth and humour will they bring to the performance? There are an innumerable number of variables which youÕre not thinking about at the writing stage. YouÕrejust thinking, ÔHow far do I want to push this on the page?Õ
ArthurÕs a air with Auntie Beaty is dealt with more directly in the
lm: young Blake rst suspects something is going on during hiscamping trip with Arthur, and adult Blake asks Beaty about it afterhis fatherÕs funeral. The book contains neither that moment of realisation nor that specic conversation, but the lm needs to set up and track through BlakeÕs arc, his need to discover the truth?
Another alarm that always goes off in my head is ambiguity in a book.On the page, itÕs absolutely Þne to have two possible outcomes, two possible truths. On screen, what are you going to do? You canÕt show them one after the other. You have to plump for one or the other. A good example of that is Tess of the DÕUrbervilles. Because of the kind of writer Hardy is, and because of the complexities of Victorian morality Ð the conservatism of their attitudes towards sex, the euphemistic language they used, the sexual politics of the time Ð youread Tess and think, ÔWas she raped or not?Õ Even in the 11 years since we made the programme, the language and attitudes have changed. Inthe unchanging, Þxed text the word ÔseducedÕ is used, but that doesnÕt mean there was no violence, that it wasnÕt what we would now call rape. So itÕs possible to read the book and think she was attracted to a handsome, devilish man, and she was a bit drunk and sleepy, but it was not a violent rape. ItÕs also possible to read the book and think it absolutely was rape, that she was a rather nave girl and very unlikelyto have given consent. Neither of those things is true or false, and you can go through the book a hundred times and make arguments on both sides. But when you Þlm it and the actor says, ÔIs this a violent rape? Do I say no, am I coerced, or do I explicitly give my consent?Õ you have to answer those questions. You canÕt just say, ÔLetÕs make it ambiguous.Õ So in adapting it, IÕm not making a deÞnitive statement about the book, IÕm only making a deÞnitive statement about the adaptation, and in the adaptation weÕve chosen to represent it as rape
Ð and to me the story makes much more sense if itÕs about a shockingabuse of power rather than a ÔseductionÕ. And in relation to BlakeÕs book, it seemed like a mistake to have people coming out of the cinema going, ÔI donÕt understand. Did they have an affair?Õ In key moments like that, the audience needs to know whatÕs happened, andyou sometimes have to ride roughshod over ambiguity to tell them.
Your dramatisation of ArthurÕs illness and death is, on the whole, more faithful to the facts than the ashbacks to BlakeÕs childhood. Is that because you felt you had to be more reverent inthe present-day scenes, whereas events in the past allow you more licence to change and rearrange?
That sounds about right. ItÕs very powerfully described in the book, and such a terrible circumstance to live through, that thereÕs not much youÕre required to change for dramatic effect. Since publication, other writers have been more frank and fearless about the nitty-gritty of death and illness, but at the time it felt like one of the most originaland radical elements of the book. One thing we did change a lot, and argue about a lot, was the extent to which Blake leaves his fatherÕs side and travels back and forth through the different stages of his fatherÕs illness. ThereÕs a lot of that in the book, but whenever we had it in the script, whenever he was sitting on a train, it felt like there was a deation, a loss of tension. In the context of the Þlm, it felt like once he was there, he should stay there.
