In the lm, Blake doesnÕt cry when his father dies, and only feels the full force of his grief at the end when he remembers his fatherÕs tears on waving him o to university. ItÕs climactic, itÕs cathartic, and itÕs also not true: in the book, Blake does cry. I imagine the challenge was nding an engaging dramatic arc for an essentially passive character through an episodic narrative?
It sounds really callous to say this, but the process of bereavement andgrief is both quite slow and quite repetitive, so you have to play those cards in quite a decided and pointed way. It was also a blatant attempt to give the Þlm a sense of climax, because itÕs very hard to Þnd those kinds of climaxes in domestic stories. We felt that there needed to be a single dramatic moment where past and present collided, and it may be contrived, but itÕs one of those times where you have to cater to theÞxed, objective rhythm of watching a Þlm as opposed to the inÞnitely variable rhythm of reading a book. With the written word, you donÕt know whether the story will be consumed in one go or over three months. But in the cinema, the timing is the same for everyone. ItÕs a real-time experience, so you have to shape it in a different way.

CASE STUDY: *PATRICK MELROSE*
UK/US, 2018 ¥ Directed by Edward Berger ¥ Produced byStephen Smallwood ¥ Screenplay by David Nicholls, based on the novels Never Mind, Bad News, Some Hope, MotherÕs Milk and At Last by Edward St Aubyn ¥ Cast: Benedict Cumberbatch (*Patrick Melrose*), Jennifer Jason Leigh (Eleanor Melrose), HugoWeaving (David Melrose), Holliday Grainger (Bridget Watson Scott), Anna Madeley (Mary Melrose), Sebastian Maltz (Young Patrick), Jessica Raine (Julia), Pip Torrens (Nicholas Pratt),Prasanna Puwanarajah (Johnny Hall), Harriet Walter (PrincessMargaret), John Standing (George Watford)
Five novels in ve, hour-long episodes. Was that always the intention?
No, it wasnÕt. The original idea was Þve 90-minute Þlms Ð or possiblyfour, with the Þrst two books conated into one and ashbacks from New York to France Ð all of them shot by different directors. Never Mind would be like a Joseph Losey Þlm, Bad News something like ScorseseÕs After Hours, and Some Hope reminiscent of Robert AltmanÕs Gosford Park. It was harder to think of archetypes for MotherÕs Milk and At Last, but I always thought of episode Þve as a sort of early seventies Þlm like Sunday Bloody Sunday or The Pumpkin Eater, something melancholy and angry in that very English way. Also, the episodes matched the chronological order of the booksuntil quite late in the process. We all knew that would change because of the casting, but we were wary of making that change Ð and even after the read-through, we were talking about swapping the episodes back and starting with PatrickÕs childhood. So it went through all kinds of versions. It was probably three years between writing episode one and writing episode four, and episode four alone went throughmany different incarnations.
The novels are very sensual, in the literal sense of the word: things seen and heard, things tasted and felt, things watched andinterpreted. How do you set about capturing that in a screenplay?
I think you have to be quite tough about reading the whole book. What I mean is, the drama isnÕt just in the dialogue or in peopleÕs actions. ItÕs in, as you say, the sensuality: the lizard on the wall, or the rotting food in the hotel room. I had to harvest the text not just for conversations and events, but also for imagery, and to put a lot of that in the stage directions. The books are about the invasion of memory, and how past experience forms and affects present behaviour, and I think we succeeded in conveying that idea. But thereÕs also a lot of philosophical speculation in the books, about consciousness and the self and how different characters see the world, that we couldnÕt really get on the screen.
Bad News and Never Mind
A good reason for starting with Bad News rather than Never Mind is that you introduce Patrick as an adult, played by your star, before showing him as a child. On the other hand, that means you meet him at his most extreme, which could be very o-putting. How did you reconcile those pros and cons?
We had a series of test screenings where we got people into the edit suite, and one night we played them childhood, then Patrick as a drugaddict, and the other night we played them Patrick as a drug addict, then childhood, and asked them how they felt about him as a character. When we played it chronologically Ð i.e. Never Mind then Bad News Ð the second episode became a little bit boring because you knew why he hated his father and his actions seemed explicable but repetitive. When we had it the other way around, itÕs true that some people were shocked at how extreme it was, but at least it was compelling, and youÕre either going to go with that or youÕre not. We talked a lot about how much drug use should be in it, because that can be quite a turn-off. ItÕs the ultimate subjective experience, and can be quite relentless for a viewer. So we thought, ÔWhich is the biggergamble, this languid but harsh childhood Þlm followed by a manic crazy hour, or the other way around?Õ And we felt that it was best the other way around: rather than, hereÕs something terrible happeningand here are the repercussions of that, it became, hereÕs someone behaving in an extraordinary way and here are the reasons why. I would never, ever suggest that anyone read the books in anythingother than the order in which they were intended to be read, but the beneÞts involved in starting with Bad News outweighed the risks. ItÕs dramatically more interesting.
But you donÕt simply dramatise one novel then the other, you interleave them. In episode one, there are ashbacks to the young Patrick walking towards his fatherÕs bedroom door, and in episode two there are ash-forwards to the adult Patrick walking down a similar corridor. And if you showed what happened behind the door in the rst episode, there would have been a danger that the audience wouldnÕt stick around for the second one.
We also talked a lot about how much of that there was going to be. The book is extremely shocking and upsetting, as it should be, but we always knew that we wouldnÕt have any of that Ð that actually, we would close the door. There would have been ways of doing it, but itwould have been unnecessary and, I think, irresponsible.
The second episode opens with a recap, as does the third, but itÕs more than just ÔPreviously on *Patrick Melrose*ÉÕ, you actuallyincorporate it into the drama: the phone call where Patrick learnsof his fatherÕs death, viewing DavidÕs body, the con being closed and then cremated, the box of ashes on which Patrick takes out his anger. It puts you rmly in PatrickÕs head.
That sequence was always in the script, the phone call then the cremation, as if you were now following David MelroseÕs story too, watching his ultimate fate then ashing back. But initially, after the phone call, there was not a single glimpse of Benedict Cumberbatch in episode two. That was a studio note. The execs were nervous about Benedict not being in the second episode at all, and also, because it has a different style, that youÕd turn on and feel like you were watching a different show, rather than a continuation of the same story. So the idea of dropping in one or two beats of Patrick goingthrough cold turkey, almost hallucinating the ashbacks, came quitelate and was shot rather grudgingly. It was one of those notes where you think, ÔI donÕt want to do that,Õ then you do it and Þnd youprobably need it. The cry of the screenwriter is, ÔWhy wonÕt they leave me alone? Why do they give me these ridiculous notes?Õ, but actuallythe people giving them are often on to something.
In episode two, we see clearly how similar Patrick and his motherEleanorÕs reactions are to the di erent kinds of abuse they both su ered Ð obliterating their sorrows in drugs and drink. In episode four, itÕs equally clear that heÕs now turning into his father: the cigar, the dressing gown, the bitterness and betrayal, the casual callousness towards his own children. Do you think the language of lm sometimes gives you a chance to do thingsmore simply, more directly and maybe better than the book did?
Yes, although I also worry about overstating things. When you put theimagery of a novel on screen, it becomes images in a much more literal way. Things that were mentioned in passing now shout, ÔLook! TheyÕve got the same body language and theyÕre in the same spotsaying the same thing!Õ You canÕt do any of those things in prose, and thereÕs a danger when you do them visually that you make them too on-the-nose. So you have to make judgments, both in the writing and in the editing, about how heavily to underline things. But you do sometimes feel with an adaptation as if youÕre giving a note to the author. ThereÕs a scene in Far from the Madding Crowd where theyÕredipping sheep, and Bathsheba is watching rather loftily and criticisesthem, and Gabriel says, ÔIf you know better, you come down and do it,Õ and she does. She gets into the trough with the workers and dips the sheep herself, and itÕs a little step in her mellowing. ThatÕs not what happens in the novel, but I like to think that if Thomas Hardy could see the Þlm he might say, ÔThatÕs a good idea.Õ With *Patrick Melrose*, the novels werenÕt conceived as a saga and Edward St Aubyn doesnÕt feel the obligation to make it work as one, so there are characters who appear in one book and never come back. PatrickÕs ex-girlfriend, Julia, is only in MotherÕs Milk and At Last, but there are other characters in Bad News and Some Hope who are very like her, so conating those characters and giving Julia an arc and exploringher relationship with Patrick actually works quite well. Similarly, his fatherÕs terrible best friend, Nicholas, isnÕt in Bad News, but heÕs one of the most interesting characters and itÕs a very interesting time to see him, when PatrickÕs strung out in New York, so of course heÕs got to be there. When youÕre exploring a family in slow decline, it doesnÕt hurt to draw out those saga elements and provide the viewer with recurring characters, so that they can see them getting older as time goes on.

Some Hope
Episode three clearly has a theme: escape. Were you aware of theother episodes having individual themes too?
I had some experience of that on the Þrst show I ever wrote, Cold Feet. There used to be quite a strict formula for those nine oÕclock TV shows: you have the ongoing story and the story of the week and a little comic plot, and they all reect each other and are often linked by a theme and will always collide at some point. It was a bit like three-act structure in Þlms, and you really did have to learn it. I didnÕt feel the same compulsion to do that with *Patrick Melrose*, but of course the books have very clear themes which were brought into the show and elaborated. I think thatÕs why I found episode Þve particularlydifÞcult, because the question I got asked most often was, ÔWhat itÕs about?Õ ItÕs an irritating question to be asked, but if youÕre not able toanswer it, that may give you a clue as to why something isnÕt working.
ItÕs two and a half episodes Ð two and a half hours Ð before Patrick says or does something likeable: the sympathy he shows towards BridgetÕs daughter during her embarrassing encounter with Princess Margaret. Were you aware of that being his rst moment of kindness, and did you ever wonder whether the audience would stick with him for that long?
I was aware of it, because it touches on childhood and kindness to children. It was clear to me as a reader, and I thought it would be clear to the audience, that people who suffer monstrously often become monsters Ð but his monstrousness, if it was entertaining, would be acceptable. ThereÕs a touch of Withnail in Patrick. In Withnail and I, WithnailÕs vulnerability isnÕt really explicit until the last minute of the Þlm. Until then, the pleasure has been from the irresponsibility and extremity of that character. I certainly never got the note, ÔCan youmake Patrick more likeable?Õ, but that was partly because my instinct was to do that anyway. I didnÕt think I was censoring the character, but there were times when I thought, ÔWeÕve got enough of that.Õ Enough of Patrick the snob, or Patrick the misogynist, or Patrick patronising people. Enough that it didnÕt need to be overstated Ð although perhaps other dramatists would have written harsher versions of the material. ItÕs also possible to imagine it being played by someone else and not having the pathos that Benedict brings to the part. So a lot of it is in the performance rather than the script.
The pivotal scene in episode three is PatrickÕs confession to Johnny that he was abused by his father, a confession which comes at a di erent point and in a di erent location in the novel. How did you arrive at the nal structure of that episode?
Some Hope was always my favourite book, and I thought that everything in it was good and strong, but there was no real climax to it. The climax of the novel Ð George having a heart attack and beingtaken to hospital Ð happens again in episode Þve, to Nicholas. You canforgive that if youÕre reading the books over the course of 20 years,but in a TV show you canÕt have two major elderly characters die of heart attacks at social events within two weeks of broadcast. So we had to cut that scene from Some Hope, which meant there was a certain amount of action missing from the latter half of the episode.Also, Patrick and Johnny arenÕt at the party until very late in the novel. PatrickÕs confession happens halfway through, in the pub, andfor the rest of the book he has this series of lovely, wise conversationswith various characters about forgiveness: whether he can ever forgivehis father and, if he canÕt, whether itÕs possible just to be indifferent to whatÕs happened to him. All of that existed in early drafts of the script, but was a bit dramatically at. You felt you could take out any of it and it wouldnÕt make much difference to the story of that episode. So putting the confession late in the episode was extremelyhelpful. It was a big change, but I think it was a good change.

MotherÕs Milk
Some Hope ends with Patrick on an up. By the time you get to MotherÕs Milk, heÕs come down again. ItÕs quite a jarring gear shift reading them back to back.
It is jarring, and also his dynamic as a 40-year-old man needs to be different from his dynamic in his twenties; he no longer has the alibi of youth. His response to things has to be different and the tone of it has to be different. HeÕs a family man with responsibilities, and drinking himself into a stupor and letching after young women could have become tiresome. The last two books were both problematic to adapt, and at one stage I pushed quite hard just to do the Þrst three. Even though I love all Þve, the Þrst three have an arc to them, which is the fatherÕs story, and then you have to ramp things up again for the last two to tell the motherÕs story. PatrickÕs problem isnÕt solved at the end of the third book, but heÕs at a moment of peace and reconciliation, so why not stop there? IÕm glad we did them all in the end, but at the time I found the fourth book very hard.
ItÕs easy to see why. ItÕs the longest of the ve novels, itÕs told from multiple points of view, and it spans four years rather than afew days.
When you sit down to do MotherÕs Milk, youÕre immediatelyconfronted with PatrickÕs son Robert being born Ð seen from RobertÕs point of view. So thatÕs not going to work. Then you get to the stuff about the nanny being sacked, which is really funny, but youÕreintroduced to a character whoÕs immediately discarded in a way that makes Patrick look quite mean. So you cross that out. And you keep going like that until you get to something you feel you canÕt live without, which is him being dispossessed by his mother and the dispossession being the catalyst for his breakdown Ð and once we decided that was what it was about, it was Þne. I worked very closely with Edward Berger on that episode; itÕs the most input IÕve ever had from a director on a script. It was originally much more faithful to the middle of the book Ð the summer where Patrick really starts drinkingand his affair with Julia kicks off, and there is a series of visitors to the house and they all have conversations with him Ð and condensed into an hour it was a bit boring. IÕm actually pretty pleased with the Þnished version. ItÕs not my favourite episode, but I always thought it was going to be a disaster, and it was rewritten a lot right up to the read-through.
What were the most obvious cuts and what were the hardest?
I found it very hard to lose the conversations with Johnny, because when youÕre adapting material, close friends are invaluable, even more so if theyÕre therapists. ThereÕs no contrivance in confession, so theyÕrethe perfect sounding boards. There are a lot of passages in the book where Johnny says, ÔSo how are you feeling?Õ and Patrick tells him: mymemories of childhood are this, and my feelings towards women are this. But I was constantly getting the note to take out Johnny, because having them walk around the grounds and conÞde in each other for pages and pages stopped things dead. The last section of the book, where they come back to London and Patrick starts to think about the euthanasia of his mother, also got cut back, because the episode is about dealing with her death, rather than dealing with how she mightdie. It was an interesting moral issue, but once Edward Berger came on board he said, ÔItÕs about a family man falling apart and Þghting not to turn into his father, and anything thatÕs not serving that youÕve got to cut.Õ
PatrickÕs children, principally Robert, play a big part on the page Ð especially in this book, given its title Ð but a small part on screen. Why was that?
Because kids are a nightmare in dramas that cover a long period of time. Irrespective of the actorÕs ability, irrespective of what works in the script, you always have to be aware of the practicalities of production, of one face morphing into the next and not being able to show intermediate stages. One of the strongest alarms that goes off when IÕm reading something with an eye to adaptation is the highest possible age of the young actor. In *Great Expectations*, Pip doesnÕt leap in the book from being eight to being 21, but when you put it on screen you have to bunch up the action on either side and cut the bit in the middle so that you can jump from one actor to the other. The same with BlakeÕs book. There are scenes where he has encounters with his father in his twenties and thirties, but we didnÕt even attempt to make the young actor look like Colin Firth and just cut the intermediate versions. ThatÕs an easy choice. There was a third incarnation of Patrick in certain drafts of the script, because there was some great stuff in the novel about the teenage Patrick going to see his destitute father and Þnding the conÞdence to taunt him for the Þrst time, but it was exhausting just thinking about casting a midwayPatrick. So the material you select is often determined by practical considerations.

At Last
The nal episode struck me as being very silent and still. Was that quality in the script, or was it in the way it was shot?
Again, Edward Berger had a lot of input. He wasnÕt around for the initiation of the project, so he was less involved in the writing of the Þrst three, but he did a lot of thinning out of the latter drafts of four and Þve. We knew that the early drafts were too faithful and that weÕd need to cut some of the chatter thatÕs in the book and replace it with essential beats that werenÕt. In the book, thereÕs a lot more jumpingaround between different groups at the motherÕs funeral, and also a lot missing. You donÕt see the break-up of PatrickÕs marriage in the book; itÕs all happened in the past, so that had to be invented. ThereÕs no scene in the book where Patrick breaks down, and no scene with his mother where they both acknowledge DavidÕs crimes, so they had to be invented. It didnÕt feel acceptable for his anger with his mother to be that internalised, so thereÕs a lot of speechifying, which is the most sustained piece of invention in the Þve episodes. I feel itÕs necessary and I think itÕs extremely well acted, but itÕs the stuff IÕm most nervous about because itÕs not entirely in tune with Edward St AubynÕs vision, which is less cathartic. So itÕs slightly out of character, but you have to weigh up the change in style against the demands of the audience.
I was going to ask you about the scene where Patrick nally tells his mother that his father abused him. ItÕs a short but devastating scene, and I didnÕt remember it in the novels.
ItÕs referred to glancingly in MotherÕs Milk, but itÕs not a scene, itÕs just a sentence, in the passage where heÕs complaining about the egotismand selÞshness of his mother, and how everything always has to be about her, and that he has been locked all his life in a competitionwith her as to who suffered most at the hands of his father. ItÕs a greatpassage, but itÕs quite violently anti-Eleanor, and we felt we had to see a more sympathetic version of that. But youÕre right, it is devastating,because he doesnÕt know how to react and the scene just stops. In the writing process we would ask, ÔWhat would happen if we kept the camera running? Is he angry? Does he reveal more? Does she sayanything else?Õ and every time we tried to answer those questions we got into trouble. We didnÕt want the sentimental version where two people who have suffered greatly give each other a big hug and say, ÔI love you.Õ Nor were we comfortable with the version where he goes, ÔFor fuckÕs sake, at least let me have this,Õ and storms off, because thatÕs extremely unsympathetic. So in a way, the short version is the only version that works. You feel both sympathy for the sufferingEleanor has undoubtedly experienced and a little of PatrickÕs shock at her response. It isnÕt quite whatÕs in the book, but I donÕt think itÕs a betrayal. And again, when you make characters esh and blood theyÕre often more touching or more maddening than they were on the page, and you canÕt predict that.
The nal episode is the one where I noticed the humour most. How important was humour to you in adapting such dark, dicultsubject matter?
In a way, itÕs what drew me to the material. I read the Þrst book when it came out in 1992: I was working in a bookshop and picked it up off the table and read it on my lunch breaks and thought it was amazing.It seemed to be part of a British Þction tradition that I loved, the tradition of Evelyn Waugh, especially the Waugh of A Handful of Dust. So the humour was absolutely essential to me, and IÕm hugelyrelieved that it comes across. If it had fallen at it would have seemed incredibly unpleasant, a frivolous approach to the most serious subject matter imaginable. Most of the best jokes are TeddyÕs, though; if IÕve invented dialogue, itÕs often been more emotional stuff. IÕm very proudboth of the scripts and of the Þnished show, but IÕm also very aware of it being an editorial contribution rather than lots of invention on my part. IÕve just given a more conventional shape to books that donÕt have that Ð and why would they? ItÕs not what the novels set out to do.



Sarah Phelps
Sarah Phelps was born in 1966 in Kingston upon Thames.
She has written 100 episodes of EastEnders, and her other television work includes episodes of No Angels (2004/2006) and Dickensian (2015Ð16). She also created and wrote the BBC series The Crimson Field (2014).
Her screen adaptation credits include: the Dickens serials Oliver Twist (2007) and *Great Expectations* (2011); Falcn (2012, novel The Silent and the Damned by Robert Wilson); The Casual Vacancy (2015, novel by J.K. Rowling); Dublin Murders (2019, novels In the Woods and The Likeness by Tana French);and four novels and a short story by Agatha Christie: And Then There Were None (2015), The Witness for the Prosecution (2016), Ordeal by Innocence (2018), The ABC Murders (2018)and The Pale Horse (2020).
APPROACHES TO ADAPTATION
Do you prefer to adapt material which chimes with your own work, or material which is completely di erent and gives you a chance to try out new things?
I would say that I prefer to do work thatÕs outside of what I would write. But when I adapt something I do a very thorough adaptation, so even if itÕs way outside what I would write about, it becomes my own work anyway. Sometimes even though a novel is really popular and everyone thinks they know it, when IÕve read it IÕve found something completely different in it. The usual way of perceivingOliver Twist is that Nancy is a victim and Bill Sikes is a thug. But in the book thatÕs not true. HeÕs incredibly tender to her. ThereÕs a bit in the book when she has a gin-soaked seizure while theyÕre all arguingabout the lunatic plot, and Sikes picks her up and puts her down verygently on some ratty straw-Þlled mattress in the corner and says,ÔSheÕll be all right now.Õ And after heÕs murdered her, when he tries to get out of London into the country, wherever he goes he sees her face. ThatÕs not a thug, thatÕs a man with a conscience whoÕs aware that heÕs snuffed out the one pure light in his life Ð and unable to bear it he returns to London to be chased across the rooftops, Þred up by that ghastly man Brownlow shouting that thereÕll be a reward for anyone who hunts him down. ItÕs a horrible sequence in the book; thereÕs a blood-letting quality to it which is really repugnant. In my version, I sent him down into the sewers to kill himself, so it wasnÕt a publicaccident, it was a private covenant with the terrible thing he had done.He had broken his own heart. And it was also his circumstances: the poverty of it, the terror of it, the not knowing if youÕre going to end the day alive. It was really important to me to tell that story. So someone else might have adapted the novel one way, and I adapted it my way.
Do you think adaptations involve a completely di erent set of creative gears to original screenplays?
It depends. ThereÕs a difference between working with an author whoÕs long dead and been adapted a million times before Ð like Dickens Ð and working with an author whoÕs been adapted before, butis very much alive Ð like J.K. Rowling Ð and working with an author whoÕs also long dead, but has an incredibly vigilant estate and whose works are so popular that thereÕs a kind of cosy familiarity about them. People often ask me, ÔDid you ever think when you were writingEastEnders that youÕd end up doing this?Õ And IÕm like, ÔOf course.Õ I mean, no, but thereÕs no difference in my mind between writing a piece of original work and writing for something like EastEnders, which is a huge factory, and turning my attention to either a familiar or unfamiliar piece of work and trying to do something different with it. Because what it comes down to, all of it, is character and storytelling. If you havenÕt got that root-and-branch DNA, then youhavenÕt got anything, regardless of what the project is.
Do you always agree a mission statement or direction of travel with whoever has commissioned the adaptation?
I donÕt generally agree a mission statement; I say what IÕm going to do and people either like it or they donÕt. IÕm not going to pitch how I want to tell the story and then have someone say, ÔYou know what, can you make it a bit more like this?Õ Obviously when youÕre working on something, especially if itÕs a long project, it goes through changes, and ultimately whatever you do is a collaboration between you, the producers, the director, the actors, not just from the point of view of being in line with Ð sounding rather poncey Ð your ÔvisionÕ, but also whatÕs achievable in terms of budget and schedule. A lot of the time when youÕre writing, after your Þrst draft, youÕre dealing with problems like, ÔThis is great but we canÕt afford it.Õ Oliver Twist was one of the Þrst big adaptations I did, and I donÕt think anyonethought, ÔLetÕs ask Sarah Phelps because sheÕs really got her Þnger on the pulse of Dickens.Õ I think someone else had dropped out and they needed someone who could write scripts fast, which I can do because of turning scripts around for EastEnders. And in one of the Þrst meetings they said, ÔWe canÕt afford a rooftop chase!Õ Ð which never occurred to me anyway, because I wouldnÕt have wanted to do it. So their vision for the book was partly predicated on the fact that theycouldnÕt afford a rooftop chase and we had to Þnd something else, but that gave me something else for Bill Sikes and helped me tell the story I wanted to tell.
When I went in for And Then There Were None, Mathew Prichard [executive producer] said, ÔI think it would be great if we did it like Big Brother,Õ and I said, ÔThatÕs a terrible idea. Why would you do that? EveryoneÕs going to fuck up, no oneÕs going to have any fun, and you canÕt tell a satisfying story. I want to tell it like this,Õ and then talked until they felt so battered by this tsunami of verbiage that they meekly agreed. It was the same when I went up to meet Jo Rowling to talk about The Casual Vacancy. It was a nervy sort of meeting with quite a few people in there; Jo is obviously surrounded by people who manage her affairs, because how many hours in the day has the woman got, sheÕs busy. SheÕs also excellent, by the way. So I said, ÔThis is the story I want to tell. This is how I want to tell it. I want to do this, this, this and this to make sure we serve the story that I think you want to tell Ð which is the story of what happens to a teenage girl who no one gives a shit about: Tess of the dÕUrbervilles in a modern-daysetting.Õ There was a silence, because it was quite a hard-core pitch,but as we left we got a phone call saying, ÔYes, do it.Õ
I think if youÕve read a piece of work and you donÕt at some gut level have a passion for what you want to do for it, then you shouldnÕt be doing it, because ultimately youÕre the writer and your script has got to lead everything: ÔThereÕs something in this book that hasnÕt been seen before and thatÕs what IÕm going to tell.Õ With adaptation, youÕre changing a lot. YouÕre going to get inside the book and beat it up, andshake it and see what comes out, and Þnd the passionate heart at the centre of it Ð and if youÕre not leading the charge on that, then seriously, go home.
Do you usually produce an outline or treatment before you start writing the script?
Not if I can help it. IÕll sometimes write potted histories of the characters, so I know why they think the way they do and walk the way they do, but I hate outlines and I hate treatments. I really, reallycanÕt bear them. When I was new in my career and people would say, ÔCan you write a treatment?Õ I was like, ÔNo. Why donÕt you just commission a script? WhatÕs the matter with you?Õ How the hell is reading four pages Ð or beat by beat, or scene by scene Ð going to tell you how a script works? A script works in a completely different wayfrom how prose works Ð although I am very prose-y and write endless,very descriptive stage directions which probably annoy people Ð and if youÕre having to spend hours and hours writing paragraph after paragraph of whatÕs in the room and how the sunlight falls, itÕs a killer. I always feel desperately anguished on behalf of writers who are asked to do another pass on a treatment. You just feel your soul die. IÕm working with writers who are coming in to work on shows with me, and sometimes they want to do an outline, but I like to go straightinto script. I think most people feel released and trusted when you say,ÔLetÕs do a Þrst draft and see how you get on.Õ Prose doesnÕt reect the muscularity of how dialogue works. When youÕre into a scene and characters are opening their mouths and speaking, everything changes.It just becomes a different beast.
If youÕre o ered material to adapt which has been adapted previously, do you take account of any previous versions when writing yours?
IÕve got a rule, and itÕs a really Þrm rule, and itÕs been there since I started doing adaptations, which is that I donÕt watch any other adaptations when IÕm working. I didnÕt want to see David LeanÕs classic adaptation of Oliver Twist. The same with *Great Expectations*. All that mattered to me was the novel. There have been countless iterations of Oliver Twist, and I just wasnÕt interested in them Ð apartfrom the musical, which my mum took me to see when I was little and I completely fell in love with Oliver Reed and Þlthy London and theback streets. Every time Oliver was in the Brownlow house I thought, ÔThatÕs boring, why canÕt he go back to Bill and Nancy and Fagin?ÕWhen I wrote my version, I wanted to Þll the Brownlow house with danger and give the world of Fagin a sense of safety Ð and give the characters surrounding Oliver, like Fagin and Dodger, a humanity and a dignity. FaginÕs lair is the Þrst time Oliver gets a suit of clothes, itÕs the Þrst time he gets a bed for himself, itÕs the Þrst time anyone is actually kind to him; thereÕs a comfort there which heÕd never found before. And I wanted it to be not just where Oliver found a home, but where Dodger found a home, and to show DodgerÕs love of Fagin and his love of Nancy, so that by the end, when Dodger has lost everyone, all the people he loved Ð Fagin, Nancy, even Sikes Ð we realise that something beautiful has been broken by the cruelty of the state and that Dodger is going to suffer, and that he will now be more dangerous than Bill Sikes ever was.
If the author of the source material is living, do you nd it useful to have their input on the script?
No. When I was doing The Casual Vacancy, Jo stood right back from it. Obviously she read drafts, but she was very good at making sure her input didnÕt intrude on me writing. SheÕs got grace when sheÕs working with other writers, which is much appreciated. When it comes to working with the Christie estate, theyÕre representing the views of the estate, not of Agatha Christie, so thatÕs an interestingtightrope to walk. We donÕt know what she thinks, so you can only go on what the books offer up.
Is it easier to navigate notes from directors, producers and scripteditors when you have a piece of source material to measure the screenplay against?
No. ThereÕs this whole thing about, ÔWell, in the novel this happens,Õ and I go, ÔYeah, but thatÕs the letter of the novel, and if we do the letter of the novel weÕre going to have 37 hours of TV and every singlehour is going to make you want to open your wrists.Õ Because the book is different, and itÕs not what weÕre working on anymore. WeÕre working on what IÕve written, which is its own beast. So unless IÕve gone way off base and suddenly introduced an elephant or something,itÕs absolutely pointless to go back to the book. The dialogue is all different, some of the motivations are different, even some of the ways IÕve represented the characters are different. Witness for the Prosecution contained some overtly sexual scenes that werenÕt in the short story, but why shouldnÕt they be there? In that situation, of course they would be. IÕm quite an argumentative person, so if someone says, ÔWe canÕt do that.Õ I say, ÔYes, we can.Õ But IÕve also made them believe the story IÕm telling. IÕve told it with all the heartand the guts that I can, and IÕve really owned it and made it compelling. If people arenÕt compelled to follow the narrative youÕretelling, of course theyÕll go, ÔI donÕt know about this.Õ But if youÕve put everything into those early drafts and gone, ÔItÕs this twist and then this turn,Õ then people will buy it and trust you. People have got to trust you. The audience has to trust you, the actors have to trust you,and the people who are making your show have to trust you.
Have you ever started work on an adaptation and found it harder to adapt than you anticipated?
Oh, yeah, IÕve been sacked. Well, not sacked Ð Ôgone our separatewaysÕ. Sometimes thatÕs about personalities and power politics, and sometimes itÕs because I like to take more licence than people want me to take, and sometimes it just doesnÕt work and everyone agrees that IÕm not the right person for the project. My friend Tony Jordan says, ÔNever underestimate the power of no.Õ So, yeah, IÕve left projectsbecause IÕm not the writer they need. They thought I was, and I thought I could do it, and then everyone goes, ÔThis is a nightmare.LetÕs just call it quits,Õ and I go, ÔAll right. Bye-bye.Õ
Are there any screen adaptations which you think are especially good or you particularly admire?
There are ones which I think transcend the original books. One Flew Over the CuckooÕs Nest: I canÕt bear the book, itÕs loads of blokes thinking theyÕre great and hating women, but I remember beingprofoundly moved by the Þlm and it being a really important part of growing up. Midnight Cowboy, by Waldo Salt, is a phenomenaladaptation. Precious Bane, which Maggie Wadey adapted for the BBC ages ago, was fantastic. It was a Christmas treat, and me and my mum sat down to watch it, and my brothers, who were just great big lumps, were like, ÔWe ainÕt watching this bullshit,Õ and by the end of it they were like [mimes wiping tears away]. Good job! I loved *The English Patient*: so hard to do, but done amazingly. Rachel Bennette did a brilliant job of Zadie SmithÕs NW, I thought. Nick DearÕs adaptationof Persuasion is wonderful: sparse and very funny and reallyemotional Ð and people get dirty. Emma ThompsonÕs Sense and Sensibility is also wonderful and gets rid of that chocolate boxy thing. YouÕd be amazed how people still want things to be chocolate boxyand clean. ItÕs difÞcult enough to keep clean in the twenty-Þrstcentury; imagine what it would have been like then. All that stuff hasto be laundered by hand, and if you havenÕt got servants youÕre doingit yourself. ThatÕs the kind of thing I like to think about when IÕm writing these things: whoÕs emptying the chamber pot?

ADAPTING FICTION
Some writers try to include as much of the novel as possible by boiling scenes down to their essence. Others are more ruthless
in editing it down to a sort of greatest hits, but being true to the spirit of it. Do you favour either of those approaches?
There are always things that youÕve got to have. Oliver in the orphanage. Pip in the forge. But youÕre going to have to be ruthless. YouÕre going to have to slice people out. So with those great big novelsI always try to have a little rubric which informs how I respond to it, otherwise IÕll end up going down various highways and byways. With*Great Expectations* it was, ÔA boy sells his soul and buys it back bybecoming a man.Õ That keeps you focused on the internal *drive*, on what the story is. The Marshes are the story. Magwitch is the story. Satis House is the story. London is the story. So I donÕt think about how IÕm going to ruthlessly edit it down or how IÕm going to make it a greatest hits, I just think about how IÕm going to tell the central story. And sometimes IÕll entirely shift the focus of the storytelling.
If a novel has an unusual structure, would you try to reproduce that in adapting it?
ThatÕs quite a difÞcult question, because IÕve never been in the situation where someoneÕs handed me something that looks reallygroundbreaking and unconventional on the page. If it was somethingreally important to the way people responded to the story, I would absolutely try to represent that in the way I told the story. The way thestory is told is the story, as it were.
Do you try to avoid voiceover in adapting a rst-person narrative, or do you see it as another tool in the toolbox?
IÕm not keen on voiceover. I always think itÕs a white ag of surrender. I donÕt like being told things. Unless itÕs about that unreliable Þrst-person narrator, but most Þrst-person narrators are unreliable Ð thatÕs the deÞnition. Or unless someone is telling you a story and yousuddenly realise youÕre not getting a voiceover, youÕre gettingsomething else. IÕm writing something at the moment where you thinkyouÕre going to get a voiceover and it turns out not to be, but whether I can play with form like that and get away with it remains to be seen. Otherwise, IÕm not keen on it. [Dramatic voice] ÔAnd there I was, all alone in the world, lost and desolatedÉÕ DonÕt fucking tell me Ð show me!
Do you use the prose from the novel in writing the dialogue and stage directions, or do you put the whole thing in your own words?
Prose, no. Once IÕve got the characters and the story, itÕs my world and I say what happens. Dialogue, no, because once youÕve decided to frame the story in a particular way thereÕs no point replicating the dialogue. I might use a familiar phrase to give the audience a feelingthat theyÕre watching an adaptation of their beloved book and not something IÕve completely bastardised and made up. With Oliver Twist, there were two things I picked out from the book: one was FaginÕs Ômy dearÕ, and the other was that they call Oliver ÔNollyÕ.ThatÕs not really well known, so that was my nod to the source material. With *Great Expectations*, the phrase I picked out was Mrs JoeÕs, where sheÕs constantly complaining about raising the boy Ôby handÕ, and that was my nod.
Do you keep the novel beside you throughout the adaptationprocess, or do you try to internalise it and set it aside somewherealong the way?
Keeping the novel beside me: IÕve just got this image of someone with a really tidy desk and the novel sitting there full of post-it notes so that they can refer to it Ð and thatÕs not what my desk is like at all! What I tend to do is, IÕve read it and itÕs in there, but the images IÕve got, and the characters IÕve got, have superseded it. So itÕs alwayssomewhere in the room, but thereÕs a very strong chance that unless someone puts a gun to my head I wonÕt pick it up again, because IÕm just looking at my script.
How much pressure do you feel when adapting a well-known andmuch-loved novel, knowing that you wonÕt be able to please everyone with the nished product?
I couldnÕt give a shit. But it is fascinating to me how passionatelypeople feel a sense of ownership over a novel. TheyÕve had this relationship with it at some point in their youth, or they picked it off the shelf when they had mumps and were comforted by its rhythms, even though itÕs the darkest story about death you can possiblyimagine. So itÕs not just how we read a book thatÕs important when it comes to adaptation, itÕs how we remember how we read a book. *Great Expectations* seems to really chime with a lot of people, and particularly chime with people who imagine themselves to have been betrayed by a girl, like Pip Ð so that was their experience of reading it. I remember my brother saying something like, ÔEstella is all the girlswho ever blew you out.Õ I said, ÔSheÕs a stolen child!Õ He said, ÔWhat?Õ I said, ÔWhy are you reading it like that, where itÕs all about yourexperience and itÕs not about anything else?Õ My brother sort of went, ÔOh, yeah,Õ but a lot of people become really defensive about novels:ÔYouÕve done it wrong!Õ
People reacted really badly to the casting of Gillian Anderson in *Great Expectations*: ÔMiss Havisham is way too young.Õ Well, show me the page where it says how old she is. ÔSheÕs always been like that.Õ Yes, because we always like to think that women soaked in grief are grotesques. But if you make her a grotesque, why does Pip fall under her spell? If you make her a grotesque, then whatÕs happening in that house, the obscenely sexualised education sheÕs giving to Estella to get her revenge, doesnÕt have the power it should have. I thought Gillian was fantastic and played the role exactly as IÕd written it, as this strange arrested child with this slightly Mandarin quality to the way she speaks, so youÕre never quite sure what it is sheÕs saying. ItÕs couched in a layered secrecy, so poor Pip thinks he hears one version and Estella knows another was intended. You need someone that dangerous to infect these childrenÕs lives. People were also very angry Ð people with minimal conceptual grasp or understanding of history Ð about the fact that we cast Sophie Okonedo in Oliver Twist: ÔBBC box-ticking, no black people in Victorian England, blah, blah, blah.Õ I never think about actors when IÕm writing, I just think about the character, but the minute I started writing Nancy I knew I wanted Sophie, for that grace and that Þre and that glorious complexhumanity.
So, yeah, people get very involved in their personal responses to particular novels, so much so that when they see something that doesnÕt represent their ideal version, their reaction is *wild*lydisproportionate. I did a panel once about adaptation and Dickens, and a woman shot her hand up and said to me, ÔI got up really early and got a coach to London just to tell you that you RUINED this book! You ruined it for me and I can never read it AGAIN!Õ To be honest, the Christie fandom can be pretty strange, too. Have you ever been to a Christie festival? I went to one in Dorset or somewhere, and when I walked into the hotel where I was staying I saw two Poirots sharing a pack of barbecued beef crisps. Really odd.

CASE STUDY: *GREAT EXPECTATIONS*
UK, 2011 ¥ Directed by Brian Kirk ¥ Produced by GeorgeOrmond ¥ Screenplay by Sarah Phelps, based on the novel byCharles Dickens ¥ Cast: Douglas Booth (Pip), Ray Winstone (Abel Magwitch), Gillian Anderson (Miss Havisham), David Suchet (Jaggers), Tom Burke (Bentley Drummle), Shaun Dooley(Joe Gargery), Vanessa Kirby (Estella), Susan Lynch (Molly),Jack Roth (Dolge Orlick), Claire Rushbrook (Mrs Joe)
YouÕve dramatised two books by Dickens: *Great Expectations* and Oliver Twist. As an adapter, what attracts you to his work?
I never liked Dickens the man until I read Claire TomalinÕs biographyof him, when I realised something that made my heart break for him a little bit and made me understand him a whole lot more: he was bipolar. He soaks his head in iced water because he canÕt sleep, and hewalks and walks and walks and walks, and heÕs either manic or heÕs so depressed he can barely get out of bed. The thing that alwaysfascinated me was the way he treated his wife, which was appalling Ð and I kept wondering why. He wouldnÕt have been the Þrst man to run two households, and itÕs not as if he made his relationship with Ellen Ternan public Ð in fact, he went to ludicrous lengths to keep it secret. But he went to equally ludicrous lengths to break up his household, toprint terrible things about his wife, to refuse to speak to his children if they carried on a relationship with their mother. I was thinking aboutthis for ages, and thinking about the way his brain would teem with life and then just stop, and I realised that he broke up his householdfor one reason and one reason only: because he needed chaos to write. And what comes out of that appalling time is all the later novels Ð *Great Expectations*, Dombey and Son Ð the great novels of grief and loss and desperate need for forgiveness.
ThereÕs a part of him that was always writing the same story Ð how we try to Þnd tenderness amidst cruelty Ð but if heÕd written Oliver Twist at the same time as he wrote *Great Expectations*, I think heÕd have told that story in a very different way. It would have been much more about what it means to be brave, not to be done to, but to do. Everything in Oliver Twist is being done to: heÕs always crying and itÕs very sentimental. Obviously it was the Þrst time, more or less, that a child had been the focus of a book, and you canÕt deny DickensÕ crusading zeal, but Oliver does collapse under the weight of his own story and he is a whining little sod. There were points where I gotreally frustrated with the book Ð every time Dickens describes Fagin as a repulsive wizened Jew, you want to say ÔFuck offÕ and throw the thing across the room Ð then on the next page he hits you with some amazing piece of description and you go, ÔYou absolute bastard, IÕm going to have to tolerate you.Õ But youÕre watching a young man Þnding his way as a writer, I think, and by the time you get to the other end of his life you can see a different level of achievement.
My dad loved Dickens and loved *Great Expectations* Ð so much so that we used to call him the Aged P Ð and he encouraged me to read it because of Magwitch and because of everything Magwitch is trying tomake better for that moment on the marshes. The orphan Pip has these two extraordinary fathers in Joe Gargery and in Magwitch, and itÕs so moving because you watch him lose and regain his soul. You watch him change from being obsessed with Estella and obsessed withBentley Drummle and obsessed with money, to being ashamed of Joe and ashamed of Magwitch and disgusted by the money, to lookingdown the river and seeing the danger coming MagwitchÕs way and knowing he has to save him. I defy anyone not to be moved byMagwitchÕs death, and by Pip holding his hand and saying, ÔI know your daughter, and I love her,Õ and Magwitch dying with that knowledge, and with that promise. And by Pip walking over the hillside and Þnding Joe alone in the forge and saying, ÔIÕm so sorry,Õ and Joe putting his arm around him and forgiving him after everything. What a miracle.
Before it became a title in its own right, ÔDickensianÕ was the word used to describe DickensÕ ctional world and its screen representations, suggesting a cosy, familiar, pantomime reduction of his novelsÕ complexities. What does that mean to you in terms of your approach to adapting him? I already get the sense that itÕs a concept you would runÉ
Éa million miles from. ItÕs like ÔAustenesqueÕ, where everyone is wearing Empire-line dresses and no one ever gets muddy. I Þnd it intensely problematic, because I think it stops being about the booksand starts being about the perceived world of the books, which to me feels less like storytelling and more like marketing. The authors getlost. I feel the same way about the Christie world. Writers donÕt care if they sell fudge. Writers want to be read and to feel like what theyÕvegot to say is important. Christie, Dickens, Austen, the Bronts, theyÕvebecome these huge great shibboleths of themselves, and youÕve got to strip away the heritage trail and the tea towels and all the other extraneous bollocks surrounding them and say, ÔWhatÕs the storyabout?Õ I actually wrote a load of episodes for the series Dickensian and loved doing it. I even wrote an episode which the Daily Mail approved of, which made me feel pretty dodgy. But again, one of the pleasures of it was, ÔYou think you know these characters? Well, what if we do this with them? What if we tell their origin stories?Õ Like, how did Lady Dedlock become Lady Dedlock, what happened to make her this character? And thatÕs a buzz, because youÕre lifting it out of that world of, ÔThis is the way itÕs always been and always must be,Õ which is simply a stranglehold that stops you inventing. ÔThatÕs not Dickensian. ThatÕs not Austenesque.Õ Who cares?
*Great Expectations* was another three-part adaptation, though the book itself seems to demand that, breaking down into three distinct acts: the marshes and Satis House; Pip coming into his expectations; and the nal reckoning. Was there ever any question that it would be anything other than three parts?
It does naturally split into three, but it all has to have a thread Ð and when everything hits you at the end, you want it to hit with the impact of when it Þrst happened: the moment that little boy unwraps the pie and says, ÔI thought you might be hungry.Õ Again, I changedthat from the book. In the book, Magwitch asks Pip to bring him foodand a Þle. In my version, he gets away from the prison boat and has toget the shackles off and what he puts in his belly is low down his listof priorities. That kid has got just one mission, and thatÕs to get him a Þle. But Pip, out of the goodness of his heart, sees a man starving and thinks, ÔIÕve got his Þle, but look, thereÕs this pie.Õ It was a bit like Oliver Twist. In the book heÕs bullied into asking for more, but in myversion he does it because heÕs brave, because he knows itÕs the right thing to do Ð which makes us love him and believe in him. I wanted Pip to be the same: we have to believe in him. WeÕre going to follow him down a very dark path and heÕs going to make us question him, so weÕve got to remember that his heart is good. When Joe welds MagwitchÕs shackles and the soldiers try to give him money, he says, ÔItÕs a poor job and I donÕt want paying for it.Õ He sees a soul in pain and he responds, and I wanted Pip to be enough of JoeÕs boy that he would do the same, because that would mean that wherever Magwitch went he would carry that little boyÕs face with him. HeÕd be sent to the other end of the world, make an absolute fortune and still go, ÔThat kid. That kid.Õ The thing that powers Magwitch is grief over his lost child and trying to Þll the gaping maw of that loss, and when Pip holds out that piece of pie on a shaking hand you can see that this is his new child. That feels right to me, that feels right to me about Dickens. Not Dickensian, but Dickens.
*Great Expectations* contains fewer characters than some of DickensÕ novels, but still more than a three-hour drama can encompass. How did you decide which ones to keep and which to cut? Why Orlick, for example, but no Biddy Ð and no Aged P?
Why would you have Biddy? Biddy is always hauling Pip over the coals and wagging her Þnger and being really pompous. Then Pipthinks heÕll marry her but sheÕs now married Joe, which makes me feel differently about Joe, and that wasnÕt the way I wanted to write Joe. In the book, Joe just rolls with the punches, so I changed him and gave him a very different intelligence, which I felt was more digniÞedin terms of his relationship to Pip. So Biddy was neither here nor there. Orlick was more important for me because I wanted Orlick to really hate Pip, and the reason Orlick really hated Pip was that he really loved Joe. Joe was like a dad and Joe was kind. I wanted myOrlick to be beside himself when he was sent away and uprooted, so that by the time he betrayed Pip there was a reason for him to do it. Why no Aged P? CouldnÕt Þt him in. It would have been nice, but it takes you out of PipÕs world and takes you somewhere else. ThatÕs all well and good if youÕve got to Þll a load of instalments in a paper and then read them to an audience who want a few laughs, but in this it would have been a distraction. Someone said to me, ÔYouÕve taken all the humour out of it.Õ Well, Christ almighty. Lies, betrayal, a broken heart, a stolen child, an obscenely sexualised education and a man whoÕs going to hang. Tell me what the fucking joke is. Because Dickens doesnÕt get it and neither do I. He tells a joke to keep people listening. ItÕs a palate cleanser. ItÕs a glass of water between the dark cups of coffee. But I donÕt have to do that because IÕve only got three hours and I donÕt think itÕs funny. I want to Þnd the beauty and the tenderness in it more than I want to Þnd some pointless joke.
Given all the familiar scenes and characters you had to Ð or chose to Ð cut, itÕs noticeable when you put something new in: the scene where Drummle takes Pip to a brothel, for example, which feels like the sort of scene Dickens might have written had he been allowed to.
In the book, Bentley Drummle doesnÕt really exist beyond what peoplesay about him, which is that heÕs spoilt and violent and that Estella is going to marry him. I wanted to write something different. When Pip looks at Drummle he sees a rock god Ð he sees what he wants to be. But when Drummle looks at Pip he sees a rival Ð and does what all bullies do, which is to Þnd his weak spot. Drummle is going to go to a brothel, heÕs going to take Pip, and Pip is going to be veryembarrassed Ð and realise that heÕs not like this person. Pip tries to impress Drummle, but will always be out of step with him, because Drummle is clothed in privilege and Pip will never understand entitlement. Drummle is a man who can sit back and say, ÔIÕll have her,her and her,Õ because he believes that esh, womenÕs esh, is a commodity to be bought and sold Ð which makes him reallydangerous to Estella. Also, because *Great Expectations* is about sex and about taking revenge through sex, I wanted to sexualise their adult life as men and to equate the Victorian euphemism for ejaculation, ÔspendÕ, with what Pip does with this new money that isnÕthis: he spends it, to pass in society. And when they go to that brothel, none of the women are white, which is a nod to the way the Victorians felt towards the world at large: itÕs ours and weÕll pay a farthing and weÕll do what we like and walk away. So itÕs about money, itÕs about class, itÕs about gender Ð itÕs about saying that Estella has absolutely no power whatsoever. You hear Jaggers say later, ÔWe hope the lady will come out on top,Õ and you know thatÕs not whatÕs going to happen. SheÕs sold into a terrible marriage with a violent man that is absolute hell, and for what? Nothing. That felt like a much more potent way to tell the story.
Dickens changed the original, downbeat ending of his novel to bring Pip and Estella together, albeit in a way which makes you doubt whether theyÕll stay together. What were your thoughts in approaching the ending and its subtle ambiguities?
I really wanted to honour what was at the heart of that book, which is damage: the damage to Miss Havisham and how it impacts so massively on everyone, and what happens when she becomes theatrically in love with that damage. In the book, she sets Þre to herself by accident, and I have her do it on purpose when she realises that she could have been happy, she could have had a house full of children. All she had to do was fall out of love with her vengeance Ð but now itÕs too late. ItÕs a really important moment when Estella says, ÔI am your blade. You cannot now complain if you also feel the hurt.Õ
Having created this cold little woman and deprived her of the opportunity for happiness, there is no joy in it, only the savagerealisation that you have destroyed yourself with your self-obsession and sent your daughter out into the world to be brutalised. So I foundthe two endings really intriguing, and I always preferred the one in which that fracture cannot be overcome. But when I came to writingmy ending, because IÕd written Miss Havisham and Joe and Magwitchin a particular way, I wanted Pip and Estella to feel that they could step out of that damage and live.
Our set designer did a brilliant thing for Miss HavishamÕs dressing room. There was this lovely chinoiserie jewellery box with little opendrawers, and in the top one was coiled a tiny string of seed pearls with a golden clasp. The camera never saw it, but we all knew it was there. And it was as if Miss Havisham had stood in front of the mirror getting ready for her wedding day, and had put her hand in there to pick up these pearls that an adoring, long dead parent might have given her on a signiÞcant birthday, and she would put them around her neck so that they were with her when she was happy, when she gotmarried, and just as she was about to pull them out the letter arrived and they were dropped back in and they never moved again. That kind of detail just thrills me, because it gives the story heart and meaning and signiÞcance and makes the suffering real. This reallyhappened and her heart broke, and her humiliation was so intense that she said to Jaggers, ÔI want a child,Õ and Jaggers found her a child, and this is what came out of it. I wrote a scene that never went in Ð which didnÕt matter, because I knew it was there Ð where you had this carriage bucketing through the night and this man holding still a little bundle on the seat, and the carriage draws up outside a house and the man lifts down this struggling bundle, drags it down cobwebbed corridors, through the green baize door where the servants enter, into this hallway lit with candles and swagged with wedding owers, where this strange woman stands on the stairs in her wedding gown.She pulls back the blanket from this child, covered in dirt and bruises,hair snarled and spitting like a *wild*cat, and takes the childÕs chin and tilts it to the light and sees how beautiful she already is and says, ÔVery well. YouÕll do.Õ
ThereÕs a scene from the book that also never went in, but reallyinformed my reaction and my adaptation, which is where Pip is exploring the back of Satis House and wanders into this brewery andlooks up and thinks he sees Miss Havisham hanging in her bridal dress Ð and itÕs not her, itÕs hop sacks. ItÕs a really arresting image. Andthe other image that really informs the cruelty and sadism that thrumsbelow the surface of the story is of JaggersÕ servant, Molly. Jaggersand all those unbearable, entitled, Boris Johnson-style young men are sitting around the table having dinner and Molly is serving them, andhe grabs her hands and drags them into the light and her arms are covered in scars: ÔThey were trying to take my life.Õ The mother of Estella. The mother of a stolen child. ThatÕs the story I wanted to tell.ThatÕs the story that matters. What it means to lose what you love.

CASE STUDY: AND THEN THERE WERE NONE
UK, 2015 ¥ Directed by Craig Viveiros ¥ Produced by Abi Bach
¥ Screenplay by Sarah Phelps, based on the novel by Agatha Christie ¥ Cast: Douglas Booth (Anthony Marston), Charles Dance (Justice Lawrence Wargrave), Maeve Dermody (Vera Claythorne), Burn Gorman (Detective Sergeant William Blore),Anna Maxwell Martin (Ethel Rogers), Sam Neill (General John Macarthur), Miranda Richardson (Emily Brent), Toby Stephens (Doctor Edward Armstrong), Noah Taylor (Thomas Rogers),Aidan Turner (Philip Lombard)
First Dickens, then Christie. Again, is there something about herstyle or her stories which particularly attracts you?
IÕd never read any Agatha Christie before I read And Then There Were None. I always thought they were just cosy parlour games. ThereÕs a body on the oor and no one really cares; itÕs just a catalyst for someone to come in and be incredibly clever. Now, itÕs interesting that the someones who come in are all outsiders. Poirot is an outsider, Marple is an outsider Ð sheÕs a spinster, heÕs a refugee. But the point is that they come in and they make everything safe and they restore your equilibrium and they return you to a sense of Englishness. What shocked and surprised me aboutÊAndÊThen There Were None, which was the Þrst one I did, was just how cold it was. It felt like Greek tragedy, because itÕs remorseless: action begets action begets action begets action. YouÕre going to be ended because of the thing you did back there Ð and everything thatÕs happened from that point on has led you here.ÊSo in some ways it is a parlour game Ð the judge, the general, the butler, the playboy, the mercenary, all in this closed-roommurder mystery Ð but you can also look at it from other perspectives. You could see it as a portrait of a psychopath, or you could see it as a disquisition into the nature of guilt, or you could see it as a thoroughly subversive portrait of Britain as it stands on the edge of being calamitously pitched into another world war: all these people on this rock cut off from the mainland, and all they have is each other and the mores they live by, and coming towards them across the sea like the unblinking eye of God is their death.
The book was written and published in the summer of 1939 and I really wanted to set it at that time, because I thought it was a great way to write about what war does to people and how it changes everyone. Here you all are in this timeÊof national crisis, when youshould stand together and say, ÔWe know who you are because youspeak with this accent and you fulÞl this role, which is how society is ordered and what we are all Þghting for, and because we know who you are, we know who we are.Õ And then you Þnd out that theyÕre all murderers. These people who you should trust have all done something terrible and not one of them has suffered. If it wasnÕt for this moment right here, right now, all of them would carry on living.So it felt like a really subversive way to say, ÔYou think you know Englishness? You think you know nationality? You think you know identity? You donÕt.Õ It was really exciting when we cast an Irish actor as Philip Lombard, because in that world if someone in the house has a gun and that person has an English accent, even if they are a mercenary and have been doing God knows what in the dark places ofthe world, you might imagine that theyÕd be on your side. But an Irishman with a service revolver in 1939 is a completely different kettle of Þsh. WhatÕs he been doing? WhoÕs he been a soldier for? I wanted to show that the world was changing, that the past is not watching people through the wrong end of the telescope, gesticulating.ItÕs now, itÕs us, without Wi-Fi. ItÕs that simple.
I also wanted to write something terrifying: where youÕre terriÞed of the thing youÕve done, but thereÕs a reason you did it, and doing it seemed totally reasonable to you at the time. You can regret it, but it came out of passion, it came out of loathing, it came out of jealousy, it came out of being angry that you were vulnerable. It felt like a thrilling way to write about what happens to us when the lights go off. A lot of what I write is about what happens to us when the lights go off, when everything drops away. When you ip a switch and itÕs not working, when you turn a tap and the water doesnÕt come out, when you pick up a phone and itÕs dead. What happens to us then? So I loved that remorselessness: youÕre going to get hunted down and youÕre not going to get away. There is no plea, there is no mitigation,there is no agency with which you can barter for clemency. ItÕs death, thatÕs it, here it comes Ð brace your fucking self.
Was it your decision to adapt the book in three episodes and wasit always clear where those episodes would fall?
I like threes. Beginning, middle, end. Thesis, antithesis, synthesis. ItÕs a really satisfying way to write a drama, and a really satisfying way to watch a drama. Once you start going into peopleÕs backgroundsthereÕs so much material, and once you start building a world youcould build it for hours. Witness for the Prosecution was two episodes, but there were times when I thought, ÔI could do with another hour of this.Õ Endlessly self-indulgent. With this, I decided where the breaks would come in the scripts, but they came out differently when it was done, especially between episodes one and two. Sometimes, for whatever reason, the way youÕve written something hasnÕt been impactful on screen, hasnÕt given you that ÔOoh!Õ moment, so you play around with that in the edit. But I was always aware of where and how I wanted to leave things, and what was going to move the story on to the next phase.
And did you try to give each episode its own three-act structure?
I donÕt think like that. Loads of people have tried to make me think like that and itÕs a waste of time. Someone once said to me, ÔWhatÕs the inciting incident?Õ I was like, ÔDonÕt ever say that to me again.Fuck off.Õ And they were like, ÔI was just trying to help.Õ And I was like, ÔBut now I hate you. How is that helpful?Õ I sometimes go and talk to people who are doing writing courses and they talk like that, and I just think, ÔThis is horrible.Õ I never think about structure like that. The story tells you what the structure is. IÕm working with another writer at the moment who thinks in terms of a Þve-act structure for an hourÕs worth of script, and I think, ÔBut that onlygives you ten minutes per act.Õ I cannot make myself think like that. Itinfuriates people, but I can only think the way I think.
Christie is better known for her plots than her dialogue, and youÕve been noticeably more faithful to the former than the latter.ThereÕs swearing, for starters.
Sometimes even the plots make you go, ÔReally?Õ I donÕt write anything which is just plot, because just plot is nothing. ItÕs emptyÞgures transitioning from one position to another. So I felt, with this, the plot will only work, and work satisfyingly and frighteningly and in a new way, if you have pumped your characters full of blood, pumped them full of the things they have been trying to keep secret from themselves Ð like the surgeonÕs trembling hand and his PTSD from having been in a front-line Þeld hospital in World War I. They sit there all barbed and polite with their lies Þzzing beneath the surface, but once a few people have died they get dirty and they get nasty. ItÕs what happens when people are scared. ItÕs what happens when they start to unravel. ItÕs part of the downward spiral. If you think someone is trying to kill you, youÕre going to start to swear, surely? I would. Do you want to hear a funny story?
Absolutely.
IÕve got to the end of the scripts, IÕm very proud of them, everyoneÕs very excited by them. WeÕve got a brilliant cast and a really great director and weÕre at the read-through. ItÕs a huge room and itÕs crammed full of people and the last line IÕd written for Philip Lombard Ð before Vera Claythorne shoots him, when sheÕs holding the gun on him Ð was for him to come really close to her and reach out and take the gun and say, ÔThe safety catch, you stupid cunt.Õ And you thought: he will kill you. It doesnÕt matter that youÕve had sex. It doesnÕt matter that youÕve danced cheek to cheek. He will kill you.Later on, a researcher came back and said service revolvers didnÕt have safety catches, but thatÕs beside the point. It read brilliantly and it got a shiver around the room. So then we had the read-through notes, and the BBC exec said, ÔIÕve got one note. YouÕve got to take ÒcuntÓ out.Õ I was absolutely livid: ÔWhat are you talking about? DonÕt be ridiculous! ItÕs completely character-*drive*n. ItÕs a huge moment in the script!Õ Ranting and raving for about 15 minutes. And this poor guywent, ÔSarah, itÕs Christmas. You canÕt have ÒcuntÓ at Christmas.Õ It was brilliant. Thirty people in a room all crying with laughter. And I was like, ÔFine. IÕm not going to argue with you anymore. Because I lose the line but gain one hell of an anecdote.Õ
The opening scenes introducing the characters, some of them receiving instructions and payment from a seedy intermediary, have a jauntier tone than what follows. Was there a sense of giving the audience a little of what they expect before the darkness draws in?
I didnÕt even think about it like that. I just wanted the uneasiness to coil inside you and the signiÞcance of that character to come out later on. It had a levity which was just going to be destroyed. Initially, whenVera says, ÔI suffer because that little boy died in my care,Õ you want tobelieve her, so that later you go, ÔJesus Christ, she planned this, she calculated it down to the last moment.Õ And when she says sheÕs not a woman who enjoys being looked at, she is a woman who enjoys being looked at. The Þrst image I had when I read And Then There Were None, the Þrst thing that stuck in my mind, was of this white-limbed woman in a red bathing suit in front of a white, folded geometrichouse, lifting her head and catching the eye of this man, and yourealise: sheÕs been lying about who she is and thereÕs no end to her deviousness Ð and she nearly gets away with it. I love that last line Igave to her: ÔTheyÕll believe me. They believed me last time!Õ A few of the others waver: the policeman, Blore, feels guilt; the soldier, Macarthur, feels guilt. Vera doesnÕt. SheÕs sorry she lost her lover. SheÕs sorry her plan didnÕt work. But sheÕs not sorry about that little boy. ThatÕs an exciting way to write women. Not sorry. No guilt.
Most of the charactersÕ backstories, the crimes theyÕre called to account for, youÕve lifted intact from the novel, but some youÕve made changes to. How did you determine which ones to change?
It was the ones which were confusing or werenÕt impactful. VeraÕs is pretty much intact. I changed the tone and inference of others. BloreÕs was the one I really changed around. In the book, Blore has lied to send someone to prison, and I thought, ÔYou donÕt want that, you want something direct,Õ so in my version we see him kick a young man to death, and that young man is gay. Everyone was like, ÔHeÕs obviously a self-hating gay man.Õ Now, there is a self-hating gay person in my version, but thatÕs Emily Brent, and thatÕs why she throws that girl out, because sheÕs got pregnant, and Emily Brent canÕt bear to think of her housemaid, whose pricked Þnger she takes to her lips, being ridden. She throws her out not because of religion, but because of violent and confused sexual emotions, but no one seemed to pick up on that. With Blore, itÕs because he saw someone more vulnerable than he was, and more lonely, and he found it unbearable, so he had to kill him to feel better. I wanted it to be something that you would feel such guilt over, such hurt. That you do things you donÕt even understand and carry them around as a burden. You sit on your allotment and eat your radishes and wish for catharsis, but itÕs not going to come.
One of the biggest changes is to the ending: no ba ed police, no message in a bottle explaining the plot. Why did you eliminate all that?
Because itÕs boring.
But doesnÕt it leave a question about how the murderer knew all of the victimsÕ backstories?
You do get Wargrave explaining, donÕt you, the fact that heÕs a judgewho gets to oversee all these sorts of things? I just didnÕt want to drag on about it. ItÕs like that thing about voiceover. YouÕve got to make it happen in the moment. YouÕve got to make it happen in that Þnal conversation between Vera and Wargrave. You know, ÔLeft all alone, he went and hanged himself and then there were none.Õ WeÕve set upthe thing about Wargrave taking painkillers and the doctor, Armstrong, recognising him because the guy he shares a clinic with is a cancer specialist, so you know heÕs been incredibly ill. But the bigthing, of course, is that youÕve got to make people believe that heÕs dead, so when he walks through the door you go, ÔFucking hell!Õ ThatÕs really important. I did play with the idea of the police turningup, but it became a little bit Agatha Christie and a lot less shocking. ThereÕs so much cruelty and calculation in what Wargrave sets up, itÕs all so solipsistic and self-aggrandising, that for there to be a kind of, ÔAnd now IÕm going to explain how I did it,Õ would have made it a bit pathetic. I thought that actually this was WargraveÕs gift to the audience: ÔI know, and you know, but they will never know, and it will *drive* them mad for years.Õ And itÕs a hell of a way to end a Christmas show, isnÕt it, someoneÕs brains running down the wall? Happyfucking Christmas!


ABOUT THE AUTHOR

ALISTAIR OWEN is the author of Smoking in Bed: Conversations with Bruce Robinson (one of David HareÕs Books of the Year in the Guardian), Story and Character: Interviews with British Screenwriters and Hampton on Hampton (one of Craig RaineÕs Books of the Year inthe Observer). He has written original and adapted screenplays and stageplays, onspec and to commission; contributed Þlmmaker interviews to Creative Screenwriting and Þlm book reviews to the Independent on Sunday; and recently published his Þrst novel, The Vetting Of. Alistair has chaired Q&A events at the Hay Festival, EdinburghInternational Book Festival and London ScreenwritersÕ Festival; and his platform with Christopher Hampton in the Lyttelton Theatre tocelebrate FaberÕs 75th anniversary was published in the anthologyFaber Playwrights at the National Theatre.
